                     DITORIAL  
                     
 
 
 
ELCOME TO THE SPRING 2025 issue of Mythlore—with a  page count 
within reasonable bounds this time ! A few bits of Mythlore business 
before I introduce the contents.  Dr. Kris Swank, a regular contributor to 
Mythlore, has joined the journal’s board with this issue. We welcome her wide-
ranging expertise on many of the authors you encounter in these pages , as well 
as their sources and inspirations in Celtic, folk, medieval, and Arthurian 
materials. And over the summer, John Rosegrant, also a frequent contributor to 
the journal, will begin assisting with the Reviews column. 
As I move towards retirement from librarianship (but not from editing 
this journal), I would like to spend more time working with students and 
scholars who want to develop their expertise in editing. Please contact me if you 
are interested in a short-tem internship of one or two semesters, or a longer-term 
committtment as editorial assistant. 
While the majority of readers will first encounter Mythlore as individual 
articles on our website or in a library database, I still  enjoy arranging the 
contents of each issue in some sort of logical or thematic progression  which 
rewards the reader willing to start from the beginning and go on to the end. This 
issue is no exception.  The first eight articles concern issues of fantasy and 
theology, some with a global perspective ; taken together, one can see ways in 
which they might be in dialogue with each oth er. The groundwork is first laid 
with Matthew Thompson-Handell’s bibliographic essay on the history of pagan, 
Christian, and blended interpretations of the religious system and values of 
Middle-earth. Tom Emanuel then addresses the sometimes -uncomfortable 
meta-issue of fandom  as a religion, proposing a framework that makes 
theological sense of the custom of visiting Tolkien’s grave.  
Then we move into the a pplication of specific concepts and theories . 
Eric Reinders examines Chinese and Thai  translations of the Ainulindalë, 
drawing interesting parallels with translations of the Christian bible into those 
languages. René Girard’s theory of imitative desire is used by Clinton Manley 
to dissect the Un -man’s strategy in Lewis’s Perelandra. Matthew McKenna 
applies G.K. Chesterton’s ideas about free will and oath -taking to the Oath of 
Fëanor, and Brett Roscoe shows how Lewis depicts the fear of God through his 
descriptions of Aslan. Taylor Driggers  uses the tenets of queer theology to 
illuminate Diane Duane’s The Tale of the Five  series. And Ishani Anwesha Joshi 
W 
Editorial  
4  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
and Sathyaraj Venketasen  discuss an example of the adaptation of 
mythic/religious symbolism in art that responded to the global crisis of COVID-19. 
The remaining four papers address issues of medievalisms in fantasy. 
Mark-Eliot Finley finds echoes of Beowulf in the Harry Potter series. Maria 
Damkjær’s exploration of book culture in Martin’s A Song of Fire and Ice provokes 
us to think about the fragility of archives more generally  (and joins the 
conversation started in Loremasters and Libraries in Fantasy and Science Fiction  
(Mythopoeic Press, 2022). Gavin S. Davis looks at how the importance of swords 
in different Middle-earth cultures represents different mediavalisms, and Laura 
N. Van Dyke explores the medieval immersion fantasy in Garner’s Elidor as it 
contrasts, deliberately, with Narnia. 
Our Notes in this issue are a  mixed bag , but all concerned with  the 
Inklings and the Inklings-adjacent. Łucasz Neubaur makes a n addition to the 
resources on Tolkien’s scholarly biography; Matthew Thompson-Handell finds 
a Miltonian parallel for a passage in a version of Túrin’s story. G. Connor Salter 
examines elements of the contes cruel in Lewis. Ian F. Alexander takes us behind 
the scenes of online reference sources on Tolkien, and Salter and Clark Sheldon 
reveal more material from the Wade’s manuscript files on Douglas Gresham and 
Joy Davidman . Finally, Robin Reid provides another thoughtful response to 
Dylan Henderson’s “‘ A Bleak, Barren Land’” (Mythlore #143); good material to 
read before our next Online Midsummer Seminar celebrating the 1 0th 
anniversary of the MythPress essay collection Perilous and Fair. 
 We close, as usual, with an extensive selection  of reviews of books 
about the Inklings, other fantasists, fantasy in general, and a few less -easily-
classified items which may be of interest to Mythlore readers. 
If you would like to keep up with news relating to Mythlore, please 
follow us on Facebook, where we post advance notice of papers accepted for 
upcoming issues, lists of items available for review, and so forth. In addition to 
the members of the Mythlore Editorial Advisory Board, the Mythopoeic Society 
Council of Stewards, and our ever -dependable referees, I’d also like to express 
my continuing gratitude to Phillip Fitzsimmons, University Archivist and 
Special Collections Librarian at Southwestern Oklahoma S tate University 
Libraries and our Administrator for Mythlore and Mythopoeic Society Archives, 
who directs the team adding content to dc.swosu.edu/mythlore/, and to his 
assistant Ben Dressler. My thanks also to David L. Emerson for moral support, 
eagle-eyed proofreading, and keeping the editor optimally caffeinated. As well 
I would like to gratefully acknowledge the assistance of Paul Irwin and Leslie 
Donovan with editing both the contributions themselves and the works cited 
lists to match MLA 9 style. 
 
—Janet Brennan Croft
  
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  5 
 
 
                     O RAGNARÖK , NO ARMAGEDDON :  
                         PAGAN AND CHRISTIAN INTERPRETATIONS  
                                   OF THE LORD OF THE RINGS  
 
                                  MATTHEW THOMPSON -HANDELL  
 
 
N HER 2014 ARTICLE “BUT WHAT DID HE REALLY MEAN?,” Verlyn Flieger refers 
to conflicting interpretations of The Lord of the Rings, citing by way of example 
the appeal of the work to neo -pagans (“an alternative to the dreary realism of 
mainstream culture”) and to Christians (“an evangelical message in its imagery 
of stars and light and bread and sacrifice”) (149). Flieger argues that such 
conflicting interpret ations are a result, at least in part, of ambiguous and 
contradictory statements by Tolkien, statements which are indicative of 
unresolved conflicts within the man himself. One of the three topics examined 
by Flieger, as she develops her argument, is the question of intentional 
Christianity in Tolkien’s fiction. The focus here is on The Lord of the Rings , and 
Flieger looks at a range of statements by Tolkien which are anywhere from 
inconsistent to incompatible with each other. Examined particularly closely are 
Tolkien’s words to Robert Murray, to whom he wrote that The Lord of the Rings 
was “a fundamentally religious and Catholic work; unconsciously so at first, but 
consciously in the revision.” However, Tolkien goes on to speak of his deliberate 
omission of overtly religious elements. Flieger suggests that such ambiguity has 
led to the same statement being used to support contradictory positions: Joseph 
Pearce saying that The Lord of the Rings  is “theologically orthodox” and Patrick 
Curry that the work has a blend of “Christian, pagan, and humanist 
ingredients.” Flieger then highlights the incongruity between Tolkien telling 
Murray about conscious revision of his work and then (a year later) telling W.H. 
Auden that there was “very little […] conscious […] intention in mind at any 
point” (150–2). Looking closely at these, and other statements, Flieger describes 
Tolkien’s “yes-but-not-yes tactics” as a means of responding to a wide range of 
readers without upsetting any of them (153 –4). This is however more than just 
people-pleasing—the fact that Tolkien “trimmed his sails to meet winds from 
different directions” reveals “a deep ambivalence in the man that produced a 
creative tension in his fiction” (162). 
Flieger revisited this theme in “The Arch and the Keystone” (her Guest 
of Honor address at Mythcon 50 in 2019), though with a subtle, but significant 
shift in the argument. In the earlier “But What Did He Really Mean?,” ambiguity 
and contradiction in Tolki en’s own statements are linked to two distinct 
I 
Matthew Thompson-Handell 
6  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
consequences: a creative tension in his fiction, and conflicting interpretations of 
his work. In “The Arch and the Keystone,” these two consequences are more 
explicitly connected to each other, and tension appears somewhat eclipsed by 
opposition: the contradictions in Tolkien the man result in contradictions in his 
work, which in turn contribute to contradictory interpretations of such work. Or 
as Flieger notes in her concluding remarks: “what they [scholars and critics] see 
is there, even when they’re seei ng contradictory things” (16). Amongst the 
numerous and varied labels that have been put on the man and his work, Flieger 
notes the pairing of “a Christian apologist and a pagan” (6), revisiting the 
opposition between Christian and neo -pagan interpretation s with which she 
opened her earlier 2014 article. She later observes that the fragmentation and 
polarization in Tolkien studies is in part a consequence of the fact that “so much 
of the primary evidence —that is to say, [Tolkien’s] writing —seems to toggle 
between diametrically opposite positions” (6–7). In The Lord of the Rings, Flieger 
identifies this opposition in the tragedy that befalls Frodo—that the hero fails in 
his appointed task only for Gollum to save the day; and that Frodo can save the 
Shire, but not for himself: “Frodo and Sam, at the Cracks of Doom and in the 
aftermath, embody between them the final tension and opposition that 
characterizes Tolkien’s masterwork” (13 –4). Here there is another subtle shift 
from the earlier 2014 article, where ambiguity (rather than contradiction) had a 
more significant role to play: Flieger observes a conflict between faith and 
imagination which gives The Lord of the Rings “a power and tension […] whose 
virtue is its indeterminacy.” Several examples are given, rounded off with 
Gandalf—or was it Saruman? —appearing to Aragorn, Legolas and Gimli: “for 
the Three Hunters the mystery is unsolved, and it seems clear that ambiguity is 
what Tolkien intended” (“But What” 164). 
Scholars and critics 1 have indeed been identifying both Christan and 
pagan (or non-Christian) elements in The Lord of the Rings  since its publication. 
What follows will look at the critical output up to 1981 (more on this date in a 
moment), in order to show that the presence and interaction of Christian and 
pagan elements is indicative of ambiguity rather than contradiction, and 
consequently that any opposition between Christian and pagan views is 
grounded to a greater extent in the perspectives of individual critics than in any 
contradiction within the work itself. In order to identify the critical responses 
that gave particular consideration to questions of Christianity and paganism, I 
went through the revised edition of Richard West’s annotated bibliography of 
Tolkien criticism (which covers the period to 1981) and noted those items where, 
in the title or in West’s annotation, the word “Christian” or “pagan” appears (or 
related terms such as “Christianity” or “paganism”). Further items were noted 
 
1 For the remainder of this article, I will use the term “critics”. 
No Ragnarök, No Armageddon: Pagan and Christian Interpretations 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  7 
where a particular word indicated some treatment of these themes. 2 
Additionally, three book -length studies were considered in view of their 
treatment (albeit relatively brief) of Christianity and paganism in The Lord of the 
Rings. The total number of critical responses examined was 32. 3  In terms of the 
period covered, 1981 is the end date not only because that is as far as West’s 
bibliography goes. It is also the year in which The Letters of J.R.R. Tolkien was first 
published, being thus a watershed after which many of Tolkien’s own 
statements would have become widely available to critics for the first time. 
Before this, there will have been less opportunity for critical responses to The 
Lord of the Rings to have been influenced by such statements.4 
I have divided the critical responses considered here into three groups: 
those which are more concerned with pagan (or non-Christian) elements in The 
Lord of the Rings; those which are more concerned with Christian elements; and 
those which consider a marriage of the two within the work. 5 Pagan views 
highlighted elegiac qualities, the sense of loss and of defeat. Other non-Christian 
views described a world in which the will and the actions of individuals play 
their part against the backdrop of a higher power, and also considered how the 
work reflects archetypal human myths. What connects many of these views is 
their recognition that overt Christianity is absent from The Lord of the Rings, and 
in this absence is an ambiguity concerning the theology of Middle -earth. Seen 
alongside the Christian views, this ambiguity comes into even sharper focus. 
The higher power may be seen as Providence, the struggle between good and 
evil may be character istically Christian, and the heroes can be Christ -like 
 
2 While this approach does not guarantee that every critical response with something to 
say about Christianity or paganism will have been considered, I suggest that it does 
provide sufficient coverage for reliable conclusions to be drawn. Aside from “Christian” 
or “pagan,” the other keywords were: Beowulf, God, Providence, religious, and theological. 
3 There were in fact four critical responses that were identified but which, on review, did 
not provide material that was sufficiently relevant to my topic, treating Christian or pagan 
elements in The Lord of the Rings  only very briefly. Using the references in West’s 
Bibliography, these were: II–80, II–437, II–547, and II–652. 
4 This does not mean that Tolkien’s own statements would have been entirely unknown to 
critics. The interview with Henry Resnik in 1966 which Flieger considers in “But What Did 
He Really Mean” and in “The Arch and the Keystone” appeared in the Saturday Evening 
Post, with a lengthier transcript appearing in Niekas 18 in 1967. Also, Tolkien’s letter to 
Father Robert Murray in 1953, which is also considered in both of Flieger’s articles, was 
extensively quoted in a piece by Murray himself which was published on 15 September 
1973 (shortly after Tolkien’s death) in the pe riodical The Tablet. At least one of the critics 
considered in my article was aware of that source—William Dowie, who quoted from it at length. 
5 This is by no means a clear -cut division. There are borderline cases which might have 
been placed in a different group, though in my view the division is a sound one, and 
supports a structured approach. 
Matthew Thompson-Handell 
8  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
figures. It is not a series of contradictions in the work that give rise to these 
competing views—it is the ambiguity that leaves the (critical) field wide open 
for interpretation. Turning to critics who acknowledged and examined the 
combination of pagan and Christian elements, they observed the elegiac tone of 
the former interacting with the moral and ethical themes of the latter, and with 
an optimistic view of good triumphing over evil. Some saw a tension and even 
an opposition between these two sides, wh ere the conflict was primarily 
identified as one of tone. Questions of tone are less clear -cut—perhaps more 
ambiguous—than those of (say) theology or doctrine, and a blending of elegiac 
and joyful tone does not of itself represent a contradiction between a pagan and 
a Christian view of the world. What all of this suggests is that it is ambiguity 
rather than contradiction that leads critics to take such opposing views on this point. 
 
PAGAN (AND NON-CHRISTIAN) VIEWS 
In the period up to 1981, there were relatively few critical responses to 
The Lord of the Rings that focused solely on a pagan interpretation. The first such 
response came early, with Douglass Parker’s article that appeared in the Winter 
1956/57 issue of The Hudson Review. Parker is definitve—in his view, The Lord of 
the Rings is a pagan work, a recreation of Beowulf as Tolkien understood it, where 
“the unknown West Saxon” wrote of a world where Fate “has not yet wholly 
become a Christian god” and governs the universe in an impersonal way. Parker 
felt that Tolkien had “toiled well and successfully after his paradigm” and had 
created a story where humans and their endeavours are confronted by evil and 
are doomed to be lost, to fade, perish, or die. In the face of all this, the only valid 
response—and one which demonstrates the heroism in the work —is to face up 
to this tragedy of the human condition. This gives The Lord of the Rings a meaning 
which Parker calls “heroic -elegiac.” Parker acknowledges the possibil ity of a 
Christian view of the work only in order to deny it—he feels that the promise of 
immortality (for elves and a few hobbits) is not sufficient to treat the work as 
anything other than pagan (608–9). 
Over a decade later, two articles appeared in 1969 which both took a 
pagan view, though from rather different perspectives. Nan Braude compares 
the differing mythopoeic approaches of Tolkien, C.S. Lewis, and Charles 
Williams. On Tolkien, she says that his materials are “traditional archetypes” 
and that the foremost literary sources are Teutonic and Celtic myth (6). Of most 
interest however, is the contrast drawn with the overt Christian elements in 
Lewis and Williams, where Braude observes that “The Lord of the Rings is ‘pagan’ 
in the sense that it is to be read only in terms of itself: it does not point outwards 
to a greater reality, thus admitting its own insufficient reality” (7). Compared to 
the heroic -elegiac qualities described by Parker, this is a rather differ ent 
understanding of what it means for a work of literature to be pagan. This serves 
No Ragnarök, No Armageddon: Pagan and Christian Interpretations 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  9 
as a useful reminder that the term “pagan” (and the term “Christian”) can mean 
different things to different people —here it might be more appropriate to 
consider Braude’s view as “non-Christian,” especially in view of the reason for 
her distinction between  Tolkien on the one hand, and Lewis and Williams on 
the other.  
The other article that appeared in 1969 was by Charles Moorman 
(”Now Entertain Conjecture of a Time”). He takes a similar view to Parker, 
though expands the analysis. Middle -earth is “essentially pagan in 
conception”—a world where “heroic patterns may evolve.” Moorman adds that 
“Middle-earth, or at least certain portions of it, is the stark, basically pessimistic 
world of the sagas in which God does not intervene in human conflicts and in 
which the hero’s or the society’s struggle against evil culminates at  best in a 
temporary victory achieved at tremendous cost” (62). He also observes pagan 
qualities both at the level of the individual (heroes such as Frodo paying “a high 
cost for victory”) and at the level of civilizations—with no hearkening back to a 
golden age (unlike in Narnia), and with outposts of light surrounded by 
darkness, which Moorman recognises as a theme from pagan heroic literature 
in the Western tradition (64 –66). It is worth noting however a subtle difference 
between Moorman’s pagan interpre tation and that of Parker —while the latter 
identified some meaning and value in the human struggle against inevitable loss 
and failure, the former suggests a more pessimistic and fatalistic tone: “evil must 
be combatted simply because it is a part of the h eroic code to do so” (63). In 
terms of the possibility of a Christian interpretation, Moorman shuts the door 
on this, saying that the work “is in no way theological either in its structure or 
in its thematic pattern” (and note the implication that paganism  cannot have a 
theology) (62). Just as Braude did, he contrasts Middle -earth with Narnia, 
primarily in terms of tone, so that the former is “bittersweet” with “no undiluted 
joy, no pure eucatastrophe” (68). He observes an opposition between Christian 
and heroic values, where forgiveness is incompatible with vendetta, and where 
the Christian view is “unflaggingly optimistic” (62). 
Several critics identified in The Lord of the Rings a religious scheme, but 
one which is not explicitly pagan nor Christian. Their views indicate a way of 
looking at the ethical or spiritual value of the work without any doctrinal 
associations. The first of these critics was Patricia Meyer Spacks, whose 19 59 
article examined the work’s moral and theological scheme, noting that “for this 
scheme, there are no explicit supernatural sanctions: The Lord of the Rings  is by 
no means a Christian work” (30). Spacks appears to take the same approach as 
both Parker and Braude —if a work displays no overt Christianity, it is not a 
Christian work. Spacks adds that religious practices are not apparent —nor is 
there an evident deity: “a clear ethos rules the virtuous, but it s derivation is 
unclear.” That ethos is exemplified by the hobbits, by their virtues —loyalty, 
Matthew Thompson-Handell 
10  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
cheerfulness in adversity, persistence, honesty, humility, and most of all, “naked 
will and courage” (quoting here from Beowulf: The Monsters and the Critics) (31). 
Spacks sees in the destruction of the Ring, in spite of Frodo’s failure at the Cracks 
of Doom, a triumph of Fate —though not the impersonal wyrd of the pagan 
tradition—over the human failure of the will (34). As will be seen below, critics 
who took a Christian view could make the very same point about the human 
will, with Christian Providence taking the place of Fate. Although Spacks takes 
no firm position on the doctrine of the work, she does still identify elements 
which she characterises as pagan or Christian: the ethos which she has described 
above is closest to a pagan ethos, where Frodo rema ins steadfast in the face of 
overwhelming opposition (33); and the theological scheme of Middle -earth has 
Christian parallels, with “the possibility of a sort of Grace” (34) and where “the 
result of repeated choices of good is the spiritual growth of the chooser” (37).  
Ten years after Spacks, Gracia Fay Ellwood would cover similar 
ground in a 1969 article whose main thrust was the treatment of good and evil 
in The Lord of the Rings . She considered archetypes and the universality of hero 
myths in human societies, as well as religious aspects and functions of such 
myths, without focusing on any particular doctrine or religion. Ellwood 
observes that in Tolkien’s work “there is no god visible beyond a hazy reference 
in an appendix to ‘the One,’ but the drawing power of the Ut termost West, the 
suggestion that Frodo was ‘meant’ to find the Ring, the preservation of Gollum, 
all show providential planning at work” (“Good Guys” 11). Ellwood makes no 
claim that such planning is the work of a Christian God —and indeed she 
includes a footnote (when making the observation quoted above) that refers to 
Spacks’s 1959 article. 
Two further critics also saw in The Lord of the Rings elements that were 
religious in character without necessarily being Christian. In 1970, Bruce A. 
Beatie sought to explain the underlying reasons for what he dubs the “Tolkien 
phenomenon,” observing that the work has a mythical structure which speaks 
to something religious but which is rooted more deeply in the human condition 
than any one particular religion or dogma. He refers to the work of 
“archaeologist-historian” Gertrude Rachel Levy as having ident ified “the 
continuity of both symbolism and structures of religious thought, though the 
function and context of those symbols and structures change, from the Stone 
Age to the golden age of Athens.” Beatie adds that Levy would recognise the 
same structures in The Lord of the Rings  (698). The Quest -myth in the work 
implies an ordered universe where the choices of an individual can have lasting 
significance. Such choices have as a backdrop “Fate, or God, or Augustinian 
predestination.” Although he allows for these contrasting backdro ps, and is 
more concerned with  concepts that are free of any particular doctrine, Beatie 
does say—though merely in parentheses—that the work “is basically Christian, 
No Ragnarök, No Armageddon: Pagan and Christian Interpretations 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  11 
in spite of the lack of overt symbols” (699). Providing no further analysis on this 
point, one is left with the impression that any such identification is, for him, 
secondary to the mythical significance he describes.  
In 1974, William Dowie’s article (which was reprinted with revsions in 
1979) also looked at religious, non -Christian characteristics of The Lord of the 
Rings. He noted that “the religiosity of Tolkien’s trilogy is neither 
conceptualized nor dogmatized within the story. Yet the sense of the sacred is 
present in blood and symbol and theme. It is the unvocalized religion of man in 
touch with nature and the cosmos” (266–67). Dowie’s analysis, which forms the 
first half of his article, draws a number of parallel s between the text, and 
concepts or themes in Mircea Eliade’s The Sacred and the Profane. In introducing 
this analysis, Dowie says that “the disagreement over whether Tolkien’s 
eschatology is guided more by Christian or by Norse ideology misses the point 
that Tolkien plunges into the sacrality of the natural” (267). This argument is 
however r ather weakened by the second half of the article, where Dowie 
identifies in the work certain Christian (but not pagan) moral and intellectual 
themes, which he also obse rves are present “in a context that has no explicit 
trappings of religion.” Dowie suggests that this obfuscation of overt Christianity 
has a purpose —a way of “unobtrusively exploring the sacred” that “allows a 
fresh emotional contact with ideas that might otherwise be categorically 
prejudged.” As will be seen below, this is a similar observation to those made 
by some other critics who have seen in The Lord of the Rings a pagan vehicle for 
delivering a Christian message—Dowie in fact cites one of these critics, Gunnar 
Urang, who had referred to the technique as “repaganizing” (274–75). 
Some key themes emerge from these pagan and non -Christian views. 
The identification of The Lord of the Rings  as pagan was (for Parker and 
Moorman, if not for Braude) a consequence of a tone which is heroic -elegiac, 
pessimistic, where human actions and achievements will ultimately be defeated 
by opposing forces. Other critics saw religious aspects which were not 
necessarily Christian—in particular, that the will and the actions of indiv iduals 
are part of a universe which is influenced, ordered or directed by  a higher 
power; and that there are structures and themes that speak to myths and 
archetypes that run “deeper” than organised religion. One important point 
relates not to something that was present, but something that was absent—many 
of these critics noted  the absence of overt signs of Christianity. What is 
particularly interesting is that this same observation could lead to directly 
opposing conclusions. For Parker, Braude, Moorman and Spacks, The Lord of the 
Rings is pagan or non -Christian, in part because of this absence. For Beatie and 
Dowie, the work is Christian (at least in part), in spite of  this absence. These 
competing views are possible because the lack of overt Christianity results in a 
Middle-earth that is ambiguous, at least in theological terms. 
Matthew Thompson-Handell 
12  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
CHRISTIAN VIEWS  
During the period to 1981, critical responses to The Lord of the Rings that 
focused on Christian elements in the work were more common than those that 
took a pagan or non-Christian view. While seven articles were considered in the 
previous section, sixteen will be considered here. These present a wide range of 
themes, characteristics and parallels which the critics identified as Christian. 
Edmund Fuller wrote about Tolkien and The Lord of the Rings  in Books 
With Men Behind Them , first published in 1962 (his chapter on Tolkien was 
revised and reprinted in Isaacs and Zimbardo’s Tolkien and the Critics  in 1968). 
Fuller observes that “In this story there is no overt theology or religion. There is 
no mention of God. No one is worshipped.” He qualifies this by acknowledging 
that a “limited eschatology” is implied, though he does not say that it is a 
Christian one (29). All of that said, Fuller proc eeds to focus on the Christian 
context of the work, by citing an aspect of the Christian tradition, before 
describing its equivalent in Middle -earth. Here he considers both the presence 
of a divine power that intervenes in the affairs of the world; and the interaction 
between Providence and free will (together with the importance of taking 
action) (29–30). Fuller also suggests that Gandalf and Frodo may be seen “not as 
Christ equivalents, but as partial anticipations of the Christ,” noting Frodo’s 
heavy burden and Gandalf’s resurrection, as well as the latter’s temptation to 
wield the Ring (35). 
Two papers presented at the Tolkien conference that took place at 
Mankato State College in October 1966 gave particular consideration to 
Christian themes. Dorothy K. Barber examines the meaning of The Lord of the 
Rings through, amongst other things, the use of primary world figurative 
language as literal in the secondary world —looking for example at the 
association of light with God. Barber’s conclusion identifies the destruction of 
the Ring as divinely ordained: “This climactic scene affirms the evangelium: 
Providence, or Good, or God, does exist and act in the affairs of Middle-earth in 
the continuing struggle against Evil” (49). In his conference paper (whose focus 
is heroism), Alexis Levitin observes that there is a Christian theme in Frodo’s 
failure: “Human weakness is an everpresent theme in Christian literature, and 
even the heroes do not completely escape its effect.” He adds that “Frodo, 
having carried the Ring, beyond all expectations, to the brink of destruction, 
cannot put the seal to all his prev ious acts. The implication seems to be that no 
fallen man could have done so” (32). This triumph of Providence over the failure 
of the will is the same point made by Spacks (noted above) though with Fate 
taking the place of Providence. One further point made by Levitin concerns the 
difference between a pagan and a Christian concept of endurance against the 
odds: “The Norse hero fights on in the face of defeat for the sake of fame, which 
he hopes will survive him. Frodo and Sam persevere out of duty alone” ( 33). 
No Ragnarök, No Armageddon: Pagan and Christian Interpretations 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  13 
However, this particular distinction seems open to debate —Charles Moorman, 
in a 1972 article which will be considered further below, quoted E.V. Gordon on 
the pagan heroic view of the universe, with an emphasis more on courage than 
on fame: “the hero resis ted to the end, and won satisfaction from fate, in being 
master of his life while he had it. [...] He might win the struggle, or he might 
know that it was hopeless; but it was better to die resisting than to live basely” 
(qtd. in Moorman, “Heroism” 30). 
In a 1966 article, Donald Reinken looked with a Christian perspective 
at the philosophical and political relevance of Tolkien’s work for the modern 
world. He argues that the plot turns on the workings of Providence, where 
Frodo’s virtues are insufficient t o complete the task on their own account. In 
accepting the “insufficient, finite good” and the “vainly rebellious evil” of 
earthly creatures, Providence “achieves good beyond human power or 
foretelling.” Reinken notes the absence in The Lord of the Rings  of religious 
practices or references to God—but says that in spite of this, the apparent divine 
ordering of the world demonstrates that the work is “a powerful testimony” to 
God. He also goes on to suggest that the absence of overt Christianity is a 
deliberate means of conveying a Christian message without seeming to do so, 
the work being addressed to a secular age, which can be led “from a vulgar 
irreligion to God” (9). As noted above, Dowie would make the same observation 
in his 1974 article. 
In a review essay for The New York Review of Books  in 1967, Matthew 
Hodgart considered a range of topics, including some brief commentary on 
Christian elements. Tolkien is, with The Lord of the Rings, conveying a Christian 
message: “isn’t the book really a parable, consciously aimed at putting across 
the general Christian view that the universe is a battlefield between the forces 
of good and evil?” He then describes Frodo as a Christ -like figure, beca use of 
his suffering, and his sacrifice which saves the S hire, not to mention being 
“stripped of his garments, flogged by the soldiery, scratched by thorns.” It is 
worth noting that Hodgart is roundly critical of such a (perceived) aim of the 
work—conveying a message which he sees as “infantile” (11).  
In a 1968 article on religious aspects of The Lord of the Rings , Sandra 
Miesel observes that “the remoteness and silence of Tolkien’s unworshipped 
One is typical of primitive theology,” but at the same time she maintains that 
“while the cultural framework is religionless, the attitudes expressed within it 
are traditio nal Christian ones” (210). Miesel says that the work presents an 
Augustinian view of good and evil (with the latter being a perversion of the 
former); that humility is a hallmark of the good and is opposed by arrogance; 
and that Frodo is a Christ figure who “willingly dies to the joy of living” (210–11). 
Paul Pfotenhauer in his 1969 article lays out several Christian themes, 
the principal one concerning Providence. He quotes the familiar words of 
Matthew Thompson-Handell 
14  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
Gandalf (“Bilbo was meant to find the Ring”) and Elrond (“the purpose for which 
you are called hither”) and says that “in all these events Tolkien is saying that 
events are finally shaped by a Power that remains unseen, goes almost 
unnoticed, and sometimes uses the most unlikely p ersons as His agents” (13 –
14). Pfotenhauer makes the point however that this does not deny free will —
indeed claiming that The Lord of the Rings  is amongst the best commentaries he 
has read on Augustine’s view that predestination an d free will are two sides of 
the same coin (14). Two further Christian elements treated rather more briefly 
are temptation (to use the Ring) (14); and self -sacrifice (“Gandalf, Aragorn, 
Frodo, and Sam, are each in their own way Christ figures”) (14). 
Catherine Stimpson’s booklet on Tolkien, in the series Columbia Essays 
on Modern Writers , appeared in 1969 and levelled criticism (at times harsh) at 
many aspects of Tolkien’s output. In this, there are some brief references to 
Christian elements in The Lord of the Rings. Gandalf and Frodo are seen as Christ-
like figures—the former experiences “Christlike suffering” which “shows most 
clearly Tolkien’s growing religious concern and use of obvious Christian 
parallels” (34), and Sam is described as “a faithful Peter to Frodo’s Christ” (38). 
Stimpson also equates the Uttermost West with a Christian afterlife: “though the 
lands the boats of Círdan seek are heathen in name, they signify the Christian 
concept of redemption” (40–41).  
One further article from 1969 appeared in the collection Shadows of 
Imagination (on Tolkien, C.S. Lewis, and Charles Williams). Although his article 
has a wider scope, Gunnar Urang identified two particular qualities which 
demonstrate a Christian theme in The Lord of the Rings . One is a providential 
design, apparent in the “sense of the interrelatedness of all the elements in this 
‘history’ and of an ordering of all these elements to one end” (103 –104). The 
other is an eschatology which is essentially bibli cal: “What the hobbits have 
become enmeshed in is a decisive struggle of cosmic scope. This is, of course, 
the imagery of biblical eschatology; the end is not an apex, but a crisis” (105). 
Urang shows conviction in his Christian view, though he also introd uces a 
pagan perspective, referring to Tolkien’s 1936 lecture on Beowulf. He quotes him 
on how the poet knew “both the newer Christian poetry and the older heroic 
verse and could contemplate the new faith and learning and the native tradition 
together.” Urang then cites Tolkien’s observation on the language of the poet: 
“partly ‘re-paganized’ by the author with a special purpose.” This brings Urang 
to his point —The Lord of the Rings  is also re -paganized, and with a special 
purpose: “Tolkien is at once committed to certain insights of traditional 
Christian belief and aware of the modern man’s scepticism about many of those 
insights. Therefore, ‘re -paganization’” (106). Here then is a view of Tolkien’s 
work as a Trojan horse for the Christian faith, a view exp ressed by Reinken in 
No Ragnarök, No Armageddon: Pagan and Christian Interpretations 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  15 
1966 and which Dowie would repeat in 1974 (quoting Urang’s use of the term 
“re-paganization”).6 
Gracia Fay Ellwood in her 1970 monograph Good News from Tolkien’s 
Middle-earth examines the concept of the hero, but more particularly the figures 
who can be seen as Christ-like. Her analysis is more detailed than the references 
to Christ-like figures made up to this point by other critics. Ellwood observes 
that “Gandalf is essentia lly a type -hero; he resembles Christ largely insofar as 
Christ is also a type -hero who fights the evil one, dies, and returns to life. The 
other two [Frodo and Aragorn] are type -heroes as well, but have much more 
definite resemblances to the specific figure of Jesus” (104). Gandalf is said to be 
Christ-like chiefly because of his fight with the Balrog, the associated descent 
into an abyss, and his eventual resurrection (much close  biblical analysis is 
provided) (113 –18). Other resemblances, such as the healing of Théoden, the 
flood of the Bruinen, and resisting the temptation to use the Ring, are “too vague 
to be convincing” (113). Frodo’s position as a Christ -like figure is closer  to the 
human figure of Jesus because of his down -to-earth nature, and his love of 
physical pleasures, providing the backdrop and contrast to the privations he 
suffers on his journey (121–22). There are also resemblances to the Passion—the 
betrayal by Gollum at Cirith Ungol, Frodo stripped and beaten by the orcs, 
falling into thorns, suffering from a terrible thirst, “but above all, the crushing 
weight of the symbol of Evil, increasing as it nears the centre of the hell where it 
originated” (125–26). Aragorn is then considered (“an image of Christ the King”) 
where the parallels include a royal lineage touched by prophecy, great feats of 
prowess (such as the descent into and emergence from the Paths of the Dead), 
and the glory of the events of the Field of Cormallen (134–41).  
In a 1971 article, Willis B. Glover considers the Christian character of 
Middle-earth. He acknowledges that “Middle Earth in the Third Age is depicted 
without religion” and that the only mention of God is to be found in the 
reference to “the One” in Append ix A to The Lord of the Rings . Glover argues 
however that Middle-earth is—in a sense—a Christian world, since Tolkien was 
a Christian. He also notes that “it is monotheistic in the peculiar sense of the 
Biblical tradition” (3). Glover then identifies a ran ge of elements to support his 
argument. First, Middle-earth is a non-dualistic world where evil is a corruption 
of good (“the Biblical world of the good creation”) (3). Second, Providence is 
directed by a transcendent power, while still allowing for free w ill (5). Third, 
 
6 These views on a covert message in The Lord of the Rings are also reminiscent of what C.S. 
Lewis said in a 1956 article in The New York Times, on the subject of fairy-stories as a vehicle 
for a Christian message: “But supposing that by casting all these things into an imaginary 
world, stripping them of their stained -glass and Sunday school associations, one could 
make them for the first time ap pear in their real potency? Could one not thus steal past 
those watchful dragons? I thought one could.” 
Matthew Thompson-Handell 
16  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
there is a Biblical structure to history in the sense of being non -cyclical (unlike 
the pagan view where “the pattern eternally recurs”)—and which can transcend 
and even control nature itself (5 –6). Finally there are some brief concluding 
remarks on elements of Christian ethics such as forgiveness and redemption 
(citing Boromir), and pity (citing Gollum) (8). Perhaps the most striking point 
made by Glover is his suggestion that a monotheistic world with no mention of 
God, but where there is an awareness of an outside agency, is a concept 
“peculiar to the Biblical tradition and traditions deriving in considerable 
measure out of it including modern non-Christian humanism.” This association 
between a Biblical tradition and humanism lies in the latter’s search for meaning 
in a world where faith in God has waned and where atheism finds no meaning. 
Here Glover finds a reason for the appeal of Tolkien’s work: “it satisfies 
vicariously a nostalgia for a more meaningful world wit hout making any 
demands of faith or change in belief” (5). 
Clyde S. Kilby contributed a chapter to the 1974 collection Myth, 
Allegory and Gospel which examined Tolkien’s work as well as that of C.S. Lewis, 
Charles Williams, and G.K. Chesterton. Kilby’s chapter, on mythic and Christian 
elements in Tolkien, surveys a wide range of Christian elements and themes. He 
identifies the struggle between goo d and evil, reflected in the dichotomies of 
light and dark, and white and black (“one of the most obvious Christian 
intimations”) (129). He observes the directing power of  Providence—and 
addressing the oft-heard argument that Lewis’s work is more overtly Christian, 
Kilby argues that Tolkien’s position here is not so different from Lewis’s—“one 
finds frequent parallels in which the veil is hardly less transparent in Tolkien’ s 
story,” before adding that “in both a sovereign Good is constantly and 
consistently operative” (132). Kilby also notes the presence of Christ -like 
figures—Gandalf’s fall and resurrection, and Frodo’s suffering and struggle 
through Mordor (133 –34). He ide ntifies the qualities of pity, mercy and 
redemption demonstrated by certain characters, exemplified by the case of 
Frodo and Gollum, though also citing the fact of Frodo contemplating the 
possibility of redemption for Saruman (137). One final theme is the exercise of 
the will, as demonstrated by Frodo’s continued efforts, especially through 
Mordor: “the element of will in the Tolkien story rises above that of simple 
Stoicism and takes on something at least of a Christian color,” adding that “Saint 
Augustine’s doctrine of the effective will is well illustrated by Frodo” (138–39). 
In a 1975 article, Cheryl Forbes argues that the tension between free 
will and Providence is one of the most compelling themes in The Lord of the Rings. 
There is siginifance in the need for characters to make choices —between the 
right way and the wrong way —and where the right choice is often unclear. 
Temptation and its interaction with the will is also considered, exemplified by 
Frodo’s relationship with the Ring: “The hobbit wants to resist the ring while he 
No Ragnarök, No Armageddon: Pagan and Christian Interpretations 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  17 
longs to yield to it. In yielding to temptation he acts what he does not will; in 
this Tolkien captures the struggle of every Christian” (11). Forbes adds that 
Frodo’s inability to cast the Ring into the fire demonstrates “the futility of 
trusting in free will” and that Providence achieves its aims as “the quest is 
accomplished regardless of Frodo’s choice” (12). This view of the inadequacy of 
the will is similar to that expressed earlier by both Spacks and Levitin.  
Of the three remaining articles, two make relatively narrow points in 
relation to Christian themes, whereas the other takes a very broad sweep. 
George Boswell’s 1972 article (only considered now as the Christian point is a 
brief one) examines the “intentio ns, materials, methods, themes, and 
achievement” of The Lord of the Rings  (188). The one observation on Christian 
themes argues that the concept of vicarious sacrifice is “the central 
communication of the entire work,” exemplified by Frodo saving the Shire  for 
others but not for himself (197). James Lynch trod a less well -beaten path with 
his 1978 article, which concerns how parties, feasts and banquets represent “a 
highly concrete pattern which indicates how Tolkien’s Christianity informs his 
thought” (13). Two particular events are associated with Christian parallels: the 
feast given by Elrond at Rivendell has a particularly spiritual quality in so far as 
it is nourishing, in spite of the fact that “the food there is never described”; and 
the lembas gifted by Galadriel “is Tolkien’s clearest description of magical, holy 
food. Here he comes closest to making an analogy between the meal and the 
Eucharistic feast” (14). Finally, Bruce Palmer’s 1976 monograph presents a wide 
sweep of Christian parallels. These include the Uttermost West (inhabited by the 
Valar) representing an afterlife (8 –9); the invocation of Elbereth, in a way 
reminiscent of invocations of the Virgin Mary, when the hobbits meet Gildor 
early in their jour ney; at the same meeting, the food and drink that is shared 
having a sacred and spiritual power (15–16); the phial of Galadriel containing a 
holy power of light, and the earth given to Sam representing something of an 
Edenic garden (21 –22); the blessing given when Faramir and his companions 
eat, being reminiscent of saying grace before a meal (23); the sacrifice of Frodo 
to save the Shire (26 –27); and the glory present in the events of the Field of 
Cormallen (25–26).  
Several recurring themes emerge from this review of Christian 
interpretations of The Lord of the Rings . Foremost is that of Providence, the 
guiding hand of a higher power, interested in the affairs of Middle -earth and 
working behind the scenes to ensure the success of good against evil. Critics did 
not however see in this a deterministic universe—they identified the importance 
of the free will exercised by the characters and sought to understand this in the 
context of Providence. Some were more interested i n the fine balance between 
Providence and will (Fuller, Pfotenhauer, Glover) whereas others took a view 
with more emphasis on the frailty of the will (Levitin, Reinken, Forbes). 
Matthew Thompson-Handell 
18  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
Although sometimes connected to the workings of Providence, some critics saw 
the opposition of good and evil as a theme in its own right, arguing that the 
cosmic struggle found in the events of Middle -earth is essentially Christian 
(Barber, Hodgart, Kilby)  and that it presents a non -dualistic, Augustianian 
perspective (Miesel, Glover). Another recurring theme is that of Christ -like 
figures in the narrative. Frodo, with his suffering and his sacrifice, is the 
foremost of these, followed by Gandalf, with his resurrection. Aragorn can be 
seen as Christ the King, and even Sam is identified by one critic as Christ -like 
(though another equates him with Peter). One further theme concerned the 
Christian significance of the pity and mercy shown by Frodo to Gollum, and the 
possibility of redemption for characters such as Boromir and Saruman. 
What then of the relationship between these Christian views of The Lord 
of the Rings and the pagan views seen earlier? To the extent that they contradict 
each other, it seems to me that the contradiction lies more in the labels being 
applied, than in the substance of what is being labelled. Providence looms large 
in the Christian themes c onsidered above, though as we have seen, it was also 
acknowledged by some that there is little overt Christianity in the work (Fuller, 
Reinken, Urang). One may recall that critics who viewed The Lord of the Rings as 
pagan or non-Christian could just as readily see this lack of overt Christianity as 
evidence for their own (opposing) view. Such opposition is therefore anchored 
more in the perspective of the critic than in any contradiction in the work itself—
after all, they agree on what they see (or don’t see!) —they just don’t agree on 
what that means. A pagan or non -Christian view could look at the hints of a 
higher power intervening in the events of Middle -earth, and label t his not as 
Providence, but as Fate, be it impersonal (Parker) or otherwise (Spacks); as some 
unnamed higher power (Ellwood); or as a manifestation of natural religion 
(Dowie). Whatever Tolkien really meant, he did not nail his colours to the mast, 
and this ambiguity opens the door to competing interpretations—but ambiguity 
is not contradiction. Similarly, a cosmic struggle between good and evil may 
appear, to a Christian, to be a very Christian affair —though adherents of many 
other religions (and those who subscribe to none) may beg to differ. Both Parker 
and Moorman identified a struggle against evil as a characteristic of their pagan 
intepretations, and good and evil were the focus of Ellwood’s 1969 article which 
was free of a Christian interpretation. Whatever the label, the substance remains 
the same —a struggle between good and evil. Another theme which reveals 
some of this ambiguity is that of Christ-like figures in the work. What do critics 
see when they identify such figures? Foremost are the suffering and sacrifice of 
Frodo, and the resurrection of Gandalf. Such qualities might point towards 
something messianic, but at the same time they are not necessarily so. Sacrifice 
and suffering can be seen as heroic qualities —indeed Ellwood’s analysis of 
Christ-figures in her 1970 monograph argues that “Christ is also a type -hero 
No Ragnarök, No Armageddon: Pagan and Christian Interpretations 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  19 
who fights the evil one, dies, and returns to life” (“Good News” 104). If some 
critics label Frodo as Christ -like, while others label him as a hero without such 
associations, his suffering and his sacrifice remain the same. 
 
A MARRIAGE OF THE PAGAN AND THE CHRISTIAN 
As we have seen, one view of The Lord of the Rings is that it is a covertly 
Christian work, delivering its message without appearing to do so, simply by 
avoiding doctrine and dogma or by presenting the whole in an attractively 
pagan setting (Reinken, Urang). Whether one subscribes to this view or not (and 
even if one does, the question remains whether this was done deliberately), it 
suggests a means by which the Christian and the pagan co-exist within the work. 
Even without a crypto -Christian purpose, a mar riage of pagan and Christian 
elements was recognised and considered by many critics in the period to 1981, 
and to them I will now turn. 
From a scholarly perspective at least, Charles Moorman was not a man 
to take a narrow view, nor to remain stuck in his ways. Already we have seen 
how (in his 1969 article) he argued for an essentially pagan view of The Lord of 
the Rings. As we will see below, just 3 years later he would repudiate (at least in 
part) that view, making a case for a Christian interpretation. Before either of 
these articles, Moorman considered both sides, in a chapter on Tolkien in his 
1966 monograph Precincts of Felicity: The Augustinian City of the Oxford Christians 
(reprinted in Isaacs and Zimbardo’s Tolkien and the Critics  in 1968). Here, he 
describes The Lord of the Rings as “essentially a Nordic myth” (201), adding later 
that “the greatest single influence on Tolkien is the eddas and sagas of the 
North.” He speaks of how “the great Nordic theme of courage —courage to 
grapple not only with human foes, but with the monsters of the outer 
darkness—runs through The Lord of the Rings  as it does through Beowulf” (212). 
Alongside all of this is a clear statement of a structure to the work that reflects a 
strongly Christian theme—the journey of Frodo, the quest of the myth hero, in 
a way that reflects in Augustinian terms “the establishment of the City of God 
on Earth” (Minas Tirith) and where the Shire and Mordor represent the Earthly 
City (210). Moorman does not however place these pagan and Christian 
elements in relation to each other. 
One of the earliest clear statements of the interaction of pagan and 
Christian elements appeared in a review essay by Michael Wood in New Society 
in 1969. Wood describes the theology of The Lord of the Rings as “an extraordinary 
synthesis of heroic northern myth and Christian promise.” He states that 
Tolkien believes in Christian Providence but also that “within Time the 
monsters win.” The capitalisation of “Time” is significant —this reveals how 
Wood sees the two concepts co-existing, for Time is terrestrial: “the treats come 
later, in the islands of the blessed.” However, such co -existence does not, for 
Matthew Thompson-Handell 
20  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
Wood, deny a tension where the heroic theme of the work “seems strangely at 
odds” with its elegiac tone (493). Another critic who saw a synthesis of the pagan 
and the Christian was C. Stuart Hannabuss, who at the start of a 1971 article set 
out his view with great clarity: “I believe that Tolkien was working out a quasi-
Christian morality in pagan terms, using a former culture and literary tradition 
to furnish the scenario to a quest which incorporated the major issues of Life” 
(87). Hannabuss observed a du alistic struggle between the “broadly good and 
bad” which he characterises as Christian in so far as the good are victorious —
and yet the victory is “almost Pyrrhic: so clearly do the heroes anticipate and 
acknowldge defeat that we are in the world of Beowu lf and the Norse sagas” 
(87–88). There is more too from a Christian perspective, with subconscious 
allusions to Christian iconography (the Nazgûl as the Four Horsemen of the 
Apocalypse, Gandalf on a white horse evoking the text of Revelation) —though 
there is something running deeper: “subsuming the overtly Biblical is the story 
which can in poetry mirror man’s dilemmas and look Life in the eye” (93–94). 
In a 1972 monograph on Tolkien, Robley Evans acknowledges both 
pagan and Christian elements in The Lord of the Rings. In similar terms to Wood, 
he recognises a fusion between the two, while acknowledging their opposition: 
“the elegiac tone of Tolkien’s fantasies” being “contrary to the Christian joy of 
affirmation associated with the romance [genre]” (83). Thus the roma nce Quest 
reveals meaning and order in the universe while the Northern European 
tradition encapsulates “man’s struggle to preserve something of civilization for 
a little while against the dark, perhaps nameless, forces that will triumph over 
him in the end” (84). Evans sees the tension and opposition as one reason for the 
work’s power: “[Tolkien] is an author who can make his sense of joy and of fate 
profitable because they can be played off against each other” (84). 
Also in 1972, Moorman issues the corrective referred to above, 
disavowing his “earlier identification of Tolkien as an underground pagan” 
(”Heroism” 31). The use of the word “underground” is curious here —I am not 
able to discern, in his 1969 article, any indication of Moorman equating Tolkien’s 
personal world view with the pagan qualities of his work. In any event, 
Moorman explains that his volte-face is grounded in a close and uninterrupted 
reading of the Frodo-Samwise narrative, “skipping the adventures of the other 
members of the Fellowship, a narrative which, though necessary, is none -the-
less peripheral” (29). His conclusion from this reading is t hat the heroism 
encapsulated in the Frodo -Samwise narrative is characterised by “specifically 
Christian ethical qualities —endurance, humility, mercy, and purity —as 
opposed to the more usual heroic values —courage, prowess, loyalty” (35). 
Furthermore, in the  same vein as Levitin, Reinken and Forbes, Moorman 
identifies a Christian theme in the ultimate failure of the will to achieve the 
destruction of the Ring, so that this climactic scene suggests that “God, using 
No Ragnarök, No Armageddon: Pagan and Christian Interpretations 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  21 
Satan’s own weakness, his greed for power, must snatch victory from defeat on 
behalf of man” (38). While those other critics took a firmly Christian view, 
Moorman continues to recognise the significance of pagan elements in the work. 
We have already seen h ow he draws the distinction between Christian ethical 
qualities and the “usual” heroic virtues, and he argues that The Lord of the Rings 
is “a mixture of pagan spirit and Christian ethos […] in which the proportions 
defy measurement” (38). Hard as it might  be to measure these proportions, 
Moorman does offer a view on a key touchpoint between the two—the exercise 
of free will. In the absence of a clear or overt divine influence, what matters are 
the actions of the hero: “it is this emphasis on the free will of the individual and 
his responsibility to common humanity which provides a link between Tolkien’s 
Christianity and his paganism” (32). Moorman, in similar terms to Evans, also 
argues that the tension and opposition between these two (an “ambivalence of 
value”) is a prime reason for the greatness of the work (38). 
Three further critics writing in the first half of the 1970s would also 
observe a combination of Christian and pagan (or “pre -Christian”) elements in 
The Lord of the Rings, though without identifying a tension or opposition between 
the two. In a 1972 article, Douglas Barbour sees some truth in how Parker 
highlights the heroic -elegiac tone of the work, but feels that Parker does not 
allow room for the optimism that reflects Tolkien’s Christian perspective, 
adding that Tolkien does not follow the Beowulf poet in accepting defeat for both 
gods and heroes as an inevitable outcome: “for his imagination is powerfully 
Christian, and he sees the victory of good as always possible” (36). Thus Parker’s 
view is too narrow: “it is not just the elegiac feeling which is p roduced” (38). 
Paul Kocher in a 1972 monograph looks at Tolkien’s treatment of good and evil, 
and sees in this “how far Tolkien wishes his treatment of evil to be considered 
not only moral but metaphysical,” (76) though adding a note of caution against 
“forc[ing] a literary masterpiece into any tight philosophical mold, Thomistic or 
otherwise. Middle -earth is avowedly pre -Christian” (77). At the same time, 
Kocher observes in the work “a general foundation of natural theology in the 
areas of moral norms and the working out of a providential cosmic order,” with 
the consequence that the free peoples of Middle -earth have a status “which if 
not precisely Christian is still very much like it in overall tone” (84–85). In a 1974 
monograph, Randel Helms looks at the fusion of pagan and Christian elements 
in the context of Tolkien’s Beowulf lecture. Helms notes how Tolkien defended 
the poem from charges that it is a “deeply flawed mixture of pagan and 
Christian” before adding that “Tolkien urges that this very fusion is the source 
of much of the poem’s power.” Helms argues that the defense o f the Beowulf 
poet (in Helms’s words, “already himself on the Christian side but still 
imaginatively alive to the ancient traditions”) is just as powerful a defense of 
Middle-earth, noting for example that the monsters in both tales are enemies of 
Matthew Thompson-Handell 
22  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
mankind as well as enemies of God. That being said, Helms sees in The Lord of 
the Rings  (as did Urang) the same suppression of Christianity that Tolkien 
observed in Beowulf—though unlike Urang, he does not infer an apologistic 
purpose on Tolkien’s part (5–6).  
The last critic to be considered here made a similar observation to 
Wood, Evans and Moorman, concerning the tension and opposition between 
pagan and Christian elements in The Lord of the Rings . In a 1980 article whose 
principal thesis concerns Tolkien’s use of humour, C.D. Stevens sees tension in 
“Tolkien’s attempt to reconcile two disparate and sometimes conflicting sources 
of inspiration for his view of human existence: the ‘Commedia’ of the Christian 
mythos with the considerably bleaker view of the unive rse typified by much 
Anglo-Saxon poetry, including Beowulf.” For Stevens, a result of this attempted 
reconciliation is “a tale in which comic reintegration and elegiac resignation 
struggle to dominate tone” (122). It is worth looking closely though at what 
Stevens says—the conflict he identifies is not in The Lord of the Rings, but in two 
sources of inspiration —the Christian and the pagan. Is this splitting hairs? I 
suggest not, for the distinction is important in considering whether (and how 
far) conflicting views on Tolkien’s work may be a consequence of conflicts in the 
work. Stevens says that the Christian mythos is opposed to the pagan view of 
the universe. However, neither of these is lifted lock, stock and barrel into The 
Lord of the Rings . Indeed, Stevens observes that in Christian belief, “tragedy is 
frequently considered to have been rendered impossible by the twin facts of 
grace and redemption” but that “this attitude is modified in literature” (for 
example, by chance or by the freedom to accept or  reject grace). He observes 
Christian elements in Tolkien’s work, especially moral and ethical qualities, but 
notes at the same time an absence of  “an assured destiny akin to the concept of 
salvation” (128). So where then is the conflict with the pagan wo rld? As noted 
above, it is the tone of the work where Stevens sees a conflict, not for example in 
theology or doctrine. 
Looking at these views of the marriage of pagan and Christian 
elements, a picture begins to emerge, where there is broad agreement that the 
dominant pagan element is the elegiac tone of the work, and that Christianity is 
represented by moral and ethical qu alities, as well as by an optimistic (indeed, 
joyous) view of the victory of good over evil. Not all of the critics considered in 
this section make a point about tension or opposition between pagan and 
Christian elements—but those who do are in agreement that it is the tone of the 
work where the conflict lies (with the possible exception of Moorman who sees 
ambivalence in the value systems of the two). As noted above, we have an 
elegiac tone at odds with the theme of heroism (Wood), or opposed to Christian 
joy (Evans), or grappling with the Christian “Commedia” (Stevens). Even critics 
who made no particular point about tension or opposition saw tone as an 
No Ragnarök, No Armageddon: Pagan and Christian Interpretations 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  23 
important part of the pagan -Christian dynamic —an elegiac feeling rubbing 
shoulders with the possibility of victory (Barber), or a Christian tone in a pre -
Christian setting (Kocher). All of this is important, because tone is more 
elusive—and arguably more s ubjective—than theology or doctrine. It lends 
itself less to binary positions, and more to a spectrum of feeling. There is no 
Ragnarök in The Lord of the Rings, nor is there an Armageddon. There is sadness, 
and loss, mingled with hope, and joy. The minglin g of such things does not 
mean that the work embodies an opposition between pagan and Christian views 
of the world. It means that it is bittersweet. So if critics and readers argue over 
whether the work is pagan or Christian, that argument is grounded to a  far 
greater degree in their own perspectives, than to any contradiction in the work 
itself. 
 
CONCLUSION 
Many critics have identified The Lord of the Rings  as essentially 
Christian. Many have identified in it a homage to a pagan world. In doing so, 
inferences have to be drawn and interpretations have to be made. Those 
inferences and interpretations all have the same point of departure —the work 
itself. Lookin g at critical responses up to 1981, the debate and disagreement 
between Christian and pagan views seems to have been a product not of 
contradictions in the work, but of its ambiguity, at least in theologica l matters. 
This ambiguity left the critical field open for scholars and critics, who would 
bring their wide range of perspectives to bear, to reach competing and even 
contradictory conclusions. For that reason, the period suggests that a more 
appropriate lens for viewing the pagan and Christian dynamic in The Lord of the 
Rings is that presented by Flieger in 2014 in “But What Did He Really Mean?,” 
where she highlights the ambiguity and indeterminacy of the work. The central 
part played by contradiction in h er keystone analogy from 2019 makes that 
particular lens less fitting. It would be of interest to examine the pagan and 
Christian interpretations of the work after the publication of The Letters of J.R.R. 
Tolkien in 1981 (and the increased awareness of Tolkien’s own statements that 
would have followed), to see what role ambiguity and contradiction play in later 
interpretations. Furthermore, the pairs of contradictory labels presented in “The 
Arch and the Keystone” provide ample opportunity to examine specific threads 
in the history of Tolkien criticism, to look behind the labels, and to see just how 
far each of these reflects a tension and an opposition in Tolkien and his works.

articles for the Journal of Tolkien Research, Beyond Bree and Angerthas (the journal 
of Arthedain, the Tolkien Society of Norway).
