                       N ASPIRATIONAL CULTUS ? 
                             TOLKIEN FANDOM AT THE  
                                   BORDERS OF BELIEF  
           
                                            TOM EMANUEL  
 
PRELUDE: A DEPLORABLE CULTUS? 
 HAVE ALWAYS FELT THAT CEMETERIES ARE TOLKIENIAN PLACES: the thoughtful 
stillness, the intertwining of natural rhythms and the work of human hands, 
the awareness of mortality and deep time. I grew up down the street from a 
historic graveyard, and it was not uncommon for visitors to find me sitting, 
cross-legged, in the shade of a honey locust tree with a dog -eared paperback in 
hand—perhaps my father’s Ballantine paperback copy of The Return of the King, 
the one with Barbara Remington’s unforgettable cover art. My younger self 
would have been overjoyed to know that twenty-odd years later he would find 
himself at J.R.R. Tolkien’s grave in Oxford with hundreds of likeminded others, 
summoned by our shared passion for Middle -earth and the man who wrote it 
into being. The occasion of our meeting is Oxonmoot, the annua l gathering of 
the U.K. Tolkien Society, and our convergence upon Oxford’s Wolvercote 
Cemetery on this early September afternoon commemorates the fiftieth 
anniversary of our favorite author’s death. 
Clad in everything from formalwear to faded T -shirts and 
handwoven cloaks, we wend our way through the headstones to the grave 
which Tolkien shares with his wife Edith. The chair of the Tolkien Society greets 
us and invites us into a space of quiet reflect ion—not difficult to achieve in a 
sunny graveyard in late summer. Having gathered our thoughts to Tolkien and 
the ways in which his works have touched our lives, we listen to a reading from 
the climax of The Lord of the Rings (LotR): the destruction of the One Ring and the 
fall of Barad -dûr, followed by Gandalf’s rescue of Sam and Frodo from the 
wrack of Mount Doom and the hobbits’ awakening amidst the green trees of 
Ithilien. When the reality of Sam’s deliverance dawns upon him and he cries 
aloud, “I feel like spring after winter, and sun on the leaves; and like trumpets 
and harps and all the songs I have ever heard” (LotR VI.4.952), tears pool at the 
corners of my eyes. Then the reading ends and the crowd lapses into 
contemplative silence. I glance around me: fellow attendees are withdrawing 
tissues from the pockets of their cosplay outfits, squeezing the hands of loved 
ones, weeping in earnest. At an unspoken signal, representatives of the Tolkien 
Society and other fan organizations lay memorial wreaths up on the Tolkiens’ 
grave. When this is done, a single unaccompanied voice rises in song: 
I 
Tom Emanuel 
28  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
“Namarië,” Galadriel’s hymn of parting to the Fellowship of the Ring in 
Lothlórien. When the music has died away, and there is nothing left but “sun on 
the leaves” (LotR VI.4.952) and wind in the grass, we breathe a sigh of relief that 
settles somewhere between nervous laughter and tears. The memorial, and by 
extension Oxonmoot, has reached its end. The time has come to bid each other 
goodbye. 
Past attendees of Oxonmoot will recognize what I have just described 
as Enyalië, a word which means remembrance in Tolkien’s invented language 
Quenya. Ever since Tolkien’s death in 1973, fans have gathered at his graveside 
each September for this ritual of memory and appreciation. Yet as moved as I 
was by Enyalië 2023, I am not sure that Tolkien would have quite ap proved of 
it. In a 1965 letter, his friend W.H. Auden wrote to him “fear[ing] that most of 
the members” of the newly organized New York Tolkien S ociety “would be 
lunatics” ( The Letters of J.R.R. Tolkien [Letters] 500, #275). Tolkien responded 
wearily: “Yes, I have heard about the Tolkien Society. Real lunatics don’t join 
them, I think. But still such things fill me too with alarm and despondency” 
(500). He would give the U.K. Tolkien Society his blessing four yea rs later and 
serves, to this day, as its president in perpetuo. But there is little question that his 
relationship to his literary devotees was always one of ambivalence, leading him 
to infamously dub his burgeoning fandom “my deplorable cultus” (Carpenter 
233). Given Tolkien’s philological background, it seems reasonable to assume 
that his use of the word “cultus,” with its strong religious connotations, was 
deliberate. The word “fan” itself  has its origins in the Latin fanaticus, referring 
to temple devotees (Jenkins, Textual Poachers [Poachers] 12), and its emergence 
during the late nineteenth century as a descriptor for ardent lovers of sports and 
music was likely intended to evoke associa tions of religious enthusiasm 
(Cavicchi, “Foundational Discourses of Fandom” 29–30). Tolkien the philologist 
seems to have intuited what the history of the word implies: there is a connection 
between the passion of the fan and the devotion of the believer. 
Given his apprehensions regarding his “cultus,” his Roman Catholic 
faith, and his insistence that his legendarium did not constitute “any kind of 
new religion or vision” (Letters 403, #211), Tolkien might well have eyed Enyalië 
with suspicion. Perhaps he would worry that we were engaged in idolatry, 
venerating him as a secular saint or literary deity. Superficially at least, there 
might be something to this: with its graveyard setting, communal readings, and 
meditative silences, the Enyalië I attended reminded me of nothing so much as 
Christian liturgy. But speaking as a practicing Christian, it did not threaten my 
religious commitments in any way; at no point was I concerned that we were 
worshiping Tolkien the man. If anything, the memorial’s worship-like structure 
made it more impactful for me, not less. What are we to make of this collision 
between Tolkien fandom and the trappings of faith? 
An Aspirational Cultus? Tolkien Fandom at the Borders of Belief 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  29 
In what follows I will attempt to answer this question, which is thornier 
than it might at first appear. I will first survey the state of scholarly debate 
around fandom and religion before outlining my own working definition of 
religion as sacred worldbuilding.  I will link this to Tolkien’s theory of fantasy 
worldbuilding in his essay “On Fairy-stories” (OFS) to propose a framework for 
understanding and researching the religious qualities and affordances of 
fandom as the activities of a secondary faith comm unity. I will then use this 
framework to exegete Enyalië as a prime example of what might be called a fan 
liturgy, a powerful collective experience that takes religious form without 
carrying overt religious content. Having undertaken this interdisciplinary 
pilgrimage, I will arrive at my conclusion: that Tolkien’s secondary world, his 
community of devoted fans, and th e lineaments of Christian ritual have 
combined to produce, in Enyalië, a fan practice which troubles the boundaries 
between the sacred and the  secular, the world(s) of fantasy literature and the 
world(s) of everyday experience. This would almost certainly have made J.R.R. 
Tolkien uncomfortable. But as I hope to show, it tells us something important 
about why his fiction remains compelling to so many people of so many faiths—
and offers, perhaps, some lessons to existing communities of meaning in a 
pluralistic, post-Christian world. 
 
GATHERING: SECONDARY BELIEVERS, SECONDARY WORLDS 
As Tolkien inferred, there are many striking parallels between media 
fandom1 and religion. Pioneering fan theorist Henry Jenkins ( Poachers 17–18) 
writes of fans’ intense identification with beloved characters and stories, while 
Matt Hills points out that fandom involves immersion in fantastic, 
“hyperdiegetic” worlds (104). John Lyden draws attention to fandom’s careful 
exegesis of “canonical” te xts (783–84), a commitment which can result in fiery 
debates with rival interpretive communities (cf. Walls -Thumma 93–95). These 
debates then result in exclusion along lines of race, gender, sexuality, and other 
axes of marginalization (Hannell 25, Pande 209, Stanfill 195, Thomas and 
Stornaiulo para. 3.1). What to make of these parallels is another matter. To paint 
an ongoing scholarly debate in broad strokes, there are three general “schools” 
 
1 I use the terms “fandom” and “media fandom” interchangeably in this essay to indicate 
fandoms which coalesce around narrative media: books, films, video games, television 
shows, and the like. Other forms of fandom obviously exist, such as music and sports 
fandom; but following Mark Duffett’s lead (3), I exclude them from consideration for the 
sake of analytical clarity. I believe that the underlying dynamics of, say, sports fandom 
and media fandom are much closer than Duffett seems to think; Tolkien himself suggests 
as much when he compares the spell cast by fantasy to the spell cast by a game of cricket 
(OFS 51). However, folding other categories of fandom into my analysis would require 
more space than the present essay allows. 
Tom Emanuel 
30  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
of interpretation within the field of fan studies. Scholars in the first school, 
including Michael Jindra (229), Candy Leonard (20 –22), Jennifer Porter (279), 
and Jennifer Otter Bickerdike (8), claim that fandom either is a new religious 
movement or that it effectively replaces religion in an increasingly secularized 
culture. Those in the second school, such as Andrew Crome (413), Mark Duffett 
(146–47), and Henry Jenkins ( Fans, Gamers, and Bloggers 17–18), reject the 
comparison, arguing that fandom lacks religion’s exclusive ethical demands and 
transcendent frame of reference. Scholars in the third and final school take the 
real similarities between the two phenomena seriously without accepting a one-
to-one correspondence between them, viewing fandom as an e xistential 
(Cavicchi, Tramps Like Us [Tramps] 188) or “neoreligious” (Hills 85) project of 
identity and community formation (Zubernis and Larsen 154–55). 
In large part these debates come down to definitions of religion. Those 
who reject parallels between fandom and religion tend to use narrow substantive 
definitions, which are concerned with formal characteristics of religion such as 
institutional arrangements, doctrines, claims for the existence of supernatural 
agents, etc. Those who embrace the parallels prefer broad functionalist definitions, 
which are less about what religion “is”  and more about what religion “does”: 
the various functions it performs in the lives of individuals, communities, and 
societies. Those in the middle tend to blend functionalist and phenomenological 
approaches; the latter center on “the lived experience and perceptions of people 
in relation to religion and the sacred” (Lynch 128)—what religions “feels like” if 
you will. The definitions we use inescapably shape what we consider to be 
religious and, therefore, worthy of study. As Michael Elliott cautions, “if we 
insist on comparing fandoms to religion, then we need to be clear about which 
scholarly approaches we are using” (109). I intend to do just that. 
For the purposes of this study, I draw upon both functionalist and 
phenomenological approaches to define religion as sacred worldbuilding. This is 
a creative adaptation of sociologist Peter Berger’s definition of religion as 
“cosmization in a sacred mode” (26). For Berger, all human society and culture 
is worldbuilding activity. First and foremost this means the construction of a 
meaningful world, a symbolic cosmos within which humans can scaffold their 
actions and experiences as part of a purposive whole ( 20). What distinguishes 
religious worldbuilding in particular from social worldbuilding in general is its 
explicitly sacred character. All socially constructed worlds seek to answer 
questions of meaning: Where do we come from? Where do we belong? What is 
our purpose? Why do we suffer? What happens when we die? Religion seeks to 
create and maintain a symbolic universe in which its answers to such questions 
retain ultimacy, and credibility, even in the face of human finitude and suffering 
(51), what Tolkien’s friend C.S. Lewis called the problem of pain. 
An Aspirational Cultus? Tolkien Fandom at the Borders of Belief 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  31 
However, I want to nuance my definition around the nature of meaning, 
the nature of the sacred, and the supposed universality of the category  religion 
itself. Religion may be a world of meaning, but it is also a genuine world, 
experienced bodily and emotionally as well as symbolically and imaginatively. 
Following feminist critics (Ammerman 103–04, McDannell 145, McGuire 13–15), 
I make space in my understanding of religion for the embodied meanings of 
affect, ethical praxis, material culture, and—importantly for the present essay—
ritual. Likewise, the sacred has historically been understood as absolute 
transcendence, the Holy Other; this is how Berger himself defines it (26). 
However, this places us in danger of turning the transcendent God of 
Christianity in to the implicit yardstick by which we measure all religion 
everywhere (Asad 42, Mandair and Dressler 12). I prefer sociologist Gordon 
Lynch’s definition of the sacred as that which is held to be and experienced as sacred 
by a given individual or community under a given set of cultural and historical 
circumstances. On Lynch’s reading, the sacred 
 
exerts a gravitational pull on the feelings, motivations and behaviour of 
individuals and groups […]. It binds people into particular kinds of 
identities, communities and ways of living which are experienced not 
simply of their own free choice, but as comp elled or inspired by the 
sacred object. (138) 
 
Following on from this, I do not make any universal claims for my definition of 
religion. Instead, I posit it as a limited, stipulative understanding of religion in 
the modern West (cf. Taylor 15 –16), one with special utility for considering the 
religious affordances of fandom and popular culture. 
One major point in favor of adopting and adapting a flexible definition 
of religion -as-sacred-worldbuilding for the study of media fandom is that it 
mirrors the importance of worldbuilding to popular narratives, especially in an 
era of transmedia “extended  universes” (Jenkins, “Transmedia Storytelling 
101,” Wagner 212, Wolf 10–13). The notion of religion-as-sacred-worldbuilding 
also accords with Tolkien’s understanding of his literary project in “On Fairy -
stories.” There, Tolkien distinguishes between the primary world of everyday 
reality and the secondary world of the text , with the related terms primary belief 
and secondary belief (OFS 52). For Tolkien, sub-creation is both the human faculty 
of creativity as well as its imaginative product. His use of the prefix sub- denotes 
a distinction between the creativity of God, understood as ex nihilo or “out of 
nothing,” and human creativity, which uses the given elements of creation as it 
exists—the so -called primary world —and rearranges them to produce new 
things. In Tolkien’s Roman Catholic faith, human beings are created in the imago 
Dei, the image and likeness of God (New Revised Standard Version Updated Edition, 
Gen. 1:26 –27)—that is to say, created in the image of a Creator. For him, 
Tom Emanuel 
32  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
humanity’s sub-creative power, while derivative of and subordinate to God’s, is 
“ultimately of the same stuff” (OFS  47). One might even say that human 
creativity is a kind of participation in the Divine life, a co-creative improvisation 
upon the Music of Creation: as Tolkien puts it, “We make still by the law in 
which we’re made” (56). Any product of human art is an exa mple of sub -
creation, but Tolkien is chiefly concerned with language and its unique capacity 
to generate a secondary world in the reader’s imagination: 
 
[T]he story -maker proves a successful ‘sub -creator’. He makes a 
Secondary World which your mind can enter. Inside it, what he relates is 
‘true’: it accords with the laws of that world. You therefore believe it, 
while you are, as it were, inside. The moment disbelief arises, the spell is 
broken; the magic, or rather art, has failed. You are then out in the 
Primary World again, looking at the little abortive Secondary World from 
outside. (52) 
 
Secondary or literary belief in a secondary world is contingent upon the 
storyteller’s successful cultivation of the “inner consistency of reality” (59), that 
is, the construction of a world whose internal laws of operation, while different 
from the primary world, are nevertheless internally coherent and provide a 
plausibility structure for the story and its fantastic events. The construction, in 
short, of a meaningful world, one with the “inner consistency of reality” for 
those who believe. 
Even in this brief sketch, suggestive parallels emerge between the 
respective projects of religious as well as fantasy worldbuilding. Tolkien uses 
that worldbuilding medium par excellence , language, and more specifically 
narrative, to invoke a world and engender secondary belief therein. This is 
precisely what religious worldbuilding does, albeit on a more ambitious scale 
and with higher  metaphysical stakes. Tolkien already viewed secondary 
worldbuilding as an implicitly religious activity; I might go so far as to define it 
as secondary cosmization in an implicitly sacred mode. Following on from this, I posit 
fandom itself as a kind of secondary faith community . If religious worlds and 
fictional worlds share structures of believing, then the individuals and 
communities who use them as resources for crafting identity, community, and 
meaning may share structures of practice too. Battles over canonicity and the 
development of “fanon” (Duffett 217), fan tourism (Zubernis and Larsen 146), 
integration of fan object s into primary -world religious activities such as 
weddings (Robertson 169 –70), fan “conversion” experiences (Cavicchi, Tramps 
42), and the production of transformative works (Barenblat 175) can all find a 
place within this framework—and so can Enyalië. 
 
 
An Aspirational Cultus? Tolkien Fandom at the Borders of Belief 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  33 
PROCLAIMING: A PILGRIMAGE TO MIDDLE-EARTH 
I am far from the first person to delve into Tolkien fandom as a subject 
of scholarly inquiry. My forerunners include Megan Abrahamson, Maria 
Alberto, Jane Glaubman, Robin Reid, Amy Sturgis, Karen Viars and Cait Coker, 
and Dawn Walls-Thumma as well as two massive global reception studies: The 
Lord of the Rings Project (Barker), a survey of fans of Peter Jackson’s film trilogy 
which received nearly 25,000 responses; and The World Hobbit Project (Barker), a 
similar survey of fans of Jackson’s Hobbit which col lected in excess of 36,000 
responses. Nor am I the first person to engage with the question of religion in 
Tolkien fandom. Previous studies include Markus Altena Davidsen’s 
ethnography of “Tolkien spirituality,” those who use Tolkien’s fictional 
legendarium as the basis for so-called “real-world” religion (188);2 Robin Reid’s 
examination of atheist, agnostic, and animist Tolkien fans (78); and Jeroen de 
Kloet and Giselinde Kuipers’ study of how viewers of Peter Jackson’s film 
trilogy “appropriate The Lord of the Rings […]  to articulate the idea of a spiritual 
self” (317). As valuable as these studies are, for present purposes I am less 
concerned with how fans’ (non)religious commitments interact with Tolkien’s 
fiction. Instead, I wish to examine how Tolkien fan practic es, in and of 
themselves, might be considered (secondarily) “religious” under the definition 
of religion which I advance above.  
If ever a practice were ripe for such consideration, it would be Enyalië. 
The graveside memorial which closes the Tolkien Society’s annual gathering is 
nearly as old as the Society itself. Founded in 1969 as a more “respectable” 
alternative to the explosio n of Tolkien fandom on U.S. college campuses in the 
late 1960s (Saler 191 –92), the Tolkien Society is not the oldest Middle -earth fan 
organization, but it is one of the longest -lived and most active (Coker 526 –27). 
Today it counts hundreds of members from around the world and numerous 
local chapters or “smials,” but it has much humbler origins as a small, tight-knit 
group of fellow-enthusiasts who communicated via mimeographed newsletter. 
Little more than a year after its namesake’s death on 2 September 197 3, a small 
group of Tolkien Society members gathered in Oxford from 13 –15 September 
1974 to tour sites associated with the Inklings, meet with Tolkien’s children 
Michael and Priscilla, and pay their respects at their favorite author’s grave. As 
Rosie and Stuart Clark, the editors of the Society’s bulletin Amon Hen, reported 
at the time, a “floral tribute was laid, and more photographs were taken. ‘A 
Elbereth Gilthoniel’ was recited over the grave, and we left his resting place with 
many a ‘Namarie [sic]!” (11). This simple ritual was the inaugural Enyalië, 
 
2 For a discussion of a similar phenomenon using very different source material, consult 
John Engle’s account of esoteric communities who incorporate H.P. Lovecraft’s Cthulhu 
Mythos into their belief and practice (87–92). 
Tom Emanuel 
34  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
though it was not yet called by that name; and the attendees swiftly agreed to 
make it, and Oxonmoot, an annual tradition.  
In the half-century since then, Oxonmoot has shifted to the beginning 
of September and expanded to involve hundreds of participants both onsite in 
Oxford as well as online. It is, if not unique, then certainly noteworthy among 
fan conventions (“fancons”) f or the ways in which it bridges conventional 
fandom and the academy. 3 Oxonmoot attendees can take part in the trivia 
competitions, amateur performances, fanart exhibitions, and costume contests 
which have long characterized fancons, as well as a slate of panels featuring 
papers from Tolkien scholars both established and emerging. This makes sense: 
many (though not all) Tolkien scholars first came to him as a subject for research 
because we love his works, making us prime examples of the somewhat clunky 
neologism “acafans.” Likewise, Tolkien’s own career as an Oxford professo r, 
and the clear influence of his professional interests on his fiction, have shaped 
how many readers engage with his work as a quasi -academic pursuit 
(Glaubman 265–67). As important as these elements of Oxonmoot may be, the 
Tolkien Society’s own website states that Enyalië “was the origin of Oxonmoot, 
and is in many ways [its] soul” (“Enyalië”). It is, in a sense, both where the 
gathering begins and where it ends, its alpha and its omega. Those of us in 
attendance at Enyalië 2023 were thus followers in a l ong line of pilgrims to 
Tolkien’s resting-place.  
I use the word “pilgrim” deliberately, for such visits to Tolkien’s grave 
are clear examples of fan pilgrimage or literary pilgrimage. There is a rich vein 
of scholarship on this subject, including work by Evgenia Amey, Anne Klara 
Bom, Lorraine Brown, Jane  Lovell and James Thurgill, Ryosuke Okamoto, 
Jennifer Otter Bickerdike, and Lynn Zubernis and Katherine Larsen. There have 
also been investigations of Tolkien -related tourism, including those by Anne 
Buchmann, Kevin Moore, and David Fisher and by Robbie Go h. But with one 
notable exception, I have not identified any studies which are not primarily 
concerned with fan travel to filming locations in New Zealand. Visits to 
Tolkien’s grave, by contrast, constitute a form of biographical tourism in which 
fans visi t sites directly associated with the author. These may include places 
where the author lived and worked, burial locations, and/or real -world locales 
which supposedly inspired their fiction. Visits to such sites reflect and reinforce, 
in the primary world, the reader’s “symbolic pilgrimage” through the secondary 
world of the text (Aden 88). This is certainly the case for Oxonmoot: the first 
attendees even referred to the famous Eagle and Child pub in Oxford as “the 
Mecca of any Inklings pilgrimage” (Clark and Clark 7). Such language mirrors 
 
3 Although I have not attended it in person as I have Oxonmoot, the Mythopoeic Society’s 
annual gathering, Mythcon, would be another example of this. 
An Aspirational Cultus? Tolkien Fandom at the Borders of Belief 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  35 
what happens on traditional religious pilgrimage, where a physical journey 
through an external geography corresponds to an internal, imaginative journey 
of faith (Barush 201–03). As liminal spaces situated upon the boundaries of life 
and death, gravesites are common destinations for both religious as well as fan 
pilgrimages. Visitors may leave offerings such as candles, coins, or flowers 
(Brown 171) or take a moment in contemplative silence (Middleton 313) as ways 
of paying their respects.  
This is exactly how fan pilgrims interact with Tolkien’s grave. When 
Mina Lukić and Dejan Vukelić surveyed 500 visitors to Tolkien-related locations 
in the U.K., they found that 63 percent of them had visited Tolkien’s grave. Of 
those, 84.4 percent reporte d that it was either a very important or the most 
important Tolkien-related location they visited (68). Nine respondents outright 
used the words “quest” or “pilgrimage” to describe the experience (72), but far 
more drew upon the emotionally and spiritually  charged language of 
profundity, beauty, and even sacredness (76 –77). They reported engaging in a 
host of “rituals of homage enacted at Tolkien and Edith’s grave” which  
 
range[d] from silent contemplation to singing, reciting, reading excerpts 
from Tolkien’s works silently or aloud, or living wreaths, flowers, notes, 
drawings, coins, figurines, jewellery, and other tributes, as well as taking 
some mementos, which are usually photos, but several participants also 
mentioned gravestone rubbings, stones, or plants. (75–76) 
 
One respondent noted that their visit was “given further weight by the 
thousands of like -minded pilgrims who had sang, wept, and contemplated at 
that exact spot for so many years” (72). Many others said that the grave was 
where they felt closest to both Tolkien and to his work, prompted by the names 
Beren and Lúthien inscribed on the headstone alongside the Tolkiens’ proper 
names. “I think it’s the ultimate fantasy homage,” one respondent wrote . “The 
connection of his works and his real life manifested in stone” (82). This all makes 
imminent sense within the framework of fandom -as-secondary-faith. Just as 
“primary believers” in Christianity or Islam go on pilgrimage to the Holy Land 
to visit sites associated with a beloved religious world, “secondary believers” in 
Middle-earth go on pilgrimage to Oxford to visit sites associated with a beloved 
secondary world. As Lukić and Vukelić  point out, however, Enyalië is a 
communal practice, not merely a personal one (84–85). If pilgrimage is typically 
an act of individual devotion, what is the relevant parallel to a collective act like 
Enyalië? 
My answer would be liturgy—and using Constance Cherry’s fourfold 
framework of Christian worship, we can see just how closely Enyalië reproduces 
the lineaments of Western Christian liturgy. Just as the narthex of a church 
serves as a liminal threshold between the secular world “outside” and the sacred 
Tom Emanuel 
36  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
world “inside” the worship space (Turner 74 –75), the commute from the 
University of Oxford to Wolvercote Cemetery and the procession to Tolkien’s 
grave can be seen as “narthetical” actions. Once participants are in place, the 
welcome and invitation to reflection constitute the gathering, as scattered 
individuals are called into a collective liturgical body (Cherry 56). The second 
movement of worship, the proclaiming, is the space in a Christian service in 
which “people may be addressed by God through the Holy Scriptures and 
thereby changed for God’s glory and kingdom” (70). At Enyalië, however, there 
are neither readings from Scripture nor a homily. It is not the Wor d in a 
theological sense which is proclaimed but rather “mere” words, a selection from 
Tolkien’s writings. Next comes the responding, in which the gathered community 
takes ritual action in acknowledgement of what they have heard proclaimed: for 
“without some response, worship fails to fulfil its purpose —that of God and 
people in relationship” (99). In Western Christian services there have historically 
been a variety of responses to the proclamation of the Word: the prayers of the 
people, the offering, and most importantly to sacramental traditions such as the 
Roman Catholic Church, communion. At Enyalië, the response corresponds  to 
the silence which follows the reading, the laying of wreaths, and the singing of 
“Namarië.” This last acts as a hinge between responding and sending, which is 
“the final movement of the corporate worship service, in which we plan to 
‘disassemble’ as intentionally as we assembled” (112). This is the point at which 
a Tolkien Society representative announces the conclusion of the gathering and 
attendees bid each other farewell and leave the cemetery. 
For as closely as Enyalië hews to the structure of a Christian worship 
service, right down to its physical setting in a churchyard, it remains 
emphatically nonreligious. At the gathering I attended, the sole reference which 
could be construed as “theologic al” occurred when the chairperson of the 
Tolkien Society alluded to Tolkien’s notion of eucatastrophe, and then only 
insofar as Tolkien called it an “echo of evangelium”—that is, the Good News (OFS 
77). This follows: the Tolkien Society is a secondary fait h community not a 
primary one. Explicit gestures toward a deity or deities make little sense in that 
context. Yet my response to Enyalië, and that of other participants whom I 
witnessed in similarly heartfelt displays of emotion, felt more worshipful than 
anything I have ever experienced at a fancon. To the point: I always carry with 
me a polished hand sample of rose quartz from my hometown, a kind of 
personal talisman. Before the memorial began, I felt compelled to place my rose 
quartz on the edge of Tolki en’s grave for several minutes, as if it would absorb 
the spiritual energy of the occasion. Even in using the term “spiritual” here I 
acknowledge something exceeding literary appreciation; for as Matt Hills 
writes, religious language “does not surrender all religious force when used by 
fans, even if this use may also be ironic or humorous” (88). 
An Aspirational Cultus? Tolkien Fandom at the Borders of Belief 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  37 
Yet here we are faced with a conundrum. According to Cherry, the 
primary purpose of Christian worship is to facilitate an encounter between the 
gathered community and God (54). Under my definition of religion -as-sacred-
worldbuilding, I might say that worsh ip, considered broadly beyond its 
Christian manifestations, is a technology of religious worldbuilding. It forms 
participants for participation in a meaningful cosmos and invites them into 
relationship with the sacred as their tradition understands it (cf. Woodhead 61–
62). No gods were invoked at the Enyalië I attended; no intercessory prayers 
were offered. Yet I felt as though the ritual brought me into contact with 
something which moved me to tears. Perhaps it was simply collective 
effervescence, Émile Durkheim’s coinage for the “electricity” which is generated 
by gatherings oriented toward a common sacred object. Collective effervescence 
reinforces group cohesion and engender s a sense of participation in an 
“impersonal, extra-individual force […] [which] makes [individuals] feel as if 
they are in contact with an extraordinary energy” (Carls). Collective 
effervescence is a common schema for analyzing fan activities such as rock 
concerts (Duffett 151 –52, Elliott 113, Leonard 29). But like many strictly 
functionalist accounts of religion, it risks reducing the sacred to the merely 
social. If my analytical framework for religion and fandom holds, then even if it 
was not inviting us into an encounter with  specific supernatural agents , the 
“secondarily sacred” space of Enyalië was still inviting us into an encounter. The 
question is: with what?4 
 
RESPONDING: THE BORDERS OF BELIEF 
I have argued that both religion and fandom share homologous 
structures of belief and meaning -making, but I have heretofore treated them as 
distinct, if related, phenomena. I now wish to consider whether religion and 
fandom ever transgress the border between one and the other; when and why 
that transgression occurs; and whether it is occurring at Enyalië. T o do this, I 
need to return to Tolkien’s theory of sub-creation and my theory of religion-as-
sacred-worldbuilding, to re -examine the relation between “pr imary” religious 
worlds and “secondary” fantasy worlds —and to see whether that apparently 
self-explanatory binary holds up under closer scrutiny. 
When Tolkien describes the means by which a human artist makes a 
“successful” secondary world, he notably does not call them a worldbuilder; 
rather, he calls them a “ story-maker” (OFS 52, emphasis mine). Tolkien 
understood intimately that narrative constitutes one of the generative logics of 
fantasy worldbuilding, serving as both an impetus to sub -creation and the 
 
4 Many thanks to Dr. Anna Milon for first posing this question to me, and for many 
thoughtful conversations to work out some answers. 
Tom Emanuel 
38  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
means by which the sub-created world is mediated to the reader (cf. Wolf 198). 
In the tradition of narrative theology, it also constitutes one of the generative 
logics of religious worldbuilding. Catholic theologian Johann Baptist Metz 
argues that, contrar y to the way in which systematic theology has often been 
done throughout Christian history, with religious narratives taken as 
illustrations of propositional truth,  
 
stories of conversion and of exodus do not serve as dramatic window -
dressing for a preformulated ‘pure’ theology. Rather, they belong to the 
fundamental way theology itself operates. […] On the contrary, talk 
about God has an essential and inalienable memo rative and narrative 
structure. (62) 
 
Likewise, in his explorations of Biblical parables, John Dominic Crossan posits 
that the Bible should itself be understood as parabolic, as a story first and 
foremost which is meant to upend the listener’s pat expectations and provoke 
spiritual and social transformation (56). Most germane to my present purposes 
is George Lindbeck, whose linguistic -cultural understanding of theology 
complements Tolkien’s understanding of the religiosity of narrative 
worldbuilding to a truly remarkable extent. 
For Lindbeck, religious symbols, stories, and rituals are themselves a 
kind of language, providing a grammar and a vocabulary in which religious 
communities communicate sacred meaning (cf. Woodhead 61 –62). To be 
religious, then, is to be fluent in the language that is religion. Lindbeck writes, 
from his standpoint as a Christian theologian: 
 
To become a Christian involves learning the story of Israel and Jesus well 
enough to interpret and experience oneself and one’s world in its terms. 
[…] A comprehensive scheme or story used to structure all dimensions 
of existence is not primarily a set of propositions to be believed, but is 
rather the medium in which one moves, a set of skills that one employs 
in living one’s life. (34–35) 
 
The Biblical tradition sings a religious world into existence by means of story, 
symbol, and ritual—and Christians enter into its story-world in much the same 
way that readers enter into Middle-earth. However, the key difference between 
the mere reader and the true believer is that for the latter, the story-world is not 
simply literary: 
 
For those who are steeped in them [religious narratives], no world is 
more real than the ones they create. A scriptural world is thus able to 
absorb the universe. It supplies the interpretive framework within which 
An Aspirational Cultus? Tolkien Fandom at the Borders of Belief 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  39 
believers seek to live their lives and understand reality. […] It is the text, 
so to speak, which absorbs the world, rather than the world the text. (117–
118) 
 
I am reminded of Tolkien’s remarks on eucatastrophe , the sudden joyous turn 
in a work of fantasy which he sees as “the true form of fairy-tale, and its highest 
function” (OFS 75). For Tolkien, this is the moment at which a fairy-story comes 
closest to offering readers an intimation of the sacred: “In such stories when the 
sudden ‘turn’ comes we get a piercing glimpse of joy, and heart’s desire, that for 
a moment passes outside the frame, rends indeed the very web of story, and lets 
a gleam come through” (OF S 76). Note Tolkien’s and Lindbeck’s shared 
language of the story transcending its own narrative frame. 5 That, I argue, is 
what is happening at Enyalië: Tolkien’s secondary world is transcending its 
narrative frame, if only for as long as the ritual lasts. 
No doubt Tolkien’s grave in general, and Enyalië in particular, enable 
some participants to feel closer to J.R.R. Tolkien the historical person (Lukić and 
Vukelić 83 –84). I now reflect that placing my hand sample of rose quartz on 
Tolkien’s grave was motivated, in part, by an awareness that his mortal remains 
are laid to rest on this very spot. But it stretches credulity in more ways than one 
to describe Enyalië as veneration of Tolkien the man in place of a saint, let alone 
a god. Nor, I think, does it necessarily imply primary belief in the events and 
characters of his secondary world (cf. Davidsen 31–32). Rather, it is Enyalië itself 
which “passes outside the frame” of the text (OFS  76). For those of us who feel 
called to gather at Wolvercote Cemetery, Middle -earth is “real” enough to 
justify, not only the expenses associated with attending Oxonmoot, but also our 
physical and emotion al presence at an embodied ritual in a literal, liminal 
churchyard—all this despite, or alongside, any primary -world spiritual 
commitments we may or may not hold.  
But that is not all: for the building -blocks that make up Enyalië are 
quarried from Middle-earth itself. In a Christian liturgy, a historically common 
way of moving from the response into the sending is by singing the Nunc dimitis, 
the Song of Simeon: “Master, now you are dismissing your servant in peace, 
according to your word, for my eyes have seen your salvation, which you have 
prepared in the presence of all peoples, a light for revelation to the gentiles and 
for glory to your pe ople Israel” (Luke 2:29 –32). As discussed above, a 
performance of Galadriel’s farewell-song “Namarië” fulfils this role at Enyalië. 
The musical setting used at Enyalië is Tolkien’s own, who consciously modelled 
 
5 At the risk of theological overboldness, I might even say that a religious world can be 
thought of us a secondary world which has come to “absorb” the primary one —but to 
tweak the classic scholarly deflection, that passes well outside the frame of the pre sent 
paper. 
Tom Emanuel 
40  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
it on Gregorian chant (Tolkien & Swann v–vi). But the Quenya text of the poem 
gestures not toward theological salvation but toward Valinor —a place of great 
spiritual significance in the legendarium, no doubt, but not exactly the Christian 
Heaven. “Namarië” has effectively transcended its narrative context to become, 
itself, liturgical song. This is even clearer during the proclaiming section of the 
ritual. At Enyalië 2023, the excerpt from The Lord of the Rings spanning the fall of 
Barad-dûr to Sam’s awaken ing in Ithilien lasted about thirteen uninterrupted 
minutes. This gave attendees more time to deepen meaningfully into the story -
world than a shorter reading might have —or, for that matter, than many 
perfunctory readings of sacred texts in primary -world re ligious rituals. 
Moreover, this passage is part of the climax of The Lord of the Rings—”the most 
stunning eucatastrophe in modern literature,” as Verlyn Flieger would have it  
(“The Arch and the Keystone” 14). The only other moment in Tolkien’s 
legendarium that comes anywhere close is when the immortal Lúthien “plead[s] 
before the inexorable Mandos” ( Letters 590, #340) for the life of her mortal 
beloved Beren. But by chance, “if chance you call it” ( LotR I.7.126), Beren and 
Lúthien are present at Enyalië as well. Their names are emblazoned upon 
Tolkien’s headstone like the words “Do this in remembrance of me” (Luke 22:19) 
may be emblazoned upon a Christian altar. 
The line I just cited about “the inexorable Mandos” comes from a letter 
Tolkien wrote to his son Christopher in July 1972, as he was making 
preparations for his wife Edith’s grave. He draws a direct comparison between 
Lúthien and Beren on the one hand and Edith and himself on the other, 
requesting that her grave be inscribed with the Elvish name “which says for me 
more than a multitude of words: for she was (and knew she was) my Lúthien” 
(Letters 590, #350). Five decades later, Lukić and Vukelić reflected on their survey 
of visitors to the grave which the Tolkiens share: 
 
[I]t is a site where not only Tolkien and Edith are present, but also his 
fictional world, evoked by a simple, but powerful reference. This double 
presence can simultaneously tap into readers’ admiration and respect for 
the author himself and their love fo r his writings. This “fusion of 
Tolkien’s personal life and his ‘sub-creation’”for many visitors makes the 
grave the most important Tolkien-related place in the world[.] (71) 
 
For Tolkien, the line between “primary” and “secondary” had already long since 
blurred, at least as far as Beren and Lúthien were concerned; the story had 
transcended its narrative frame (cf. Flieger, “Tolkien’s Lúthien” 235). None of 
Tolkien’s fans will ever have the same relationship to those characters that their 
author did. But it does not seem unreasonable to me to infer that at least some 
Enyalië attendees are experiencing the interpenetration of Middle -earth with 
our own lives—or at least that is what I experienced at Enyalië 2023. By this I do 
An Aspirational Cultus? Tolkien Fandom at the Borders of Belief 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  41 
not mean that I came to believe literally in the destruction of the One Ring and 
the fall of Sauron, any more than Tolkien believed in the Tale of Beren and 
Lúthien as a factual account of historical events.6 Rather, I became so immersed 
in the story -world—bodily, emotionally, imaginatively, symbolically —that 
sharp distinctions between “primary” and “secondary” no longer adequately 
described my engagement with it. Truth be told, I am not convinced that they 
ever did. 
 This brings me back to my as -yet unanswered question: what, if 
anything, do we encounter at Enyalië? Or to put it another way: what do we 
glimpse beyond the narrative frame? For the Roman Catholic Tolkien, it is an 
intimation of the Good News of Christian ity (OFS 77, cf. Letters 579, #328). 
Christian Tolkien commentators such as Paul E. Kerry (244), Alison Milbank 
(111–12), and Ralph Wood (275) would go further, viewing Middle -earth itself 
as a prism which refracts Christian truth, even to non -Christians who do not 
recognize its source. Within that framework, the question of what we encounter 
at Enyalië is not really a question: it is the God of Christianity, mediated to us 
by Tolkien’s legendarium. I would respond that if Tolkien had wanted his 
readers to only find Christian truth(s) in his fiction, he ought to have sub-created 
a world that was less open to interpretation (cf. Emanuel 47–48). For as much as 
it resembles one, Enyalië is not a Christian worship service; it is a fan ritual. The 
criterion for participation is not primary belief in a religious world but 
secondary belief in a fantasy world. The sub -creator of that world held certain 
religious views, but the readers who enter therein are in no way obligated to 
share them. We should hardly expect a fan ritual based on Middle-earth to leave 
any less room for interpretation than Middle-earth itself. 
Again: what do we encounter at Enyalië? Perhaps now I can give a 
proper answer —or better, several answers. We encounter Middle -earth and 
Tolkien its maker. We encounter one another, called together in this place by a 
shared love of these stories. And we encounter whatever intimation of “Joy, Joy 
beyond the walls of the world, poignant as grief” (OFS  75) it is that we 
experience when a story transcends its narrative frame and becomes our story—
as fans, as fellow-pilgrims, as (non)believers. We encounter, in short, the sacred 
as Gordon Lynch defines it, not as theological transcendence  but rather as that 
“gravitational pull on the feelings, motivations and behaviour of individuals 
and groups that [...] binds people into particular kinds of identities, communities 
and ways of living” (138). We need not pin a particular religious interpret ation 
to Enyalië, any more than we need pin a particular religious interpretation to 
 
6 Though it may be worth noting that Tolkien also appears not to have believed in the 
Garden of Eden, a central narrative of his own Christian tradition, as a factual account of 
historical events (Letters 158–159, #96). 
Tom Emanuel 
42  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
The Lord of the Rings . It is not a worship service; it has little need of formal 
doctrines. But it is a liturgy, a memorial, a collective worldbuilding practice of 
the community of fans who return to Tolkien’s grave year after year to celebrate 
his sub-creation and weave it more deeply into the fabric of our own lives. 
 
SENDING: AN ASPIRATIONAL CULTUS? 
Having wandered into the perilous realm where primary and 
secondary worlds touch, I would now like to take a step back and offer some 
important clarifications. Firstly, I want to re-emphasize that for the vast majority 
of readers, Tolkien fandom is not a religion in the same sense that, say, Islam or 
Sikhism or Eastern Orthodox Christianity would be. To be sure, there are 
individual cases where a fictional secondary world has, in fact, “absorbed” the 
primary world in Lindbeck’s sense. Markus Altena Davidsen’s aforementioned 
Tolkien religionists (31–32) are one example; Star Wars-inspired Jedis (Grant 39), 
Lovecraft spiritualists (Engle 85), and “Snapewives” in the Harry Potter fandom 
(Burton 64 –65) are three others. This is a marginal phenomenon in any fan 
community, however. I would wager that for most, The Silmarillion is no more a 
substitute for Holy Writ than Enyalië is a substitute for the Hajj. Viewing 
fandom as a simplistic “replacement apparatus” (Bickerdike 8) for religion is 
problematic for any numb er of reasons, but perhaps most obviously because 
many Tolkien fans, myself included, already belong to a religious tradition. Our 
love of Middle -earth—of any fictional story -world—exists alongside and in 
conversation with our faith (cf. Crome 413). The sa me goes for nonreligious 
readers, who might understandably bristle at being told that their fandom, 
however deeply felt, was “actually” religion in disguise (400–01). Fandom is less 
religious than religioid, Georg Simmel’s word to describe attitudes, affec ts, 
practices, and communities which reproduce religious forms without embracing 
religious content (Motak 112–14). 
Second, I want to acknowledge again that my definition of religion-as-
sacred-worldbuilding is a definition, not the definition. Drawing as it does on 
religious studies, fan studies, and fantasy studies alike, I submit that it enables 
us to better understand how worldbuilding contributes to the development of 
both religion and media fandom, which in turn enables us to unpick the religious 
affects, effects, and affordances of narrative worlds both sacred and secular. But 
other definitions will draw our attent ion to other important features and 
manifestations of the religious impulse in other cultures and eras. I have tried to 
be flexible and non -essentialist in constructing and applying my model, and I 
believe it has cross -cultural relevance. Yet I have relied  primarily on Western 
thinkers and frameworks to theorize a British fan organization’s Anglophone 
fan ritual, one which itself mirrors the form of Western Christian liturgy and is 
grounded in the works of a White, male, English, Roman Catholic author of the 
An Aspirational Cultus? Tolkien Fandom at the Borders of Belief 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  43 
mid-twentieth century. Whether my analytical framework would hold up 
similarly well in non-Western contexts remains an open question. For example: 
in 2023, the journal Transformative Works and Cultures published a special issue 
wholly dedicated to fandom(s) in China. My intuition is that things might shape 
up rather differently in a country of 1.4 billion people, grounded in non -
Christian and non -Western cultural and religious traditions, officially 
communist yet deeply embedded in the global capitalist eco nomy. More work 
remains to be done. 
Third, I want to offer a caution to my fellow “secondary believers.” If 
Tolkien fandom is a secondary faith community as I have argued, then we must 
engage Tolkien’s story -world ethically and responsibly, just as Christians like 
myself must grapple with th e real tensions in our own tradition. Regarding 
controversy over the inclusion of dark -skinned Elves and Dwarves in Tolkien 
fanfic and fan art, Walls-Thumma writes: 
 
Again and again, what some fans thought Tolkien meant or believed he 
intended was elevated to the status of canon where it was not open to 
question and where canonicity became a convenient diversion from 
discussions of sexism, racism, and homophobia in the legendarium and 
fandom. (95) 
 
In many ways the Christian Bible is no different: powerful narratives of 
resurrection, liberation, and radical grace coexist alongside “texts of terror” 
which ecclesiastical authorities have weaponized to subjugate women and 
queer people for centuries (Trible 3, Bauer-Levesque 390). In a time when stories 
of all stripes are being wielded as cudgels against marginalized communities, 
we must confront the fact it is precisely the multivalent meanings of our stories 
and rituals which open them up them to noxiou s interpretations (Emanuel 47). 
It is incumbent upon us to actively build communities that are hospitable to as 
many different expressions of humanity as possible, in Tolkien fandom and 
beyond. 
Bearing these caveats in mind, however, I want to conclude with that 
openness to interpretation which I just mentioned. Even if the Tolkien Society is 
not the Church of Rome —even if Enyalië is not Easter Sunday —there is no 
denying that Tolkien’s stories of Middle-earth are profoundly moving to many 
people. In my ongoing research into the reception of The Lord of the Rings  
amongst twenty-first century readers, I am consistently struck by the depth of 
feeling with which people express their love and appreciat ion for Tolkien’s 
masterpiece. Likewise, even if fandom is not a replacement for religion per se, 
formal religious affiliation has been declining in the so -called “developed” 
world for decades. Political scientist Ryan Burge reports that 30 percent of 
Americans now describe themselves as nonreligious, the highest percentage 
Tom Emanuel 
44  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
ever (31). There are no signs that this downward trend of disaffiliation from 
traditional spiritual communities will stop anytime soon: among young Gen Z 
Americans, only 36 percent identify as Christian, in contrast to 48 percent who 
identify as atheist, a gnostic, or “nothing in particular” (83). In a very real way, 
Westerners like myself are living in a post -Christian world. Religion has not 
disappeared, of course. But as philosopher of secularity Charles Taylor writes, 
“a society in which it was virtually impossible not to believe in God” has given 
way “to one in which faith, even for the staunchest believer, is one human 
possibility among others” (3). Under such conditions, a person’s love of Tolkien 
might well prove an important tool for crafting identit y, finding community, 
and making meaning. It has certainly proven to be so for that bookish boy 
huddled in the graveyard up the road, obsessively rereading The Return of the 
King for the umpteenth time. 
In a pluralistic world of many faiths and no faiths, perhaps Enyalië can 
serve as an exemplar of a “post-Christian liturgy.” It draws upon familiar ritual 
technologies and the story -worlds that are already meaningful to people, 
offering a glimpse of “somet hing more” without needing to delimit or define 
the something in question. It cracks open “secondarily sacred” space in which 
individuals can meet and be moved but whose religious and ideological 
differences might otherwise prevent them from practicing alo ngside one 
another. Whatever a person’s ethical and metaphysical commitments regarding 
the primary world, there is nothing preventing them from finding common 
meaning in a secondary world. For there remains a key difference between 
religion and fandom, Tol kien’s misgivings notwithstanding. Religious 
practitioners (typically) consider the religious world and the primary world to 
be one and the same. Fans, on the other hand, (typically) proceed from the 
assumption that there is a boundary between the secondar y worlds of their 
favorite texts and the primary world —or perhaps I should say worlds—of 
everyday life. If I have accomplished nothing else, I hope to have shown that 
this boundary may be more porous than we have been led to believe.
