THE REV. TOM EMANUEL is a theologian and doctoral researcher with the University 
of Glasgow’s Centre for Fantasy and the Fantastic. His current project explores the 
reception of J.R.R. Tolkien’s The Lord of the Rings among nonreligious readers and is 
funded by an Arts and Humanities Research Council (AHRC) doctoral partnership 
with the Scottish Graduate School of Arts and Humanities (SGSAH). His work has 
appeared in the  Journal of Tolkien Research, Mallorn, Mythlore, Tolkien Studies, and 
Zeitschrift für Fantastikforschung. Tom is an ordained minister in the United Church 
of Christ and lives in Glasgow with his spouse and their two children, whom they 
are already inducting into the “deplorable cultus” of Tolkien fandom. 
 
 
 

  
 
  
 

  
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  51 
 
 
                IVINITY  AND THE VOID IN CHINESE AND  
                     THAI TRANSLATIONS OF THE AINULINDALË  
 
                                           ERIC REINDERS  
 
OLKIEN’S WORK HAS BECOME A GLOBAL PHENOMENON , and I argue for more 
attention to readings outside of English. What new readings of his texts 
emerge from placing them in the contexts of different languages, such as in this 
case Mandarin Chinese or Thai? I want to think of these stories circulating 
globally.  
This is not a question of what the author may have intended, but of 
how the stories are perceived and re -told. The canonicity of the original is 
immutable, but millions of fans can’t read the original, except through a 
translation. My approach is to treat the translations as stories in their own right, 
and then to try to articulate some of these alternatives into my native tongue. It 
is in multiple translations of the same source text that we begin to see Tolkien’s 
works as a kind of narrative tradition of greater complexity than that of any 
single book in one’s native language —especially if it’s under copyright. 
Everyone’s first reading of The Lord of the Rings —even if it is not in English —
remains foundational in any subsequent reading; and disputes over 
interpretation quickly become disputes over translations. Translation is a precise, 
linguistically intimate exchange of meaning, and I hope that a focus on these rich 
negotiations of meaning might contribute to a larger project of a comparative 
study of the imagination. I am looking at Mandarin Chinese and Thai simply 
because these are within my own ability, and I genuinely encourage studies 
adding to the existing literature on Tolkien’s works in all its many languages. 
Looking at two different languages, and  at multiple translations in the same 
language, provides the opportunity to shift perspective and make more complex 
comparisons.  
Previous studies of Tolkien in translation have dealt primarily with 
Western translations —such as Tolkien Through Russian Eyes  (2003), Tolkien in 
Translation (2011) and a number of articles and chapters. Here I want to expand 
that comparative scope. The scholarship has (in my view) tended to focus on a 
qualitative evaluation of fidelity. If we imagine translation primarily in terms of 
a transfer of semantic content, our questions tend to be about fidelity, the degree 
to which the translation has replicated or “captured” the precise meaning of the 
original text, including all of its nuances. But taken literally, this is to put 
translation in an unwinnable battle with itself, since in fact no two words ever 
mean exactly the same thing. Instead, current work in  translation theory, such 
T 
Eric Reinders 
52  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
as that of Emmerich (2017), Venuti (2019), and Briggs (2017), has emphasized 
the creativity of new re-tellings.  
Though Tolkien was cautious about translations of his own work, his 
discussion of story -telling in “On Fairy -stories” supports the view that two 
“versions” of the “same story” are to be considered each in their own right, not 
only as an “original” and subsequent “derivatives.” Though it would be quixotic 
to deny the actual sequence of publication, I set aside the hierarchy of original and 
translations, in order to consider translated texts as circulations of the 
imagination, as elaborations of a mythos, and  as intercultural exchange. 
Although a translator might simply misread the original, for the most part we 
are dealing with varying interpretations, expressions of translators’ individual 
and idiosyncratic understandings. Fidelity is certainly a valid conce rn, but it is 
not the only concern.  
To reveal to English -readers some of what is happening in the 
translated texts, I use a literalistic style of back -translation, combined with 
annotation and discussion. While no two words can ever mean exactly the same 
thing, we easily recognize degrees of proximity. One translation may represent 
the original’s words in a relatively literal way, while another is less “faithful” but 
communicates what we perceive as the intention of the story more accurately. 
In the one case the text is relatively foreignized (by its inclusion of phrases 
deemed strange in the target language); in another case the text is relatively 
domesticated (by its use of idioms which diverge from the source text but are 
meaningful in the target language). This contrast is also known as formal vs. 
dynamic translation, or source-language-oriented and target-language-oriented 
translations. In making this contrast, I emphasize “relatively,” because although 
absolute literalism in a translation is simply impossible, and absolute 
domestication would no longer be considered a translation at all; yet on a case -
by-case basis we can still see the relative distinctions quite clearly. Back -
translation leans heavily towards the formal, source -language-oriented, and 
foreignizing style. One caveat is that these back -translations should not be 
mistaken for good or finished translations. In order to evoke in Engl ish what is 
happening in the Chinese or Thai, my back-translations are often awkward and 
ungrammatical, which of course is not true of the Chinese and Thai texts.  
In Reading Tolkien in Chinese: Religion, Fantasy and Translation  (2024), 
using the original and three Chinese translations, I explored a variety of themes, 
including the afterlife and fate. One theme I did not address in that study was 
cosmogony, primarily described in the Ainulindalë and Valaquenta. Here I 
examine a 2001 translation by Li Yao for the Yilin Press, a 2002 translation by 
Deng Jiawan (Joy Teng), and her 2015 revised translation —that is, two 
translations by the same translator. And to this list  I can add a 2010 Thai 
translation by Thida Thanyaprasertkul. For ease of reference, these works will 
Divinity and the Void in Chinese and Thai Translations of the Ainulindalë 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  53 
be cited by translator, name of text, and page number; and in the case of the two 
Silmarillions translated by Deng, with the year of publication added.  
Firstly, the titles. In the Chinese translations, The Silmarillion  is 
paraphrased as “the elves’ jewels.” Jingling baozuan 精靈寶鑽/ 精灵宝钻. In the 
Yilin edition, there is an added prefix, Mojie qiyuan 魔戒起源 (“The Beginning of 
The Magic Ring ”); mojie is the general name for the whole franchise. So the 
Chinese title is a gloss rather than a transliteration of “The Silmarillion.” In the 
text, the word “Silmaril” is transliterated as Xi-ermali-er 熙尔玛利尔, or Ximali-er 
茜玛丽尔 but more often jingling baozuan, “the elves’ j ewels” is used. The Thai 
title is not precisely The Silmarillion either, but: ต ำนำนแห่งซิลมำริล Tamnaan haeng 
Sinmarin, “legend of the Silmarils” which tells us something of the text’s genre, 
and transliterates Silmaril as Sinmarin. (In Thai, the ‘l’ sound in final position 
within a syllable such as ‘sil’ is converted to an ‘n’ sound.)  
As for the Ainulindalë, Li retitles this text simply as Airu (i.e. Eru, the 
name of Ilúvatar), though the Valaquenta is given as “the legend of the Valar 
(Fanla de chuanshuo ).” For the Ainulindalë, Deng uses Ainu de da yuezhang  
埃努的大樂章 (the great musical movement of the Ainur). For Valaquenta, Weila 
benji (the annals of the Valar); benji is usually used for royal annals, pertaining 
to the deeds of an emperor. In her revised translation, the Ainulindalë is retitled 
Chuangshilu 创世录 (record of the creation of the world). The Thai translation 
simply renders Ainulindalë and Valaquenta phonetically.  
In examining these multiple versions, I am looking specifically for 
instances where something stands out as different—not in error but assuming a 
conscientious effort to translate well. In the case of these cosmogonic narratives, 
the first line in the original and in translations gives us some different senses of 
the name Eru, and of Eru’s pronouns, and therefore the nature of Eru. Slightly 
further in the narrative we find what I consider to be some intriguingly different 
connotations of the Void.  
 
THE FIRST LINE 
The Ainulindalë begins with a disembodied unity creating a series of 
successively less powerful and more physical beings. The first generation of 
such beings manifests as musical notes, which form chords, and hence harmony, 
complexity, and inevitably discord. Vision is created to express that music, and 
then the materialization of that vision is the world. The world is then populated. 
We are presented with a descent from spirit to matter, from unity to multiplicity 
and specificity. The Valar, the elves, the n humans (or as Tolkien called them, 
“Men”), then dwarves, orcs. There are a few quirks to this descent. Against the 
divine plan, dwarves were created before elves, but Ilúvatar put them to sleep. 
Also, we don’t hear about animals or bacteria, but the general sequence is clear. 
As with all mythologies, certain obvious questions are unanswered.  
Eric Reinders 
54  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
In drawing a continuous narrative line from the very beginning of 
everything to the Fourth Age under the “dominion of Men,” the entire oeuvre 
moves from a high mythological register to chronicles in historical time. There 
are several markers of this descent. We note the shift in tone from The Silmarillion 
to The Hobbit. The thinning of the elves is also a transition from myth to history. 
Or perhaps the Valar themselves might represent a kind of pivot: initially very 
active, then gradually disappearing from the story. Even early on, they could 
take form, or not. We read that their taking form is a kind of clothing or disguise. 
The Vala Ulmo “will seldom clothe himself in a body after the manner of his 
peers” (Silmarillion [Silm.] 17). Li gives us an overly literalistic “seldom wears 
clothes like his peers” (Li Silm. 17), which suggests that Ulmo is usually naked. 
Deng has, Ulmo “very rarely like [his] peers puts on a flesh -body form 
(肉身的形體 roushen de xingti )” (Deng Silm. 2002 19). The Thai: “He ( phra-ong) 
does not like to walk on the land, and does not like wearing a body (rang, figure) 
disguise ( jamlaeng) such as the other devas ( thep, gods) prefer” 
(Thanyaprasertkul Silm. 56).  
The opening phrase from the Ainulindalë is: “There was Eru, the One, 
who in Arda is called Ilúvatar” ( Silm. 3). Li has “ 惟一者” 
埃如,在阿尔达王国被叫做伊路瓦塔” (Li Silm. 3). Back -translated, this is: “‘The 
only one’ Airu, in the A-er-da royal domain is called Yi-lu-wa-ta.” The phrase “the 
only one” is in quotation marks for some reason, perhaps to suggest whatever 
the capitalization of “One” suggests, such as that “the One” is a title or name, 
not merely “there was just the one of him.” Notice also the active verbs: this 
version reduces the sense of beginning the story at the start (“There was . . . ”), 
instead starting with a naming (“. . . is called . . . ”).  
Deng’s two versions have almost the same basic text. “始有一如, 
‘独一之神’, 其名在世界間稱為伊露維塔.” “In the beginning was yiru, ‘the only 
god,’ its name in the world ( shijian) was yi-lu-wei-ta” (Deng Silm. 2002, 3). This 
2002 edition has a series of footnotes on key terms (or rather, sidenotes, since as 
a vertical text they appear to the left of the page, not at the bottom. The sidenotes 
were imported into the body of the text, parenthetically, in the 2015 version). For 
example, after yiru, Deng gives the term in Roman lett ers, notes that it is a 
Quenya word, and gives a general description of that deity. For the 2015 
publication, she changed the paraphrase of Arda as “the world” to a 
transliteration (a-er-da 阿尔达) (Deng 2015 Silm. 31). In both cases, for “the One,” 
Deng gives us duyi zhi shen  (the only god). In this context it is clear that shen 
should be “god,” though the word 神shen can also mean a spirit, or 
consciousness, or soul. Since Chinese does not have capital letters, there is no 
distinction between “god” and “God.” Shen might also be plural, though here 
the context makes it clear we are talking about a singular god, not gods.  
 
Divinity and the Void in Chinese and Thai Translations of the Ainulindalë 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  55 
THE NAME ERU  
The first thing to note here is the differences of dealing with the proper 
name Eru. The Chinese script is in the form of characters. All characters have 
meanings, however obscure, but certain characters are customarily used for 
transliteration. Phrases transliterated into Mandarin Chinese have a distinctive 
look. Li gives Eru as 埃如 airu, and these characters are fairly commonly used in 
transliteration, so I think readers would immediately perceive it as a proper 
noun, despite the lack of capitalization in Chinese. In English, if the text said, 
“There was eru,” we might be slightly confus ed, but with the capital E, we 
accept that “Eru” is the name of “the One,” a proper noun, and we do not 
therefore try to find “eru” in a dictionary, or wonder if it is a typo. In itself, “Eru” 
does not mean anything outside of Tolkien’s usage (Halsall 31 –32). But what is 
the meaning of Li’s airu? Ai means dust, but it is often used in transliterations—
such as the first syllable of the Chinese for Eiffel Tower, Exxon Corporation, 
emir, and Ethiopia. Ru 如 has a range of meanings related to if, as if, like, eq ual, 
for example, thus, and according to. It is not commonly used for transliterating 
names beginning with Ru–. Nonetheless, again, airu closely resembles in sound 
the original Eru, easily perceived, in Chinese as in English, as a proper noun but 
not much else. I doubt anyone would read it as “dust-like.”  
Here, though, we see a fascinating move by Deng: she chose characters 
closely resembling the sound of Eru —yiru—but these characters have potent 
meanings. Yi is “one,” and combined with ru we get: 一如 (yiru). In a Buddhist 
context, yiru is a literary term for absolute reality, the “one thusness,” a synonym 
of zhenru (“true thusness,” namely, reality as it “really is like”); the nondual 
whole of existence, in contrast with our fragmenting perceptions. In saying that 
ultimate reality is like what it is like, one is reminded of the phrase, “it is what it 
is,” or even Exodus 3.14, where God says to Moses, “I am who I am” or “I am 
that I am,” though the Buddhist “thu sness” is certainly not meant in a theistic 
sense. At least for those readers with some knowledge of Buddhist philosophy, 
yiru then belongs to a register of place -markers for a non -dual ultimate reality 
beyond words. For readers without such knowledge, it would still have a certain 
metaphysical or spiritual sound. This yi一is not common in transliteration, 
where yi 伊, yi 以, or yi 意 are more common (as in Iran, Israel, and Italy).  
Starting from a strong assertion of the limits of language, Buddhist 
philosophers developed a vocabulary for talking about reality while avoiding 
the pitfalls of saying too much about it —this is the idea of language as the 
“finger pointing to the moon.” Yiru is a descriptor or label for ultimate reality, 
which posits that reality is non-dual (yi, one), and furthermore “like that” or “is 
what it is” (ru). Yiru is thus a name for the fundamental nature of reality here and 
now, much like Buddhist emptiness. The idea of emptiness does not say that 
things simply don’t exist, but rather they don’t exist in themselves. In a Buddhist 
Eric Reinders 
56  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
sense the term does not refer to a creator god, because the true nature of reality 
does not have such things as intentions, desires, or individual consciousness, so 
perhaps this choice makes the cosmogony slightly less theistic. But if so, only 
momentarily—as the story goes on, Eru sees, speaks, and makes, like a god.  
Since Thai is phonetic, the Thai translation gives us a rather different 
linguistic phenomenon: “A long time ago, there was eru the one who is one 
(พระผู้เป็นหนึ่ง phra pu pen neung ) who was called in the arda world iluwata” 
(Thanyaprasertkul Silm. 39). Pu pen neung is literally “person who is one.” Phra 
is an honorific prefix for various sacred persons, most commonly monks but also 
gods. As with Chinese, lacking the definite article and capital letters, simply to 
say “the One” would sound like just “one” and not make much sense, hence this 
more formal ph rasing. Phra is followed in the next line by the use of พระองค์ 
phra-ong, combining the sacred-honorific phra with ong, the Thai count noun for 
sacred objects; phra-ong becomes a pronoun, putting Ilúvatar in the class of gods, 
kings, Buddhas and Buddha -images. (For a sense of how count nouns work, 
think of “one piece of paper,” “two pairs of trousers,” etc.) 
In the course of the Southward migration of the Tai peoples, they 
adapted a Khmer script for their writing, a script which is phonetic in nature. 
The language absorbed vocabulary of Indic origin, which is now particularly 
visible in the jargons of royalty and Buddhism. Monarchs of the current dynasty 
for example are known as พระรำม Phra-raam the first, the second, etc.—in other 
words the Hindu deity Rama. This distinctive honorific or “royal” language 
(raajasap) is not merely a set of specialized terms but a series of different lexical 
registers. In English we can say “The King ate breakfast” and “the dog ate 
breakfast” using the same verb; in Thai there are entirely different terms for 
various social contexts. In the third paragraph of the Ainulindalë, Ilúvatar “said” 
such-and-such; in Thai the verb is trat ฅรัส which is only used for royalty or 
deities. Of course English is capable of distinguishing such things: though 
Tolkien used a simple “said,” we might say a god like Ilúvatar “pronounced” or 
“declaimed,” but these do not approach the systematic, specific hierarchies of 
Thai royal language. Consistently the Thai uses verbs in raajasap, for Ilúvatar 
and the Valar: trat for speak, ถวำย tawai for give, and so on. The text also uses 
เทพ thep (a Thai pronunciation of the Sanskrit word deva, meaning “god”) as a 
prefix before the proper names of Valar: the deva Ulmo, the deva Mandos. This 
is entirely natural in Thai, but now, we might return to the English and find “he” 
and “said” rather flat. Almost disrespectful. In English as in Thai, we l earn of 
Ilúvatar’s divine nature semantically (through what is said of Him), but the Thai 
adds a layer of lexical distinction, which sounds like something you would hear 
in a temple, or a palace.  
 
 
Divinity and the Void in Chinese and Thai Translations of the Ainulindalë 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  57 
HE AND HE  
It came to pass, “he made first the Ainur, the Holy Ones, that were the 
offspring of his thought, and they were with him before aught else was made. 
And he spoke to them, propounding to them themes of music; and they sang 
before him, and he was glad” ( Silm. 3). In the original, Ilúvatar has a male 
pronoun. Let us set aside the complex theological question and cultural history 
of gendering something like the Christian God or the cosmic and formless 
Ilúvatar, and instead observe that “he” is a lower-case pronoun. Surely Ilúvatar 
deserves a He?  
Neither Chinese nor Thai have capital letters, so the distinction 
between “He” and “he” is either invisible, or must be dealt with in other ways. 
Li gives us: “he (ta 他) first made the “holy worthies” [or sages, shengxian] a-yi-
nuo. These ‘holy worthies’ were all born from what his heart -mind thought. 
Before other people were created, there were only them and him together. 
Ilúvatar and they spoke of many things [ tantian shuodi, talked of heaven and 
spoke of earth ], and he gave them musical themes; and so th ey sang for him 
songs, he was very happy” (Li Silm. 3). The third-person pronoun ta 他 is either 
gender-neutral or male, or ambiguously both, because it is a default ungendered 
pronoun for people in general, much like the now -outdated use of “he” or 
“man” for people in general. And yet, ta 他 is in contrast with ta 她, “she,” a 
character never used for males. So with Li’s translation the male gender is 
implicit, easily assumed, strongly implied. But again the “he” here (ta 他 ) is the 
exact same pronoun as any other, non-divine “he” (ta 他 ).  
Deng however uses another variation: “It (ta 祂) first made from [Its] 
own thought many ai-nu, ‘the sacred envoys’ ( shensheng de shizhe ), they were 
before the myriad things were made and were with It. It spoke to them, It 
proposed to them the topics of song ( 樂曲的主題 yuequ de zhuti ). So before It, 
they loudly sang, which made It completely happy” (Deng Silm. 2002 3). In her 
later translation, she changed “ the sacred envoys” to “ holy ones ( shenshengzhe 
神圣者)” (Deng Silm. 2015, 31).  
Deng uses a less common variant of the third person pronoun, ta 祂 
rather than the more common ta 他 as Li uses. They are pronounced the same 
(just as “He” and “he” are pronounced the same), but ta 祂 has a radical 
component which indicates it is used for gods. Since Tolkien gendered Ilúvatar 
male, it makes sense to read ta 祂 in this line as male, but whereas “he” is always 
male, ta 祂 is not particularly gender -specific. This is why I have translated 祂 
as “It,” despite the discourtesy of calling someone (let alone a god) “It.” Still, this 
pronoun choice presents a lexical analogue of the typographic distinction 
“He/he.” In a larger frame, it does raise the question of why Tolkien did not use 
Eric Reinders 
58  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
“He,” since Ilúvatar is obviously a fictional analogue of the Christian God —
perhaps it was intended to create a little distance between Ilúvatar and the 
Christian God. In Deng’s translations there is no such issue —she used the 
pronoun for a God.  
The Thai version similarly resists the absent capitalization of Ilúvatar’s 
pronoun though a lexical choice of phra-ong. Back-translating this particular phra-
ong, I would either use a literalistic “the holy one,” or given the context, “He.”  
Capitalization of deities is not a trivial matter, as shown by a 
remarkable example from a Russian translation. Nataliya Rakhmanova 
systematically turned phrases in which a deity is implied, such as “Bless me,” 
into explicit terms: “God bless me.” (Ferré et al note a similar pattern in French 
translations; 55). But as Mark Hooker writes, “At the time that she did her 
translation, God was a sensitive word to Soviet editors and censors. It was never 
spelled with a capital “G”, unless it began a sentence” (71) . Some translators 
even contrived to put the word always at the start of the sentence so that it 
would be capitalized, and censors contrived to move the word later in the 
sentence so it would not be capitalized (71).  
 
BUDDHIST CONNOTATIONS OF THE VOID  
How do the translations speak of what is not Ilúvatar—not created by 
Ilúvatar, namely: the Void? The melodies filled up the space of Ilúvatar, “and 
the music and the echo of the music went out into the Void, and it was not void” 
(Silm. 4). Li has: “The music and its echo flowed out into the void [空虚 kongxu], 
and thereby the world was not again void [ kongxu]” (Li Silm. 4). When the 
original says, “the Void, and it was not void” ( Silm. 4), we have the term as 
nominal and adjectival, with and without the definite article, with and without 
capitalization. Since Chinese does not have capitalization, there i s no parallel 
typographic way to distinguish between Void and void. For both, Li uses 
kongxu, which is also both nominal and adjectival. Deng uses kongxu and kong, 
but frames them more explicitly. She renders it as: “this movement [in the 
musical sense: 樂章 yuezhang, music -chapter] and its echo floated into ‘the 
domain of emptiness’ (kongxu zhi jing), and yet it was not empty (不是空的 bushi 
kong de)” (Deng Silm. 2002 4. 2015 32 is very similar). As a “domain,” the first 
use is clearly nominal and spatial, where as the construction bushi . . . de  clearly 
marks the second usage as an adjective. Deng creates a lexical solution to the 
puzzle of the unrepresentable typographic distinction.  
The Thai translation for Void involves the word สุญ soon (zero, empty). 
The music “went out to struggle with empty space ( สุญภูมิ soonpumi), which at 
this time was not vacant and empty ( ว่ำงสุญ wangsoon) again.” 
(Thanyapratertkul Silm. 40). The Thai translation differentiates them by using 
the nominal soonpumi (empty-place or zero -place) and a more abstract 
Divinity and the Void in Chinese and Thai Translations of the Ainulindalë 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  59 
compound wangsoon (empty-zero or empty-empty). Similarly, in translating “it 
seemed to him that Ilúvatar took no thought for the Void, and he was impatient 
of its emptiness” ( Silm. 4), the Thai gives “the Void” as soonpumi, though 
“emptiness” here is ควำมว่ำงเปล่ำ khwam wangplao, very literally “empty -ness” 
or “nothing-ness” (Thanyaprasertkul Silm. 41). There is an etymological lineage 
here: Thai soon is derived from Pali suñña, itself derived from Sanskrit śūnya, the 
abstract noun of which is suññatā in Pali (more widely known by the Sanskrit 
śūnyatā), and usually translated as the Buddhist term “emptiness.” Soonpumi 
also uses the Sanskrit -derived bhūmi (place). In Thailand, Buddhist 
philosophical terms tend to be derived from texts in the scriptural language Pali. 
In “the Void, and it was not void,” the endless emptiness is given a 
proper noun status, where small -v void means only empty. In Li’s translation, 
the Chinese term for both of these is kongxu. Deng uses kongxu for the noun, and 
kong for the adjective. Kong in particular has strong Buddhist associations, 
namely the Chinese translation of śūnyatā, “emptiness.” Some translators of 
Buddhist sutras or ideas into English have rendered śūnyatā as the Void, though 
this usage has fallen out of fashion. The use of Ch inese kong or Thai soon does 
not mean the story is necessarily read as Buddhist, however. Though kong has a 
Buddhist resonance, it also just means, empty. As in: empty bowl, empty room. 
Besides, the Buddhist idea of emptiness implies, whatever might be in the bowl, 
it and the bowl itself  are both empty —that is, have no independent essential 
existence—an entirely different point. Furthermore, the Buddhist rhetoric of 
“the emptiness of emptiness” asserts that in Buddhist terms emptiness is not a 
Being in itself. In Christianity there is an interesting parallel history of the 
theology of Nothingness (see Harrison).  
For Tolkien, is the Void a Being in itself? Tolkien gives us some clues 
about the surrounding absence. Darkness is sometimes the mere absence of 
light, but when capitalized it is a thing in itself. We see the same pattern with 
Light/light: “The Light failed; but the Darkness that followed was more than loss 
of light. In that hour was made a Darkness that seemed not lack but a thing with 
being of its own: for it was indeed made by malice out of Light” ( Silm. 81). The 
endless expanse of emptiness surrounding them is conceived as a kind of Being 
in itself or anti-Being. The translations lack the option of capital-D Darkness. The 
Li translation does nothing about the problem but relies on the context, which 
in this case makes the meanings of the words clear enough (Li Silm.76). But Deng 
is more perceptive of the nuance. For the simple “but the Darkness”, Deng shifts 
the grammar a little: “There was at that time created a kind of darkness” (Deng 
Silm. 2002 107). For Darkness as “ a thing with being of its own,” Den g has “it 
was a kind of concrete existence ( 實體存在 shiti cunzai)” (Deng Silm. 2002 103). 
And in the later version, the Darkness “possessed its own life ( 拥有自己的生命 
yongyou ziji de shengming)” (Deng Silm. 2015 107).  
Eric Reinders 
60  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
 
EMPTINESS AND HUNDUN 
In the original, Melkor “had gone often alone into the void places 
seeking the Imperishable Flame; for desire grew hot within him to bring into 
Being things of his own, and it seemed to him that Ilúvatar took no thought for 
the Void, and he was impatient of its emptiness” ( Silm. 4). We see void, Void, 
and emptiness. Deng treats both “the void places” and “the Void” as “‘the 
domain of emptiness’ ( 空虛之境 kongxu zhi jing )” and elaborates on emptiness 
as kong with the phrase 空無一物 kong wu yi wu, “empty without a single thing” 
(Deng Silm. 2002 4–5; 2015 32–33). But Li introduces a different Void: “He often 
went alone to the empty and spacious ( 空旷 kongkuang) places, looking for the 
‘unextinguishing flame.’ In his breast there was a hot desire, he thought to make 
life (生命 shengming) himself. In his view, Ilúvatar basically had not thought of 
the hundun unmanifested world (混沌未开的世界 hundun weikai de shijie). Facing 
an expanse of emptiness (kongxu), he was very impatient” (Li Silm. 4). Here we 
get three voids: 1. “the void places” is 空旷之地 kongkuang zhidi, empty and vast 
place, big open space; 2. “the Void” is  kongxu, meaning empty, emptiness; and 
3. “its emptiness” is  混沌 hundun, which is further clarified  by the phrase 
“unmanifested world” or “the world not yet begun.”  
Hundun is a term of Daoist origin, usually glossed as a primordial 
chaotic potentiality, a seething foment which allows no divisions. It is not empty. 
Whereas in a void there is nothing, in hundun there is no (particular, 
individualizable) thing. Everything is there, but distinctions cannot be made, so 
we are unable to make selfish subject -object distinctions, let alone distinctions 
such as chair-table-room-person.  
This notion of hundun, if not the term itself, appears for example in 
chapter 69 of the Daodejing (here using Lau’s translation of the Mawangdui 
version):  
 
There is a thing confusedly formed (混成 huncheng), 
Born before heaven and earth. 
Silent and void (寥 liao, silent, deserted, open) 
It stands alone and does not change. 
Goes round and does not weary. 
It is capable of being the mother of heaven and earth. 
As yet I do not know its name.  
I style it ‘the way’ (dao 道). (Lau) 
 
In this sense there is no thing in hundun. If an image of emptiness is a 
bowl with nothing in it, the image of hundun would be a bowl with a gloppy, 
Divinity and the Void in Chinese and Thai Translations of the Ainulindalë 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  61 
squishy soup in it—indeed, the term comes to us in its English pronunciation of 
its Cantonese pronunciation: wonton. Next time you have wonton soup, 
remember that you’re looking at a Daoist image of the original and ultimately 
true form of the universe. Ex cept that the bowl isn’t part of the metaphor, of 
course. Hundun does resemble śūnyatā, in that neither posits a simple absence 
(like an empty room), but hundun emphasizes the chaos (in which no single 
thing exists separately) where śūnyatā emphasizes abse nce (of any separate 
thing).   
Similarly, when Ilúvatar says, “And I will send forth into the Void the 
Flame Imperishable” ( Silm. 9), Li gives us hundun: “I want to send the 
unextinguishing flame into an expanse of hundun (yipian hundun zhizhong)” (Li 
Silm. 9). Consistent with other cases, Deng uses “domain of emptiness” (Deng 
Silm.2002 11; 2015 37 is almost the same). The Thai, of course, has no hundun: 
“we will send eternal fire into the soonpumi)” or empty place (Thanyaprasertkul 
Silm. 46).  
A synonym for hundun is dakuai 大塊/大快—the great clod, the big 
lump. An image of lumpy inertia. But also the imagery of eggs, the womb, and 
fetal gestation are frequently part of this concept. There is some overlap with the 
story of Pangu, first recorded in the 3 rd century CE but undoubtedly older. 
Initially there is only formless chaos, which eventually forms a cosmic egg. 
Inside the egg, yin and yang harmonize and create a humanoid figure, Pangu. 
He then creates the world in a division and separation of earth and sky (again, 
a binary polarity). Then Pangu dies and falls apart, becoming parts or aspects of 
the world such as the sun and moon, the wind, mountains and rivers, and 
animals—here death is associated with multiplicity and physical substance —
with our world.  
The Daoist philosopher Zhuangzi  莊子/庄子 (ca. 369–286 BCE) tells a 
story about the egg -like hundun: as a primordial unity it was whole and filled 
with life; the Emperors of the North and South (representing binary distinction) 
wanted to be helpful, and thought: we have seven holes in our bodies, let’s give 
hundun seven holes. They drilled a hole a day, and on the seventh day hundun 
died (Mair 71). In Daoism this story illustrates a basic distinction between 
qiantian and houtian, literally anterior heaven and p osterior heaven. We 
shouldn’t take “heaven” too literally here, but read it more as “world.” The 
world before the fall, and the world after. The world before is “ the hundun 
unmanifested world (hundun weikai de shijie),” in which all things are potential, 
whole, and integral, if as yet unrealized. We are living in the world after, in 
which we make the millions of distinctions whereby we cut the world up into 
tiny pieces, and obscure the fundamental integrity of the world. This is imagined 
as a lamentable fall from potential to actual, from infinite to finite, from 
deathless to mortal.   
Eric Reinders 
62  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
There are other examples of hundun as Void. “But when they were 
come into the Void, Ilúvatar said to them: ‘Behold your Music!’ […] and they 
saw a new World made visible before them, and it was globed amid the Void, 
and it was sustained therein, but was not of it” (Silm. 6). As George MacDonald 
said of God’s creation, “ He utters them into the visible” (4). Li’s re-telling has, 
“Coming to the place of hundun, Ilúvatar said to them: ‘Look at your music!’ […] 
Before the Ainur’s eyes appeared a new world, that was a giant globe which 
formed in the midst of hundun. It seemed to be continuously suspended there, 
constant and not decreasing” (Li Silm. 6). And Deng: “They came to ‘the domain 
of emptiness,’ Ilúvatar said to them: ‘Look! Your musical movement! (yuezhang)’ 
[…] Before their eyes appeared a new world (yuzhou, universe, space and time), 
in the emptiness ( 空無 kongwu) there was a globe ( 球體 qiuti, sphere -body) 
manifested, existing ( 存立 cunli, existing and established) and not falling in 
emptiness (kongxu), yet not belonging to ( 不屬于 bu shuyu) emptiness” (Deng, 
Silm. 2002 6; 2015 34 is similar). Whereas Li breezes past it, the more 
theologically aware translator, Deng, picks up on the notion of the world being 
“in” but not “of” the Void, very much analogous to John 17:13 –14, with its 
contrast of Christ and Christians being “in” but not “of” (shuyu) the world.  
 
VOID IN THE VALAQUENTA AND GENESIS 
Just as Genesis outlines the process of creation (Gen . 1:1–2:4) and then 
backs up and goes over part of the narrative in more detail (2:4 –2:14), when we 
move from the Ainulindalë to the Valaquenta we get another variation of the 
cosmogony. The opening lines from the Valaquenta more or less repeat the same 
initial starting point, the manifestation of the Ainur, their music, and the 
granting of vision. “Therefore Ilúvatar gave to their vision Being”( Silm. 15). The 
capital-B “Being” is a very abstract concept , even if it refers to things actually 
existing. It is paraphrased by Li as, “Ilúvatar let this view be born in the world” 
(Li Silm. 15), whereas Deng’s 2002 translation spells it out a bit more: “Soon after, 
Ilúvatar made the beautiful sight that they saw into concrete (具體 juti) material 
existence (物質存在 wuzhi cunzai)” (Deng Silm. 2002 17; 2015 43 is very similar). 
And we read, Ilúvatar “set it amid the Void” ( Silm. 15). Li: “also put it in an 
expanse of hundun (yipian hundun zhizhong)” (Li Silm. 15). Again we see the Void 
as hundun, whereas Deng has, “placed this existence in the emptiness (xukong)” 
(Deng Silm. 2002 17; 2015 43 very similar).  
Another reference to the Void suggests a rather different meaning: “But 
in after years he [Sauron] rose like a shadow of Morgoth and a ghost of his 
malice, and walked behind him on the same ruinous path down into the Void” 
(Silm. 24). Here, “into the Void” would seem to mean, into utter destruction and 
non-existence. But Li continues the usage: “Later, he simply became Morgoth’s 
shadow, became a ghost ( 幽灵 youling, dark spirit) of cruelty. Into the hundun 
Divinity and the Void in Chinese and Thai Translations of the Ainulindalë 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  63 
world with his master he followed like a shadow, following closely” (Li Silm. 
24). Li’s text implies he became an actual ghost, rather than a continued presence 
of Morgoth’s cruelty. Similarly, the original clearly means he was like a shadow 
of Morgoth—he was a lesser form, mimicking his master, and Li loses the simile. 
Usually, I find the metaphors of the original are turned into similes in the 
Chinese; here the reverse is true. Li also reads “the Void” using the term hundun. 
Deng’s translation is particularly vivid and colorful: “he rose suddenly like the 
monstrous shadow ( 魅影 meiying, mei = demon, evil spirit + ying, shadow) of 
Morgoth, like his evil -poisonous (惡毒 e-du) ghost ( 鬼魂 guihun) continuing to 
be tangled in the world, until in the end he stepped on Morgoth’s dust (houchen, 
dust raised by someone walking ahead), fell into emptiness ( kongxu)” (Deng 
Silm. 2002 30; see also 2015 52). She makes the shadow monstrous. And walking 
behind Morgoth now has a more vivid and dismal quality.  
Given the widespread presence of Christianity in China today, the 
translations of Void may not only suggest Daoism and Buddhism. Genesis 1:1, 
from the RSV: “In the beginning God created the heavens and the earth. The 
earth was without form and void.” A modern Catholic Mandarin Chinese 
translation has: “At the start of the original beginning, when the Lord of Heaven 
made heaven and earth, the great land was one expanse of hundun, without form 
and without pattern.” I have checked several Protestant translatio ns and they 
also use hundun. Reading the Ainulindalë, a reader may just as likely hear an echo 
of the Bible as a Daoist text.  
Of course, there is another cosmogony in the Bible, one that is sound -
based: the start of John’s Gospel: “In the beginning was the Word, and the Word 
was with God, and the Word was God. He was in the beginning with God; all 
things were made through him, and without him was not anything made that 
was made” (John 1:1–4). The same Chinese Catholic translation has: “At the start 
of the original beginning, there was ‘dao’, ‘dao’ and the Lord of Heaven existed 
together, ‘dao’ was the Lord of Heaven. In the beginning, ‘dao’ existed together 
with the Lord of Heaven. The myriad things ( wanwu) were made according to 
‘dao,’ without ‘dao’ there would be nothing at all.” Here we have Logos glossed 
as Dao, the key concept after which “Daoism” (or “Taoism”) is named. (This 
choice is further complicated by the use of dao as a verb, meaning “to speak,” a 
pun we find in the first line of the Daodejing.) We also have “all things” 
translated as 萬物 wanwu, myriad things, 10,000 things. This is another 
intertextual moment: In the Daodejing, chapter 42, the transition from the world-
before to the world-after is phrased as “The dao gave birth to one, one gave birth 
to two, two gave birth to three, and three gave birth to the ten thousand things 
(wanwu)” (author’s translation).  
 
 
Eric Reinders 
64  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
CONCLUSION 
The beginning of the world was an emergence from some 
undifferentiated and non -dual state into differentiated form, and this 
fragmenting process is associated with speaking or naming. This is a familiar 
pattern from Daoist philosophy, and from the Christia n Bible, and from 
Buddhist philosophy (sort -of). It also resonates with chaos theory, the 
emergence of cognition, and childbirth. Time is a descent: from good to bad, from 
spirit to form, from long life to short life.  
The Daoist, Buddhist, or Christian origins of certain terms, and the 
intertextual flow among these categories, do not of course require us to see those 
religions in every use of a word. Hundun is also used in the phrase hundun lilun, 
“chaos theory.” Dao can also simply mean a way (to get somewhere or do 
something). But even secularized terminology, when re-inserted into a scripture 
or a scripture -like fiction, is re -animated with religious meaning. An 
examination of the Buddhist or Daoist elements, whether in Chinese or Thai 
translations or in the original, is certainly not equivalent to claiming they were 
intended. Finding them in a translation assumes only that translators use what 
is available to them, and that no language is entirely free of its form of life. As 
Wittgenstein wrote, “to imagine a language means to imagine a form of life” (8).

and M.Phil are from Hull University, UK, and his Ph.D. is from the University of

JOIN T H E  M Y T H O P O E I C  S O C I E T Y TODAY AND ENJOY :  
 
 THOUGHT-PROVOKING AND RESPECTED PUBLICATIONS 
Our reviews quarterly Mythprint (free with membership) keeps readers up to date on 
newly published fantasy literature, films, and academic titles. Members also receive the 
Annual Report  and Member Directory . The peer -reviewed journal Mythlore features 
scholarly articles on mythopoeic literature. Our annual literary magazine, Mythic Circle, 
publishes new fiction, art, and poetry with mythopoeic themes. The Mythopoeic Press 
publishes both scholarly editions of literary works and critical essays. The Society also 
produces specialized works on Tolkien’s invented languages and other topics. Members 
receive advance notice of new publications and special discounted rates. 
 OUTSTANDING CONFERENCES 
Our annual conference (Mythcon) encourages scholars, authors, and readers to exchange 
ideas and share their insights and love of mythopoeic literature. Guests of Honor have 
included authors such as Neil Gaiman, Ursula K. Le Guin, and Tim Powers, as well as  
noted scholars such as Michael Drout, Verlyn Flieger, Douglas Anderson and Christopher 
Tolkien. As a small conference, we enjoy an intimacy with our guests that most 
conferences cannot provide. The Society has been instrumental in the development and 
encouragement of young scholars. There is a special award for best student paper at 
Mythcon, and funding to help “starving scholars” defray conference costs. Society 
members receive big discounts on registration and can share in other benefits. 
 LITERARY AND SCHOLARLY AWARDS 
Our Society’s book awards nominees are selected by our members. Society members can 
nominate books for the Mythopoeic Fantasy Awards for both adults and children or for 
the Mythopoeic Scholarship Awards in Inklings Studies and Myth and Fantasy Studies. 
Society members may also serve on the juries to review nominees and select winners. 
 COMMUNITIES OF READERS AND SCHOLARS 
 Society members share their love of and scholarly interest in fantasy literature through 
organized discussion groups in various regions across the United States. They can also 
join an email discussion group, connect through our Facebook group, follow us on Twitter, 
or use other social media options. From our “Members” page you can link to your own 
web site or blog.  
 BECOME A LIFETIME MEMBER 
In addition to the benefits of regular membership, Lifetime Members receive the electronic 
version of Mythlore for free.  
 KEEPING THE MYTHOPOEIC FLAME ALIVE 
The Mythopoeic Society (founded in 1967) is one of the oldest organizations extant for 
those interested in fantasy and its study and appreciation. Though we have made 
impressive contributions to Mythopoeic Studies, we have always been a small nonprofit, 
run totally by unpaid volunteers. Without memberships, we’ d cease to exist. We cover all the 
Inklings. We welcome scholars from a variety of disciplines, independent scholars, and 
anyone who reads and loves fantasy literature. As a member you can have influe nce on 
the direction of the Society by voting on its leadership —or become part of that 
leadership yourself.

                  HE MIDDLE -(U N)MAN OF DESIRE :  
                          A GIRARDIAN READING OF PERELANDRA  
 
                                              CLINTON MANLEY  
 
In the birth of desire, the third person is always present.  
—René Girard1 
 
HY DO WE DESIRE WHAT WE DO ? WHY DO WE STRIVE  for a certain 
education, aim at a certain career, read certain books, pursue certain 
lovers, or attend certain conferences? In short, why do we want what we want? 
French polymath René Girard answers that we desire what we do because we 
imitate the desires of others. According to Girard, we don’t actually know what 
to want so we look at our heroes and neighbors to see what they want and follow 
suit. Girard identifies this mimetic desire as the engine that drives human culture.2 
Girard famously argues that all great works of literature “reveal the 
imitative nature of desire” ( Deceit 14).3 The greater the work, the more 
mimetically perspicacious the author. Given Girard’s theory, we would expect 
the greatest fantasy authors to erect their sub -creative world with an intimate 
awareness of mimetic desire. And when we turn to C.S. Lewis, this is just what 
we find.  
Lewis repeatedly explores imitation and rivalry with hyper -mimetic 
characters like Edmund Pevensie, Orual, and the unstable Studdocks. But 
perhaps Lewis’s most insightful exploration of this middle place of desire comes 
in the middle book of his Ransom tri logy. Perelandra presents a supposal of an 
Eden-like temptation in an unfallen world, and Lewis envisions this event as 
inexorably mimetic. In this paper, I argue that the Un -man’s temptation 
intentionally leverages the power of mimetic desire. Therefore, a Girardian 
reading of Perelandra offers fruitful insight into Lewis’s take on Satanic 
deception.  
 
1 Girard, Deceit, Desire, and the Novel: Self and Other in Literary Structure [Deceit] 21. 
2 Burgis defines mimetic desire as “[d]esire generated and formed through the imitation of 
what someone else has already desired or is perceived to desire. Mimetic desire means that 
we choose objects due to the influence of a third party, a model or mediator of desire” (223). 
3 “[T]he great writers apprehend intuitively and concretely, through the medium of their 
art, if not formally, the system in which they were first imprisoned together with their 
contemporaries” (Girard, Deceit 3).  
W 
Clinton Manley 
68  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
Before exploring Perelandra directly, I will briefly summarize Girard’s 
mimetic theory and present Lewis’s exposition of that same desire in his essay 
“The Inner Ring.” 
 
LOVE BY ANOTHER’S EYES 
Love in one person is infectious in kindling it in another.  
—Augustine, Confessions IV. xiv (21) 
GIRARD’S MIMETIC THEORY 
Girard’s mimetic theory is vast and deep, but even a child can grasp 
the basic concept. If a toddler walks into a room with a hundred 
indistinguishable toy cars and another toddler is playing with one of those cars, 
which car will the new child desire? Of course, the car being played with. Why? 
Because desire is contagious . The original child 
indicates the value of the toy to the second. And 
if there are no checks in place, their shared 
desire will result in rivalry and toddler violence. 
Without outside int erference, the only way to 
avoid the conflict would be to introduce a third 
notably different toddler into the mix that the 
first two could vent their aggression on. Behold 
mimetic theory in miniature.  
All desire (apart from basic survival needs) is the result of imitating the 
desires of others. Girard explains, “We assume that desire is objective or 
subjective, but in reality, it rests on a third party who gives value to the objects” 
(Girard, I See Satan Fall Like Lightening  [Satan] 9). Our desires spring not from 
within ourselves or from the object's intrinsic value but from the presence of a 
third party —what Girard calls a mediator.4 Mediators are “go -betweens” or 
middlemen of desire, “acting as agents between the individual imitating them 
and the world” (Williams ix). For Girard, the shape of desire is triangular, 
existing in this middle place between a subject, a model, and an object. When the 
desired object can be shared, mimesis leads to friendship. Thus, Girard affirms, 
“Mimetic desire is intrinsically good” (Satan 15). However, when the object cannot 
be shared, rivalry and violence inevitably ensue. “The mimesis of desire is both the 
best of friendship and the worst of hatred” (Girard, A Theater of Envy [Envy] 10).5  
 
4 Mimetic theory does not deny objective values or the intrinsic worth of things, but how 
we come to see and desire those values relies on a mediator.  
5 “Friendship is this perpetual coincidence of two desires. But envy and jealously are 
exactly the same thing” (Envy 10).

Consider one more example. 
When you see a commercial for an ice -cold 
Coke, what do you immediately long for? 
Coke, of course. All advertising is 
intrinsically mimetic, indicating what you 
ought to want. But more importantly, how is 
that value indicated? Typically, an 
obviously attractive person models the 
drink. Girard exposes the tactic: “[T]he most 
skillful advertising does not try to convince that a product is superior but that it 
is desired by Others” ( Deceit 104). Objects are much like sports jerseys. They 
have little value to us in themselves, but when we don the jersey with the star’s 
name, we become like the star.  
Mimetic theory posits that all desire is ultimately metaphysical or 
ontological. We long to be the mediator. The desired object is simply a means to 
that end.6 This longing-to-be is exactly what Lewis describes in his seminal essay 
on desire.  
 
LEWIS’S INNER RING 
What has been observed of Shakespeare is undoubtedly true of Lewis, 
he “explains everything he dramatizes and dramatizes everything he explains” 
(Envy 146). The desire he dramatizes elsewhere (especially in That Hideous 
Strength), he explains in “The Inner Ring.” He states, “I believe that in all men’s 
lives […] one of the most dominant elements is the desire to be inside the local 
Ring and the terror of being left outside” (146). For Lewis, human beings have a 
ubiquitous desire to get in. The desire may be 
sparked by a shared object of interest—a hobby 
or work of art —but the real aim is the sense of 
being that is imagined to come with entry into 
the Ring. This “longing to be inside” is nothing 
other than Girard’s metaphysical desire. To be in 
is to be like. Those in the desirable circle mediate 
the desire for the status of being in the circle.  
Just like mimetic desire, Lewis sees 
the Inner Ring as inevitable —in itself neither good nor bad. “This desire is one 
of the great permanent mainsprings of human action” (151). But it is dangerous 
and easily manipulated. The lust to get in energizes competition, rivalry, and 
advertisement, often leading to moral compromises. “Of all passions the passion 
 
6 “However desirable the object may be, it pales in comparison with the model who gives 
it value” (Envy 43). 
 
Subject 
Mediator 
Object 
Outsider 
Inner Ring 
Inner 
Ring 
Clinton Manley 
70  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
for the Inner Ring is most skilful in making a man who is not yet a very bad man 
do very bad things” (154). Or we might say the passion for the Inner Ring can 
make a Lady who is not yet bad at all do very tragic things.  
Like Girard, Lewis sees this imitative desire ending in disappointment 
and eventually disaster unless recognized and renounced. “The quest of the 
Inner Ring will break your heart unless you break it” (156). In a sense, 
Perelandra’s central drama embodies this one statement.  
 
THE MIDDLE-(UN)MAN OF DESIRE 
I will excite their minds with more desire.  
—Satan, Paradise Lost (4.522–23)7 
 
The temptation scene in Perelandra is a supposal of what might have 
happened in Eden. The Un -man, a Satan figure who stood before Eve in the 
garden (Lewis, Perelandra [P] 103) and remembers the cry from the cross (130), 
tempts the Green Lady, a new Eve, to disobey Maleldil’s (God’s) single 
command not to sleep on the Fixed Lands. However, Maleldil sends the man 
Ransom to thwart the Un-man’s assault.  
Lewis presents three conversations between the Un-man and the Lady. 
After the final one, he summarizes the Un -man’s tactics, “He was making her 
mind a theater in which that phantom self should hold the stage. He had already 
written the play” ( P 118). This strategy has three mimetic tactics that roughly 
correspond to the three cycles of conversation: 1) make her mind a theater, 2) 
write the play, and 3) put self on stage. We will examine each in turn. 
 
1. MAKE HER MIND A THEATER 
If every Pandarus is a kind of playwright, all playwrights are panders in disguise. 
—René Girard8 
 
If we were to imagine an average tempter, say the demonic apprentice 
in The Screwtape Letters , attempting to cause the Lady to fall, the brunt of the 
assault would likely focus on the Fixed Lands themselves. The tempter would 
wax eloquent about the Lands’ desirability and the injustice of God to deny 
them. However, the Un-man takes a far more subtle route.  
 
7 It is beyond the scope of this essay, but the parallels between Milton’s Satan and Lewis’s 
Un-man are legion. The tactics both use to temp the Eve-character are essentially the same. 
Both leverage the power of mimetic desire through the faculty of imagination. Of course, 
we would expect these parallels since Lewis was writing A Preface to Paradise Lost around 
the same time as Perelandra. But the mimetic nature of Milton’s Satan confirms we are on 
the right track in seeing the same dynamic in Lewis.  
8 Envy 158. 
The Middle-(Un)man of Desire: A Girardian Reading of Perelandra 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  71 
A PANDER OF DESIRE 
The Un -man acts as a pander of desire . As Girard observes of 
Shakespeare’s Pandarus, the Un -man acts as “the midwife and engineer of 
desire, its Alexander and Napoleon. […] Like 
the God of Descartes, his initial push sets 
entire worlds in motion” ( Envy 123). Or at 
least, so he hopes. He channels the zeitgeist 
of advertising and targets the Lady as his sole 
demographic. Like the advertiser, the pander 
role allows the Un-man to foster desire in the 
Lady without becoming her rival. He can 
orchestrate the f all of Perelandra from 
outside the mimetic triangle. So he acts as the 
great stage manager of desire. 
 
THEATER OF THE MIND 
From the beginning, the Un-man creates in the Lady a desire to be a certain 
kind of person. He erects a stage of metaphysical desire and lures the Lady on to 
it. The desire is to be “a full woman […] like the women of my world” ( P 90—
91). When the Lady asks what those women are like, the Un-man paints a grand 
picture: 
 
They are of a great spirit. They always reach out their hands for the new 
and unexpected good, and see that it is good long before the men 
understand it. Their minds run ahead of what Maleldil has told them. 
They do not need to wait for Him to tell them what is good, but know  it 
for themselves as He does. They are, as it were, little Maleldils . And 
because of their wisdom, their beauty is as much greater than yours as the 
sweetness of these gourds surpasses the taste of water. And because of 
their beauty the love which the men have for them is as much greater than 
the King’s love for you. (P 91, emphasis mine) 
 
Welcome to the Un-man’s theater. He fills the stage of her mind with what I will 
call the eminently desirable Women of Earth (WoE). These WoE have four 
noteworthy traits to spark the Lady’s mimetic envy.  
First, the WoE are great  and great because they are progressive. They 
always reach out for the new good. Later, in a classic case of propaganda, the Un-
man redefines the word Death to capture this progressiveness. Only through 
“Death in abundance” (i.e., constant progress) can “the real oldness and the

strong beauty and the uttermost branching out” come ( P 98).9 These traits 
characterize the WoE, and the Un-man makes Death-as-progress the pathway to 
become them.  
Second, the WoE are beautiful. However, for the Un-man, beauty is but a 
tool of envy. It only serves as an aphrodisiac of sorts. 10 Beauty is desirable 
because it inflames desire. The Un-man returns 
to this at the end of this first temptation. As he 
expands his portrait of the tragic heroine, he 
explains that by attaining rivalrous knowledge, 
a few women became “more beautiful and 
excelled their fellows a s you excel the beasts; 
and thousands were striving for their love” ( P 
103). Here the WoE are the victorious rivals of 
all lesser women and, thus, the objects of desire for all men. Beauty can make 
the Lady a new Helen, “the woman with the thousands of lovers” (P 103).  
Third, WoE are “old.”  Lewis weaves the idea of “becoming older” 
throughout this section, making it roughly synonymous with gaining wisdom 
or maturity. However, the Un -man equivocates with the term, flitting between 
wisdom, age, and loss of childlike innocence. 11 Girard captures the Un -man’s 
ambiguity well, “Hell begins when the heaven of childhood gives way to 
mimetic rivalry” ( Envy 77). The Un -man meditates a way of getting old that 
smells like sulfur. However, he makes this ill -gotten wisdom the source of the 
beauty and greatness of the WoE (P 97). He tells the Lady, “When you are fully 
grown you will be even wiser and more beautiful than the women of my own 
world” ( P 99). The Un -man makes growing old the ticket to becoming a 
victorious rival over the WoE.  
Finally, WoE are “little Maleldils.”  The Un -man presents them as 
goddesses and defines divinity as independence. They are goddesses because 
they need no mediator to show them what is good. Neither men nor Maleldil 
model desire for them. Girard calls this the Romantic self that denies the imitative 
nature of desire and instead embraces the “lie of spontaneous desire” (Deceit 16). 
The Un-man reinforces this independence later by asserting “your own reason” 
acts as the final arbiter of what is good ( P 100). Re ason stands above the 
commands of Maleldil, allowing the Lady to be “really separate from Him” ( P

101). Again, this is just the Romantic lie12 in new clothes, but behind the king of 
all temptations stands the ancient, naked claim, “You shall be like gods” (Gen. 3:4).  
With his deft stage management, the Un-man parades the WoE as an Inner 
Ring the Lady must get into. He creates in her a desire to be “a full woman” like 
them—great, wise, beautiful, and 
divine. He fathers envy.13  
Mimetic desire to be like the 
WoE forms the cornerstone of the Un-

strange absence of the Fixed Lands in 
the Un-man’s initial assault. The Lady 
hints that he has spoken of the Fixed 
Lands often (P 89), but Lewis presents 
none of it. The Un -man hardly 
mentions the “object” he is trying to 
sell. Why? Because he knows if he can make the Lady desire the WoE, living on 
the Fixed Lands will serve as a means to that end. Again, Girard exposes the 
illusion of the desired object, “The object is only a means of reaching the 
mediator. The desire is a imed at the mediator’s being” (Deceit 53). The Un-man 
must make her mind a theater of desire before he incites her to action. 
 
2. WRITE THE PLAY 
Assaying, by his devilish art, to reach the organs of her fancy, and with them forge 
illusions, discontented thoughts, vain hopes, vain aims, inordinate desires.  
— Paradise Lost (4.800–09) 
  
In the second stage of the Un-man’s temptation, he writes the play the 
Lady must perform to become one of the WoE. To that end, the Un-man wields 
perhaps the most powerful means of awakening desire —storytelling. Stories 
appeal primarily to the imagination, not reason, and grant access to new worlds 
with new models. But the imaginary nature of literary models does not limit 
their influence over us. The mediator may be imaginary, but the mediation is 
not (Girard, Deceit 4, 23). 
The Un-man himself recognizes this power of story. When the Lady 
asks what purpose his flights of fancy serve, he responds, “The world is made 
up not only of what is but of what might be” (P 89). The Un-man knows denizens 
 
12 Burgis defines the Romanic lie as “the idea that our choices are completely autonomous, 
independent, and self -directed. Someone under the power of the Romantic Lie never 
thinks of their behavior as mimetic” (Burgis 224). 
13 “Envy subordinates a desired something to the someone who enjoys a privileged 
relationship with it,” says Girard. “Envy involuntarily testifies to a lack of being” (Envy 4).

of imagination shape the real world, especially by influencing what we want. In 
true mythopoeic fashion, the Un-man weaves new worlds to capture the Lady’s 
fancy.14 
THE MIMETICS OF MYTHOPOEIA 
Lewis leaves us in no doubt as to what 
world the Un-man constructs. He tells the 
Lady “with extreme beauty and pathos, a 
number of stories” ( P 107). The theme 
running through these stories is the tragic 
heroine: “Each one of these women had 
stood forth alone and braved a terrible risk 
for her child, her lover, or her people. Each 
had been misunderstood, reviled and 
persecuted: but each also magnificently 
vindicated by the event” ( P 108). The Un-
man cares not if the Lady understands all 
of these tales. He aims to shape her 
imagination, not enlighten reason: 
 
What emerged from the stories was rather an image than an idea—the picture 
of the tall, slender form, unbowed though the world’s weight rested upon its 
shoulders, stepping forth fearless and friendless into the dark to do for others 
what those others forbade it to do yet needed to have done. (P 108) 
 
Jadis the White Witch, Lady Macbeth, Clytemnestra, Anna Karenina —the Un-
man conquers the Platonic form of the tragic heroine, and it is a potent 
enchantment. Even Ransom admits the spell “was very well done” ( P 108). By 
imitating these “goddess shapes,” the Lady herself can become a goddess. With 
their storyline, the Un-man sets the Lady up to be a tragic Don Quixote.15  
 
14 Girard argues extensively that myths often conceal the source of our desires, the 
inevitable violence that emerges from those rivalries, and the innocence of the scapegoat. 
Curtis Gruenler has shown that Lewis recognized this same function of myth in Till We 
Have Faces (“C.S. Lewis and René Girard on Desire, Conversion, and Myth: The Case of 
Till We Have Faces”). However, Lewis also had a high view of myth and myth -making as 
a pathway to find truth and know God. There is work to be done here reconciling these 
three so I plan to make this interaction between the Un-man’s storytelling, Girardian myth, 
and Lewisian mythopoeia the topic of a future paper.  
15 Girard reads Don Quixote as a paradigmatic example of mimetic desire meditated 
through story ( Deceit 1–4). The chivalric tales, especially about Amadís, awakened 
Quixote's desire for the being of a chivalric knight. “Amadís was the polestar, the morning 
star, the sun to valiant, enamored knights, the one who should be imitated by all of us who 
serve under the banner of love and chivalry” ( Cervantes 229). That desire—the apple of
