MIMETIC DOUBLES 
The Un-man’s success follows quick on the heels of his storytelling. In 
Girard’s system, as mimetic rivalry increases, the subject and the mediator 
become more and more alike. He calls this mimetic doubling.16 As the subject's 
desire for the mediator increases, she will increasingly imitate them and 
differences will disappear. Lewis shows us this exact phenomenon.  
Immediately after the Un-man pens his play in the Lady’s imagination, 
she begins to look like “a tragedy queen” ( P 108). The transformation appalls 
Ransom. Until now, the Lady has been heavenly, but now she looks “so like a 
woman of our own race.” She has begun to take on the “rôle” of the WoE, 
wearing the borrowed robes of “invited grandeur” and “enjoyed pathos” ( P 
109). Through the power of suggested desire, the Un -man writes the script of 
the Lady’s fall, and she starts practicing the part.  
 
DESIRE FUELED BY DENIAL 
However, in the second phase of the temptation, the Un-man does not 
limit his tactics to the imagination. He repeatedly returns to the command —
Maleldil’s prohibition against living on the Fixed Lands. At one level, an 
explanation of this fixation springs r eady to hand. The Un -man aims at 
disobedience; therefore, he must undermine the legitimacy of the call to obey. 
This tactic is as old as Eden. But mimetically, the Un -man does something far 
more powerful. He knows the desirability of a good withheld. As any parent can tell 
you, “Don’t do that!” makes “that” all the more attractive.  
Girard explains, “The disadvantage of the prohibitions […] is that they 
don’t finally play their role in a satisfying manner. Their primarily negative 
character […] inevitably provokes in us the mimetic urge to transgress them” 
(Satan 14). A lawgiver sets himself up as an obstacle to the prohibited good—in 
Girardian terms, a rival —and “[o]pposition exasperates desire” (10). The 
Apostle Paul makes the same observation: “Sin […] through the commandment, 
produced in me all kinds of desire” (Rom. 7:8).17  
 
his eye—grew rotten and spoiled his imagination. He ended up tilting with windmills. 
The Un-man aims to make the Lady equally quixotic as she imitates the tragic trajectory 
of the heroines he portrays.  
16 “‘Mimetic doubles’ refers to the situation in which rivals become so obsessed with each 
other that they mirror each other's emotions and actions. The doubles act alike but they 
mistakenly see a great difference between them. Mimetic doubles can be quite dangerous 
to one another and to others and quite self-destructive” (Satan 22n2). 
17 Paul goes on to point out that the commandment itself is not at fault. “The law is holy, 
and the commandment is holy righteous and good” (Rom. 7:12). Lewis seems to maintain 
this same view of Maleldil’s prohibition. The prohibition in Eden against the tree of the 
knowledge of good and evil and that against sleeping on the Fixed lands, both were indeed 
prohibitions against goods, desirable in their time and place. And so, it seems like the 
Clinton Manley 
76  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
The Un-man has already stoked desire in the Lady to become like the 
WoE, that is, “little Maleldils” (P 91). Now he can return to the object as a means 
to that end.  
 
THE FINAL TOUCH 
At the end of this cultivation of the imagination, the 
Lady is almost ready for her debut. However, the 
Lady’s reaction to the Un -man’s suggested desire is 
still motivated by love for others—“love for the King 
and for her unborn children, and even, in a sense, of 
Maleldil Himself” (P 113). But the seed of selfishness 
is there, ready to produce toxic fruit: 
 
[F]rom the moment when the Un -man began its tragic stories, there was the 
faintest touch of theatricality, the first hint of a self-admiring inclination to seize 
a grand rôle in the drama of her world. It was clear that the Un -man’s whole 
effort was to increase this element. (P 113) 
 
The final step remaining for the Un-man is introducing the “unselfconscious” Lady 
to herself (P 109). The stage is set; the play is written. Now for the star of the show.  
 
3. PUT SELF ON STAGE 
Art thou afeard to be the same in thine own act and valour  
as thou art in desire […] letting ‘I dare not’ wait upon ‘I would’?  
—Lady Macbeth, Macbeth 1.7.39–44 
  
When Augustine pondered man’s original fall, he coined the term 
incurvatus in se  to capture the picture of man turned in on himself. Instead of 
being rightly ordered toward God and creatures, fallen man looks only to self. 
The Un-man tries to produce that scoliosis of the soul in the Lady in this final 
section of the temptation. As of  yet, she remains innocent. But though she had 
not fallen, “half her imagination was already filled with bright, poisonous 
shapes” (P 114). If the Un -man can inflate her self -esteem, her dreaming will 
become doing.  
 
 
 
 
“disadvantage” Girard identifies may not be the whole story. The increased desire brings 
the test to a point. Will one trust God to give the desirable good in his time and his way 
and, thus, be friends with God? Or allow the prohibition to create rivalry, as God is seen 
withholding the good (as the Un-man and the Serpent try to frame the matter)? 
The  
Lady 
Maleldil’s 
Command 
Fixed 
Land 
The Middle-(Un)man of Desire: A Girardian Reading of Perelandra 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  77 
PLAYING DRESS-UP 
To produce that effect, the Un-man introduces the Lady to clothing. In 
a twisted parody of God clothing our first parents, the Un -man kills birds and 
weaves their feathers into a dazzling robe. The Lady wears the cloak “to be 
beautiful,” harkening back to the original rivalrous desire the Un-man fostered 
in her (P 115). At first, Ransom thinks the Un-man’s only aim is to create vanity, 
but he soon realizes the more insidious target.  
The robe is part of the Lady’s assumption of the role of tragic heroine 
mediated by the WoE. Again, Ransom observes the doubling effect: “She 
appeared to him at that moment as a woman whom an earth -born man might 
conceivably love” (P 116). By dressing her up, the Un-man instigates a full-dress 
rehearsal. The Lady becomes an actor in costume. Aristotle observed that we 
love the theater because we love imitation —mimesis. He doesn’t explain why, 
but mimetic theory does. Theater allows one to try on the being o f the other 
(Envy 59). I can put on Hamlet like a coat. I can wear Macbeth and don Brutus.  
Thus, Ransom laments the Lady’s transformation into an actor because 
it constitutes one more step down the mimetic path. When the Lady takes on the 
appearance of others, she participates in the opposite of what Lewis calls good 
pretending. For Lewis, pretending is a way of becoming. Who we pretend to be 
today determines who we become tomorrow. Desires today reveal being 
tomorrow. Thus, in assuming a role, the Lady is becoming her model. 
 
SELF, MEET SELF 
However, the Un-man uses the clothing for an even greater effect. The 
clothing provides an excuse to give the Lady a mirror and introduce her to 
herself. After the Lady laments, “We cannot see ourselves” (P 116), the Un-man 
produces a mirror from his pack and hands it to her. The effect is electric. 
“‘That thing’ (she pointed at the mirror) ‘is me and not me’” (P 117). Of 
course, that is the point. She speaks better than she knows. On one level, she sees 
herself dressed up like the WoE. The process of mimetic doubling is making her 
not-her. But at a deeper level, the physical reflection in the mirror is her but not 
all of her. “The image of her beautiful body had been offered to her only as a 
means to awaken the far more perilous image of her great soul” ( P 118). The 
physical reflection merely begins t o coil her inward, but the curve ends with 
“the dramatic conception of the self” ( P 118)—beautiful, independent, tragic, 
imperious, and desirable.  
Clinton Manley 
78  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
This romantic view of the self is critical to the Un-man’s plan. Mimetic 
desire most affects us when we least suspect it, and we least suspect it when we 
imagine ourselves most independent. The more romantic, the more mimetic. If 
I think my desires originate in me (subjective) or the object (objective), I will not 
suspect a mediator dictating them. Thus, one philosopher warns, “There are 
always models of desire. If you don’t know yours, they are probably wreaking 
havoc in your life” because “models are most  
powerful when they are hidden” (Burgis 21). By 
turning her in on herself, the Un -man blinds the 
Lady to the mimetic engine that drives the 
temptation.  
However, although the mirror elevates 
the Lady’s self -esteem and self -consciousness, it 
could not succeed without the Un -man’s praise. In 
this section, he calls her beautiful three times, a 
tactic he doesn’t use in the first two stages. Why? The flattery —the expressed 
desire couched as praise—indicates her worth and makes the mirror effective.18 
The Lady absorbs the repetitive praise of the Un -man and, as a result, loves 
herself all the more.  
 
PRIDE BOUND TO ANOTHER 
Ironically, her self -love is wholly dependent on another. As Girard 
repeatedly asserts, pride depends on the other to uphold it. “The more ‘proud’ 
and ‘egotistic’ we are, the more enslaved we become to our mimetic models” 
(Satan 15). 
Here we see how utterly self -defeating the Un -man’s tale of 
independence proves to be. In trying to create separation from Maleldil, the 
Lady must resort to other mediators. The choice is not independence or not; the 
choice is who to be dependent on. “Metaphysical autonomy” is a myth the Un-
man weaves (Girard, Deceit 56). “[Self] renounces the divine mediator only to 
fall back on the human mediator,” explains Girard. “True freedom lies in the 
basic choice between a human or divine model” (58).19 
 
18 Shakespeare captures the effect in As You Like It. Rosalind explains how Silvius’s praise 
of Phebe enflames her value of herself: “‘Tis not her glass [mirror], but you that flatter her, 
/ And out of your eyes, she sees herself more proper / Than any of her lineaments can 
show her” (3.5.54–56). 
19 The full quote is worth noting: “[Self] renounces the divine mediator only to fall back on 
the human mediator. […] Christianity directs existence toward a vanishing point, either 
toward God or toward the Other. Choice always involves choosing a model, and  true 
freedom lies in the basic choice between a human or divine model. The impulse of the soul 
toward God is inseparable from a retreat into the Self. Inversely the turning in on itself of 
The 
Lady 
Un-man’s  
Praise 
The 
Lady 
The Middle-(Un)man of Desire: A Girardian Reading of Perelandra 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  79 
If the Lady remained rightly oriented to Maleldil and his desire 
expressed in command, she would become more and more herself. But as the 
Un-man twists her toward self, she becomes more like others —namely, the 
WoE.20 Lewis highlights this same reality repeatedly throughout his corpus. 
Through obedience, we become more fully the individual God intended us to 
be. For both Girard and Lewis, the attempt to dethrone God can only succeed in 
erecting men as gods.21  
So the Un-man crafts the theater of desire, writes the drama of the WoE, 
and casts the Lady in her role. Nothing now remains but to watch the Lady’s 
tragic fall—nothing, that is, except the eucatastrophic triumph of Ransom.  
 
THE TRIUMPH OF RANSOM 
God in his wisdom had foreseen since the beginning that the  
victim mechanism would be reversed like a glove, exposed,  
placed in the open, stripped naked, and dismantled in the Gospel.  
—René Girard22 
 
For Girard, rivalrous desire always ends in one of two ways. Either the 
subject totally renounces the desire by finding a new model (i.e., the toddler 
leaves the room) or mimetic conflict escalates, eventually resulting in violence 
(i.e., a toddler war of all -against-all). That violence is often targeted at a 
scapegoat (i.e., the different third toddler), 23 who is arbitrarily assigned blame 
for the conflict and abused, exiled, or even murdered. 24 Violence against the 
 
pride is inseparable form a movement of panic toward the Other. […] The imitation of 
Christ becomes the imitation of one’s neighbor” (Deceit 58–9). Elsewhere Girard expands 
the point: “What Jesus invites us to imitate is his own desire, the spirit that directs him 
toward the goal on which his intention is fixed: to resemble God the Father as much as 
possible. […] In inviting us to imitate him, he invites us to imitate his imitation. […] [O]nce 
we imitate Jesus, we discover that our aspi ration to autonomy has always made us bow 
down before individuals” (Satan 13–14). 
20 “[The] atomization of the personality is the final stage of internal mediation” (Deceit 92). 
21 “The romantic is always falling on his knees before the wrong altar; he thinks he is 
sacrificing the world on the altar of his Self whereas the real object of his worship is the 
Other” (Deceit 87).  
22 Satan 151. 
23 In mimetic theory, a scapegoat is “[a] person, group, or thing that a community chooses 
to expel or eliminate in the midst of a mimetic crisis in order to bring about a resolution. 
The scapegoat absorbs all of the mimetic tension and violence, which had previously been 
undirected and chaotic, onto itself. The scapegoat is often chosen randomly, through a 
mimetically driven process of judgment” (Burgis 224). 
24 Girard points to Joseph as a prime example of this mimetic cycle, but instead of 
concealing the innocence of the scapegoat as myths do, the Bible exposes the collective 
violence of Joseph’s brothers against an innocent victim (Oedipus Unbound 105–13). 
Clinton Manley 
80  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
scapegoat is cathartic but does nothing to remove the root of rivalry. For Girard, 
all human culture grows from this scapegoat mechanism  and “the founding 
murder.”25 
However, Girard argues that Christ, as the innocent and voluntary 
scapegoat, exposes the mechanism. Christ really does bear the sins of the world 
and so secures real peace. He is the divine model of rightly ordered desires. 
Therefore, for both Girard and L ewis, the cross is the corner of cosmic history, 
the turning point after which there is no return ( P 54). The triumph of the cross 
unveils the deceptive catharsis of the scapegoat. We can no longer take refuge 
in the faux happy ending it offers.26 
Therefore, it is fitting —and utterly Girardian —that the mimetic 
escalation in Perelandra ends in violence that imitates the cross—imitates but does 
not duplicate. Ransom realizes the Un-man’s mimetic tactics are bound to succeed 
eventually. At the end of each temptation cycle, he laments, “This can’t go on” (P 
109, 114, 119). Finally, he concludes he must sacrifice himself to destroy the Un-
man’s power over the Lady. Maleldil confirms Ransom’s fears, “It is not for 
nothing that you are named Ransom. […] My name also is Ransom” (P 125–26). 
To use Girard’s categories, Ransom looks to Christ as a non-rivalrous model.  
But Ransom would not endure a second cross for “Maleldil never 
repeated Himself” ( P 123). Instead, Ransom gives himself to be a preemptive 
scapegoat of mimetic violence. No sin needs redemption on Perelandra, but there 
is mimetic tension to deescalate. Both symbolically and physically, Ransom 
bears the teeth and claws of mimetic violence unleashed by the Un -man. He, in 
a sense, preemptively rescues an unfallen world with a new kind of redemption. 
Ransom is a scapegoat that prevents sin instead of atoning for sin.  
It is hard to imagine anything more Girardian or Lewisian or Christian 
than this ending. Ransom averts the mimetic crisis instigated by the Satanic Un-
man by imitating the sacrifice of Christ. The mimetic reading of Perelandra 
 
25 “Sacrifice lies behind everything distinguishing humans from animals, behind  
everything enabling us to substitute properly human desire for animal instinct, namely, 
mimetic desire. If becoming human involves, among other things, acquiring mimetic 
desire, it is obvious that humans could not exist in the beginning without sacrificia l 
institutions that repress and moderate the kind of conflict that is inevitable with the 
working of mimetic desire” (Satan 90). 
26 Many of Shakespeare’s plays employ the scapegoat mechanism to offer the semblance 
of a happy ending to unperceptive readers. Uncovering Shakespeare’s brilliant use of the 
scapegoat is one of Girard’s major themes in A Theater of Envy. Elsewhere, he explains how 
the Gospels exposed the scapegoat mechanism: “The Passion accounts reveal a 
phenomenon that unbeknownst to us generates all human cultures and still warps our 
human vision in favor of all sorts of exclusions and scapegoating. I f this analysis is true, 
the explanatory power of Jesus’ death is much greater than we realize, and Paul's exalted 
idea of the Cross as the source of all knowledge is anthropologically sound” (Satan 3). 
The Middle-(Un)man of Desire: A Girardian Reading of Perelandra 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  81 
indeed opens up “a new dimension of glory” (P 184) and new insights into both 
the power and impotence of Satan's deception. To quote Tor of Perelandra, “We 
have learned of evil, though not as the Evil One wished us to learn. We have 
learned better than that” (P 179).

                            HE LIBERTY TO BIND ONESELF :  
                                 CHESTERTON AND THE OATH  
                                               OF FËANOR  
 
                                           MATTHEW MCKENNA  
 
INTRODUCTION 
T IS WELL -KNOWN THAT G.K. CHESTERTON WAS INFLUENTIAL ON TOLKIEN.1 This 
paper will read the oath of Fëanor in The Silmarillion  through the lens of 
Chesterton’s philosophy of oaths, and it will suggest that Tolkien was 
speculatively influenced by Chesterton on this topic. It will do this by an 
exposition of Chesterton’s philosophy of vows followed by an in -depth 
exposition of the oath of Fëanor throug hout The Silmarillion. The thesis is that 
the oath of Fëanor works according to the mode of Chesterton’s philosophy of 
oaths, that is, the oath compels the oath-taker to act. Viewing the oath of Fëanor 
through the framework of Chestert on’s philosophy of oath s helps make it 
explicit to the reader how the oath drives the sons of Fëanor to commit many 
evil acts.  
Additionally, this paper will respond to a claim regarding the oath of 
Fëanor made by Alexander Bruce. This will show a practical benefit of reading 
the oath of Fëanor through the lens of Chesterton’s philosophy of oaths by 
highlighting the diminished freedom of the sons of Fëanor.  
 
CHESTERTON ON THE VOW 
 Chesterton admires vows and sees them as eminently good things.2 He 
values the capacity to make a vow and considers it the very height of freedom. 
 
1 Milbank’s thesis is that “the essayist, poet, Christian apologist and writer of fantastic tales 
G.K. Chesterton was an important influence both on Tolkien’s fiction and his literary 
criticism of the fairy tale” (Milbank viii). See also Pearce 161 –81; Pear ce argues that 
Chesterton was an indirect influence on Tolkien (164).  
2 Some people think that Catholicism (the religion of both Chesterton and Tolkien) should 
forbid oaths (Meier 53). Their argument is simple, during the Sermon on the Mount Jesus 
teaches: “Again you have heard that it was said to the men of old, ‘You shall n ot swear 
falsely, but shall perform to the Lord what you have sworn.’ But I say to you, do not swear 
at all, either by heaven, for it is the throne of God, or by the earth, for it is his footstool, or 
by Jerusalem, for it is the city of the great King. And do not swear by your head, for you 
cannot make one hair white or black.  Let what you say be simply ‘Yes’ or ‘No;’ anything 
more than this comes from evil” (Matt. 5:33 –37, RSVCE). A simple reading of the text 
implies that taking oaths at all is wrong. Yet, Augustine and Aquinas hold that this cannot 
be taken literally since Paul takes oaths within his epistles several times ( Augustine

He writes, “I could never conceive or tolerate any Utopia which did not leave to 
me the liberty for which I chiefly care, the liberty to bind myself. […] If I vow to 
be faithful I must be cursed when I am unfaithful […]. I should ask to be kept to 
my bargain, to have my oaths and engagements taken seriously; I should ask 
Utopia to avenge my honour on myself” ( Orthodoxy 130). He immediately 
applies this idea to marriage as an example of such a vow, wherein people freely 
choose to bind themselves together for the rest of their lives.  
 The gravity of vows, even rash vows, appears in Chesterton’s novel The 
Man Who Was Thursday. In it, the main character Syme makes a vow to Gregory 
(a street-preacher of anarchy whom Syme does not believe is a real anarchist) 
not to reveal what Gregory will tell him (18 –19). Gregory then reveals and 
proves that he is a real anarchist with deadly plans. Syme, in turn, makes 
Gregory swear that he will not reveal to anyone what he is about to tell him (28). 
Syme then reveals to the anarchist Gregory that he is  a police detective. Both 
characters feel bound by their oath and keep their oaths throughout the story—
Syme even lies to prevent breaking his vow (81 –82).3 At one point when Syme 
is strongly tempted to break his vow he says, “Theoretically I ought to drown 
myself first. I promised a poor fellow, who was a real modern pessimist [i.e., 
Gregory], on my word of honour not to tell the police. I’m no hand at casuistry, 
but I can’t break my word to a modern pessimist. It’s like breaking one’s word 
to a child” (105). One of Syme’s companions who is in the same situation 
comments that, “I tried to tell the police and I couldn’t, because of some silly 
oath I took” (105). Even to save lives from the anarchists and their deadly plans, 
Chesterton indicates that it would be wrong to break one’s vow, even an 
admittedly rash now.  
 So, a vow or oath for Chesterton combines “the fixity that goes with 
finality with the self -respect that only goes with freedom” ( The Superstition of 
Divorce 267). It is the use of one’s greatest freedom of the will to remove one’s 
freedom in the future. Elsewhere he writes, “The man who makes a vow makes 
an appointment with himself at some distant time or place. The danger of it is 
that [he] himself should not  keep the appointment” (“A Defense of Rash 
Vows”).

Cor. 1:9, 2 Cor. 1:23, Rom. 1:9) . Thus, due to the principle of the unity and content of 
scripture from Dei Verbum (the interpretation of any passage must take into account and 
be compatible with the rest of scripture —scripture passages enlighten each other), 
Catholics can take oaths (Dei Verbum, #12).

The Liberty to Bind Oneself: Chesterton and the Oath of Fëanor 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  85 
THE OATH OF FËANOR 
 I propose that in the oath of Fëanor we can see Chesterton’s influence 
on Tolkien. Chesterton valued freedom and the capacity to take oaths which 
bind us and limit our actions in the future. This appeared in Orthodoxy and The 
Man Who Was Thursday (key and well-known Chestertonian texts), which were 
written around the same time. The same idea will be shown to be at work in the 
oath of Fëanor for the oath-takers are bound by their oath and forced to act upon 
it. They are even moved to act against the ir consciences—the oath truly binds 
them. At the very least we see the same idea in both their thought, a convergence 
in thought. Thus, Tolkien and Chesterton agree on the binding power of oaths 
or vows. If one does not want to say that Tolkien was influenced by Chesterton 
on this point, then one can approach this idea as simply taking Chesterton’s 
philosophy of oaths as a framework for reading the oath of Fëanor.4 Taken thus, 
using Chesterton’s view on oaths helps us to see how intensely and totally the 
oath of Fëanor compels the sons and forces them to take action, even when they 
would prefer not to act.  
 The oath of Fëanor and his sons plays a key role throughout The 
Silmarillion.5 After Morgoth steals the Silmarils  and flees, Fëanor leads the 
Noldor to recover them. As they depart Valinor they take the following oath:  
 
   Then Fëanor swore a terrible oath. His seven sons leapt straightway to 
his side and took the selfsame vow together, and red as blood shone their 
drawn swords in the glare of the torches. They swore an oath which none 
shall break, and none should take, b y the name even of Ilúvatar, calling 
the Everlasting Dark upon them if they kept it not; and Manwë they 
named in witness, and Varda, and the hallowed mountain of Taniquetil, 
vowing to pursue with vengeance and hatred to the ends of the World 
Vala, Demon, Elf or Man as yet unborn, or any creature, great or small, 
good or evil, that time should bring forth unto the end of days, whoso 
should hold or take or keep a Silmaril from their possession.  
 
4 There are two chief reasons one might not want to admit an explicit influence of 
Chesterton on Tolkien regarding oaths. First, Chesterton’s philosophy of oaths is small 
and appears infrequently in his writings—it is not something for which he is known nor  
is it well drawn out by him. Second, there is no biographical evidence that Tolkien is 
drawing from Chesterton about oaths —he never mentions Chesterton’s philosophy of 
oaths or any source for the oath of Fëanor. 
5 “Fëanor inspires his sons with his terrible oath, a prime mover in the story of the 
Silmarils. The efforts of the seven sons to keep that oath lie behind the machinations of 
much of the latter part of Quenta Silmarillion, while the flight of the Noldor, inspired by 
Fëanor’s lust for vengeance against the killer of his father and the thief of the Silmarils, 
will result in the events of the middle parts of the work, the establishment and history of 
the kingdoms of the elves” (Helms 54).

   Thus spoke Maedhros and Maglor and Celegorm, Curufin and 
Caranthir, Amrod and Amras, princes of the Noldor; and many quailed 
to hear the dread words. For so sworn, good or evil, an oath may not be 
broken, and it shall pursue oathkeeper and oathbreaker t o the world’s 
end. (Silmarillion [Silm] 9.73–74)6  
 
Immediately, the narrator points to the binding nature of the oath—it cannot be 
broken. There is some vagueness in precisely what this means because the 
narrator says the oath would pursue an oathbreaker—such an oath “may not be 
broken” but also one can be  an oathbreaker. Perhaps it means that the oath 
would psychologically affect an oathbreaker relentlessly and drive him to fulfill 
the oath. Further, it is clear that the oath is a rash oath —an evil oath. It is 
described as “terrible,” “dread words,” and the narrator plainly says that “none 
should take” such an oath. A particularly evil part of the oath is that it names 
even good people as enemies if they should keep a Silmaril from them.  
 Before examining how the oath works in the rest of The Silmarillion, we 
should note that Morgoth does not fear this oath, despite it being primarily an 
oath to attack him unceasingly. On the contrary, Morgoth likes the oath because 
he understands how it drives the sons of Fëanor to ruinous deeds. Chapter 
twenty-three of The Silmarillion says: 
 
Morgoth thought that his triumph was fulfilled, recking little of the sons 
of Fëanor, and of their oath, which had harmed him never and turned 
always to his mightiest aid; and in his black thought he laughed, 
regretting not the one Silmaril that he had lost, for by it as he deemed the 
last shred of the people of the Eldar should vanish from Middle-earth and 
trouble it no more. If he knew of the dwelling by the waters of Sirion, he 
gave no sign, biding his time, and waiting upon the working of oath and 
lie. (Silm 23.233)7  
 
Morgoth is, of course, right about this. At three previous points the narrator has 
said that the oath hurt the elves. First, in the curse of Mandos the elves hear that, 
“On the House of Fëanor the wrath of the Valar lieth from the West unto the 
uttermost East, and upon all that will follow them it shall be laid also. Their Oath 
shall drive them, and yet betray them, and ever snatch away the very treasures 
that they have sworn to pursue” ( Silm 9.78). Second, in chapter twelve about 
men, the narrator describes how the Elves and Men strove together against 
Morgoth but failed, and the narrator mentions one of the mechanisms by which 
 
6 Throughout Tolkien’s other writings on the oath, there is great consistency in it being 
described as unbreakable (The Lays of Beleriand 36, 162–63, 253–54, 259).  
7 “Even the loss of the Silmaril aided [Melkor], for the Oath of Maedhros and the Doom of 
the Noldor caused his enemies to slay each other” (Foster 322–23).  
The Liberty to Bind Oneself: Chesterton and the Oath of Fëanor

they fail to be the oath of Fëanor, listed along with Morgoth’s lies (12.94). Third, 
when Maedhros is gathering strength for the Nirnaeth Arnoediad, it is said that 
the oath prevented him from gathering more strength, especially that of 
Orodreth (Nargothrond) and Thingol (Doriath). Due to the acts of the sons of 
Fëanor which stemmed from their oath Orodreth is not allies with the sons, and 
they are openly enemies with Thingol (20.179).  
 How does the oath function? How does Tolkien present it as affecting 
those who took it? The language used consistently is that the oath has a power 
to force the action of the sons of Fëanor. 8 Chesterton said that an oath included 
“the liberty to bind myself” ( Orthodoxy 130). This is what we see consistently 
throughout The Silmarillion. The oath -takers are bound by their oath and feel 
forced to act upon it. Tolkien uses language which implies this binding power, 
and the plot shows characters being forced by their oath to act against their 
better judgment.  
Twice it is said that the oath “drives” the sons. First in the curse of 
Mandos “Their Oath shall drive them,” and then Finrod Felagund says to Beren 
about the sons of Fëanor, “the Oath drives them” ( Silm 9.78 and 19.159). Twice 
it is said that the oath “constrains” them. First, following Fëanor’s death when 
Maedhros is captured by Morgoth and tells the Noldor to abandon their war in 
exchange for Maedhros’s freedom, the sons are unable to agree to these te rms 
because “they were constrained also by their oath , and might not for any cause 
forsake the war against their Enemy” (13.98). Second, after Beren and Lúthien 
successfully take a Silmaril from Morgoth, the sons of Fëanor ask Thingol for it: 
“Maedhros and his brothers, being constrained by their oath had be fore sent to 
Thingol and reminded him with haughty words of their claim, summoning him 
to yield the Silmaril” (20.179). Once it is said that Maedhros “was bound by the 
oath,” and thus he could not abandon the leaguer around Angband (13.102). The 
language Tolkien uses in these places clearly implies that the oath has binding 
power over the sons of Fëanor. These five places are the clearest linguistic signs 
of convergence between Tolkien and Chesterton concerning oaths —that the 
oath binds the oath-taker.  
At three key additional places in The Silmarillion  Tolkien uses other 
language which does not so clearly imply the binding power of the oath that 
Chesterton describes. But it remains clear in the plot that the oath forces the sons 
of Fëanor to act, thus its binding power is still present. Accordingly, usi ng 
Chesterton’s philosophy of oaths as a lens is helpful because it reminds the 
 
8 Others have noted the driving power of the oath: “In Middle-earth the House of Fëanor, 
led by Maedhros, often tried to act with moderation, but the fateful pressure of the Oath of 
Fëanor subverted their intentions, and time after time the sons of Fëanor, impelled by their desire 
for the Silmaril, became enmeshed in treachery and violence” (Foster 258, emphasis added).  
Matthew McKenna 
88  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
reader that the oath still binds the sons of Fëanor at these three pivotal moments. 
In these three places the oath drives the sons to do terrible deeds which they 
know are evil. These are the most critical acts which the oath drives them to 
commit. Importantly, before they commit these acts, they sometimes repent of 
the previous evil deeds which their oath drove them to do. This shows that 
Tolkien’s understanding of the way that oaths function is thoroughly 
Chestertonian in that oaths limit the person’s choices in the future and force the 
oath-taker to act according to his oaths, even against what he knows is right.  
The first place this appears is when Dior possesses the Silmaril and the 
sons of Fëanor demand it back (this takes place after Thingol is killed by 
dwarves, the dwarves sack Menegroth, Beren reclaimed the Silmaril with the 
help of the Ents, and Luthien wea rs it until her death). When Dior refuses, the 
sons of Fëanor attack Doriath. The narrator describes the chain of events: 
 
But now the rumor ran among the scattered Elves of Beleriand that Dior 
Thingol’s heir wore the Nauglamir [a necklace with a Silmaril set in it], 
and they said: “A Silmaril of Fëanor burns again in the woods of 
Doriath”; and the oath of the sons of Fëanor was waked again from sleep. 
For while Lúthien wore the Necklace of the Dwarves no Elf would dare 
assail her; but now hearing of the renewal of Doriath and of Dior’s pride 
the seven gathered again from wandering, and they sent to him to claim 
their own. (Silm 22.225) 
 
While not explicitly saying that the oath drives them, it is clear that the waking 
of the oath is a causal factor in their demanding the Silmaril from Dior. Lúthien’s 
beauty was too great and terrible. Her greatness made it impossible for them to 
reclaim the Silmaril, so they waited as they did when Morgoth held it (and while 
he still holds the other two). But now that the Silmaril has come to Dior, they 
have some chance to reclaim it (it is possible for them to reclaim it), and thus 
they try to.  
 The second time is when sons of Fëanor learn that the Silmaril is kept 
at the mouth of the river Sirion by Elwing and attack the elven havens to reclaim 
it. Tolkien writes: 
 
Now when first the tidings came to Maedhros that Elwing yet lived, and 
dwelt in possession of the Silmaril by the mouths of Sirion, he repenting 
of the deeds in Doriath withheld his hand. But in time the knowledge of 
the oath unfulfilled returned to tormen t him and his brothers, and 
gathering from their wandering hunting-paths they sent messages to the 
Havens of friendship and yet of stern demand. Then Elwing and the 
people of Sirion would not yield the jewel which Beren had won and 
Lúthien had worn [...]. And so there came to pass the last and cruelest of 
The Liberty to Bind Oneself: Chesterton and the Oath of Fëanor 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  89 
the slayings of Elf by Elf; and that was the third of the great wrongs 
achieved by the accursed oath. (Silm 24.236) 
  
Again, there is no direct description of the oath driving the sons, but given that 
Maedhros repented of earlier deeds from the oath —slaying Dior9—and yet the 
sons again assault other elves, it seems that the oath drove them. Also, the 
narrator indicates that the unfulfilled oath had a causal role in making them ask 
for the Silmaril and then attack when it was not yielded. So, again Tolkien uses 
other language, but it remains clear that the oath is forcing the sons to act. This 
episode is especially important for it follows the sons’ regret of their previous 
assault on Doriath (which the oath drove them to do), yet they assault the elven 
havens. This emphasizes the binding power of the oath.  
 The third time Tolkien uses other language to describe the oath driving 
the sons to act is after the Great Battle when the host of Valinor defeats Morgoth. 
The remaining two Silmarils are taken from Morgoth and Eönwë keeps them 
until the host returns to Valinor. Maedhros and Maglor (the only sons of Fëanor 
left alive at this point in the story) “prepared, though now with weariness and 
loathing, to attempt in despair the fulfillment of their oath; for they would have 
given battle for the Silmarils, were the y withheld, even against the victorious 
host of Valinor, even though they stood alone against the world. And they sent 
a message therefore to Eönwë, bidding him yield up now those jewels” ( Silm 
24.242). Eönwë replies that the evils deeds provoked by the oath have destroyed 
any right to the Silmarils that the sons formerly had —Eönwë does not say that 
their oath is void, only that their right to the Silmarils is void (24.242). He then 
instructs Maedhros and Maglor to return to Valinor to face the judgment of the 
Valar regarding their actions, their oath, and their claim on the Silmarils. At this 
point Maglor wants to return to Valinor and hopes to fulfill the oath through the 
mercy of the Valar. But Maedhros argues that if the Valar do not pardon them, 
then their oath would be impossible to fulfill. He also points out that Manwe 
does not have the power to release them from their oath since they made it to 
Ilúvatar, not Manwë. 10 Maglor thinks that trying to fulfill the oath by force, 
again, would be a worse evil than leaving the oath unfulfilled, but he eventually 
yields to Maedhros and they attack at night and successfully regain the Silmarils 
 
9 Maedhros also repented of this deed earlier in the story, immediately after his attack on 
Doriath (Silm 22.226).  
10 This was mentioned previously as well: “The wise have said that the hour was not yet 
come, and that only one speaking in person for the cause of both Elves and Men, pleading 
for pardon on their misdeeds and pity on their woes, might move the counsels of t he 
Powers; and the oath of Fëanor perhaps even Manwë could not loose, until it found its end, and 
the sons of Fëanor relinquished the Silmarils, upon which they had laid their ruthless claim” (Silm

Matthew McKenna 
90  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
(24.242–43). Yet, the Silmarils burn their hands and Maedhros recognizes that 
Eönwë was correct. Their claim on the Silmarils was void due to their crimes. 
Maedhros then reasons that, due to no longer having a claim on the Silmarils, 
the oath is void (24.243). So,  Eönwë’s point that they no longer have a right to 
possess the Silmarils further implies that the oath is void.  
Yet, despite the oath being void, it still drove them to act and steal the 
Silmarils. This is evidenced by the fact that they have previously regretted many 
acts that the oath drove them to commit, they tried to avoid further evils 
occasioned by the oath, and on this occasion they both are weary of the oath, but 
they still killed elves in order to steal the Silmarils. Maglor was not driven as 
strongly this time since he wanted to wait for the judgment of the Valar (the oath 
was not urgently driving him), this hints that the driving force of the now-void 
oath was indeed weaker. Thus, the oath’s driving force had diminished, but 
clearly still had some power despite being void. This further implies that the 
oath was not completely void, contrary to what Maedhros seems to conclude as 
the narrator explains, “But the jewel burned the hand of Maedhros in pain 
unbearable; and he perceived that it was as Eönwë had said, and that his right 
thereto had become void, and that the oath was vain” (Silm 24.243).  
It seems this can be explained by the fact that the oath simply names 
the Silmarils to Fëanor and his sons and does not explicitly base this claim on 
any rightful sense of ownership (such as the fact that Fëanor made them), 
“vowing to pursue with vengeance  and hatred to the ends of the World Vala, 
Demon, Elf or Man as yet unborn, or any creature, great or small, good or evil, 
that time should bring forth unto the end of days, whoso should hold or take or 
keep a Silmaril from their possession” ( Silm 9.73). T hus, when due to their 
crimes, the sons have lost any right to possess the Silmarils, their oath to try to 
possess the Silmarils still drives them. Basically, their oath was not well worded 
(i.e., based on justice) but instead was based on greed. It was ba sed on an 
excessive desire to possess the Silmarils. Thus, the oath still has driving force 
(i.e., is not totally void) despite the right to possess the Silmarils being null. The 
narrator’s prelude to these events also implies that the oath is not void: 
 
Maedhros and Maglor would not hearken, and they prepared, though 
now with weariness and loathing, to attempt in despair the fulfillment of 
their oath; for they would have given battle for the Silmarils, were they 
withheld, even against the victorious host of Valinor, even though they 
stood alone against the world. And they sent a message therefore to 
Eönwë, bidding him yield up now those jewels which of old Fëanor their 
father made and Morgoth stole from him. (Silm 24.242)  
 
This also portrays their claim to Eönwë as based on their right to the Silmarils, 
hence his response (that they no longer have a right to them) makes sense.  
The Liberty to Bind Oneself: Chesterton and the Oath of Fëanor 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  91 
 This fact (i.e., the bad wording of the oath which separates the oath 
from a rightful claim to possess the Silmarils), is a common motif in myth. John 
Holmes points out that such rash oaths bind the oath -taker to the letter of the 
oath and not to the spirit of the oath (Holmes 258). Sticking to the strict letter of 
the oath drives the sons to attack Eönwë.  
Chesterton’s philosophy of oaths makes it explicit that the oath binds 
the oath-taker; one cannot ignore one’s oath. The words of the oath have power 
over the oath-taker. Without the explicit knowledge of this given by Chesterton, 
a reader, especially a mo dern reader without a rich appreciation for oaths and 
their importance in ancient and medieval society, could easily be confused by 
the behavior of the sons of Fëanor and their adherence to the oath despite all the 
evil it has caused (and which they readily admit and repent of). Alexander Bruce 
will be shown to make just this mistake. In the three crucial places examined 
above, the oath drives the sons of Fëanor to evil deeds which Maglor and 
Maedhros repent of, but they keep finding themselves unable to br eak the oath 
they now hate. The repeating theme of their attacks on fellow elves followed by 
regret, only to do it all over again, makes little sense apart from the binding 
power of the oath (i.e., apart from Chesterton’s philosophy of oaths).  
 
RESPONSE TO ALEXANDER BRUCE 
 What are the results of the oath of Fëanor? Consistently throughout The 
Silmarillion the results are evil. As previously seen, Morgoth likes the oath and 
knows it makes the sons fight against the other elves. Specifically, it is the chief 
and explicit cause that drives the sons of Fëanor to attack Beren and Lúthien, 
Dior at Doriath, Elwing at Sirion, and Eönwë after Morgoth’s defeat.  
 Although the oath drives the sons to do many evil acts listed above, not 
all the sons are totally corrupted, specifically Maedhros and Maglor. As 
previously mentioned, Maedhros repents of the attack on Doriath and tries to 
find and rescue the sons of Dior  who had been released into the wild ( Silm 
22.225–26). Later, he tries to avoid attacking Sirion because he repents of the 
attack on Doriath and does not want to repeat it (24.236). After the attack on 
Sirion, Maglor “took pity upon Elros and Elrond, and h e cherished them, and 
love grew after between them, as little might be thought; but Maglor’s heart was 
sick and weary with the burden of the dreadful oath” (24.237). Maglor, like 
Maedhros, repents and, like Maedhros tried to do with Dior’s son, cares for Elros 
and Elrond (sons of Eärendil and Elwing). Both Maedhros and Maglor are glad 
when they see Eärendil’s star with the Silmaril rise in the sky: 
 
And when this new star was seen at evening, Maedhros spoke to Maglor 
his brother, and he said: “Surely that is a Silmaril that shines now in the 
West?” 
Matthew McKenna 
92  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
And Maglor answered: “If it be truly the Silmaril which we saw cast 
into the sea that rises again by the power of the Valar, then let us be glad; 
for its glory is seen now by many, and is yet secure from all evil.” ( Silm 
24.239–40)  
 
Instead of being possessive and angry that a Silmaril is out of their reach, they 
are glad that many people can now enjoy its light and beauty. This shows that 
they are not totally corrupted and greedy for the Silmaril as Fëanor was, who 
locked them away in Valinor and denied the sight of them to others, which led 
to their capture by Morgoth ( Silm 8.65).11 Finally, as seen, Maglor tries to avoid 
doing further evil in pursuit of the Silmarils when Eönwë takes them and 
Maedhros is said to seek them with “weariness and loathing” (24.242). The fact 
that Maedhros and Maglor are not completely corrupted, but stil l do terrible 
deeds (attacking fellow elves multiple times) due to the oath highlights the 
power of the oath —that it is driving or forcing them to act. This is similar to 
Chesterton’s novel The Man Who Was Thursday, wherein the characters take rash 
oaths (thus similar to the oath of Fëanor) but feel compelled to keep them, 
despite the evil outcomes from them (in Thursday these evil results are the 
anarchist plots potentially succeeding and hurting innocent civilians). Thus, 
both Tolkien and Chesterton portray their characters as compelled to keep rash 
oaths, despite the evil outcomes of doing so. This shows their shared belief in 
the binding power of vows.  
 Alexander Bruce disagrees with this moral analysis of Maedhros and 
Maglor and argues that they are ultimately villains : “Whatever their deeds of 
valor might have been, because of their commitment to the oath, in the end 
Maedhros and Maglor must be counted among the great foes of Middle -earth, 
as corrupt as Carcharoth, as corrupt as Morgoth himself” (Bruce 69). There are 
three issues with this reading.  
First, it seems absurd to claim they are as evil as Morgoth. The narrator 
consistently presents these two as trag ic and deeply flawed, but trying to be 
good. Indeed, after Maglor throws away his Silmaril he spends his remaining 
life in regret ( Silm 24.243). Thus, while Maedhros may be more of a Judas -like 
character who commits suicide after his great sin in despair (24.243), Maglor is 
more of a Peter -like character who repents of his great sin (but unlike Peter in 
the gospels, Maglor never rejoins th e commu nity). Maedhros also did not 
participate in the burning of the Teleri ships at Losgar (9.80). Early in the 
Silmarillion when the narrator describes the princes of the elves, Maglor is 
described as a mighty singer and Maedhros as merely “Maedhros the tall,” 
while other of the sons of Fëanor are described by less flattering terms —
 
11 For an analysis of Fëanor’s hiding of the Silmarils see Bernthal 108–09.  
The Liberty to Bind Oneself: Chesterton and the Oath of Fëanor 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  93 
“Caranthir the dark” (5.50).12 Second, Bruce’s reading overlooks the many times 
Maedhros and Maglor repent and try to avoid further evil (22.225 –26 and 
24.236–37).  
Third, Bruce’s argument overlooks what I have tried to prove in this 
paper—the driving force of the oath. Bruce claims it was precisely the brothers’ 
“commitment to the oath” that made them villains; they chose to fulfill the oath 
and thus continued to choose evil deeds until the end (69). But, as I have argued, 
they were not so much committed to the oath (in fact they resented it and Bruce 
earlier adm itted this [65]) as driven by it. Essentially, Bruce fails to see the 
binding power the oath has on them; he fails to see that they are not totally free 
to break their oath. Since they are driven by their oath, they are less free to ignore 
it. This suggests to us that we should rather pity them than condemn them. 
Especially in their final attack on Eönwë, Maedh ros and Maglor very much 
appear to desire to be free from their oath but are themselves its prisoner. 
Certainly, it is an evil act to attack Eönwë and try to reclaim the Silmarils. But 
they are not as morally culpable for this act (because they are not totally free) as 
Bruce wants to say. Therefore, Chesterton’s philosophy of oaths helpfully 
enlightens how the oath functions in The Silmarillion by making its binding 
power more explicit and clearer. This shows that we should soften our moral 
condemnations of Maedhros and Maglor. Thus, one practical effect of examining 
the oath of Fëanor through the lens of Chesterton’s philosophy of oaths is that it 
tempers our moral judgment of Maedhros and Maglor by showing they are not 
totally free to choose their acts due to the binding power of their oath.   
 
CONCLUSION 
 This paper has done several things. First, it has read the oath of Fëanor 
through the framework of Chesterton’s philosophy of oaths and has suggested 
that Tolkien may have been influenced by Chesterton’s philosophy of oaths by 
showing how strongly Chesterton’s philosophy is at work in the oath of Fëanor. 
Other oaths in Tolkien’s writings which could also be studied along similar 
lines: Thingol’s oath not to kill Beren, Beren’s o ath to retrieve the Silmaril, and 
especially Gollum’s oath to serve Frodo. 13 For both Chesterton and Tolkien, 
oaths are acts of freedom and will which bind the oath taker in the future. At the 
very least, this paper has demonstrated that Tolkien shares Chesterton’s view of 
oaths and their binding power. While in the real world, oaths do not absolutely 
necessitate or force our wills (people can break an oath), the presentation of the 
 
12 I read this as a moral descriptor and not a physical one. 
13 For more on oaths in Tolkien see Holmes 249 –61; for Gollum’s oath to Frodo see 
Donnelly 24 –25; for a comparison of Pippin’s oath to Denethor and Merry’s oath to 
Théoden see Bernthal 227–29.  
Matthew McKenna 
94  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
oath of Fëanor as having such true binding power highlights the seriousness of 
oaths in the real world.  
Second, this paper has nuanced a claim by Alexander Bruce about the 
oath of Fëanor. Specifically, he argues that Maedhros and Maglor are ultimately 
villains due to the oath. But Maedhros and Maglor are not unambiguously 
villains in The Silmarillion  and viewing their oath through the lens of 
Chesterton’s philosophy helps reveal this to us by making it explicit, rather than 
merely implicit in the story, that their oath bound them.
