Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  95 
 
 
                        ODELLING THE FEAR OF GOD  
                                        IN C.S.  LEWIS ’S  
                              THE CHRONICLES OF NARNIA  
 
                                               BRETT ROSCOE  
 
 
ART OF THE VALUE THAT C.S. LEWIS SAW IN MYTHOPOEIC LITERATURE  is its 
ability to affect readers, to evoke a rich emotional experience. He therefore 
believed a myth is not just something to be assented to, but something to be 
imagined and felt. “If God chooses to be mythopoeic,” he writes, “[…] shall we 
refuse to be mythopathic?” (“Myth Became Fact” 59 –60). In an article originally 
published in The New Yorker, he admits his hopes that The Chronicles of Narnia 
would have this mythopathic effect on readers: “I thought I saw how stories of 
this kind could steal past a certain inhibition which had paralysed much of my 
own religion in childhood. Why did one find it  so hard to feel as one was told 
one ought to feel about God or about the sufferings of Christ? I thought the chief 
reason was that one was told one ought to” (“S ometimes Fairy Stories” 57–58). 
The Narnia stories, he suggests, have the potential to evoke religious affect in 
readers, helping them learn how they ought to “feel about God,” but without 
the sense of duty that stifles such emotions. The goal of this article is to study 
one of thes e feelings about God —the fear of the Lord —as it is evoked by and 
represented in The Chronicles of Narnia . First I draw on psychological studies 
of emotion and the kinesic approach to literature developed by Guillemette 
Bolens to appreciate how the concrete imagery in Lewis’s books evoke a fear of 
Aslan. Then I place this fear in the context of Rudolf Otto’s The Idea of the Holy, 
looking at the influence this book had on Lewis’s assumptions about the fear of 
God and his depictions of Aslan. My basic argument is that, in The Chronicles 
of Narnia , the physical encounters with Aslan invite characters (and by 
extension readers) to experience the fear of the Lord, an emotion that Lewis 
portrays as analogous to, though significantly different from, other kinds of fear. 
 That creative literature can arouse emotions in readers is not a new 
insight. Aristotle, for example, famously defined the Greek tragedy by its 
emotional effects, saying that it arouses pity and fear in the audience (54). But 
what more recent cognitive ap proaches to literature demonstrate is how much 
goes on in our brains underneath the surface when we read. Two insights in 
particular concern me in this study. The first has to do with the quick emotional 
and physiological responses that can be triggered by  texts. Cognitive literary 
theorist Patrick Colm Hogan writes that there are three sources of emotion: (1) 
P 
Brett Roscoe 
96  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
innate sensitivities, (2) critical period developments (i.e. childhood experiences), 
and (3) emotional memories (57).1 The first of these, innate sensitivities, is often 
exemplified in cognitive science with reference to fear. As cognitive scientist 
Joseph Ledoux puts it, “Each species is innately prepared to treat certain stimuli 
as threatening. Innate/prepared threats i nclude pictures of people with facial 
expressions of anger or fear, or pictures of venomous animals like snakes and 
spiders” (203). Studies of the amygdala, a part of the brain that is activated when 
a person feels fear, demonstrate that it is sensitive an d can be activated by an 
angry facial expression, a sudden sound or movement, or by the sighting of a 
dangerous animal. Our innate threat sensors are so sensitive, in fact, that they 
can arouse a fear state even before we are conscious of any fear. In one study, 
adults with fears of snakes or spiders were shown images of these threatening 
animals, but the images were shown so briefly that the adults did not notice 
them consciously or report any feeling of fear, and yet their minds reacted with 
a fear state nonetheless (Kagan 105). 2 We might say that the subjects had a 
“‘ghost’ feeling,” a partial, unconscious experience of fear. 3 As this and other 
studies demonstrate, the cause of the activation does not need to be real; a mere 
picture or mental image is sufficient. And, as will be demonstrated below, The 
Chronicles of Narnia provide plenty of such threatening images of the lion 
Aslan, focusing on his roars, his teeth, and his claws and thereby evoking an 
experience of fear. 
 The second insight of cognitive approaches to literature that concerns 
me here is the growing realization that reading—like thinking in general—is an 
embodied experience. As Raymond W. Gibbs puts it, “ the brain does not work 
in isolation, but is part of an organic whole that includes the nervous system and 
kinesthetic sensations of the body in action” (279).  Studies using fMRI scans 
have demonstrated that when a person reads action words like “kick,” “chew,” 
or “run,” their motor and premotor cortex is act ivated in a way similar to how 
they would be activated if the person were physically performing the motion 
(Hauk, et al. 301 –07; Pulvervmüller, et al. 793 –97). This effect is called “motor 
resonance,” “the process by which action observation activates the same neural 
substrates as those recruited when a perceiver performs an action by 
themselves” (Bellock and Lyons 21). The experience of motor resonance 
suggests that readers create perceptual simulations when they read, enacting the 
spatial orientation, mov ements, and gestures described by a text. This insight 
from neuroscience has been applied to literary texts by Guillemette Bolens in 
 
1 This threefold division is similar to Jerome Kagan’s typology of fear. Kagan identifies 
three kinds of fear: fear of innate threats, conditioned (learned) fear, and fear of the 
unfamiliar (89–95). 
2 Cf. Ledoux 203–05. 
3 I have taken the phrase “ghost feeling” from Terence Cave (36). 
Modelling the Fear of God in C.S. Lewis’s The Chronicles of Narnia 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  97 
The Style of Gestures. Bolens develops a kind of reading called “kinesic analysis,” 
a method of reading that pays especial attention to the ways in which texts 
“trigger sensorimotor perceptual simulations in the reader” (28). This kind of 
reading foregrounds minute details of gesture, facial expression, motion, and 
bodily orientation, details that are often passed over in analyses of literary texts. 
In The Chronicles of Narnia , it is precisely details such as these that allow 
readers to create perceptual si mulations of the strength and power of the lion 
Aslan and to enact the trembling, freezing, running away, and stumbling of the 
characters who encounter this fearsome, divine presence. 
 Such an approach, I believe, can do justice to the concrete ways Lewis 
portrays God in this series. Jesus is famously portrayed as Aslan, a lion, and 
while the lion might be a reference to the “lion of Judah,” one of Jesus’ biblical 
titles ( ESV Bible, Rev. 5:5), 4 and while the lion might symbolically represent 
qualities of God —qualities such as strength, power, and royalty —at the same 
time the image of the lion is not something to be abstracted and discarded. This 
is the very mistake made by the Narnian horse Bree . Sharing his “knowledge” 
of Aslan with the Calormene girl Aravis, Bree explains that when Narnians call 
Aslan a lion, they “of course” do not actually mean he is a lion: 
 
“No doubt,” continued Bree, “when they speak of him as a Lion they only 
mean he’s as strong as a lion or [...] as fierce as a lion. Or something of 
that kind. Even a little girl like you, Aravis, must see that it would be 
quite absurd to suppose he is a real lion. Indeed it would be disrespectful. 
If he was a lion he’d have to be a Beast just like the rest of us. Why! [...] If 
he was a lion he’d have four paws, and a tail, and Whiskers!” (The Horse 
and His Boy 14.200)5 
 
What Bree does not realize is that, during this lengthy attempt to allegorize the 
lion, the actual lion himself has walked up behind him. And when Bree 
mentions the “absurd” thought of Aslan having whiskers, Aslan’s whiskers 
brush his ear, causing Bree to jump and run to the other side of the garden. It 
turns out that the very qualities Bree ridicules —the paws, the tail, the 
whiskers—are some of the most important qualities of Aslan. These physical 
qualities are, for example, emphasized in the prophecy of A slan’s coming, a 
prophecy the Pevensie children hear in The Lion, the Witch, and the Wardrobe (LWW): 
 
 
 
 
 
4 Lewis himself makes this biblical connection (Letters III.160). 
5 All citations to The Chronicles of Narnia books are to chapter and page number. 
Brett Roscoe 
98  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
Wrong will be right, when Aslan comes in sight,  
At the sound of his roar, sorrows will be no more,  
When he bares his teeth, winter meets its death, 
And when he shakes his mane, we shall have spring again.  
(8.79, emphasis added) 
 
Lewis repeatedly emphasizes the physical qualities of Aslan, never letting his 
readers forget that Aslan is, in fact, a lion, complete with mane, teeth, and claws. 
And in so far as Aslan is a lion, he is, from a human standpoint, a danger and a 
threat, or, in other words, something to be feared. In a letter Lewis admits that 
his Narnia stories were inspired by his “nightmares about lions” (Letters III.807), 
suggesting that he associated this animal with the emotion fear. And it is the 
threatening, fearsome qualities of lions that are emphasized in The Chronicles 
of Narnia.  
 Aslan’s mouth is foregrounded through the frequent roars and 
rumblings. When the White Witch suggests that Aslan might not be true to his 
word, Aslan does not argue or offer any assurances; instead he puts on a display 
of power: “‘Wow!’ roared Aslan half rising from his throne; and his great mouth 
opened wider and wider and the roar grew louder and louder, and the Witch, 
after staring for a moment with her lips wide apart, picked up her skirts and 
fairly ran for her life” ( LWW 13.144). The mouth opening “w ider and wider” 
draws our attention to one of the most dangerous parts of the lion, a detail that 
is not lost on the Witch, nor on the Pevensie children. The children want to ask 
Aslan about his discussion with the Witch, but Aslan’s “face was stern and 
everyone’s ears were still ringing with the sound of his roar and so nobody 
dared” (14.145). The roar is thus experienced as a fearsome event by both 
enemies and friends of the lion. In fact, anyone, even an endearing character like 
Lucy, can become a target  of the lion’s roar. While looking through a magical 
book, Lucy is tempted to use a spell that she knows she should not. Before she 
gives into the temptation, though, she sees something she does not expect:  
 
[W]hen she looked back at the opening words of the spell, there in the 
middle of the writing, where she felt quite sure there had been no picture 
before, she found the great face of a lion, of the Lion, Aslan himself, 
staring into hers. It was painted such  a bright gold that it seemed to be 
coming towards her out of the page; and indeed she never was quite sure 
afterwards that it hadn’t really moved a little. At any rate she knew the 
expression on his face quite well. He was growling and you could see 
most of his teeth. She became horribly afraid and turned over the page at 
once. (The Voyage of the “Dawn Treader” [VDT] 10.154–55) 
 
Modelling the Fear of God in C.S. Lewis’s The Chronicles of Narnia 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  99 
Here the sound of growling is not necessarily heard, but it is nevertheless 
experienced through the facial expression of the lion, who bares his teeth in a 
threatening gesture. Aslan often refrains from speech, instead using growls and 
other gestures to communicate. In Prince Caspian (PC), Lucy sees Aslan, but does 
not follow him because the other members of her party do not believe Aslan is 
there. Upon meeting Aslan, Lucy starts making excuses for her behaviour, and 
these excuses are only met with physical  messages: “’I saw you all right. They 
wouldn’t believe me. They’re all so —’ From somewhere deep inside Aslan’s 
body there came the faintest suggestion of a growl” (10.142). This growl, which 
is more felt than heard, is Aslan’s way of telling Lucy that she should not blame 
others for her own shortcomings. Apparently, the growl can say more than one 
thing, and Aslan communicates through it on more than one occasion. In The 
Last Battle (LB), he roars a command to wake the giant Time (13.170), and then 
later he growls to challenge the false teaching that Aslan and Tash are the same 
god (15.188–89). 
Often these roars and growls result in shaking or trembling of some 
sort, showing Aslan to be an unsettling presence both emotionally and 
physically. In Prince Caspian, for example, Aslan commands silence in a voice 
that even moves the ground: “‘Peace,’ said Aslan in the low voice which was 
nearest to his growl. The earth seemed to shake a little and every living thing in 
the grove became still as stone” (15.216 –17). The humans and animals respond 
to this shaking with one of the most typical responses to fe ar. According to 
Ledoux, most animals, including humans, have three choices when reacting to 
fear: “freeze first; flee if you can; and fight if you must” (54). When the animals 
and humans hear Aslan’s roar, they feel the earth shake and they become frozen 
in fear. Whether by freezing suddenly, running away, or trembling and shaking, 
humans, animals, and the land itself respond in bodily fear to Aslan’s roars and 
rumbles. 
 The other weapons of the lion, his claws, are also foregrounded in these 
stories. They are sometimes mentioned directly and sometimes hinted at by 
mention of the lion’s paws. The main characters in The Horse and His Boy go on a 
mission of urgency in a race to warn the king of Archenland about an imminent 
attack. Somehow, though, they find themselves going slower than they need to, 
lacking the motivation to run as fast as they can, at least until the lion Aslan 
gives them the “new strength of fear” (11.164), chasing after them from behind, 
snapping at them with his mouth and even attacking Aravis: “The lion rose on 
its hind legs, larger than you would have believed a lion could be, and jabbed at 
Aravis with its right paw. Shasta could see all the terrible claws extended. Aravis 
screamed and reeled in the saddle. The lion was tearing her shoulders” (10.144). 
We later discover that Aslan is not trying to kill Aravis; he is instead trying to 
punish or discipline her for causing her stepmother’s servant to be bea ten 
Brett Roscoe 
100  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
(14.201–02). For Aravis, then, the discipline of God mentioned frequently in the 
Bible (for example in Prov. 3:11–12 or Heb. 12:5–6) is not some abstract idea but 
a bodily experience made concrete by the swipes of the lion’s claws. Similarly, 
in The Voyage of the “Dawn Treader,” Eustace is transformed into a dragon, and 
he finds that the only way to get rid of his scales and become human again is 
with the help of Aslan’s claws. After the experience, Eustace says, “I was afraid 
of his claws, I can tell you [...]. The very first te ar he made was so deep that I 
thought it had gone right into my heart. And when he began pulling the skin 
off, it hurt worse than anything I’ve ever felt” (7.108 –09). Here Eustace learns 
that “to put off [the] old self” and “to put on the new self” as Paul  describes 
Christian transformation in Ephesians (4:22, 24), is much more difficult and 
much more painful than changing t-shirts, and any reader who has ripped off a 
scab or a band aid can imagine—and through this mental simulation somewhat 
experience—Eustace’s pain. 
Even when the claws are not mentioned explicitly, they are hinted at 
by the mention of Aslan’s paws and the gestures he makes with them. When the 
Pevensies first meet Aslan, the lion’s paws quickly become the focus of the 
narrative: “The Lion shook his man e and clapped his paws together (‘Terrible 
paws,’ thought Lucy, ‘If he didn’t know how to velvet them!’)” ( LWW 12.129). 
A little later, when speaking with Peter, Aslan lays his paw on Peter’s shoulder, 
and the narrator points out that “though it was velvet ed it was very 
heavy”(LWW 12.129). By telling us the paw is velveted, the narrator reminds us 
that there are dangerous claws hidden in it, and the weight of the paw suggests 
the strength behind those claws.6 
 There are, admittedly, numerous scenes in the Narnia books that 
describe an intimacy between Aslan and other characters, who touch his fur and 
his mane, kiss him and receive licks of his tongue, and even play with him. After 
Aslan is killed on the Stone Table, he comes back to life and begins to play with 
Lucy and Susan. The girls run around trying to catch Aslan’s tail, and they roll 
around together in a “laughing heap of fur and arms and legs” ( LWW 15.164). 
But even this playful description never completely loses sight of the lion’s power 
and potential danger. Lucy has a hard time deciding whether playing with 
Aslan is “more like playing with a thunderstorm or playing with a kitten” 
(15.164). Details like this, or the combination of intimate experiences o f Aslan 
with the roars and growls described above suggest that the dangers of the lion 
may be backgrounded, but they never disappear completely. Lucy, Susan, and 
other human and animal characters can become intimate with Aslan, but not 
familiar with him; he is not an animal to be taken for granted.  
 
6 An argument could similarly be made that Aslan’s gentle carrying of Jill in his mouth 
reminds readers Aslan has dangerous teeth and jaws (Lewis, The Silver Chair [SC] 8.117). 
Modelling the Fear of God in C.S. Lewis’s The Chronicles of Narnia 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  101 
 The lion Aslan may symbolically represent God, or more specifically 
Jesus Christ, but he is experienced physically by characters and imaginatively 
by readers as a lion, a wild animal whose strength and size is itself a threat to 
humans, and this threat is a constant in the Narnia books. Even though various 
characters learn to trust and depend on Aslan, there is never any assurance that 
this lion is completely safe. In an often -quoted part of LWW, Susan and Lucy, 
surprised to hear that Aslan is a lion, ask whether he is safe, seeking some sort 
of assurance, but that assurance is never given: 
 
“Ooh!” said Susan, “I’d thought he was a man. Is he —quite safe? I 
shall feel rather nervous about meeting a lion.” 
“That you will, dearie, and no mistake,” said Mrs. Beaver, “if there’s 
anyone who can appear before Aslan without their knees knocking, 
they’re either braver than most or else just silly.” 
“Then he isn’t safe?” said Lucy. 
“Safe?” said Mr. Beaver. “Don’t you hear what Mrs. Beaver tells you? 
Who said anything about safe? “Course he isn’t safe. But he’s good.” (8.79–80) 
 
Instead of trying to rid Susan and Lucy of fear, Mrs. Beaver says that “knees 
knocking,” a physical sign of fear, is the appropriate response to such a being. 
In The Silver Chair, when Aslan asks Jill to drink from a stream of water, Jill asks 
for similar assurances of safety, and these assurances are again denied: 
   
“Will you promise not to—do anything to me, if I do come?” said Jill. 
“I make no promise,” said the Lion. 
Jill was so thirsty now that, without noticing it, she had come a step nearer. 
“Do you eat girls?” she said. 
“I have swallowed up girls and boys, women and men, kings and 
emperors, cities and realms,” said the Lion. (2.22) 
 
Aslan does not respond with “Don’t worry. I won’t hurt you” or any other such 
assurances, but instead with a comment that would only make Jill more 
uncomfortable. When characters encounter Aslan —the God -figure in the 
stories—fear is an important emotion, not something to overcome and get rid of. 
However, important as the concrete details of Aslan are, this fear of 
God is for Lewis more than just a fear of lions. In portraying the experience of 
God as he does in these books, Lewis reveals an understanding of the fear of the 
Lord that is influenced by Rudolf Otto’s The Idea of the Holy, a now-classic study 
of religious experience published in German in 1917 and first translated into 
English in 1923. While Corbin Scott Carnell claims that Lewis read and was 
influenced by Otto’s book in the 1920s (96),  I believe Adam Barkman is correct 
Brett Roscoe 
102  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
when he argues that Lewis first encountered the book in 1936 since this is when, 
in a letter, Lewis mentions purchasing a copy (Barkman 123; Lewis, Letters II.204). 
Lewis’s subsequent correspondences and nonfictional writings 
provide evidence of the favourable impression this book made on him. In one 
letter he claims that he was “deeply influenced” by Otto’s book ( Letters III.980). 
In two letters he recommends the book to others (II.528 –20, III.125–26), and on 
other occasions Lewis makes mention of the “numinous,” a term coined by Otto 
and popularized by his book. In a letter to Martyn Skinner dated December 31st, 
1959, for example, Lewis challenges Skinner to make his  writing “numinous,” 
assuming this is a quality of powerful storytelling ( Letters III.1118–19).7 In The 
Problem of Pain, Lewis mentions Otto by name and lists the numinous as one of 
the three strands or elements of “developed religion” (4 –9). Later, in an essay 
originally published in 1952, Lewis cites Otto’s book and says that “the seed of 
religious experience” is located “in our experience of the Numinous” (“Is 
Theism Important?” 189). Then, near the end of his life, when The Christian 
Century asked him what books had influenced him most, Lewis listed ten books, 
including Otto’s The Idea of the Holy (“Ex Libris” 719). 
According to Barkman, however, “influence” might be too strong of a 
word. Barkman argues that Lewis’s thinking had already developed in ways 
that were merely re -affirmed by Otto’s book (Barkman 132). Nevertheless, 
Barkman admits that certain of Otto’s idea s, such as the description of the 
mysterium tremendum, seem to have left a mark on Lewis (129). 8 The connection 
between Lewis’s works and Otto’s the numinous has been noted before, but 
usually with only a cursory treatment. 9 One important exception, the study by 
Barkman, provides an extended treatment of the topic, but whereas his focus is 
on how Otto’s book relates to Lewis’s concept of joy, my focus in the rest of this 
article will be on how Otto’s concept of the numinous c an help us understand 
the fear of God in the Narnia books.   
 Both Lewis and Otto are interested in religious experience, in how 
people encounter the divine. Otto’s book is largely about a quality of religious 
experience that he calls “numinous,” a difficult concept that includes various 
emotional responses to the divine, including the fear of the Lord. He claims that 
the numinous cannot b e precisely described by language, and so any study of 
the numinous must proceed through metaphors and analogies, comparisons 
 
7 Also see a letter to Sister Penelope where he comments on Penelope’s description of a 
haunted island in relation to Otto’s book: “I was interested in your account of the haunted 
island of your childhood —a good example of what Otto would call the lowest gr ade of 
the ‘Numinous’“ (Letters II.285). 
8 The mysterium tremendum is discussed in more detail below. 
9 See, for example, Como 74, 234–35; Duriez 357; Christopher 117; Carnell 57, 96; and the 
slightly more extended discussion in Downing 33–34, 109–10, 128–30. 
Modelling the Fear of God in C.S. Lewis’s The Chronicles of Narnia 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  103 
and contrasts. “This X of ours,” he says, meaning the numinous, “is not precisely 
this experience, but akin to this one and the opposite of that other” (Otto 7). So 
when Otto talks about an emotional aspect of the numinous that he calls the 
“mysterium tremendum ,” he explains the concept with recourse to everyday 
emotional terms like fear, but he stresses that these “ordinary” emotions are 
analogies of the numinous: “‘Tremor’ is in itself merely the perfectly familiar 
and ‘natural’ emotion of fear,” he says, “But here the term is taken aptly enough 
but still only by analogy, to denote a quite specific kind of emotional response, 
wholly distinct from that of being afraid, though it so far resembles it that the 
analogy of fear may be used to throw light upon its nature” (13). 
 In the same way, Lewis portrays the fear of the Lord as a numinous, 
emotive experience that is related analogically to ordinary fears. The fear of the 
Lord is indeed “what is often called fear, but of a different kind” (The Problem of 
Pain 5). In The Problem of Pain , Lewis points to The Wind in the Willows for an 
example, quoting a part of the story when Rat and Mole approach the god Pan: 
 
“Rat,” he found breath to whisper, shaking. “Are you afraid?”  
“Afraid?” murmured the Rat, his eyes shining with unutterable 
love. “Afraid? of Him? O, never, never! And yet—and yet—O, Mole, I am 
afraid!” (Grahame 7.77; Lewis, The Problem of Pain 6) 
 
Rat is not afraid and yet afraid because he is experiencing a numinous fear, an 
experience akin to but different from regular fear. Similar language can be found 
scattered throughout The Chronicles of Narnia. In The Silver Chair, when Jill first 
encounters Aslan, she is at first—and understandably—afraid because she is in 
front of a lion. But when that lion speaks, her emotional experience takes on a 
numinous quality: “Anyway, she had seen its lips move this time, and the voice 
was not like a man’s. It was deeper, wilder, and stronger; a sort of heavy, golden 
voice. It did not make her any less frightened than she had been before, but it 
made her frightened in rather a different way” (2.22). In The Voyage of the “Dawn 
Treader,” Eustace likewise experiences a strange fear, even though he is in the 
shape of a strong dragon. He says, “[The lion] came nearer and nearer. I was 
terribly afraid of it. You may think that, being a dragon, I could have knocked 
any lion out easily enough. But it wasn’t that kind of fear. I wasn’t afraid of it 
eating me, I was just afraid of it—if you can understand” (7.106 –07). A final 
example can be found in The Horse and His Boy, when Shasta finds himself face-
to-face with the lion: “Shasta was no longer afraid that the Voice belonged to 
something that would eat him, nor that it was the voice of a ghost. But a new 
and different sort of trembling came over him. Yet he felt glad too” (11.165). In 
all these examples, the characters describe their experience a s one of fear, but 
they also distinguish that fear qualitatively from regular fear.  
Brett Roscoe 
104  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 The final example above also suggests how these two fears are 
different, for Shasta experiences a gladness at the same time that he is afraid. 
Similarly, Peter, Susan, and Lucy upon seeing Aslan feel “as glad as anyone can 
who feels afraid, and as afraid as anyone can who feels glad” ( PC 11.153). This 
strange combination of emotions points to another part of the numinous as Otto 
describes it in The Idea of the Holy . Otto argues that there are two sides to a 
numinous experience, the mysterium tremendum et fascinans, or the daunting and 
the fascinating: “The daemonic-divine object may appear to the mind an object 
of horror and dread, but at the same time it is no less something that allures with 
a potent charm, and the creature, who trembles before it, utterly cowed and cast 
down, has always at the same time the impulse to turn to it” (31). We can see 
this paradox at work in The Lion, the Witch and the Wardrobe, when Peter says of 
Aslan, “I’m longing to see him […] even if I do feel frightened when it comes to 
the point” (8.80). Similarly, when Digory and Polly first encounter Aslan in The 
Magician’s Nephew, they experience a combination of fear and desire:  
 
The children could not move. They were not even quite sure that they 
wanted to. The Lion paid no attention to them. Its huge red mouth was 
open, but open in song not in a snarl. It passed by them so close that they 
could have touched its mane. They were terribly afraid it would turn and 
look at them, yet in some queer way they wished it would. (9.116–17) 
 
This mixture of fear and desire or tremendum et fascinans  is also displayed 
somewhat humorously by Hwin, one of the talking horses in The Horse and His 
Boy. When Hwin approaches Aslan, she is “shaking all over” with fear, but she 
approaches nevertheless and says, “Please [...] you’re so beautiful. You may eat 
me if you like. I’d sooner be eaten by you than fed by anyone else” (14.200–01). 
 This paradoxical fear and attraction is one of the most important 
qualities that separates the fear of God from other types of fear. Lewis describes 
the difference between regular fear and fear of God in his essay “Is Theism 
Important?”:  
 
We have in English an exact name for the emotion aroused by the 
Numinous, which Otto, writing in German lacked; we have the word 
Awe—an emotion very like fear, with the important difference that it 
need imply no estimate of danger. When we fear a tiger, we  fear that it 
may kill us: when we fear a ghost —well, we just fear the ghost, not this 
or that mischief which it may do us. The Numinous or Awful is that of which 
we have this, as it were, objectless or disinterested fear—this awe. (189)10 
 
 
10 The example of a tiger and a ghost is repeated in Lewis, The Problem of Pain 5. 
Modelling the Fear of God in C.S. Lewis’s The Chronicles of Narnia 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  105 
This awe is so similar to fear that Lewis repeats the word “fear” at the end of the 
passage, even after having made a distinction between the two terms, but they 
are at the same time significantly different. Base or regular fear, for Lewis, is a 
self-directed emotion that encourages people to look out for their own safety. 
This kind of fear is usually demonstrated by the morally deprived characters in 
the books, characters who encounter Aslan and run away from him to save their 
lives. In Prince Caspian, for example, Aslan, accompanied by Bacchus and the 
dancing Maenads, leads a rowdy procession through a town, and all of the 
townspeople respond with one of two responses to the sight of Aslan: people 
either run away from or join the procession, and their resp onses imply a 
judgement about their moral character. For instance, when they come upon a 
school with an overly strict teacher, one student (Gwendolyn) is drawn to Aslan, 
whereas the teacher and the rest of the students —whom the narrator criticizes 
as “dump y, prim little girls with fat legs” —all run away (14.200). This same 
divisive effect—some fleeing, some approaching —is seen repeatedly as Aslan 
makes his way through the town. 
Returning to The Magician’s Nephew we see a similar contrast between 
the fear of Digory and Polly on the one hand and the fear of the Witch and Uncle 
Andrew on the other: 
 
Though [Aslan’s] soft pads made no noise, you could feel the earth shake 
beneath their weight.  
         The Witch shrieked and ran: in a few moments she was out of sight 
among the trees. Uncle Andrew turned to do likewise, tripped over a 
root, and fell flat on his face in a little brook that ran down to join the 
river. The children could not move. T hey were not even quite sure that 
they wanted to. The Lion paid no attention to them. Its huge red mouth 
was open, but open in song not in a snarl. It passed by them so close that 
they could have touched its mane. They were terribly afraid it would turn 
and look at them, yet in some queer way they wished it would. (9.116–17) 
 
In this passage, both the children and the adults are afraid, but they experience 
this fear differently. The Witch and Uncle Andrew try to run away; the children, 
though frozen with fear, at the same time do not want to run. They even wish 
that the lion wo uld turn his attention to them, demonstrating both the 
tremendum et fascinans of an encounter with the numinous. As the narrator later 
explains, “What you see and hear depends a good deal on where you are 
standing: it also depends on what sort of person yo u are” (10.136). Uncle 
Andrew, because of the “sort of person” he is, does not see the numinous when 
he looks at Aslan; all he sees is a lion who might attack him, and so he tries to 
run away. The children, on the other hand, look at the lion and experienc e a 
similar fear, but also something more than fear.  
Brett Roscoe 
106  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 This strange combination of emotions is aroused by the numen, Otto’s 
term for the source of numinous experience, which in this case is Aslan, who is 
both dreadful and desirable. “People who have not been in Narnia,” the narrator 
explains, “sometimes think that a thing cannot be good and terrible at the same 
time” but that is a mistaken assumption that an experience of Aslan can “cure” 
(LWW 12.126). Aslan is regularly described as both “terrible” and “beautiful”, 
the first term not meaning “bad” but “terrifying” or “dreadful.”11 In fact, Lewis 
frequently uses the word “terrible” in The Chronicles of Narnia  to denote the 
mysterium tremendum  of the numinous, the fear -like emotion evoked by an 
encounter with the spiritual or the divine. And so with a world of images, filled 
with magical beings, talking animals, and a divine lion, Lewis provides his 
readers with imaginative simulations of encounters with God, and these 
simulations evoke the very emotions that young Christians might be told they 
“ought to feel” but cannot feel because they ought to. Stripping the fear of God 
from its “Sund ay school associations,” the books provide affective training, 
arousing fear so that readers can, through the concrete, embodied analogy, 
experience a fear of a different kind.

Edmonton, Canada, where he teaches courses in general and medieval literature. His 
research interests include medieval wisdom literature, cognitive approaches to 
literature, and intersections of literature and theology. 
 
 

  
 
 

  
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  109 
 
 
                  EASTING AT THE THRESHOLD :  
                             EUCHARISTIC EROTICISM AND  
                       HOMONATIONALISM IN DIANE DUANE ’S  
                               THE TALE OF THE FIVE  
 
                                         TAYLOR DRIGGERS  
 
INTRODUCTION 
N HIS ESSAY “ON FAIRY-STORIES” (1947), J.R.R. Tolkien suggests that by re -
arranging and offering variations on elements of the primary world, fantasy 
can reinvigorate our appreciation of everyday things “such as stone, and wood, 
and iron; tree and grass; house and fire; bread and wine” (69). The eucharistic 
applicability suggested by the inclusion of bread and wine in this formulation is 
likely no accident; as a devout Catholic, Tolkien’s theorization of fairy-story, by 
his own acknowledgement, shares a complex relationship with his 
understanding of the Christian story of salvation. Alana M. Vincent goes so far 
as to link fantasy to the Eucharist by way of the Catholic principle of 
sacramentality. While “sacraments, such as the Eucharist […] confer grace 
immediately” (23), within a Christ ian framework literature can be understood 
as sacramental, a facet of everyday life that awakens a sense of the numinous and 
“leaves the soul disposed toward the reception of grace” (24). Fantastic recovery 
in particular carries a sacramental potential to “inspire entirely new dimensions 
of theological understanding” (25) because “the aura of mystery which pervades 
the fantasy world is well situated to illuminate the real world by proxy” (43). 
While fantasy is incapable of serving as a direct stand -in for t he eucharistic 
sacrament per se, a Christian theological reading of fantasy positions it at the 
threshold where the material concerns of everyday life turn towards 
communion with the divine, a communion that remains rooted in fleshly acts 
centered around the fulfilment of bodily needs and desires (eating and drinking) 
even as it gestures toward transcendence. 
Fantasy’s ability to critically reimagine the primary world, and matters 
of the body in the primary world in particular, also enables it to re -vision 
sacramental practices from the margins of gendered and sexual difference, 
uncovering subversive queer alte rnatives to the doctrines that govern them. 1 
After all, if recovery consists in “viewing the Other not as an extension of one’s 
self, but as an entity both outside of and equal to one’s self” (Vincent 47), then 
the sacramental revelation enacted by fantasy can also challenge dominant 
 
1 See Taylor Driggers, Queering Faith in Fantasy Literature. 
I 
Taylor Driggers 
110  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
expressions of theology to commune with, brush up against, and commingle 
with the image of God as embodied by the sexual other often institutionally 
barred from receiving the material and spiritual goods of communion in the first 
place. As a sacramental production, fantasy that takes up this queer theological 
task can dispose theology towards the grace of what Marcella Althaus-Reid has 
described as “God, as a stranger at the gates of Hegemonic Theology, amongst 
loving expressions of relationships at the mar gins of the defined decent and 
proper in Christianity” (The Queer God [Queer] 171).  
Diane Duane’s ongoing fantasy series The Tale of the Five (1979–present) 
explicitly articulates a queer sacramentality, with specific applicability to the 
Eucharist, along these lines. In this essay, I demonstrate how The Tale of the Five 
emphasizes acts of eating and drinking as important components not only of its 
secondary world’s Goddess-based religion, but also of its characters’ pansexual, 
polyamorous relationships. These instances provocatively link religious 
devotion with the pursuit of erotic pleasures and relational formations deemed 
indecent within many primary -world expressions of Christianity. The 
Goddess’s affection for humanity and humanity’s love for the Goddess spill 
over into queer sexual encounters between humans, which in turn are reveale d 
as extensions or permutations of the former, and human characters additionally 
experience personally transformative sexual encounters with the Goddess. Yet 
the series’ adherence to standard conventions of popular fantasy at the time of 
writing, rooted in fantasies of monarchy, race, and nation in a manner that exists 
in uneasy tension with, and raises serious questions about the limits of, its queer 
theological imagination. 
The Tale of the Five is part of a body of fantasy literature explicitly aimed 
at addressing issues of gender and sexuality emerging in the 1970s, 1980s, and 
1990s, alongside works such as Elizabeth A. Lynn’s The Chronicles of Tornor  
(1979–80), Samuel R. Delany’s Nevèrÿon cycle (1979 –87), Ellen Kushner’s 
Riverside novels (1987 –2006), Mercedes Lackey’s The Last Herald -Mage trilogy 
(1989–91), and Laurie J. Marks’s Children of the Triad  series (1989–91). Of these, 
only Duane’s and Kushner’s series are set in worlds in which non-heterosexual 
orientations are the default norm across all areas of society. 2 The Tale of the Five 
is set in a secondary world called the Middle Kingdoms and consists of three 
novels—The Door into Fire (1979), The Door into Shadow (1984), and The Door into 
Sunset (1992)—in addition to the novelette The Levin-Gad (2018) and the novella 
The Landlady (2019), with at least one more spinoff novel, The Librarian, and a 
fourth main series novel, The Door into Starlight, still to be published at the time 
 
2 Marks’s series is a difficult one to categorize, since most of its main characters are intersex 
nonhumans. While nonheterosexual unions between these characters and other species 
exist, the humanoid Walkers are implied to live in a default patriarchal society. 
Feasting at the Threshold: Eucharistic Eroticism and Homonationalism in Duane 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  111 
of writing.3 Despite the series’ fluid and expansive approach to sexuality, as well 
as its unique cosmogony, it has received virtually no scholarly attention from 
either queer or religious studies-informed scholars of fantasy. The religion of the 
Middle Kingdoms, stru ctured as it is around the tripartite goddess motif of 
“Maiden and Mother and Wise Woman” (Duane, The Door into Fire [Fire] 11) and 
animistic magic rituals associated with the earth, draws some notable influences 
from neo-Pagan beliefs and practices. However, these are also intertwined with 
heterodox, but nonetheless clear, variations on Christian doctrines and 
theological concepts, such as the belief that humans “are made in the Goddess’s 
image” ( The Door into Sunset  [Sunset] 70), the concern with a deity who is 
incarnated in human flesh and exists alongside humanity, and its investment in 
motifs of eating, drinking, and transubstantiation that gesture toward 
eucharistic sacrament.  
In the latter case, the sharing of food and drink becomes a sacramental 
act that draws together, and blurs boundaries between, religious devotion; filial 
and platonic love; erotic desire; sexual pleasure; and bodily transformations, 
expansions, and multipl ications in a manner that prefigures Linn Marie 
Tonstad’s queer reading of the Eucharist and its relationship to the Body of 
Christ. In Tonstad’s formulation, the multiplication of the body of Christ to co -
inhere in bread and wine (and therefore the labor of those who produce them) 
and its subsequent distribution among the bodies of believers creates a complex 
and potent image of loving coinhabitance within the church as the Body of 
Christ that transgresses bodily, identitarian, geographical, and institutio nal 
boundaries without assimilation into an undifferentiated sameness. To analyze 
The Tale of the Five in terms of the Eucharist and the body of Christ is by no means 
to suggest that a Christian reading is the only available one of its religious 
imaginary; in fact, its syncretic blend of Christian and feminist neo -Pagan 
influences makes such a claim impossible. Rather, eucharistic theology is merely 
one area of many —though a significant one —in which the series enters into 
conversation with primary-world religious observation. 
And yet, as much as its worldbuilding and narrative evocatively 
suggest this queer portrait of religious life, The Tale of the Five also betrays it to 
some extent. The fact that a major narrative arc of the series involves restoring 
one of the protagonists, Freelorn, as the rightful hereditary monarch of the 
nation of Arlen, coupled with the mystical import attributed to noble bloodlines 
and titles  throughout, jars awkwardly with and in some instances alters the 
meaning of its otherwise radical eucharistic theology. While these narrative 
 
3 Other works by Diane Duane set in the Middle Kingdoms include the novelettes Parting 
Gifts (1981), Lior and the Sea (1985), and The Span (1999), but as they do not concern any of 
the principal characters of The Tale of the Five, they are beyond the scope of this study. 
Taylor Driggers 
112  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
attributes are far from unique in the broader context of twentieth -century 
fantasy more generally, these aspects of the texts become more disconcerting 
when, beginning with The Door into Shadow, a significant portion of the narrative 
conflict involves protecting the Middle Kingdoms from an othered race that 
threatens their stability. This note of dissonance provokes consideration of the 
need for fantasy’s queer theological imaginaries to take care that they challenge 
rather than merely extend the logic of western Christian triumphalism. 
 
GOING (IN)TO TOWN: SEXUAL, MATERIAL, AND SPIRITUAL GOODS OF THE MIDDLE 
KINGDOMS 
Chapter 6 of The Door into Sunset  opens with an epigraph from a 
fictitious text telling the tale of a woman who roams the earth seeking the 
Goddess, but is unable to find her. Returning home, dejected, to an empty larder, 
she ventures into the Darthis city marketplace, where she encounters 
 
a Woman wearing that Cloak which is the night sky, and with a basket 
over her arm, and bread in it and wine. And the woman looked at the 
Goddess in amazement, and the Goddess sighed and smiled, and said to 
her, “It’s such a nuisance, but sometimes you just have to go into town.” 
And She kissed the woman, and was gone . . .  (Sunset 124) 
 
This scene communicates much about the eucharistic and incarnational theology 
that characterizes the Middle Kingdoms’ cosmogony. Both evoking and 
subverting the biblical account in which the prophet Elijah encounters God not 
in the great wind or the earthquake or the fire, but in “a sound of sheer silence” 
(1 Kings 19:12, NRSVUE), the Goddess is found not in the remote corners of the 
earth but among ordinary women in the bustling marketplace. The eucharistic 
motif of bread and wine appears here both to unde rscore the incarnate nature 
of the Goddess’s existence —since in the eucharistic sacrament bread and wine 
correspond to the body and blood of the incarnate Christ —and to signify the 
material goods that they literally are. As a setting for this exchange, the  market 
is particularly laden with meaning for queer theology. Althaus -Reid’s Marxian 
analysis of traditional theology, for instance, observes that “[e]conomic desires 
walk hand in hand with erotic desires and theological needs” (Indecent Theology 
[Indecent] 166) and that 
 
theology deals with a market of souls and the definition of their needs: 
sacraments, prayers, ritual ordinances and an allegiance to beliefs which 
regulate people’s lives, in order to effectively distribute the spiritual 
goods of redemption or forgiveness or even eternal life amongst them, 
the spiritual clientele. (166–67) 
Feasting at the Threshold: Eucharistic Eroticism and Homonationalism in Duane 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  113 
As in the Eucharist, material and spiritual goods, here distributed directly by 
the Goddess herself, appear simultaneously in bread and wine at the moment of 
fulfilment for the unnamed woman’s material needs and theological desires. 
And yet, the Goddess’s playful, almost camp remarks to the woman (it is not at 
all difficult to imagine this line being casually tossed off in a drag performance) 
avoid conflating the Goddess with the marketplace itself. The sly suggestion that 
“[i]t’s such a nuisance” to go int o town but that “sometimes you just have to” 
positions the Goddess as immanent within the everyday business of economy 
and its material goods, but lovingly transcending any regulatory system 
structuring it (although, as I demonstrate later, this becomes complicated when 
considering the political structures that govern the Middle Kingdoms as a 
whole). 
The encounter with the Goddess in the marketplace is instructive in 
tracing the links between worship of the Goddess, the shared consumption of 
food and drink, and the exchange of queer sexual pleasures. I argue that The Tale 
of the Five is permeated with instances of transubstantiation and transfiguration 
in which relations and encounters slide freely between these categories or 
inhabit more than one simultaneously. The series is already notable from a queer 
standpoint thanks to the open and dynamic nature of its sexual landscape. While 
inhabitants of the Middle Kingdoms are subject to a divine obligation to have at 
least one child in their lifetime —similar to, yet distinct from, the injunction to 
“[b]e fruitful and multiply” in the biblical creation narrat ive (Gen . 1:28, 
NRSVUE)—their sexual lives otherwise are open and varied, with most 
characters taking on multiple partners of all genders. The central pairing in the 
series initially appears to be a gay romance between two of the central 
protagonists, Herewiss and Freelorn, but these characters’ romantic and sexual 
lives branch out over the course of the texts to encompass multiple relationships 
of varying types and durations with characters of multiple genders. These 
entanglements also continually reshape the dynamic between these two men 
even as they remain committed to one another. Already we can see here a fluid 
and shifting sexual landscape that markedly contrasts with what Althaus -Reid 
analyses as the regulatory frame of sexual “decency” that dictates who is worthy 
of receiving material and spiritual goods within Christian institutions ( Indecent 
167–68). 
Yet even beyond this, The Tale of the Five ’s world and narrative are 
marked by constant bodily transgressions and surprises: a human and dragon 
can occupy the same body and become lovers, human subjectivities can expand 
to inhabit the tectonic plates beneath a mountain range, and a one -night stand 
can bring one face-to-face with the divine. At the time when the series’ narrative 
takes place, the world is at a critical juncture in the constant struggle between 
the Goddess who creates and loves, and Her falle n offspring the Shadow who 
Taylor Driggers 
114  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
devours and destroys, and as such, the rules and norms that govern the world 
are shifting even further. Herewiss is the first man in centuries to wield the 
mystical power of the Blue Fire usually reserved for women, and one of his 
sexual partners is a fire  elemental named Sunspark, for whom sexuality and 
romantic love should be impossible and whose physical appearance shifts 
fluidly between human and nonhuman forms as well as between genders. 
Throughout the series, human bodies and subjectivities expand, ch ange shape, 
and multiply through various magical and divine means to exceed and 
destabilize the bounds of self-identity. Always weaving Her way through these 
experiences of queer excess and impossibility is the Goddess, who plays an 
active role in the sexual lives of all Her creations.  
 
A WONDERFUL EXCHANGE: EUCHARISTIC EROTICISM 
The Door into Fire  establishes that “[o]nce in a lifetime, a person will 
know the joy of being held in the Goddess’s arms,” although “She comes as just 
another person, with human quirks and wrinkles; sometimes She comes in the 
form of someone you know—perhaps even your own loved” (Fire 45–46). Later 
in that novel, Herewiss, Freelorn, the sorceress Segnbora, and the rest of their 
travelling companions happen upon an inn in the wilderness, where the woman 
who keeps the inn agrees to let them stay the night in  exchange for sex in place 
of money, an apparently commonplace occurrence within the Middle 
Kingdoms’ sexual economy. After sitting down to a sumptuous meal, the 
companions depart to their respective rooms, with Segnbora chosen to go to bed 
with the innkeeper per their agreement. However, the innkeeper surprises each 
of the others in turn by appearing to all of them simultaneously at Moonrise, 
where they each eventually recognize her as the Goddess, and she spends the 
night with them. 
As in the encounter with the Goddess in the marketplace, here the 
Goddess first appears in the midst of everyday exchanges before exceeding 
them in the abundance of the material, sexual, and spiritual gratification She 
bears. The initial sexual arrangement  She negotiates with Segnbora as the 
innkeeper becomes multiplied sevenfold among the company and leaves them 
fulfilled far beyond any normal sexual encounter. Reminiscing about the 
experience afterward, Segnbora contrasts it with ordinary sex in which, “give as 
you will, there’s only so much that can be shared in one evening, or one day, 
before the body gives out, gets sore, gets tired,” whereas the Goddess’s 
attentions satisfied “all of me, all the depths, the corners, the little fantasies I 
never dared” (Fire 160). The bounty of the Goddess’s table at the inn and the rest 
and shelter She provides for the travelers gives way to a fulfilment of sexual 
needs beyond the usual bounds of possibility within human relations, which in 
turn leaves the company spiritually enriched and edified for their journey. 
Feasting at the Threshold: Eucharistic Eroticism and Homonationalism in Duane 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  115 
The bountiful multiplication and loving commingling of divine and 
human bodies in this exchange, and its mediation through the hospitality of the 
meal table, bears a strong resemblance to Tonstad’s queering of the eucharistic 
feast. Rather than the straigh tforward transmutation that characterizes 
traditional readings of transubstantiation, Tonstad proposes an additive model 
that she refers to as “impanation” to make sense of the presence of the body of 
Christ within the Eucharist (240). In this model, “the Word assumes a bread 
nature in addition to a human nature” that is freely distributed among, and 
comes to dwell within the bodies of, all who take part in the feast (240). This 
additive understanding of the Eucharist challenges us to see the distribution o f 
spiritual goods as intimately bound up with the free sharing of material goods, 
since “the multiplication of the breaded Christs means enough Christ for all” 
(242), and signals the form of good relations that the grace of Christ initiates 
among humanity. As Tonstad puts it, 
 
our participation in (taking part in) Christ brings us together in face -to-
face relations that show us to each other more clearly —or at least have 
the potential to do so, for better and worse. The sufficiency of the 
eucharist, and the intensified relations hips among humans that it 
symbolizes and makes possible, suggests reconfiguration of the 
conditions of bodied, material existence themselves. (242) 
 
Christian theology tends not to explicitly imagine God as pursuing sexual 
relationships with humanity in the same way that the Goddess does in The Tale 
of the Five , and certainly Tonstad’s reading of the Eucharist makes no literal 
claims to this effect either. Yet Tonstad, following Althaus -Reid, is heavily 
invested in showing how Christianity’s theological metaphors and practices are 
always already sexual in nature ; their inherent sexuality simply tends to be 
invisible within a society that accepts patriar chal and heterosexual views of 
reality as natural. The Eucharist is no exception. Althaus -Reid, for instance, 
identifies that in the Catholicism of her youth, the traditional pose in which one 
receives the Eucharist involves kneeling at the altar, which pl aces the 
communicant in proximity to the priest’s penis ( Queer 11). A whole matrix of 
power relations is thus implied by this gesture, linking the Catholic Church’s 
enforcement of patriarchal and heterosexual gender relations to the 
innumerable instances of sexual abuse within the Church that have come to light 
in the past few decades, as well as the Church’s historical role in colonial 
exercises of domination and conquest. As Althaus -Reid puts it, “hierarchical 
church models [are] constructed from the axis of priests’ penises” (12). Similarly, 
at stake for Tonstad is finding an alternative to patriarchal redemption models 
in which the church is positioned as the submissive bride to Christ the 
penetrative bridegroom. The prevailing image in her eucharistic t heology is 
Taylor Driggers 
116  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
instead one of loving coinhabitance, where “[t]he body’s limits do not disappear, 
but spatial location becomes coinhabitable, and the colocality of different 
bodies—presence in the same place at the same time —transforms the nature of 
relationality and community” (239). Impanation thus envisions a form of loving 
relation in which communicants commingle with God and one another without 
assimilation or loss of particularity. 
The additive logic of impanation materializes in Duane’s series in the 
way the Goddess multiplies herself to initiate pleasurable relations with the 
human characters; in the way that these encounters in turn intensify the close 
bonds that the protagonists share while preserving the distinctions between 
them; and in the way that food and drink serve as reminders of this love and 
intimacy. The Door into Sunset expands on this notion in its conclusion  with a 
seven-way wedding between Herewiss, Freelorn, Segnbo ra, the fire elemental 
Sunspark, the resurrected dragon Hasai, Queen Eftgan of Darthen, and her 
husband Wyn, in which they all vow “to do right by one another, as the Goddess 
would were She marrying in (which of course She was)” before partaking of 
sacral wine (Sunset 377). While this latter instance is the only depiction in the 
series of an official religious sacrament in which the act of drinking signifies a 
good relation with the Goddess as among and within the loving relationships of 
Her creation, insta nces of eating and drinking elsewhere in the series serve as 
articulations of this relationship that exist on the sacramental threshold between 
everyday life and religious observance. 
 
LOVERS AND THE LOVING-CUP: THRESHOLD THEOLOGY 
Herewiss’s own encounter with the Goddess at the inn is mediated by 
a shared cup of Brightwood white, a wine significant to Herewiss’s homeland. 
Later, under the influence of the Soulflight drug that the Goddess gives him to 
help unlock his Fire, Herewiss perceives within the wine 
 
the cries of ecstatic agony uttered by the vines as their burden was ripped 
from them, and […] the silver touch of rain. He caught the langorous 
thoughts of one of the young girls who had helped to press out the 
vintage, and he felt how it had been for her , the night before, under the 
pomegranate trees with her lover. […] The tangled, vivid selfhoods of 
bees and vintners and young girls flowed down his throat like cinnamon 
fire, and left an aftertaste like a summer dawn. (Fire 252) 
 
For Herewiss, the simple act of drinking a cup of Brightwood white is revealed 
to be a sacramental act in which he comes into profound contact with other lives, 
including nonhuman ones. This revelation embodies what Tolkien identifies as 
the sacramental role of recovery in fantasy, insofar as it involves “seeing things 
[…] as things apart from ourselves” (67). Though it occurs via fantastical means, 
Feasting at the Threshold: Eucharistic Eroticism and Homonationalism in Duane 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  117 
the realization is ultimately a mundane one regarding the interconnectedness of 
all life. The sacredness of wine even in everyday contexts is further underscored 
by the events of The Levin -Gad, in which Liri, the bouncer at a tavern, saves 
Herewiss from the Shadow by smashing a bottle of wine, which, “flaming like 
sunlight made molten, splashed and sprayed everywhere. […] In the flare of 
liquid light the darkness’s limbs flailed out and it went  down like so many 
dropped rocks, sprawling crooked and limp on the slaty floor” (7.38). 
Drinking wine, via the ceremonial object of the lovers’ -cup, is also the 
means by which lovers in the Middle Kingdoms pay homage to their love for 
each other and the Goddess made manifest in and through that love. In The Door 
into Fire, the cup’s role in daily life is established as follows: 
 
It was the Goddess’s cup, the cup poured for Her at each meal to remind 
those who ate that all set before them was, one way or another, the 
product of Her love—as were the people with whom they ate. When the 
meal was done, if there were lovers there, the y oungest of them would 
drain the cup together in Her name. If one was alone, one said the 
Blessing for the Sundered and drank it in his own name and the name of 
his lover, wherever that one might be. (Fire 216) 
 
The lovers’-cup features in most scenes featuring Herewiss and Freelorn and, 
eventually, their five other spouses, serving as a reminder to the reader as well 
as the characters themselves of the Goddess’s presence within and mediation 
through the love betw een them. In both name and function, it invites 
comparison to the loving-cup used at love-feasts within the Methodist Christian 
tradition. Both theologically coextensive with and liturgically distinct from the 
official sacrament of the eucharistic feast, t he love -feast can be read as an 
example of what Rachel Muers and Rhiannon Grant have termed “threshold 
practices”: that is, “practices that are, theoretically and institutionally, neither 
church nor non-church” (47). The use of “threshold” here implies the liminality 
of such practices —the point at which worship crosses over into the work of 
everyday life, and vice-versa—but also, and importantly for an account of quasi-
eucharistic practices, etymologically evokes the threshing -floor, a space where 
the theol ogical values of a community are “threshed out” like wheat and 
constituted via everyday encounters (47). 
Instituted by Methodism’s founders, Charles and John Wesley, in the 
eighteenth century, love -feasts were thought at the time to be a revival of the 
early church practice of the Agape, which Frank Baker characterizes as “a 
common meal symbolizing the union of Christians with their Lord and with 
each other” (62). Central to the Methodist reimagining of this practice was the 
loving-cup, “a two-handled mug passed round from hand to hand” from which 
participants share a drink of what initially would have been wi ne, and later, as 
Taylor Driggers 
118  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
the practice has evolved, water or tea (Baker 15). Although the love -feast is not 
a substitute for the Eucharist, it shares many of its theological concerns: from the 
standpoint of bodily theology, the passing of the loving -cup provokes a 
meditation on wha t it means to share in the Body of Christ as made up of a 
gathering of bodies that exist in relation to each other. As a quasi-liturgical object 
designed for the sharing of drink, the loving-cup mediates profoundly intimate 
physical contact that crosses bodily boundaries between participants as it passes 
between lips, signifying participants’ unity in partaking of the meal while 
maintaining the difference that enables their loving interrelation. 
In transforming the loving -cup into the lovers’ -cup, Duane explicitly 
eroticizes this quasi -liturgical object and by extension names desire, in all its 
unpredictability and all its forms, both as a central part of religious observance 
and as a crucial site  at which characters in the series turn from theological 
reflection to engagement with each other in the world. 4 Sitting athwart the line 
between religious observance and everyday life, the cup is a ritual means of 
acknowledging the presence of the Goddess in the hospitality of the meal table, 
the community and common labor of all those present, and the intimacy of those 
joined in erotic and romantic love. It also represents, for the romantically 
unattached, a messianic hope for the Goddess’s love expressed as erotic longing, 
as they “call on the Loved Who Will Be to await [their] coming” (Fire 216) when 
they drink of the cup. Here, not only are queer desire and sexualities named as 
revelatory incarnations of the Goddess’s love, but hospitality itself is reframed 
as a complex and multi-directional flow of love, desire, and care-oriented labor. 
Importantly, the lovers’ -cup’s status as a threshold object, both liturgical and 
mundane in function, would seem to imply that the eucharistic theology of The 
Tale of the Five  is liberated from the forces that normally regulate the flow of 
spiritual, material, and sexual goods in the primary world. 
 
ROYAL BINDINGS: THEOLOGICAL HOMONATIONALISM 
The queer utopianism and radical eucharistic theology potently 
suggested by The Tale of the Five ’s worldbuilding make for strange bedfellows 
with its narrative, which is in large part concerned with restoring Freelorn to his 
hereditary position as King of Arlen. Nearly every principal character in the 
series claims noble lineage to an extent that jar s with the non -hierarchical 
communion suggested by the series’ eucharistic motifs; most egregiously, the 
entire plot of The Landlady concerns Segnbora’s quest to restore her family name 
 
4 As Baker’s historical account of the love -feast relates, the potentially erotic dimensions 
suggested by the feast’s name and the intimate gesture of passing the loving -cup did not 
escape the notice of Methodism’s early detractors, and accusations of sexua l excess were 
common in anti-Methodist propaganda (32–33). 
Feasting at the Threshold: Eucharistic Eroticism and Homonationalism in Duane 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  119 
and maintain her land holdings. The restoration of a rightful monarch, marking 
a reign of peace and a happy conclusion to the narrative, is a common feature 
within post-Tolkien fantasy literature of the twentieth century, taking its cues 
both from the crow ning of Aragorn in The Lord of the Rings  and the more 
generalized feudal setting of much fantasy written in this vein. Queer and 
feminist fantasy fiction of the time had varied approaches to the question of 
monarchy. Lackey’s Herald-Mage trilogy centers on  a gay man living in a 
heteronormative patriarchy, but, like Duane’s series, takes its monarchical 
setting at face value, with the world of Valdemar ruled by a benevolent king. 
Even the resolutely anarchist Ursula K. Le Guin concludes The Farthest Shore  
(1972), the third volume in her Earthsea series, with the good king Lebannen’s 
coronation, although its sequel Tehanu (1990), published two years prior to The 
Door into Sunset, casts significant doubt on whether this has any bearing on the 
lives of everyday people, especially women, in Earthsea. (Le Guin would later 
remark that “too many modern fantasies are intolerably trivial and complacent 
in their half-baked feudalism” [316]). Delany’s Nevèrÿon cycle and Marks’s Triad 
novels are notable outliers, with the former rigorously interrogating its Robert 
E. Howard -esque setting with realpolitik and a frank treatment of 
homosexuality and sex work, and the latter set in a world that is politically very 
far removed from any primary-world historical analogue. Meanwhile, Elizabeth 
A. Lynn’s Watchtower (1979), published the same year as The Door into Fire, slyly 
subverts its own narrative of feudal conflict as part of its critical contention with 
heteropatriarchal violence; here, the ostensible rightful heir refuses the throne 
and departs to live in the anarchic commune of Vanima, with radical social 
change also to come in the realm of Tornor. To interrogate the collusion between 
religion and monarchy in The Tale of the Five is therefore not to single the series 
out rela tive to the rest of the genre, but rather to examine its performative 
function—in other words, what it means and does —as part of a theological 
imaginary. 
Leaving aside the incoherence of a world as sexually open as the 
Middle Kingdoms —where lineage, inheritance, and familial structure should 
therefore be far messier matters than depicted —being ruled by hereditary 
monarchies and preoccupied with noble lineag es, it is especially strange that 
this system of governance appears to be divinely sanctioned. Not only is 
Freelorn personally invested in winning back the throne from his usurping half-
brother, Cillmod; his ascent is crucial for maintaining the magical Ro yal 
Bindings forged from the alliance between Darthen and Arlen that keep the 
Shadow at bay. In The Door into Shadow, the stakes of installing Freelorn as King 
become apparent when the Bindings come under direct threat; the text makes 
clear that “[t]he end of the Royal Bindings was unthinkable. Such a calamity 
would turn the Shadow loose in the Kingdoms as It hadn’t bee n loose in 
Taylor Driggers 
120  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
centuries” (The Door into Shadow  [Shadow] 147). The Goddess’s struggle is thus 
yoked to Freelorn’s quest for the throne. An Arlene commoner remarks to 
Freelorn, travelling through the Arlene countryside in disguise, that “the young 
king is an instrument of the Goddess, a tool of Hers,” and that “[s]uch a one’s to 
be pitied, poor thing” ( Sunset 163). Similarly, when advising Freelorn on the 
responsibilities of rule, Eftgan declares that “even if I do die, the Goddess who 
gave me this responsibility will hold  me responsible still” (88). That hereditary 
monarchs are positioned by the narrative as specially ordained instruments of 
the Goddess’s will, and that moreover they are to be pitied for this 
responsibility, is an odd recapitulation of the divine right of kings, and a rather 
galling reframing of the real power monarchs wield. Where the eucharistic 
threshold practices commonplace in the Middle Kingdoms hint at a dissolution 
of institutional forces that regulate the distribution of spiritual, material, and 
sexual goods, monarchy rears its head as yet another regulating body in the 
market of souls. 
It would be tempting to read this facet of the series as merely in conflict 
with its otherwise egalitarian queer worldbuilding and theological imagination. 
Yet I contend that when The Tale of the Five’s fascination with monarchic rule is 
examined alongside its eucharistic imaginary via Jasbir K. Puar’s critique of 
homonationalism, a different and more complicated picture emerges. As 
theorized by Puar, homonationalism is the process by which “[a]spects of 
homosexuality have come within the purview of normati ve patriotism, 
incorporating aspects of queer subjectivity into the body of the normalized 
nation” (46), often to legitimize military and imperial incursions into non -
western territories by western states. We can see this process literalized in the 
way that the queer eucharistic motif of impanation extends to the ritual involved 
in maintaining the Royal Bindings. As Freelorn and Eftgan spill their blood upon 
the earth and declare themselves embodiments of the lands of Arlen and 
Darthen, respectively, Segnbora hears their chanting as “the Goddess speaking 
to Herself: one Lover speaking to the Other, in solemn celebration of Their 
eternal relationship” ( Shadow 304). Here, Freelorn and Eftgan’s  blood also 
becomes the land while they in turn also become the Goddess speaking back to 
the land and to Herself, the commingling of bodies that accompanies 
communion with Her here extending to the national body. This motif continues 
in The Door into Sunset  when Freelorn increasingly perceives a mystical 
connection between himself and the landscape of Arlen, leading to an 
understanding of kingship as “[t]he sense that your fleshly body and that earthy 
body were the same, and that earthy body and the Goddess ’s were the same: 
that you were Her lover […] and the land’s lover too” ( Sunset 177–78). What 
seems to materialize here is a form of erotic religious nationalism, where erotic 
desire and religious devotion translate into erotic fidelity to the nation-state. 
Feasting at the Threshold: Eucharistic Eroticism and Homonationalism in Duane 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  121 
  This sense of religious homonationalism becomes more disquieting 
when it is directed against the othered race of the Ladha, who are more 
commonly referred to as “Reavers” throughout the series. The Ladha are first 
properly introduced in The Door into Shadow as a faceless horde enlisted by the 
Shadow to pillage the lands of the Middle Kingdoms. They are a curious 
construction of the text in that, while they do not map cleanly onto Ebony 
Elizabeth Thomas’s theorization of the “Dark Other,” the racialized mo nster 
inhabiting the margins of western fantasy (19), they share some points of 
commonality. Descriptions of them tend to foreground their difference and their 
seeming primitivism; they are a nomadic, tribal people whose “language was 
utterly different fro m any spoken in the Kingdoms” ( Shadow 132), and who 
“wore no trews or breeches, but [a] strange long undivided shin -length 
garment,” wielding “crude curved swords in rude sheaths” (86). These would 
seem to hearken to a racialized evocation of non-western cultures in contrast to 
the Middle Kingdoms’ medieval European -inflected one, similar to the 
depictions of barbarian cultures that Helen Young critiques in Robert E. 
Howard’s Conan series (see Young 26 –28), yet The Door into Sunset 
conspicuously takes pains to describe their “fair hair” ( Sunset 87) and “pale” 
faces (90).5 They are objects of fear and loathing, initially appearing as a threat 
to the stability of the Middle Kingdoms’ queer utopia; we are told that 
“[h]istorically, there had never been much use in talking to Reavers” and that 
their constant raids on the farms  and wheat fields of the Kingdoms mean that 
“[i]t was rare to know anyone from the south […] who did not hate them with 
the same resigned and impersonal hatred one usually resigned for plague or 
root-wilt” (86–87).  
Following the racial counter -reading strategy proposed by Thomas, 
“[reading] literary and cultural texts from the perspective of the monster” (22), 
we may ask how the Reavers figure into the sacred banquet of the Goddess, 
whether the goods She proffers als o extend to these strangers at the gates and 
whether the secondary world of the texts can in turn recognize the Goddess’s 
grace within and among them fully, and not just fleetingly. The series’ move to 
rehabilitate these characters beginning in The Door in to Sunset prolongs rather 
than resolves these questions. A diplomatic meeting with a Ladha chieftain 
 
5 On this point, however, it bears mentioning that, as Young states, “[w]hiteness […] is not 
just the state of bodily being dependent on phenotypical features, but also encompasses 
culture” (10). Likewise, Puar conceives of racialization “as a figure for sp ecific social 
formations and processes that are not necessarily or only tied to what has been historically 
theorized as ‘race’” (xii). Thus, the Ladha can still be read as racialized figures even as 
their phenotypical descriptions would seem to distance th em from Thomas’s conception 
of Dark Otherness. 
Taylor Driggers 
122  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
reveals to Freelorn that the Ladha raids were due to hunger; he demands of 
Freelorn, 
 
what gives you right to keep all the good for yourselves, to shut us out 
for ever? Our beasts have little, our children die; here the grass is green 
and the sun is warm, so why must we die and you live? (Sunset 92) 
 
That the Ladha are kept in poverty and starvation while, in the rest of the series, 
expressions of the Goddess’s love abound with images of consumption in 
abundance and plenty is conspicuous. While this encounter does result in 
Freelorn granting the Ladha a more fertile plot of land within Arlen’s borders to 
settle, this is done only in exchange for their tacit support of his bid for the 
Arlene crown. The text does not linger on this exchange much further, and when 
it does come up, it is in terms that prese rve the presumed innocence of the 
Kingdoms, their rulers, and their inhabitants. When a party of Ladha turn up to 
the royal wedding at the novel’s conclusion, Freelorn chalks up their past 
cultural and racial animosities to the fact that “we didn’t know any better” (378).  
Such a pat dismissal of past injustices, coupled with the fact that this 
new arrangement positions Arlen as the paternalistic benefactor instructing the 
primitive Ladha in proper agricultural techniques, preserves hierarchies of 
power between the two cultu res. In this way it echoes the self -congratulatory 
message of inclusion that Tonstad identifies within many nonhomophobic 
liberal Christian institutions, one that does not humble itself before the grace of 
God as the stranger at the gates but instead seeks  “to be left as the good in our 
(own) goodness, as those mediating the goods we already enjoy to those we 
identify as others” (270). With The Librarian promising to center around Freelorn 
embarking on a quest with a Ladha ally, the full story of the Middle Kingdoms’ 
relationship with the Ladha is yet to be told. Yet the one that already exists tells 
of a queer theological community unwilling or unable to part with a sense of its 
own supremacy and privileged access to the divine, or with the national bodies 
that regulate that access. 
 
CONCLUSIONS 
In speaking to the queer potentiality latent within all fantasy, Jes Battis 
acknowledges that fantasy “can also merely recapitulate homophobic and 
patriarchal tenets” (261). Yet grappling with queer fantasy’s capacity to 
reproduce homonationalist conceptions of queerness forces us to contend with 
the further possibility that any given text —much like any given queerness —
may contain a mixture of radical and reactionary elements that demand to be 
read in the context of each other. The Tale of the Five  articulates a eucharistic 
theology that radically re -maps the relationship between the goods of queer 
Feasting at the Threshold: Eucharistic Eroticism and Homonationalism in Duane 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  123 
sexual pleasure, divine communion, and the sharing of food and drink. In this 
imaginary, divine, human, and nonhuman bodies coincide with each other in a 
loving coinhabitance at the threshold of religious observance and everyday life 
that leaves all fulfil led beyond imagining. Yet the fluidity of bodily and 
identitarian borders suggested by this figuration fails to translate into a 
dissolution of national, cultural, and racial ones. Instead, both social status and 
national superiority are essentialized via, ironically, the same fantastical devices 
through which the series’ queer religious imaginary is conceived. Read from the 
perspective of queer Christian theology, this homonationalist fantasy suggests 
the danger of queer expressions of Christianity that do  not sufficiently unsettle 
the self-identity of the church and the histories of white Christian supremacy, 
colonialism, empire, and class oppression with which both the church and 
theological conceptions of gender and sexuality are unavoidably bound up.  
By contrast, Tonstad argues for a queer theology in which “[t]he 
failure, rather than the success, of the church is the means of its symbolic 
pointing toward the body of Christ that lies outside the walls in whomever the 
church understands at its others” (271). That the theology of the Middle 
Kingdoms occupies a space of bodily chaos that can break primary-world codes 
of gender, desire, and identity, but not those of monarchy and nation, indicates 
that fantasy’s queer theological potential exists in fraught relation to what 
Young has identified as the “habits of Whiteness” and troubled histories  of 
racialization in the genre (Young 10). With this in mind, queer theologians may 
approach fantasy both as an object of critique and as the grounds on which to 
continue to seek to tell a different story about Christianity: one in which the 
distribution of the goods of the body of Christ is not the sole domain of kings 
and nobles; in which the church bears witness to its failure and humbles itself in 
the face of its others; in which stewardship of the goods of creation is articulated 
in terms other than blood and soil.
