TAYLOR DRIGGERS holds a PhD in English Literature from the University of 
Glasgow. He is an independent scholar currently researching how fantasy characters 
model reading and archiving practices that can inform queer history -making and 
kinship-building in our world. Taylor  is the recipient of a 2024 Le Guin Feminist 
Science Fiction Fellowship, and his debut monograph, Queering Faith in Fantasy 
Literature (Bloomsbury Academic, 2022), was a finalist for the 2023 Mythopoeic 
Scholarship Award in Myth and Fantasy Studies. 
 
 
 
 
 
  

 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  125 
 
 
                     ANDEMIC MYTHMAKING :  
                           COVID -19  AND MYTHOLOGICAL  
                           SYMBOLISM IN INDIAN FOLK ART
 
                                 ISHANI ANWESHA JOSHI AND  
                                  SATHYARAJ VENKATESAN  
 
INTRODUCTION 
HE USE OF MYTHOLOGY IN ART DATES BACK  to ancient civilizations, where 
myths served as cultural reservoirs that preserved collective beliefs, values, 
and archetypes. In the context of COVID -19, artists have drawn upon these 
mythological traditions and archetypes to tackle multifaceted issues arising 
from the pandemic. Ancient myths that resonate with contemporary 
circumstances and express societal anxie ties and resilience during the COVID -
19 pandemic contribute “to the collective grand narrative on contagion”  
(Venkatesan). The archetypes embedd ed in mythologies offer a universal 
language that transcends specific cultural contexts, which allows artists to evoke 
deep-seated—and often subconscious —connections with their audience. By 
employing universal themes in mythological stories, artists tap in to the 
collective unconscious to craft narratives that speak to the common humanity 
within us all.  
 Incorporating folk art traditions, with their deeply rooted cultural 
symbols and narratives, within the corpus of COVID art enriches the visual 
language of the pandemic by lending the themes a renewed depth and 
significance while also placing such artistic creations on a broader civilizational 
canvas. COVID folk art forms a part of the larger corpus of ResiliArt, a term 
borrowed from a “UNESCO initiative of the same name which addresses the 
concerns of artists and arts organizations impacted by the pandemic” (The Marg 
Foundation). Through a comprehensive analysis of Durga Bai Vyam’s “ Samura 
Manthan” and Rupsona and Bahadur Chitrakar’s “ Corona Rakshasa,” this article 
aims to investigate how Indian mythological stories in folk art have fused with 
pandemic-related themes to create to a new visual register during COVID -19 
pandemic. By analyzing the interplay of mythological motifs and contemporary 
themes, the article attempts to unravel the levels of meaning, symbolism, and 
emotion that these artists have introduced in their works. 
Simultaneously, by infusing folk art with modern themes, folk artists 
have created a vernacular modernism by blending the traditional and the 
modern, hence, reflecting distinctive regional identities while engaging with 
global modernity. A term borrowed fr om an edited volume on architecture 
T 
Ishani Anwesha Joshi and Sathyaraj Venkatesan 
126  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
titled “Vernacular Modernism: Heimat, Globalization, and the Built 
Environment,” vernacular modernism advocates that modern architecture, 
instead of eradicating local traditions and styles, integrates them into its design 
and creates a unique amalgamation of the regional and the global (Umbach and 
Hüppauf). The essence of such a juxtaposition is seen in various disciplines, and 
in the context of this article, in folk art such as Vyam’s “Samudra Manthan” (2021) 
and Rupsona and Bahadur Chitrakar’s “ Corona Rakshasa” (2021). Incorporating 
folk art traditions, with their deeply rooted cultural symbols and narratives, 
within the corpus of COVID art enriches the visual language of the pandemic, 
lending the themes a renewed sense of depth and significance. In doing so, 
contemporary events acquire fresh forms of expression, while folk art is 
revitalized, becoming more relevant and attuned to the present. 
 
INDIAN FOLK ART DURING THE COVID-19 PANDEMIC 
Folk art refers to visual arts made in the context of folk culture, which 
refers to “products and practices of relatively homogeneous and isolated small-
scale social groups living in rural locations” often associated with “tradition, 
historical continuity, sense of place, and belonging” (Revill). By this definition, 
folk art encompasses a wide range of creative expressions, including folk 
painting, sculpture, music, dance, and more. Unlike traditional painting and 
academic art, typically taught in formal ar t schools and dedicated university 
departments worldwide, folk art is primarily learned through apprenticeships 
with local folk artists, who may or may not hold a mainstream art degree. 
Rooted in tradition, historical continuity, sense of place, and cultur al practices 
of communities, folk art is often passed down through generations and reflect 
the values and identities of the people practicing it. Employing natural materials 
such as wood, straw, clay, and others, folk art utilizes traditional art forms and  
motifs to depict stories and characters ranging from mythological gods and 
heroes to nature, and in recent times, even the modern lifestyle.  
India has several folk art traditions, especially folk painting traditions, 
that are unique to specific regions of the country. For instance, Madhubani 
paintings (also known as Mithila paintings), named after the eponymous village 
in Bihar and traditionally used to decorate walls, floors, and furniture, is 
characterized by bright colors, horizontal and vertical sectioning, geometric 
patterns, little to no blank space, two-dimensional images, and stylized (at times 
distorted and abstract) figures. In contrast, Warli painting, originating from the 
Warli tribe, an Adivasi (tribal) community in Maharashtra, uses simple geometric 
shapes, stick figures, and a limited color palette (generally b lack and white) to 
depict day -to-day life and nature. Cheria Scroll painting, a stylized form of 
Nakashi art from Telangana, was used by wandering storytellers to 
predominantly narrate stories from mythology and folk culture. Similarly, Phad 
Pandemic Mythmaking: COVID-19 and Mythological Symbolism in Indian Folk Art 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  127 
painting from Rajasthan consists of large-scale artworks, typically ranging from 
twenty to thirty feet, painted on handwoven, coarse cotton cloth. These 
paintings feature figures with prominent, almond -shaped eyes facing each 
other, rendered in a flat, sty lized manner, and are framed by intricate borders, 
often depicting mythological narratives or stories of local deities. Other notable 
folk painting traditions in India include Sanjhi art  (Uttarakhand), Tanjore 
painting (Tamil Nadu), and Bhil painting (Madh ya Pradesh). Many of these 
folk-art traditions, like the Warli and Cheria Scroll, have been recognized as a 
region-specific cultural treasure through the Geographical Indication (GI) tag. 
In India, where much of the rural and tribal social structure is steeped 
in practices passed down through generations via folk wisdom, folk art assumes 
a pivotal role in disseminating information about COVID -19 and shaping 
desired behavioral responses. Fro m “building social awareness to humorous 
interpretations,” artists from nearly every major folk tradition in India “have 
come up with their own view of the pandemic and its protocols” 
(Gangopadhyay). For instance, combining the aesthetics of Warli paintings with 
the theme of COVID -19, Amit Dhombare painted Corona ek Vishva Mahamari  
(Corona, a global pandemic), Ramesh Hegadi painted Save the World from 
Corona, Anil Chaitya Vangad created Life in Covid-19, and Sanjay Parhad painted 
Corona 2020 . Om Prakash Dhurve’s Total Lockdown  and Choti Tekam’s Social 
Distancing in Covid in the Gond (a prominent tribal community) art tradition are 
a few other COVID -related folk paintings. Furthermore, D. Vinay Nakas used 
the Cheria Scroll folk art style to illustrate Stories of Covid-19. Prakash Joshi, in his 
Phad painting Humanity to Community , illustrates masked people maintaining 
social distancing and going about daily activities, such as buying groceries, 
feeding livestock, and receiving COVID aid while wild animals such as deer 
roam freely in the forest in the absence of human beings. Simil arly, Madhubani 
artist Pushpa Kumari’s Covid Bride illustrates a bride getting married even as an 
airplane zips “across the paper, representing people travelling and transmitting 
the virus, and a series of trains” ferry “migrant workers home, at the bottom of 
the work” (Bhuyan). 
Apindra Swain, from Odisha’s famous artists’ village Raghurajpur, 
drew Pattachitra paintings, such as the Covid Code of Conduct . Unlike their 
counterparts in Odisha, Pattachitra artists in West Bengal (called Patuas) such as 
Sonia Chitrakar and her father “created a scroll song on safety norms that 
prompted the state government to ask them to travel from village to village, 
showcasing their Pattachitra and song to create awareness” (Bhuyan). Similarly, 
Swarna Chitrak ar, Gurupada Chitrakar, Yakub Chitrakar, Jaba Chitrakar, 
Khaleda Chitrakar, and Rani Chitrakar, among others, have composed songs 
and Pattachitra paintings on COVID -19 and shared the videos online on the 
Youtube channel The Scrollpainters of Naya, Pingla.  
Ishani Anwesha Joshi and Sathyaraj Venkatesan 
128  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
DURGA BAI VYAM’S “SAMUDRA MANTHAN” 
“Samudra Manthan” (2021) is a Gond painting by Durga Bai Vyam, one 
of the leading artists working in the Pardhan-Gond tradition of tribal art. 
“Samudra Manthan,” loosely translated as ‘the churning of the ocean,’ engages 
with motifs drawn from the Indian myth of Samudra Manthan  to construct a 
complex intertextual framework that establishes parallels with the realities of 
the COVID-19 pandemic, simultaneously serving as an allegorical commentary 
on the necessity of precautionary measures and broader discours es on 
environmental degradation and ecological harmony. Vyam’s painting appears 
in the 2021 pictorial diary (titled Indian Art Traditions in Transition) published by 
the Marg Foundation for the Indian multinational conglomerate, The Tata 
Group. Drawing inspiration from the art practiced in her native village of 
Burbaspur in Madhya Pradesh (India), Bhopal-based Durga Bai Vyam captures 
the essence of her indigenous heritage in her art. Vyam has contributed to 
several books, including The Night Life of Trees  (2006), One, Two, Tree!  (2003), 
Sultana’s Dream (2005), and Bhimayana: Experiences of Untouchability  (2011). For 
preserving indigenous cultural heritage and her contributions to sustain and 
popularize Gond art, Durga Bai Vyam was awarded the Padma Shri, India’s 
fourth-highest civilian honor, in 2022.  
Traditionally, Gond (derived from Kond, meaning ‘green mountain’) art 
is associated with the Gond tribal community of India. Gond art presents a range 
of goddesses, rivers, trees, a variety of animals (tigers, deer, peacocks, fish, 
garden lizards), fantastical creatures (such as Anand Singh Shyam’s cat with 
four grinning heads), tribal gods (with abstract and at times grotesque form s) 
and occasionally mainstream Hindu gods in bright colors. Apart from nature 
(especially trees), Gond art heavily draws on stories and symbolism from Hindu 
mythology and folk tales. Though Gond paintings are two-dimensional with no 
horizon and depth, the patterns impart the “illusion of texture and movement 
in figures that are rendered flat” (Chatterji 111). Initially drawn on mud, stone 
walls, and courtyards of homes, Gond art later gained popularity through 
Jangarh Singh Shyam, who first drew Gond art on canvas and paper, thereby 
paving the way for its internationalization as a distinct art form. Each artist uses 
their own disti nctive pattern like pointillist dots, fine dashes, semicircles, and 
minute waves, among others, which becomes their “unique signature” style 
(“Indian” 3:39). Therefore, even if two artists draw the same figure, the art 
would be different based on the kinds of pattern they choose to work with. Such 
preoccupation with individual style and pattern, however, is not organic as it 
rarely grows out of creativity; as Gond artist Anand Singh Shyam highlights, “At 
first Narmada Prasad and I both did bindi (dot) work like Jangarh, but when we 
saw that his work was selling and ours was not we decided to change our style” 
(Chatterji 1). They began “using different kinds of lines instead,” since city 
Pandemic Mythmaking: COVID-19 and Mythological Symbolism in Indian Folk Art 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  129 
visitors to their exhibitions made them self-conscious about style, leading every 
Gond painter to now choose a distinctive pattern as their signature (1). Vyam’s 
painting exhibits a hybrid pattern characterized by an intricate interplay of 
elongated lines, short dashes, and parabola-like motifs, creating a dynamic and 
textured visual composition. 
Vyam’s painting deftly reimagines the churning of the primordial 
ocean as a metaphorical representation of the global crisis engendered by the 
COVID-19 pandemic. Though various versions of Samudra Manthan  can be 
found in Indian epics such as the Mahabharata and Ramayana, and scripture such 
as Kurma Purana, Bhagavata Purana, and Brahma Purana among others, the myth 
is popularly associated with the Vishnu Purana. Puranas (meaning ‘ancient’ or 
‘old’) are the Hindu scriptures containing encyclopedic information about the 
universe’s origin, mythology, genealogy and exploits of gods, goddesses and 
kings, astronomy, geography, and religious sites among others. Similarly, the 
Hindu Mahapuranas (meaning ‘greater Puranas’) are influential religious texts 
that transmit knowledge through various anecdotal stories. One of the eighteen 
Mahapuranas, Vishnu Puran centers around the stories involving one of the key 
deities in Hinduism, known as the preserver of the world, Lord Vishnu, and his 
various avatars (incarnations). Containing 126 chapters and 23,000 verses in 
Sanskrit (ancient Indian language), Vishnu Purana is one Mahapurana that has 
survived the test of time and has been preserved and widely translated into 
English, German, and other regional Indian languages.  
According to the original narrative, the churning of the milk ocean 
(mythical ocean in Hindu mythology) was facilitated by Lord Vishnu  in his 
Kurma (tortoise) incarnation. In post -Vedic literature, the Kurma avatar  is the 
second incarnation —among the Dasavatara (ten incarnation) —of Lord Vishnu,  
who appears to stabilize a sinking Mandara mountain. Using Mount Mandara as 
the churning rod and Vasuki (King of Serpents) as the churning rope, the Devas 
(celestial beings) on one side and the Asuras (demons) on the other, churned the 
ocean for the divine nectar of immortality, Amrita. The churning revealed 
various celestial treasures (varying from nine to fourteen in different versions of 
the text), such as the Goddess Lakshmi  (the divine consort of Lord Vishnu), 
Kamadhenu (the wish-fulfilling cow), Apsaras (divine women), the deadly poison 
Halahala (which Lord Shiva  heroically consumed to safeguard the cosmos), 
Chandra (the moon), Varuni (the goddess of wine), Parijata (night-flowering 
jasmine plant), gems such as t he Kausthubha (worn on the chest of Lord Vishnu) 
and Chintamani (wish-fulfilling gem), and finally Amrita.  
Drawn on paper using acrylic colors, Vyam’s “ Samudra Manthan”  
depicts a long serpent coiled around the figure of a woman at the center of the 
painting. Vyam’s striking portrayal serves as a poignant reminder of the fragile 
balance of life and the global interconnectedness, especially, between human 
Ishani Anwesha Joshi and Sathyaraj Venkatesan 
130  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
health and the natural world. Humans and animals are seen simultaneously 
holding onto the head and the tail end of the snake, much like the Asuras and 
Devas in the original mythic story of the Samudra Manthan. Through her vivid 
imagery, she depicts human figures grasping the head of the snake —
symbolizing the Asuras or demons—while various animals gather near its tail, 
representing the Devas or celestial beings. This powerful juxtaposition highlights 
the enduring tension between chaos and harmony in the shared ecosystems that 
sustain us. In depicting humans as a metaphorical equivalent of demons, Vyam 
holds humans responsible for ecological di sbalance, pollution, and 
environmental degradation and urges them to work with (and not against) other 
organisms surrounding them. Indian mythologist Devdutt Pattanaik highlights 
the unique nature of the Samudra Manthan episode in Hindu mythology, where 
Devas and Asuras harmoniously unite to accomplish a shared objective. Unlike 
a competitive game of tug of war that focuses on strength and rivalry, churning 
requires a meticulously orchestrated collaboration, wherein one group pulls 
while the other releases (“Symbolism” 2:00–2:41).  
Such a well -choreographed collaboration highlights the posthuman 
interdependence between humans, animals, microbes, and the environment, 
challenging anthropocentrism by positioning humans as one actor in the 
broader ecological network. Posthumanism is a “m ode of thinking about the 
intersecting human, nonhuman, and technological worlds that has gained 
theoretical currency in the late twentieth and twenty -first centuries, especially 
in the wake of ecological consciousness and environmental campaigns that call  
into question the role of humans in shaping the fate of the Earth” (Nayar). The 
COVID-19 pandemic served as a stark reminder that even an invisible virus like 
SARS-CoV-2 can deeply impact global human existence. Following the 
pandemic, there has been a re newed emphasis on traditional modes of living 
that aim to strike a balance between human needs and the well -being of other 
living beings and the environment. The posthuman perspective aligns with the 
ethos of Gond folk art, which Vyam uses to reaffirm huma n being’s 
interconnectedness with the natural world and the importance of universal well-
being. Hence, in simultaneously depicting animals and humans working 
together, Vyam establishes the fundamental relationality and entanglement of 
all living beings in our interconnected ecosystem. 
Vyam is proficient in the motifs that characterize the Gond art form, 
such as “the undulating trees, the striking plumage of birds and the delicate 
features of forest animals” (Shankar). Concurrently, Vyam elevates these motifs 
beyond mere representations of mythic or folk tales by imbuing them with 
metaphorical significance. For instance, Vyam superimposes the figure of a 
salwar-clad (long, loose-fitting tunic and trousers worn in India) woman on the 
outline of a tree with spread roots and branches to sign ify mother nature on 
Pandemic Mythmaking: COVID-19 and Mythological Symbolism in Indian Folk Art 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  131 
whose branches reside human beings. Vyam interestingly depicts houses (with 
people inside them) hanging from the branches of this woman -tree, signifying 
that we are only a single leaf, fruit, and flower of a tree representative of nature. 
In so doing, the artist deliberately conjures a vision of our interconnected, 
networked existence in a well -painted tapestry as delineated in the painting. 
Though humans are not the central focus in Vyam’s painting, the visual 
underscores the idea that every individual is an integral part of a larger 
ecosystem, sustaining diverse life forms and that individuals have the social 
responsibility to adhere to pandemic guidelines. 
On the extreme top left corner of the painting, Vyam depicts a man 
drinking from a blue-colored leaf. This striking image reminds one of Lord Shiva 
drinking the poison Halahal (one of the 14 treasures of Samudra Manthan), which 
turns his neck blue. As a metaphor, this image is representative of all the 
negative consequences of the pandemic, such as contracting the disease, death, 
deteriorating mental health, stress, and anxiety, among others. Similarly, in the 
extreme top right corner of the painting, Vyam depicts a man beating a metal 
instrument with a stick. This image refers to a common practice in India during 
COVID, when people used to come out to the balconies of their houses and beat 
utensils, clap, and blow conch shells to thank the front -line workers, especially 
doctors, for their service to the community. The painting also features a pot 
representative of the Amrit Kalash (pot containing the nectar of immortality), on 
top of which is a lit clay lamp. Both the pot and the lit lamp are positive symbols 
that tie in with the image of individuals staying at home to convey how adhering 
to COVID -19 precautions can help combat the pandemic successfully. By 
leveraging the emotive power of folk art, Vyam skillfully conveys hope, 
resilience, and unity, encourag ing viewers to confront the challenges of the 
COVID era with renewed strength and solidarity. Ultimately, “ Samudra 
Manthan” is a poignant testament to the enduring capacity of art to transcend 
time, culture, and circumstance to offer solace and inspiration  in the face of 
adversity. 
 
“CORONA RAKSHASA”: DRAWING MALEVOLENCE 
Rupsona and Bahadur Chitrakar’s “ Corona Rakshasa ” (2021) is a 
Pattachitra painting from the Indian state of West Bengal, published in the Indian 
Art Traditions in Transition. By invoking the imagery of a Rakshasa, a Sanskrit 
word denoting a malevolent demon in Indian mythology, folklore, and religious 
traditions, the artists underscore the sinister and menacing nature of SARS-CoV-
2 that transcends geographical boundaries. Belonging to a lineage of Patuas, 
traveling storytellers who used scroll paintings to narrate (and sing) 
mythological tales, Rupsona and Bahadur Chitrakar are a daughter-father artist 
duo from the Naya village of Pingala block in west Medinapur district of West 
Ishani Anwesha Joshi and Sathyaraj Venkatesan 
132  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
Bengal. Starting painting at the age of six, Rupsona Chitrakar learned the 
Pattachitra from her father and grandmother and has since exhibited her 
paintings on various national and international platforms. In terms of form, 
“Corona Rakshasa”  embodies the distinctive stylistic features of Pattachitra 
painting, including elongated and slightly hooded eyes, delicate brush strokes, 
colorful composition, and aesthetically refined figures. The depiction of the 
Corona demon, the dragon, and the human characters al l exhibit the 
characteristic slender and elongated forms associated with this traditional art 
style. 
A combination of the Sanskrit word patta (woven) and chitra (art), 
Pattachitra is a folk art form from Odisha and West Bengal and is distinguished 
by its intricate mythological narratives painted on cloth, dried palm leaves, and 
in later times, canvas, featuring detailed borders, bright colors, sharp details, 
and lacquer coated surf aces. While the Pattachitra tradition in Odisha remains 
primarily focused on visual art, its counterpart in West Bengal, particularly 
within the Patua tradition, integrates both music and painting as part of its 
performative tradition. The scroll format of  Pattachitra enables the vertical 
depiction of sequential images, a structural feature that “allows for a succession 
of frames,” thereby “expand[ing] the spatial field of the pictorial narrative” 
(Chatterji 215). The act of “rolling and unrolling the painted scroll” to reveal one 
frame at a time further introduces the “suggestion of movement and temporal 
depth” (215). Typically ranging from 10 to 20 feet in length, Pattachitra scrolls 
are “lined with fabric on the back to give them their characteristic roll ed shape 
and make them more comfortable to carry and preserve” (Zanatta and Roy 61). 
Traditionally, Pattachitra scrolls were not intended for commercial sale; instead, 
they were repeatedly used by artists in various storytelling performances. 
However, with the advent of new forms of entertainment and the gradual 
decline of the traveling storyteller tradition, Pattachitra paintings have 
increasingly been commodified.  
Rupsona and Bahadur Chitrakar’s “ Corona Rakshasa”  visually 
articulates the pervasive impact of the SARS -CoV-2 virus by representing the 
pandemic at its most fundamental, viral level, emphasizing its global spread and 
catastrophic consequences. Set against a striking yellow background, the 
painting features a red-hued demon with horns and elongated nails alongside a 
dragon-like figure that encircles the Earth, which is nestled within its coiled 
body. Two human figures are positioned at the bottom left and ri ght corners, 
further grounding the composition in a human-centric perspective. Notably, the 
Corona Rakshasa is composed of numerous individual virus cells, a motif that 
extends to the similarly red-colored dragon above it. This visual representation 
evokes the myth of Raktabīja, a demon from Hindu mythology whose spilled 
blood generates endless clones of himself, thereby symbolizing the virus’s 
Pandemic Mythmaking: COVID-19 and Mythological Symbolism in Indian Folk Art 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  133 
widespread proliferation and ability to self-replicate, underscoring the insidious 
nature of the pandemic. 
The myth of Raktabīja, derived from the Sanskrit words rakta (blood) 
and bīja (seed), narrates the rise and fall of a formidable demon of the same name 
who, through a boon granted by Lord Shiva , acquired the ability to generate 
identical replicas of himself, equal in form and strength, whenever a drop of his 
blood touched the ground. Raktabīja’s myth is part of Devi Mahatmya  or Devi 
Mahatmyam, a seminal text within the Mārkandeya Purāna that contains the oldest 
myths regarding female deities who are depicte d as powerful (and even 
wrathful) gods. Though Lord Shiva’s boon renders Raktabīja nearly invincible, 
Goddess Kali (Goddess of death and destruction), acting at the behest of Goddess 
Durga (a major Hindu goddess), consumed every drop of his blood directly from 
his body and his innumerable clones on the battlefield, thereby ensuring his 
ultimate demise. However, such relentless violence plunged Goddess Kali into an 
uncontrollable frenzy of destruction, setting her off a rampage that halted only 
when she stepped on Lord Shiva (her husband), awakening from her bloodlust 
and, in a moment of realization, biting her tongue, an image central to her 
iconography. 
By depicting the Corona Rakshasa  in the likeness of Raktabīja, the 
painting underscores the inherent challenge s of containing the COVID -19 
pandemic at its source, as the SARS -CoV-2 virus replicated at exponential 
spread and continuous mutations lead to the emergence of multiple variants in 
the later stages of the pandemic. At the viral level, there existed no defin itive 
means to halt its spread, leaving individuals reliant on precautionary measures 
that, while necessary, remained only partially effective due to the airborne and 
invisible nature of the virus. This characteristic rendered accidental 
transmission highly plausible, reinforcing the perception of the virus as an 
almost invincible force. However, much like the mythological defeat of Raktabīja 
by Goddess Kali, the eventual development and distribution of vaccines such as 
Moderna, AstraZeneca, and Covishield played a crucial role in mitigating the 
virus’s impact. These vaccines effectively transformed the SARS -CoV-2 virus 
from an existential threat into a largely manageable health concern. 
The imagery of the dragon in the painting holds significant symbolic 
weight, as it represents China, where the SARS -CoV-2 virus is widely believed 
to have originated. The dragon, depicted in red to mirror the corona demon, is 
composed of countless viral pa rticles, signifying the initial proliferation of the 
virus within China before it spread globally. This interpretation is further 
reinforced by the multi-colored globe resting on the curve of the dragon’s body, 
visually suggesting the virus’s global reach and capacity to traverse borders. The 
two figures in the lower part of the painting represent the global population 
observing the unfolding of the COVID-19 pandemic. Additionally, the pervasive 
Ishani Anwesha Joshi and Sathyaraj Venkatesan 
134  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
nature of the virus is emphasized through the border of the painting, which is 
populated by various stylized anthropomorphic depictions of the virus, further 
reinforcing its omnipresence and global impact. The repetition of these 
individualized viral faces  across the painting’s borders suggests a sense of 
inescapability, emphasizing the virus’s ability to multiply, mutate, and persist 
despite human efforts to contain it. 
Notably, the virus in the painting is represented through two distinct 
visual strategies: first, as minute circular particles dispersed across the body of 
the dragon and certain parts of the Corona Rakshasa , and second, as 
anthropomorphized, individualized faces integrated into the body of the corona 
demon. In the first instance, the depiction of the virus as tiny particles in the 
painting aligns with its biological reality as a microscopic pathogen, 
emphasizing its omnipresence thereby reinforcing its near -imperceptibility, 
uncontrollable spread, and capacity to embed itself within hosts in ways that 
challenge human perception and control. In the second instance, the 
anthropomorphized face transforms an invisible pathogen into a recognizable, 
almost mythica l antagonist and evokes the longstanding human tendency to 
attribute agency and malevolence to diseases. In conclusion, by employing both 
microscopic realism and mythic personification, the painting bridges scientific 
understanding and cultural imagination, underscoring the idea that the COVID-19 
pandemic is not just a biological and virological phenomenon but also a collective 
socio-cultural experience.  
 
CODA 
Artists infuse mythological elements into pandemic -themed works to 
bridge temporal and cultural gaps and tap into a collective reservoir of shared 
emotions and experiences. Since mythologies are not just stories that entertain 
but also metaphors that encom pass age-old wisdom, values, and philosophies, 
artists all over the world deployed different mediums of representation (such as 
cartoons, comics, and folk art) and repurposed mythological stories to illustrate 
COVID-related issues. Through mythological ret ellings, artists can 
contextualize these emotions, drawing parallels between ancient tales of chaos, 
disease, and heroism with the current pandemic’s challenges. Vyam ingeniously 
weaves the mythical tale of the Samudra Manthan  into her artistic narrative, 
utilizing its allegorical power to advocate for the adoption of COVID 
precautionary measures. Moreover, Vyam criticizes the current neo -capitalist 
societal framework that prioritizes human interests to the detriment of other 
species and advocates for a more sustainable way of life that acknowledges and 
accommodates a networked and relational existence. Similarly, Rupsona and 
Bahadur Chitrakar’s “ Corona Rakshasa” draws on the Indian myth of Raktabīja 
and uses the motif of a demon to frame the SARS -CoV-2 virus not merely as a 
Pandemic Mythmaking: COVID-19 and Mythological Symbolism in Indian Folk Art 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  135 
biological entity but as an active, threatening, and malevolent entity. 
Furthermore, the pattachitra painting highlights the all -pervasive nature of the 
SARS-CoV-2 virus and alludes to the difficulty in containing and managing the 
viral spread. Put together, the utilization of mythological elements in both these 
paintings has enabled a richer exploration  of the pandemic’s impact, 
transcending geographical boundaries to establish a universal connection with 
the audience.

Social Sciences at the National Institute of Technology, Tiruchirappalli, India. She 
specializes in Graphic Medicine, Health Humanities, Pandemic Studies, and Comic 
Studies. Her research articles have been published in various indexed journals such 
as Medical Humanities, Visual Studies, Perspectives in Biology and Medicine, Journal of 
Medical Humanities , and Journal of Graphic Novels and Comics  among others. Her 
forthcoming co-authored monograph, Drawing the Pandemic: COVID-19 and Graphic 
Medicine, is under contract with Palgrave Macmillan and is scheduled for publication 
in 2025. 
 
DR. SATHYARAJ VENKATESAN is a Professor of English at the Department of

Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  137 
 
 
         ARRY POTTER AND THE GOBLET OF BEOWULF :  
                           ROWLING ’S BEOWULFIAN ALLUSIONS ,  
                              ANALOGUES , AND INVERSIONS  
 
                                       MARK-ELLIOT FINLEY  
 
 
E MUST PERFORM THREE TASKS , including battling a dragon, diving into 
hellish water, and fighting against a primordial evil figure in single combat. 
He is mythic but has character flaws. If this description were given to Generation 
Z or Millennial fantasy literature fans, they might assume this sketch belongs to 
“The Boy Who Lived” —Harry Potter —and specifically his three tasks in the 
Triwizard Tournament within J.K. Rowling’s Harry Potter and the Goblet of Fire . 
However, if an Old English scholar were given this character description, 
Beowulf, King of the Geats, might come to mind.  
In this essay, I suggest Rowling’s Goblet of Fire literarily echoes the Old 
English1 epic poem Beowulf, while simultaneously updating the medieval epic 
to reflect the novel’s place within the modernized, Christian-influenced fantasy 
tradition. While Laurie Beckoff, in her blog on MuggleNet.com, links Beowulf’s 
swimming contest (lines 499 –581) with Harry’s second task —the lake 
challenge—this essay engages in a sustained dialogue between Beowulf and 
Goblet of Fire. Both heroes engage in one -to-one combat (Beowulf with Grendel 
and Harry with Voldemort), venture into an unknown watery abyss (Beowulf 
diving into Grendel’s mother’s underwater cave and Harry rescuing Ron and 
Gabrielle from the merpeople in Hogwarts’s Lake ), and fight a dragon 
(Beowulf’s final fight and Harry’s fight with the Hungarian Horntail). I argue  
 
1 I use the term “Old English” rather than “Anglo-Saxon” due to recent critical discussions 
surrounding the latter term and its racial undertones. David Wilton notes “the term 
[Anglo-Saxon] is an identity label associated with whiteness and is often the sel f-
identification preferred by white supremacists” (425). Substitutes for “Anglo -Saxon” 
include, as Wilton notes, “Old English” or “early medieval English,” with use of the latter 
phrase commonly used “when one wants to blur the silos of periodization betwe en Old 
and Middle English” (453). “Early English,” while a possible substitute, poses issues due 
to the “vagueness” of the term: “Not only is it already a term for the Gothic architectural 
style of the twelfth and thirteenth centuries, it risks confusion with ‘early modern English,’ 
and there is no general agreement on what its chronological boundaries are, it being used 
to encompass anything prior to 1800” (453). For more on the term “Anglo -Saxon,” its 
linguistic history, racial undercurrents, and problema tic usage within pre -Conquest/ 
medieval studies, see Wilton. 
H 
Mark-Elliot Finley 
138  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
Goblet of Fire , if read in parallel with Beowulf, presents us with mirrors and 
inversions of the Beowulfian duel sequence, highlighting thematic and 
characterological analogues between Harry and Beowulf. The fourth Potter 
novel, whether consciously or otherwise, reflects symbolic elements from the 
Old English epic poem, and such Beowulfian reflections allow us to further 
appreciate Rowling’s unique themes, which sometimes align with and 
sometimes depart from the novel’s Old English predecessor. 
Beowulf and Goblet of Fire balance Christian and pagan elements 2 and 
have been read as hagiographies 3 featuring a salvific redemptive epic hero 4 
casted within an archetypal mold, 5 contributing to what Annie Hiebert Alton 
calls “a generic mosaic,” as Rowling “fuses” genres together to “creat[e] 
something new” (159). I contend Rowling’s “generic mosaic” can also 
incorporate allusions, or at least analogues, to the Old English epic, m apping 
Beowulf’s challenges onto Harry’s three tasks. This comparative reading 
progresses chronologically through Harry’s three Triwizard challenges, 
illustrating how Harry’s first task echoes Beowulf’s third fight with the dragon 
and Beowulf’s mere episod e aligns with Harry’s second task. Harry’s final 
challenge, fighting the risen Lord Voldemort, 6 borrows from the Grendel and 
dragon encounters, with Voldemort encapsulating Beowulf’s first and third 
antagonists. While Goblet of Fire’s instances of thematic and visual echoes from

Potter, see the Appendix. 
3 “[H]agiographies,” writes Jordan Jones, “tell the stories of saints’ lives and are written to 
edify readers or listeners. […] [T]hey can focus on the death of the saint (these stories are 
called ‘passions’) or they can focus on the life of the saint (these stories are called ‘vitae’)” 
(2). For Beowulf as a hagiography, see Jones; for Harry Potter, see Hennequin. 
4 For Beowulf as a redemptive salvific figure see Fisher 173, Taylor 233, Cabaniss 195 –96, 
and McNamee 191. For readings refuting Beowulf as a Christian redemptive figure, see 
Finnegan 54 and Reynolds 28. Harry is also viewed as an epic hero, but not exclusively as 
a redemptive savior, even though he exemplifies salvific attributes. For treatments of 
Rowling’s representation of heroism, see Heroism in the Harry Potter Series, edited by Katrin 
Berndt and Lena Steveker. For Harry’s selfless actions, see Berman 48 and Rosser 240. For 
Harry’s connection with Christian redemption and salvation, see Anatol xxiin4. 
5 McNulty observes Rowling’s “epic saga” pattern, as each novel can be traced onto Joseph 
Campbell’s hero’s journey (55), as does Boll (85). Pharr claims Rowling combines elements 
from the epic tradition and postmodern style into her heptalogy. Mills reads Harry as a 
Jungian archetypal child, a salvific figure facing the risk of death (7 –8). Similarly, 
Helterman suggests we read Beowulf as a Jungian archetypal character (3).

Krum’s Cruciatus Curse (31.626–27), and a riddling sphinx (31.628–30). However, Harry’s 
true challenge is his duel with Voldemort.  
Harry Potter and the Goblet of Beowulf 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  139 
Beowulf could be read as alluding7 to the epic, where I discuss Old English words 
or passages, such connections are at the most analogues.8 However, Goblet of Fire 
reflects yet transforms elements from Beowulf within its narrative, allowing us 
to further appreciate Rowling’s piece as a work of mythopoetic literature. 
 
THE DRAGON 
Perhaps the dragons are the most obvious connections between Beowulf 
and Goblet of Fire. Beowulf’s dragon appears last in the epic’s monster cycle, after 
waking up and realizing his treasure has been robbed. The dragon was 
“writhing with anger / When he saw the footprints of the prowler who had 
stolen [a cup] / Too close to his dreaming head ” (lines 2288–90).9 Similarly, the 
Triwizard champions’ first challenge is to get past their respective dragons and 
grab a golden egg (20.340 –41). Lauren Berman claims Harry is comparable to 
mythic “dragon –slayers” who seek treasure guarded by an evil creature, like 
“Beowulf and the unnamed dragon he encounters in his final adventures” (61).10 
Expanding on Berman’s observation, I begin with connections between the 
dragon episodes, then examine shared thematic motifs present in Beowulf and 
Goblet of Fire, illustrating how Goblet of Fire uses the Beowulfian dragon episode 
for unique thematic ends.  
In Beowulf and Goblet of Fire —understandably—anxiety pervades the 
heroes’ encounter with their dragon. Hearing the dragon’s destructive rampage 
upon his kingdom, Beowulf’s “mind was in turmoil, / unaccustomed anxiety 
and gloom / confused his brain” (lines 2331 –33). The dragon’s attacks present 
not only anxiety -ridden confusion for the old king, but also the threatening 
unknown. Beowulf conquered Grendel and his mother but never faced a foe as 
fierce as “the fire-dragon / [who] had rased the coastal region and reduced / forts 
 
7 In Rowling’s novel, all Beowulfian connections are at the most implicit allusions and at 
the least analogues. The text never explicitly mentions Beowulf or any Old English terms, 
but due to the sustained connections between Beowulf and Goblet of Fire, specific links, such 
as the matching tasks, can be termed allusions. I cannot, however, claim Rowling 
intentionally incorporated Beowulfian elements into her novel. However, this caveat does 
not preclude us from a fruitful comparative study between these works. 
8 “[A]n analogue,” writes Chris Baldick, “is another story or plot which is parallel or 
similar in some way to the story under discussion” (11). An analogue could be a literary 
echo, either intentional or unintentional, by the author. 
9 Seamus Heaney’s modern English Beowulf translation will be used, unless otherwise noted.  
10 See Gibka 151–52 for similarities and differences between Rowling’s dragons with the 
dragon in Beowulf and Smaug in Tolkien’s The Hobbit. Gibka notes Rowling’s dragons are 
not shown as greedy and do not want to guard the golden eggs, which is why they do not 
attack the surrounding area (151). Berman claims Rowling departs from the traditional, 
early medieval understandings of dragons as sy mbols of greed and sin, rather 
characterizing her dragons as not tied to either good or evil (45), but as neutral (54).

and earthworks to dust and ashes” (lines 2333–35). The dragon represents a new 
threat, Beowulf’s last test of kingly courage. Likewise, Harry, having learned 
about the first task from Hagrid, is in “a state of nervousness so advanced that 
he wondered whether he mightn’t just lose his head when they tried to lead him 
out to his dragon” (20.347). Harry, like Beowulf, learns about his opponent 
before the confrontation, but this prior knowledge yields to our heroes’ anxiety. 
Harry thinks he might “lose his head ” and Beowulf’s “mind was in turmoil,” 
demonstrating the dragons’ effects on the heroes’ mental states. Beowulf, having 
had combat experience ruling the Geats “well / For fifty winters” (lines 2208 –
09), “planned and plotted his revenge” against the dragon (line 2336).  
By contrast, Harry feels completely unprepared for the dragon and the 
Triwizard Tournament on the whole. Whereas Beowulf had fifty years of ruling 
his kingdom to prepare for the dragon, Harry, a fourteen -year-old misfit, has 
only two months, near zero prep aration time. While Harry has fought 
Voldemort and a basilisk in his first and second years, a full -grown dragon is a 
completely different challenge. Reading Goblet of Fire and Beowulf in parallel, we 
see how Harry’s maturation process is contrasted and inverted with Beowulf’s, 
demonstrating Harry’s heroic intensification as he prepares for his fight with the 
dragon. However, regardless of the heroes’ anxiety or how much preparation 
time they are afforded, both must face their gold-guarding dragons.  
Beowulf’s dragon dwelled amongst “a treasure -trove of astonishing 
richness” (line 2757), which showed “signs that someone had stumbled upon / 
the golden treasures” (lines 2301 –02), causing the dragon to destroy the Geats’ 
kingdom because a “gold -plated cup” was stolen (line 2282). 11 Likewise, 
Triwizard champions must take the golden egg which is guarded by their 
dragons (20.349). Each champion becomes a Beowulfian “prowler” stealing 
treasure from a brooding dragon (line 2289).12 During their battles, Beowulf and 
Harry receive similar bodily injuries. Beowulf is caught “in a rush of flame and 
 
11 While outside the scope of this essay, in Rowling’s seventh and final installment, Harry 
Potter and the Deathly Hallows, the trio break into Gringotts, requiring them to sneak past a 
dragon, which guards the vaults. Inside Bellatrix’s vault, the friends steal one of 
Voldemort’s horcruxes, “the cup that had belonged to Helga Hufflepuff, which had 
passed into the possession o f Hepzibah Smith, from whom it had been stolen by Tom 
Riddle” (26.538). The sequence in Deathly Hallows provides a literary rhyme to the first 
task in Goblet of Fire, demonstrating possible Beowulfian influences throughout the series.  
12 In Rowling’s text, the Hungarian Horntail does not escape the enclosure. But in the 2005 
film adaptation, Harry, retrieving his Firebolt with a summoning charm, takes flight, 
causing the dragon to break from its chains and chase Harry around the school’s grounds 
(1:00:20–1:02:44). While I am not claiming the filmmakers consciously pulled from the Old 
English epic, analogues also exist between Beowulf’s dragon raiding the Geats’ kingdom 
and the Hungarian Horntail terrorizing the Hogwarts Castle in the film.  
Harry Potter and the Goblet of Beowulf 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  141 
[the dragon] clamped sharp fangs / into his neck” (lines 2691–93). Similarly, the 
Hungarian Horntail injures Harry: its “tail came whipping up to meet him […] 
and as he swerved to the left, one of the long spikes grazed his shoulder, ripping 
his robes—He could feel it stinging” (20.355). These observations invite dee per 
interrogation between these dragon episodes to uncover how Goblet of Fire  
shares and departs from Beowulfian themes. The first shared thematic motif 
exemplified in Beowulf and Goblet of Fire are the heroes’ courage.  
Beowulf’s dragon duel underscores courage as an underlying thematic 
principle. Beowulf tells his retainers, “I shall win the gold / by my courage, or 
else mortal combat, / doom of battle, will bear your lord away” (lines 2535 –37). 
If Beowulf’s courage fails, he will meet “the doom of battle,” accepting his death 
as a consequence for relinquishing courage. Rowling’s fourth novel also 
structures the first task around the same virtue found in Beowulf. “The first task,” 
says Barty Crouch, “is designed to test your daring, […] so we are not going to 
be telling you what it is. Courage in the face of the unknown is an important 
quality in a wizard . . . very important” (17 .281). While two champions are not 
surprised by the dragons (Harry and Cedric), courage is nevertheless required 
for the young wizards when facing the creatures. Similarly, Beowulf does not 
shy from danger; rather, the possibility of retrieving gold imbues his motivation 
with the kingly courage needed to face his final monster.  
Arguably, Beowulf’s courage leads to his pride. Beowulf states he 
“shall win the gold” (line 2535). Further, the poet claims Beowulf  
 
                     was too proud  
to line up with a large army 
against the sky-plague. He had scant regard 
for the dragon as a threat, no dread at all 
of its courage or strength […]. (lines 2345–49) 
 
Some critics suggest Beowulf’s pride must be purged during his dragon duel. 
Margaret E. Goldsmith claims Beowulf struggles with “the sins of the dragon”—
pride—as he vaunts the claim that only he can win the dragon’s treasure (“The 
Christian Theme” 95).13 Goldsmith posits Beowulf gave himself to the Christian 
God during prior battles (95), but in his final combat, the temptation of gold —
possibly greed? —is symbolized in the dragon’s hoard, alluding to Beowulf’s 
pride (100). Beowulf must fight a literal dragon and the prideful dragon within.14 
 
13 See Marshall 2 –3 for other critics’ readings of Beowulf’s pride in connection with his 
desire for the dragon’s gold.  
14 Leyerle claims Beowulf becomes more prideful with each monster he defeats (101 –02), 
but Baker notes Beowulf’s action—risking his life for his kingdom —was not “irrational” 
as such defensive actions from Beowulf and his troops “must have exceeded the risk o f

Pride tempts Harry, as well. Before his dragon fight, Harry must resist 
the pride coming with Gryffindor House “all treating him like some sort of 
hero” (18.288). The Goblet selected Harry, an inexperienced teen wizard, when 
Dumbledore’s Age Line supposedl y prevented students under seventeen from 
competing. Harry, already “The Chosen One,” must constantly check his ego; as 
Hermione tells Harry, “it’s always you who gets all the attention[.] […] [Y]ou’re 
really famous” (18.289 –90). When Harry is selected as a Triwizard champion, 
Ron feels left out, and asks Harry how he did it, causing Harry to defend himself 
(17.286). Ron, Hermione observes, is “always shunted to one side whenever 
people see you [Harry], and he puts up with it, and he never mentions it, but I 
suppose this is just one time too many . . . . “ (18.290). Thinking Harry lied to 
him, Ron becomes embittered, leading to a fight between the boys. Harry feels 
like “he could have coped with the rest of the school’s behavior if he could just 
have had Ron back as a friend” but he refuses to swallow his pride, deciding “he 
wasn’t going to try and persuade Ron to talk to him if Ron didn’t want to” 
(18.296), causing Harry to start “hat[ing] everything about Ron” (19.335). 
Harry’s stubbornness reveals his “‘st reak of pride” (35.677). 15 The first task 
pushes Harry to his limit, causing him to recognize his prideful tendencies: “it 
was time to shelve his pride and see if Cedric’s hint [taking a bath with the egg] 
was worth anything” (24.457). Harry’s pride is the dominating character flaw he 
battles against when coming to the dragon fight. Harry and Beowulf must face 
and rein in their pride, an attribute personified in their respective dragons.  
Goblet of Fire , while borrowing the courage and pride themes from 
Beowulf, infuses original thematic elements into its Beowulfian dragon sequence. 
Harry grows in his confidence, interdependence, moral reasoning, and sense of 
fair play through his experience with the first Triwizard Task. Before facing the 
dragon, Harry, feeling  utterly unprepared, speaks to Sirius through the Floo 
Network. Believing someone is trying to kill him through the Tournament, 
Harry states, “They’ll just have to stand back and let the dragons do their stuff” 
(19.334). Similarly, in the film adaptation, Harry exclaims to his godfather, “I’m 
 
loss. We need to understand this aspect of the heroic economy” (218). For Baker, Beowulf 
acts according to traditional Old English kingly customs. And while Beowulf takes pride 
in being at the forefront of battles, standing as a fighting king until the las t stroke (lines 
2497–500), Baker acknowledges the Old English peoples did not critique this mindset 
(220).  
15 Barty Crouch Jr. says this when revealing himself to Harry after the third task. While his 
comments reflect aspects of Harry’s character, the text demonstrates Harry’s need and 
willingness for interdependence with the coming of the first task. Ironically, Crouch Jr., in 
the guise of Mad-Eye Moody, gives Harry the idea to fly in the first task (20.344), leading 
Harry to enlist Hermione’s help with the summoning charm (20.345). However, Crouch 
Jr.’s words illuminate a character flaw against which Harry must defend.

Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  143 
not ready for this [the dragon and the Tournament], Sirius” (0:45:19 –0:45:21), 
doubting his natural abilities. The novel highlights Harry’s trepidation, allowing 
other characters to encourage him. Ironically, Barty Crouch Jr. (disguised as 
Mad-Eye Moody) emboldens Harry to “play to your strengths” (20.344). After 
Harry claims he lacks strengths, Crouch Jr. retorts, “you’ve got strengths if I say 
you’ve got them” (20.344). Crouch Jr. pushes Harry to ruminate on his abilities, 
causing Harry to realize his natural proclivity for Quidditch and uplifts Harry’s 
morale, telling him, “You’re a damn good flier from what I’ve heard” (20.344). 
Crouch Jr. speaks to Harry’s strengths when he feels inadequate, highlighting 
Harry’s need to realize his potential. Harry also  demonstrates his need for 
interdependence during his preparation for the first task. After Crouch Jr. tells 
Harry to use his broomstick against the dragon, Harry realizes he needs 
Hermione’s help (20.345). Hermione practices the summoning charm with him 
until a dictionary “flew across the room, and Harry caught it. ‘Harry, I really 
think you’ve got it!’ said Hermione delightedly” (20.347). Encouragement from 
others highlights the novel’s interdependence motif exemplified through the 
Beowulfian dragon task.  
 The first task also illustrates the dragon’s testing of Harry’s “moral 
reasoning and instinctive sense of fair play” (Berman 62). Harry implicitly 
acknowledges his pride, making amends with Ron (20.358). Berman claims the 
dragon represents Harry’s anger t oward Ron following their fight and the 
Hungarian Horntail mirrors Harry’s “internal struggle” (62). Such internalized 
toil might be read as Harry’s struggle to dominate his prideful Beowulfian 
tendencies. Harry’s triumph over the dragon, Berman claims, ep itomizes 
Harry’s ability “to tame his unruly passions” (62). In defeating the dragon, such 
passions, like pride, are defused and exemplify Harry’s moral reasoning, the 
same reasoning allowing Harry to reconcile with Ron following the first task. 
Further, Harry’s sense of fair play is illustrated when Harry tells Cedric about 
the first task (20.340 –41), personifying his ethical morality, themes not 
necessarily present in Beowulf’s final fight. While Beowulf recognizes his pride 
after the dragon fight, giving  thanks “‘To the everlasting Lord of All, / to the 
King of Glory’” (lines 2794 –95), there is no mending of a friendship, as there is 
between Harry and Ron following the first task.  
While Rowling’s novel inverts Beowulf’s duel sequence, within 
Harry’s dragon task, internalized inversions are also present, specifically related 
to Harry’s and Beowulf’s contrasted character states. Beowulf’s defeat by the 
dragon, as noted, seems foreshad owed by his prideful tendencies. Only by 
dying in battle with the dragon is Beowulf able to purge his pride. While Harry 
battles with pride when entering his battle with the Hungarian Horntail, the 
narrative inverts the Beowulfian model by having Harry not  only come out of 
his trial alive, but also sees him purging his pride through meekness, actualized 
Mark-Elliot Finley 
144  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
in his forgiveness of Ron: “Harry knew Ron was about to apologize and 
suddenly he found he didn’t need to hear it” (20.358). Whereas Beowulf’s pride 
is purged through death, Harry’s pride is released through forgiveness, seeing 
the continued friendship between him and Ron.  
Not only does the novel invert the pride tendency present in Beowulf, 
but it also inverts the timeline of the hero’s age in relation to maturation. Goblet 
of Fire ’s divergence from the Old English epic emphasizes Harry’s expedient 
maturation, as Beowulf faces the dragon after having ruled the Geats “well / For 
fifty winters” (lines 2208 –09). While an exact age is not given by the poet, we 
might assume Beowulf to be around seventy years old at the time of the dragon 
fight, if he fought Grendel around the age of twenty. Beowulf fights his 
penultimate foe after having lived a long life, ruled a nation, and defeated 
numerous foes. Harry, conversely, is about fifty-six years younger than Beowulf 
and lacks refined battle experience, being but a young teenager. The novel’s 
inversion of Harry’s age at the time of his dragon fight suggests Harry’s eminent 
need for maturation, especially when read in parallel with Beowulf. Harry’s 
maturation process, while rushed in comparison with Beowulf’s, is underscored 
by his reliance on others and his willingness to forgive, thematic elements 
brought to bear in light of such inversions from Beowulf. The novel’s linkages 
with the Beowulfian dragon fight within Harry’s first task mythopoetically add 
thematic elements to the narrative, departing from and inverting the Old 
English poem. While Rowling’s themes are original, we can read the Beowulfian 
dragon duel as a conduit for unique moralistic messages within the novel, a 
pattern continued in the Triwizard Tournament’s second task. 
 
THE LAKE 
 While the novel inverses the battles, presenting Beowulf’s last 
challenge with the dragon first in the Triwizard Tournament, Beowulf and Goblet 
of Fire intersect in their second tasks. Beowulf and Harry venture into a mere, an 
undertaking holding visual, symbolic, and thematic allusions and analogues 
between the works. In Beowulf and Goblet of Fire, the heroes’ lakes are linked with 
ice. Hogwarts’s Lake is “a great, iron-gray mass of chilly water” with “dark and 
icy depths” (26.483). Hogwarts’s Lake is dark with “icy depths”; likewise, while 
Beowulf’s mere boils, it has icy associations: “They [Grendel and his mother] 
dwell apart […] / where cold streams / pour down the mountain” (lines 1357, 
1359–60). The poet continues: “a frost -stiffened wood waits and keeps watch / 
above a mere” (lines 1362–63). Icy coldness defines the atmosphere surrounding 
Harry’s and Beowulf’s meres. The lakes exude cold despair, turning away all 
thoughts of hope. 
Another symbolic image links Beowulf with Goblet of Fire : the hart. In 
Beowulf, the poet makes a comparison, showing how terrible the mere is: 
Harry Potter and the Goblet of Beowulf 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  145 
On its bank, the heather-stepper halts:  
the hart in flight from pursuing hounds  
will turn to face them with firm-set horns  
and die in the wood rather than dive  
beneath its surface. That is no good place. (lines 1368–72) 
 
Robert Emmett Finnegan acknowledges the hart “unnaturally performs a kind 
of suicide” in turning to face the dogs rather than diving into the mere (47). The 
hart, or stag, holds medieval Christological iconographic associations and 
Marcelle Thiébaux observ es the medieval Christ was often depicted as “the 
transpierced stag” (46). But the hart in this scene does not act in a recognizably 
Christ-like manner, as Finnegan acknowledges; fear and suicide are not 
character attributes associated with Christ. However , inclusion of the hart 
within the scene at least recalls Christological iconography, even if the literal 
hart does not perform Christ -like actions. While the hart faces the hounds 
valiantly with “firm-set horns,” it is scared to jump into the mere. Fighti ng the 
hounds represents a lesser terror for the hart than venturing into the mere, 
which “is no good place.” The hart’s inclinations towards the mere are 
contrasted with Beowulf, who “was impatient to be away, and plunged 
suddenly: / without more ado, he dived in the heaving / depths of the lake” 
(lines 1493 –95). Beowulf does what the hart cannot, taking on symbolic 
significance through his juxtaposition with a Christological symbol. While the 
frightened hart’s actions might not completely symbolize attributes linked with 
Christ, Beowulf’s fearlessness and sacrificial action of venturing into the hellish 
mere work in symbolic tandem with the Christological hart image. The hart 
prefigures a more fully realized Christ-like action on Beowulf’s part.  
The hart also holds significance for Harry, as Harry’s Patronus 
(wordplay on “patron,” or saint), is a “silver stag” (31.623). Harry shares his 
Patronus with his late father, James Potter, and Nadine Böhm claims Harry’s 
Patronus demonstrates Harry’s “desir ed presence” of his father (129). In 
addition to Böhm’s observations, Rowling, through medieval iconography, 
associates Harry as a salvific character, possibly even Christ -like. Howard 
Helsinger writes, “Throughout the Middle Ages stags were allegorized in terms 
of the bestiary account emphasizing the legendary antipathy of stags and 
serpents” (164). Harry’s stag Patronus could link him with Christ and prefigure 
his confrontation with Voldemort, the serpent standing in “antipathy” to the 
stag. Harry, following Beowulf’s lead, “pulled off his shoes and socks, […] and 
waded out into the lake” (26.493). Whereas the hart would rather die by the 
hounds, Harry, the stag, willingly ventures into the lake’s depths, fusing the hart 
and Beowulfian imageries into one. Harry is both Beowulf and the Christian hart. 
Mark-Elliot Finley 
146  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 Beowulf’s and Harry’s meres also share associations with burning. The 
Beowulf-poet describes “At night there [at the mere], something uncanny 
happens: / the water burns” (lines 1365 –66). The mere is also a “hot gore / […] 
wallowing up” (lines 1422–23). Similarly, in Hogwarts’s Lake, while cold, Harry 
“felt the skin on his legs searing as though this were fire, not icy water” (26.493). 
When Harry defends himself, “his wand, instead of sending sparks at the 
grindylows, pelted them with what seemed to be a jet of boiling water” (26.496). 
Hogwarts’s Lake parallels the burning mere in Beowulf, but that which hides 
beneath the waters’ depths solidifies links between Rowling’s novel and the Old 
English epic poem.  
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Beowulf’s mere “was infested / with all kinds of reptiles. There were writhing 
sea-dragons / and monsters slouching on slopes by the cliff” (lines 1425–27). Just 
as Beowulf dives into a monster -infested abyss, similarly Hogwarts’s Lake 
contains creepy creatures. While Harry predominantly encounters merpeople, 
he is also attacked by grindylows: “Harry twisted his body around and saw a 
grindylow, a small, h orned water demon, poking out of the weed, its long 
fingers clutched tightly around Harry’s leg, its poi nted fangs bared” (26.495). 
Aia Hussein notes the name “grindylow” could “be derived from […] Grendel 
[Grendel-grindylow].” While grindylows may or may not link with Grendel’s 
name, the attack on our heroes furthers such connections. The “water demons” 
assault Harry as he goes down to the merpeople’s territory. Creatures also attack 
Beowulf while swimming down to Grendel’s mother’s lair: “a bewildering 
horde / came at him from the depths, droves of sea -beasts / who attacked with 
tusks and tore at his chain -mail / in a ghastly onslaught” (lines 1509 –12). 
Beowulf is assailed underwater and so is Harry: “By the time he had grasped it 
[his wand], two more grindylows had risen out of the weed, had seized handfuls 
of Harry’s robes, and were attempting to drag him down” (26.496). Harry’s 
robes are the Beowu lfian version of chainmail, as the water demons assault 
Harry. Beowulf and Harry are violently broadsided while swimming down into 
watery depths.  
Fig. 1. Grindylows in Hogwarts’s Lake during the second 
task, from the 2005 film adaptation (1:39:49-1:40:20).   
Harry Potter and the Goblet of Beowulf 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  147 
These creatures personify Beowulf’s mere and Hogwarts’s Lake as 
hellish. Finnegan notes Beowulf’s mere is presented as “a type of hell” because 
it is Grendel’s and his mother’s lair, “monsters formally aligned with Cain, 
himself a type of devil or Antichri st” (46). 16 Trenton J. McNulty observes 
Hogwarts’s Lake’s “death-connection[.] […][T]he text describes the underwater 
world of the merpeople as a demonic, underworld realm” (57). Richard A. 
Spencer similarly notes a “close parallel” between the second Triwizard Task 
and Aeneas’s journey to the underworld in Book VI of Virgil’s Aeneid (54). Harry 
fights off “water demons” and encounters merpeople with “grayish skin and 
long, wild, dark green hair. Their eyes were yellow, as were their broken teeth” 
(26.497). Beowulf does not battle merpeople but encounters his second monster 
in Grendel’s mother, “that water -witch, / a great mere -wife” (lines 1518 –19).17 
The Old English mere-wīf is used to introduce Grendel’s mother. Wīf, according 
to the Bosworth Toller Anglo -Saxon Dictionary, refers to “a woman” or “a 
female person” (i). 18 Mere in Old English translates to “a mere, lake” (ii); thus, 
mere-wīf can be translated as mere -wife. Beowulf faces a mere -wife and Harry 
confronts merpeople ruled by a “chief merperson, a particularly wild and 
ferocious-looking female” (26.505; emphasis added). Both meres are ruled by 
monstrous, devilish mere-wives, positing the lakes as representations of hell.  
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
These hellish landscapes are the necessary atmospheric backdrops for Beowulf’s 
and Harry’s salvific baptisms. After Beowulf cuts off Grendel’s head (line 1590), 
he returns to the surface bringing with him his “prize, / the mighty load he was 
lugging to the surface” (lines 1625–26). This act cleanses the mere, becoming no 
longer a boiling, infested pool, but a “settled” lake (line 1630). Such cleansing 
 
16 See Fisher 176 –77 and McNamee 197 for further discussions on the mere’s hellish 
landscape in Beowulf. 
17 Liuzza’s translation. 
18 Wīf can also mean “a married woman” or “a wife” (iii). 
Fig. 2. Mermaids in the 2005 film adaptation (1:38:23).

action, for the Christian reader, could invoke baptismal associations, as Beowulf 
surfaces from a newly purged mere with the evidence of a defeated demon. M.B. 
McNamee claims the scene drips with Christian baptismal “liturgical echoes,” 
more familiar to th e Old English audience than to modern readers (196) and 
Finnegan claims the mere’s purging can be read through “[b]aptismal typology 
and liturgy” (47). 19 Not only has Beowulf gone through a spiritual gauntlet in 
the underworld, but he has come back a new man, expelling the demons from 
Hrothgar’s kingdom. Beowulf travels to hell, defeats a demon, and brings back 
proof of the demon’s defeat, performing a baptism for himself and the land.  
Harry follows the same narrative structure in his second task. The 
golden egg’s song states, 
 
We’ve taken what you’ll sorely miss, 
An hour long you’ll have to look, 
And to recover what we took, 
But past an hour—the prospect’s black, 
Too late, it’s gone, it won’t come back. (25.463) 
 
Taking the song seriously, Harry thinks Ron and Gabrielle will die if not saved 
within the hour. While Harry is allowed to rescue Ron, his allotted captive 
friend, Fleur, the Beauxbatons’ champion, was attacked by grindylows and her 
sister, Gabrielle, is l eft without a rescuer. Harry, after attempting to take 
Gabrielle, is confronted with a merperson: “‘You take your own hostage,’ one of 
them said to him. ‘Leave the others . . . ’ ‘No way!’ said Harry furiously[.] […] I 
don’t want them to die either’” (26.499 –500). Harry disobeys the merpeople’s 
commands, grabbing Ron and Gabrielle (26.501). By denying the merpeople’s 
directives, Harry defeats the merpeople in their own realm, like Beowulf did to 
Grendel’s mother, bringing people back from the depths, analogous  to 
Beowulf’s actions, albeit Harry’s friends are alive, contrary to Grendel’s corpse 
head. 
But it is this departure which solidifies Goblet of Fire ’s salvific and 
baptismal symbolism. Harry gives the hostages a new birth as they come to the 
lake’s surface. Even Harry experiences new life. Resurfacing, Harry “gulped it 
[air] down, feeling as though he had never breathed properly before, and 
panting, p ulled Ron and the little girl up with him” (26.502). The air makes 
 
19 While the mere episode can be read as a baptism, Finnegan cautions against reading 
Beowulf as a Christ figure: 
It is one thing to recognize suggestions of baptism here, but quite another to see Beowulf as 
a type of Christ. […] [W]e would agree that a Christian audience might see the hero as a 
type or precursor of Christ. But the abstract is not the poem itself, and the details of the work 
preclude such an easy identification in whole or in part. (47) 
Harry Potter and the Goblet of Beowulf 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  149 
Harry feel new, as if he was not breathing (or living) quite properly before. It is 
only after Harry ventures into hell and returns with the others that he is truly 
revived and given a new birth. This imagery reflects baptism liturgy and the 
heroic salvific motif of venturing to the underworld. Spencer, comparing Harry 
with Aeneas, notes “the murky worlds of peril and death” show them as 
“saviors […] of their people” (55). McNulty claims Harry’s descent into the 
Chamber of Secrets, wherein he “enters the un derworld and undergoes a 
figurative death before being reborn into the land of the living,” prefigures the 
second Triwizard Task (56). Beowulf and Harry fit the salvific archetype, 
demonstrating what Charles Huttar terms the “principle of voluntary self -
sacrifice on another’s behalf […] tak[ing] the form […] of a descent into the 
underworld, a journey to danger and death, to perform a rescue” (131). 20 
Beowulf acts as a salvific sacrificial figure, in that he destroys Grendel’s mother, 
who represented a threat to Hrothgar’s hall, effectively rescuing the Danish 
society from a devilish villain. However, Harry, in his explicit rescue mission, 
embodies a C hristian expansion upon the Old English exemplar, seeing how 
Harry dives into hell, rescues multiple individuals, and returns with them to the 
surface. Whereas Beowulf emerges with Grendel’s decapitated head, Harry 
surfaces with rescued and revived life in  the figure of Ron and Gabrielle, 
suggesting a Christianized development upon the Beowulfian model. While 
saving the Danes, Beowulf reappears with death, while Harry comes back with 
renewed life for his friends.  
In the second task, Goblet of Fire does not depart significantly from its 
Beowulfian linkages, rather staying consistent with the chronological placement 
of the mere challenge and its symbolic motifs. But in Harry’s third and final 
Triwizard task, I argue Harry’s challenge is a combination of Beowulf’s first and 
third combatants, as Voldemort embodies aspects of Grendel and the dragon 
concurrently. Rowling’s novel follows the Beowulfian duel sequence and inverts 
it simultaneously. 
 
VOLDEMORT AS GRENDEL AND THE DRAGON 
The Triwizard champions’ final challenge, a maze, is a conduit for 
Harry’s true task: his confrontation with the risen Dark Lord. Voldemort’s 
introduction invokes dragon imagery, drawing parallels with Beowulf’s dragon:

The thing Wormtail had been carrying had the shape of a crouched 
human child, except that Harry had never seen anything less like a child. 
It was hairless and scaly-looking, a dark, raw, reddish black. Its arms and 
legs were thin and feeble, and its face—no child alive ever had a face like 
that—flat and snakelike, with gleaming red eyes. (32.640) 
 
Voldemort’s physicality recalls snakes, linked with death in Rowling’s  series 
(Berman 53). Wendy Hennequin observes a correlation between Voldemort’s 
action with “his vices: pride, anger, selfishness, arrogance, power hunger, 
sadism, and murder” (75). These traits echo Beowulf’s dragon who “guard[s] 
heathen gold” (line 2276) and “bu rn[s] bright homesteads […] / that scared 
everyone, for the vile sky-winger / would leave nothing alive in his wake” (lines 
2313–15). Due to his snake-like physicality and dragonish character, Kerrie Anne 
Le Lievre claims Voldemort is “the dragon (Latin draco: serpent, dragon) which 
Harry, in his role as defender, must defeat to protect his people” (32). 21 Such 
elements mirror Beowulf’s situation and actions in his final fight against the 
“sky-winger.” Kenneth Sisam observes Beowulf seeks to contain the evil dragon, 
for if the creature escaped, Beowulf’s retainers would be at the dragon’s mercy 
(136). Beowulf and Harry are both working to contain the dragon. While most 
Old English scholars identify Beowulf’s dragon a s explicitly a dragon 22 and 
Voldemort is a man (albeit one who has irreversibly corrupted his soul), 
character and physical linkages open interpretive possibilities for reading 
Voldemort as analogous to Beowulf’s dragon. 
The following is the Old English describing Beowulf and the dragon in 
combat: “Stīð -mōd gestōd   wið stēapne rond / winia bealdor,   ðā se wyrm 
gebēah / snūde tōsomne;   hē on searwum bād” (lines 2566 –68). Heaney 
translates this passage into “Unyielding, the lord [Beowulf] of his people 
loomed / by his tall shield, sure of his ground, / while the serpent looped and 
unleashed itself” (lines 2566 –68; emphasis added). Heaney translates the Old 
English word wyrm to serpent, which, according to the Bosworth Toller Anglo-
Saxon Dictionary, means either “a reptile, [or a] serpent” (i). 23 Obviously, 
Beowulf fights a large, reptilian -like monster, and while the Old English does 
 
21 Dragons and snakes have close literary associations, as Berman observes: “It is important 
to note that even though the term ‘dragon’ is designated by the Latin draco while ‘snake’ 
is designated serpens, in many literary texts and sources the symbolism of ‘serpent,’ 
‘snake,’ and ‘dragon’ are either synonymous or closely related” (45). See Rose 327 for 
discussion on dragons and their mythic connections with snakes and Gibka 146 for 
serpents’ transformation into dragons within medieval literature.  
22 Taylor claims the creature might be a “man-dragon or beast-dragon—Levithan, Python, 
or fire-drake” (229). 
23 Liuzza translates wyrm as worm, but describes the beast as “coiled, burning, [and]

Harry Potter and the Goblet of Beowulf 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  151 
use the word draca at certain points in the poem to refer particularly to a 
dragon,24 with usages of wyrm, the Old English resists neat closures, refusing to 
delineate the final monster exclusively as a dragon. I do not claim Rowling 
intentionally referenced the Old English in Beowulf when crafting Voldemort; 
however, such a linguistic and literary analogue reveals Harry as a modern 
Beowulf fighting the dragon.  
In Beowulf’s and Harry’s confrontations, there are rounds of fighting. 
Beowulf fights three rounds with the dragon (lines 2542 –91, 2592 –2687, and 
2688–2711), and Sisam notes “Each round represents an attack […] against 
which Beowulf must stand firm, yielding no ground” (136). 25 Beowulf tries 
containing and subduing the dragon, as the creature has proven deadly in its 
attack on Beowulf’s people. In contrast, the novel differs from the dragon 
containment motif, as Goblet of Fire’s narrative crux is Voldemort’s rise to power. 
While Le Lievre claims Harry is crafted as a protector, and a case could be made 
for his efforts to contain Voldemort, as Beowulf attempts in his confrontation, a 
more likely reading acknowledges Harry’s efforts  to survive. His focus, if ever 
on Voldemort’s containment, quickly shifts to surviving a duel with the great 
dark wizard. Beowulf’s primary goal is containing and subduing the dragon, 
effectively preventing the dragon from continued attac ks upon his kingdom, 
whereas Harry’s primary objective is survival. Voldemort is reborn; the cat’s 
already out of the bag. During their fight, Voldemort uses the three 
Unforgiveable Curses, each representing a round in their duel. First, Voldemort 
uses the Cruciatus Curse on Harry (33.657–58), then the Imperius Curse (34.661–
62), and finally the Killing Curse, which Harry defends against with a disarming 
charm (34.663–69). In Beowulf’s and Harry’s final battles, their fights are divided 
into three separate rounds, paralleling Harry’s fight with Beowulf’s dragon 
duel.

Voldemort can be read analogously alongside Beowulf’s dragon and 
also with Grendel, Beowulf’s first combatant. Grendel and Voldemort share 
similarities, particularly in relation to their eyes and darkness. The Beowulf-poet 
describes “a baleful light, / flame more than light, [that] flared from his 
[Grendel’s] eyes” (lines 726 –27). The flame in Grendel’s eyes recalls a red fire 
emanating from his pupils: “while Grendel is alive the baleful glow of his eyes 
in the dark is noticed” (Sisam 133). Similarly, Voldemort has “gleaming red” or 
“livid scarlet eyes” which “gleamed still more brightly through the darkness” 
(32.640, 643, 644). 26 The darkness surrounding Voldemort and Grendel makes 
their eyes piercing, but also characterologically analogous. Alton notes 
Voldemort symbolizes “darkness [and] confusion” and “his name, with volde 
being the Old English word for earth or land and mort the French word for 
death” reinforces such symbolism (218). Voldemort also has a modern Old 
English epithet, “the Dark Lord”, 27 and is embodied by the darkness: “small 
animals […] had met their deaths by a dark shadow that possessed them” 
(33.655). As Voldemort is a “dark shadow,” Grendel is “that dark death-shadow 
/ who lurked and swooped in the long nights / on the misty moors” (lines 160–
62).28 Both villains personify darkness and are “dark death -shadows.” And as 
Beowulf and Grendel have a fair fight, so too do Harry and Voldemort. 
 In his dragon fight, Beowulf exclaims to his retainers “This fight is not 
yours, / nor is it up to any man except me / to measure his strength against the 
monster / or to prove his worth” (lines 2532 –35). Similarly, Voldemort, when 
dueling Harry, tells his Death Eaters, “Do nothing! […] Do nothing unless I 
command you!” (34.664). Rowling crafts an inversion, aligning Voldemort with 
Beowulf, as both characters demonstrate pride, wanting to fight their combatant 
alone. The Beowulf poet also notes, “As long a s either lived, / he was hateful to 
the other” (lines 813 –14) echoing the prophecy in Rowling’s fifth installment: 
“EITHER MUST DIE AT THE HAND OF THE OTHER FOR NEITHER CAN LIVE 
WHILE THE OTHER SURVIVES ” ( Order of the Phoenix 37.841).29 Voldemort’s 
characterological and physical attributes shared with Grendel and the dragon 
illustrate Goblet of Fire ’s fusion of Beowulf’s first and third fight into Harry’s 
third task. The novel also follows and departs from thematic elements
