Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  183 
 
 
              HE BRIGHT SWORD AND ITS SHARPNESS :  
                     SWORDS , SYMBOLISM , AND MEDIEVALISM  
                              IN THE LORD OF THE RINGS  
 
                                            GAVIN S. DAVIS  
 
WORDS ARE PARADOXICAL OBJECTS  that hold in tension both aesthetic beauty 
and repulsion. On the one hand, swords project glamor as finely crafted 
objects of self -display and social power, adorning the belts of countless heroic 
figures in history and literature alike. On the other hand, it must be recognized 
that swords are weapons that kill, maim, and injure —a purpose-built tool for 
the morally complex business of homicide. How one goes about resolving this 
paradox has much to say about one’s personal ideology. When discussing the 
ongoing crises in Middle -earth, Faramir offers his own ideology towards war 
and weapons, succinctly summed up with the statement: “I do not love the 
bright sword for its sharpness, nor the arrow for its swiftness, nor the warrior 
for his glory. I love only that which they defend […]”—a statement which is also 
considered to express J.R.R. Tolkien’s own personal attitude towards weapons 
(LotR IV.5.672; Letters 337, #180; Croft, “War” 471). But Faramir is only one 
individual in a world rich with distinct characters , cultures, and swords. What 
can be said about the other cultural attitudes towards swords represented in The 
Lord of the Rings? 
 Tolkien represents the sword as an ideologically loaded object with 
shifting symbolic significance between the cultures of Rohan, Gondor, and 
Hobbits in The Lord of the Rings . These changes in significance produce three 
kinds of medievalism—post-medieval reconstructions of the Middle Ages—that 
each facilitates different postures towards the cultural imagination of the 
medieval world. 1 Through the Rohirrim, Tolkien depicts a medievalism of 
spectacle—an idealized world of valor to be marveled at from the safe distance 
that separates the Secondary World of fantasy from our own. 2 With Gondor, 
 
1 The seminal definition of medievalism provided by Leslie Workman is the “ process of 
creating the Middle Ages” and “the study not of the Middle Ages themselves but of the 
scholars, artists, and writers who […] constructed the idea of the Middle Ages that we 
inherited” (quoted in Utz and Shippey 5, emphasis theirs, and Workman 439). The se 
quotes and their critical context are cited and discussed more thoroughly in Matthews 7. 
For a more thorough discussion of medievalism, see Matthews. 
2 Tolkien uses the terms “Primary World” and “Secondary World” in “On Fairy -stories” 
to distinguish between the Primary World of the reality we experience and the Secondary 
World of sub-created fantasy.  
S 
Gavin Davis 
184  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
Tolkien’s medievalism critiques the shortsightedness of a culture that prioritizes 
the sword’s functional utility over the values that would characterize its 
symbolism and govern its justified use. Finally, Hobbits offer a third interaction 
with medievalis m as their characteristic unfamiliarity with swords connects 
their experience with that of the similarly non -medieval reader. By filtering the 
warrior-cultures of Middle -earth through the experiences of Hobbits, The Lord 
of the Rings  closes the space betwe en the Secondary World of fantasy and the 
Primary World of reality, turning the reader’s attention to their own experience 
of the imagined medieval past. Without providing easy answers, The Lord of the 
Rings interlaces contrasting ideological perspectives into a sophisticated 
imagination of the medieval sword that, rather than solving the paradox of the 
sword’s beauty and complexity, subsumes the whole with spectacle, critique, 
and self-reflection. 
 
SWORDS: “USEFUL TOOLS TO THINK WITH” 
 The methodology of this paper combines recent developments in the 
study of swords by cultural historians with theories of medievalism to structure 
our understanding of Tolkien’s substantial contribution to the cultural 
imagination of the medieval sword. Swords are currently enjoying a renaissance 
of scholarly investigation in the fields of archaeology and history which seek to 
uncover the sword’s social functions as a highly symbolic cultural object. While 
such investigation directly relates to the study of swords in medieval literature, 
the same methods contribute to our understanding of the continued, developing 
symbolism of swords in medievalism and in Tolkien’s fantasy. 
Accompanying a new wave of scholarly interest in the study of the 
medieval sword, Kristen Neuschel’s Living by the Sword (2020) reworks the 
structures used to understand swords as culturally significant objects with 
diverse meanings to argue that what swords mean may not be as productive as 
considering how they mean. Neuschel writes, “Objects are not merely the 
product of thought, [sic] they become in turn shapers of thought and of feeling. 
Things, especially certain things, are useful tools to think with, to shape thought, 
habits, behavior—culture, in short. In this way, objects are like texts: they are 
out in the world, detached from the producer, gaining and giving meaning in 
action in the human lifeworld” (9). While Neuschel applies this framework to 
the historical swords of the Middle Ages, the concept of swords as textual objects 
that carry meaning detached from their users also applies to the reimagined 
signification of swords in medievalism. In other words, the systems that 
determine what swords “mean ” have not ceased, but persist in medievalisms 
that perpetuate and reinvent the past. If medievalism is the “process of creating 
the Middle Ages” (Workman, qtd. in Utz and Shippey 5) and swords —icons of 
the Middle Ages in their own right —are useful tools t o think with, then an 
The Bright Sword and its Sharpness: Swords, Symbolism, and Medievalism 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  185 
understanding of the sword in its cultural context provides insight into both the 
culture as well as the nature of that culture’s engagement with the memory of 
the Middle Ages. 
 Using this framework, Tolkien’s engagement with the medieval sword 
(and Tolkien’s medievalisms generally) may be analyzed according to the 
different mechanisms by which Tolkien’s works use, intervene into, and are 
themselves received into the cultural memory of the Middle Ages. First, we can 
attune to the preexisting literary conventions that Tolkien mobilizes to ground 
the swords of his Secondary World in a sense of reality. This kind of study—not 
unlike the early comparative studies that connected the sw ords of Old English 
literature with their archeological counterparts —identifies the connections 
between source material (overwhelmingly medieval, in Tolkien’s case) that are 
invoked in the creation of an author’s symbolic language —in other words, 
“Resonances of Medieval Swords and Sword -lore in J.R.R. Tolkien’s The Hobbit 
and The Lord of the Rings,” to borrow the subtitle from Whetter and McDonald’s 
substantial contribution to this line of inquiry.3  
Second, we may discern how Tolkien provides his own contribution to 
the cultural imagination of swords through adaptation and original 
representation in his literature. Rather than studying what swords have meant 
historically before Tolkien, this line of i nquiry considers Tolkien’s own 
intervention into the meaning of swords. Whetter and McDonald hint at such 
analysis, noting that swords can mark the transition from the familiar world of 
the hobbits to the adventures of the heroic world (24), but other scholarship has 
explored Tolkien’s contributions more thoroughly, such as Janet Brennan 
Croft’s analysis of Tolkien’s named weapons and Michael Brisbois’s analysis of 
the social significance of swords, represented especially by the semiotics of 
Andúril. 
Third, we may observe how a changing readership accesses Tolkien’s 
intervention from different perspectives. Tolkien’s impact on the modern 
cultural imagination of swords has shifted over time as it shares space with other 
works of medievalism. While The Lord of the Rings  left an indelible impression 
on the development of high-fantasy that is difficult to overstate, it nevertheless 
makes up an increasingly smaller percentage of high -fantasy available to the 
consumer market. Still, many popular medievalisms contain traces (perhaps 
several degrees removed) of Tolkien’s contribution to the popular imagination 
 
3 While Whetter and McDonald offer the most comprehensive study of medieval sword -
culture invoked by Tolkien, see also Beach. For comparative studies between the 
archaeology and literature of early medieval swords, see Davidson 121–28; Brunning 111–
38.  
Gavin Davis 
186  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
of the Middle Ages. Just as the reception of Tolkien’s works has changed over 
time, so has the nature of Tolkien’s contribution to medievalism. 
 To investigate the second and third of these mechanisms, we now turn 
to how three major cultures in The Lord of the Rings —Gondor, Rohan, and the 
Hobbits—each exhibit different ideological priorities towards swords and their 
symbolism, and how these representations shape the nature of The Lord of the 
Rings itself as medievalism. 4 These different representations of swords fit into 
the greater whole of Tolkien’s work by offering a compelling imagination of 
swords as objects of spectacle and criticism that engage directly with our 
contemporary distance from medieval sword culture. 
 
THE ROHIRRIM: SINGING, SWORDS, AND SPECTACLE 
 Nowhere else in Middle -earth are swords more visually striking than 
in Tolkien’s depiction of the Rohirrim. Through their spontaneous use of swords 
as symbols of fealty, their poetry, and their battlefield valor, Rohan’s sword 
culture steeps their weapons in symbolism that enriches their value both to the 
user and to the reader. This culture, drawn from the often tragic warrior cultures 
preserved in Old English poetry, is not without its complexities. However, 
within The Lord of the Rings , Rohan’s sword-wielding warrior culture treats the 
audience to a fantasy of idealized martial valor rendered as spectacle. 
 Central to Rohirric culture is the social work performed by the sword 
as a symbol of fealty between a lord and his warriors, representing both the 
strength of the lord to rule his people as well as the loyal service of the lord’s 
warriors. The chapter “The King of the Golden Hall” demonstrates this symbolic 
work as the narrative moves from the absence of weapons to the dramatic 
presentation of swords, signifying the restoration of Théoden as king and the 
renewal of the Rohirric court. The outset of the cha pter prompts attention to 
weapons as Gandalf counsels his companions against drawing their swords to 
avoid unnecessary suspicion from the wary and warlike people (III.6.507). 
Disarmament increases when the weapons of Legolas, Aragorn, Gimli, and 
Gandalf ar e demanded from them at the doors to Théoden’s hall, marking a 
narrative low -point of anxiety and isolation as would -be allies have their 
weapons removed. This focus on disarmament is contrasted by the subsequent 
 
4 Besides considerations of length, the omission of elves, dwarves, and orcs requires some 
justification: although elvish swords feature prominently in the Lord of the Rings, they are 
most often wielded by non -elvish characters. Moreover, any comprehensive study of 
elvish sword-culture ought to engage with The Silmarillion, and is therefore outside the 
scope of this project. Similarly, dwarvish culture is primarily represented in The Hobbit, 
and even then rarely engages with swords. The relationship of orcs with weapons —
though an intriguing line of inquiry —would require a consideration of Tolkien’s 
metaphysics which is also well beyond the focus of this study.  
The Bright Sword and its Sharpness: Swords, Symbolism, and Medievalism 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  187 
rearmament of Rohan following the restoration of Théoden to good counsel by 
Gandalf. Notably, it is not the reintroduction of light or hope that finally returns 
Théoden’s strength, but the restoration of a sword to Théoden’s hand by Éomer: 
 
Slowly Théoden stretched forth his hand. As his fingers took the hilt, it 
seemed to the watchers that firmness and strength returned to his thin 
arm. Suddenly he lifted the blade and swung it shimmering and 
whistling in the air. Then he gave a great cry. H is voice rang clear as he 
chanted in the tongue of Rohan a call to arms. 
           Arise now, arise, Riders of Théoden! 
Dire deeds awake, dark is it eastward.  
Let horse be bridled, horn be sounded! 
Forth Eorlingas! 
The guards, thinking that they were summoned, sprang up the stair. 
They looked at their lord in amazement, and then as one man they drew 
their swords and laid them at his feet. ‘Command us!” they said. 
(III.6.517)  
 
Éomer’s spontaneous presentation of his sword to Théoden elicits three major 
effects on both the narrative’s development and our understanding of Rohirric 
sword-culture: first, the symbolic act of accepting a sword from faithful retainer 
renews Théoden and Éomer’s relationship which had been marred by Gríma’s 
influence. Second, Théoden’s personal strength returns to him, signifying his 
renewed agency and authority as a warrior-king. Third, this leads to the sudden 
mustering of the Rohirrim who respond to th eir king’s call to arms by laying 
their own swords at the feet of their lord. This dramatic sequence reveals the 
sword’s centrality to Rohan’s warrior culture which the narrative uses to signify 
the king’s ascendant personal authority and renewed relations hip with his 
retainers.  
An appreciation of the full significance of these dramatic exchanges of 
swords requires an awareness Rohan’s roots in Old English poetry which 
establish the conventions of traditional warrior-culture to which Rohan (mostly) 
adheres. Although Tolkien dismis ses the similarities between Rohan and the 
early medieval English in the appendices to The Lord of the Rings , this note has 
fooled no one, and the close resemblance between Rohan and traditional Old 
English literature is well attested in secondary scholarship.5 A prominent feature 
 
5 “This linguistic procedure [connecting Rohan’s depiction as linguistically similar to Old 
English] does not imply that the Rohirrim closely resembled the ancient English otherwise, 
in culture or art, in weapons or modes of warfare, except in a general way  due to their 
circumstances” ( LotR.App.F.1136n). Major scholarly contributions on the similarities 
between Rohirric culture and Old English literary culture include Shippey 90–97 and 
Gavin Davis 
188  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
in Old English poetry is the relationship between the lord and his thegns (early 
medieval warrior aristocrats), signified by the symbolic exchange of weapons 
and other gifts as the warrior promises fealty and loyal martial service in 
exchange for the lord’ s promise of leadership and protection. Medievalists 
primarily associate this social organization (sometimes called “the heroic code”) 
with earlier, continental warrior cultures which are remembered nostalgically 
in the Old English renditions of Germanic legend, although it occurs as a literary 
phenomenon in other genres as well. 6 In these texts, weapons manifest the 
values of traditional warrior culture by symbolizing the warriors’ oaths of fealty 
and equipping them to perform deeds of valor in fulfillment of those oaths. In 
Beowulf, weapons signify  Beowulf’s renown upon his arrival at Heorot, and 
exchanges of gifts of weapons cement his service to Hrothgar and celebrate 
Beowulf’s accomplishments. The symbolic presentation of a sword reignites a 
blood-feud during the Finnsburh episode contained within  Beowulf, a moment 
which Tolkien compares with precedent from Old Norse literature in which 
swearing on swords was a customary way of making oaths of fealty ( Finn and 
Hengest 133). In the Battle of Finnsburh  fragment, swurdleoma—“the gleam of 
swords”—describes the spectacle of Hengest’s retinue fighting to the death for 
the honor of their slain lord. 7 The Battle of Maldon  and The Battle of Brunanburh  
follow this literary tradition by rendering later Old English kings, lords, and 
warriors in the fashion of legendary figures, depicting them as sword -wielding 
warriors willing to die for their lord, and Tolkien considers the words of 
Beorhtwold from The Battle of Maldon, “Will shall be the sterner, heart the bolder, 
spirit the greater as our strength lessens,” to be the “finest expression of the 
northern heroic ethic.”8 In short, the “heroic code,” symbolized by the exchange 
of swords and their subsequent use, provides a social purpose for the warrior -
heroes of early medieval English literature, establishing the precedent for the 
behavior of the Rohirrim. 
 But despite their similarities in martial culture, at least one major factor 
distinguishes the traditional warrior culture of Old English legend from its 
counterpart in Tolkien’s fantasy: the martial culture of Germanic legend is 
overwhelmingly represente d as tragic. In The Battle of Maldon , nearly all the 
English warriors (and all the ones with honor) are slain by vikings —a pointed 
criticism of the heroic spirit which was discussed by Tolkien in “The 
Homecoming of Beorhtnoth” and his accompanying essays. The Finnsburh 
 
Kightley. Whitt doesn’t deal directly with Rohan but identifies major thematic elements that 
are readily apparent in Rohirric culture.

episode is remembered as brutally tragic in both Beowulf and the fragment, and 
Beowulf itself ends with the death of its eponymous hero, leaving his nation 
leaderless and on the brink of war. As symbols of the idealized heroic code, Old 
English weapons are also problematized: Beowulf’s famous swords fail him in 
battle (twice), a return of vi olence is foreshadowed by the recognition of an 
heirloom sword by a rival warrior, and upon Beowulf’s death, a minstrel 
imagines a time when the sound of the harp shall be replaced by “a cold spear 
grasped in the morning” (Beowulf 3021–27). 9 
In stark contrast to the tragic warrior figures of Old English poetry, 
Rohan’s warrior culture juxtaposes the business of swords with mirth, using 
swords to create a spectacular eucatastrophe at the Battle of the Pelennor fields. 
In Tolkien’s lifted prose that moves “The Ride of the Rohirrim” into “The Battle 
of the Pelennor Fields,” Théoden —the once ineffectual old king, bent with age 
and despair—is compared to “a god of old, even as Oromë the Great in the battle 
of the Valar when the world was young,” and  his battle cry is “a sword -day, a 
red day, ere the sun rises!” (V.5.838). Detail and metaphor further elevate the 
actions of the horsemen who express their joy through song as they charge onto 
the fields of the Pelennor: “and they sang as they slew, for t he joy of battle was 
on them, and the sound of their singing that was fair and terrible came even to 
the City” (V.5.838). When the battle turns against the Rohirrim, Éomer defies 
despair with a grim humor, but this defiance turns again to even greater elation 
when hope returns to the battle through the arrival of Aragorn’s host:  
 
These staves he [Éomer] spoke, yet he laughed as he said them. For once 
more lust of battle was on him; and he was still unscathed, and he was 
young, and he was king: the lord of a fell people. And lo! Even as he 
laughed at despair he looked out again on t he black ships, and he lifted 
up his sword to defy them.  
         And then wonder took him, and great joy; and he cast his sword up 
in the sunlight and sang as he caught it. (V.6.847) 
 
Notably, Éomer manifests both defiance and joy in the hope of victory through 
his sword as he raises it against his foes and playfully throws the weapon in his 
exuberance, using the sword to express a full range of emotion. The narrative 
further deepens the association between swords and Rohan’s v alor by likening 
Éowyn to a sword during her great deed of courage: when she cuts down the 
Witch-king, Éowyn is described as a “maiden of the Rohirrim, child of kings, 
slender but as a steel-blade, fair yet terrible” (V.6.842). Finally, the funeral song 
 
9 “Forðon sceall gār wesan / moniġ morgenċeald mundum bewunden” (Therefore shall 
the spear shall be grasped in the grip many a cold morning). Text taken from Fulk, et al.; 
English translation is my own. 
Gavin Davis 
190  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
for Théoden memorializes Rohan’s juxtaposition of joy with the action of swords 
which repeats Éomer’s lines from the battle, cementing the connection between 
Théoden’s victory with the symbolism of the sword (V.6.847):  
 
 Out of doubt, out of dark, to the day’s rising 
he rode singing in the sun, sword unsheathing. 
Hope he rekindled and in hope ended;  
over death, over dread, over doom lifted 
out of loss, out of life, unto long glory. (VI.6.976) 
 
This close association between joy and the sword indicates the ideological 
underpinnings of the sword for the Rohirrim: it is not only a tool of battle, but a 
symbol of a warrior’s promised service to his lord through deeds of valor on the 
battlefield. When the sword is used to accomplish deeds of valor, it provides the 
joy of fulfilled purpose and moral accomplishment, even when martial victory 
is not guaranteed. The catastrophe of the destruction of Gondor is averted by 
the courageous behavior of the Rohi rrim in the face of certain doom, and the 
sudden aversion of that doom creates the ideal circumstances for 
eucatastrophe—the “sudden, joyous ‘turn’” that Tolkien considered the mark of 
a good fairy -story (“On Fairy -stories” 75). Departing from the tragedy and 
gloom traditionally associated with warrior culture in Old English poetry, the 
arrival of singing, sword -wielding warriors marks the climax of Rohan’s 
achievement, creating a spectacle of swords at the pinnacle o f their martial and 
symbolic function. 
Rohan’s sword culture should therefore be considered a medievalism 
of spectacle because its aesthetic beauty is uncompromised, even though the 
narrative context resists a full endorsement of its underlying ideology. Tolkien’s 
recreation of an Old English w arrior culture revives the ancient aesthetic value 
of the sword as a symbol, but despite the glamor of joyous, singing horsemen, 
this image of fantasy is not to be mistaken for a holistic depiction of war or a 
comprehensive peacetime ideology. Following the Battle of the Pelennor Fields, 
Rohan’s warrior culture has little to contribute to the survival of Middle -earth, 
especially since their warlike ideology struggles to maintain relevance during 
the aftermath of the War of the Ring. This is acutely expresse d by Éowyn’s 
struggle to reintegrate to peacetime society following the termination of her 
ability to participate in the War of the Ring. Éowyn’s final renunciation of the 
Rohirrim’s warrior culture has been received as both an indictment of a culture 
that glorifies war as well a somewhat unconvincing change of character 
considering Éowyn’s earnest justification for the necessity of warriors a few 
pages earlier (Dickerson 37–39; Croft, “War” 469). But while The Lord of the Rings 
does contain other more articulate critiques of martial culture that resist full 
endorsement, Rohan’s aesthetic beauty in its moment of accomplishment 
The Bright Sword and its Sharpness: Swords, Symbolism, and Medievalism 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  191 
remains intact: the sword is both fair and terrible. Even Faramir —while 
acknowledging the tragic elements of her martial ethos—declares Éowyn  “high 
and valiant and have yourself won renown that shall not be forgotten,” 
expressing his admiration for her embodiment of the heroic ethic before her 
subsequent change of heart (VI.5.964). In short, Tolkien’s depiction of Rohan’s 
sword culture preserves the sword’s ancient beauty as a symbol of faithfulness 
and valor derived from Old English literature, but localizes this idealization to 
specific narrativ e circumstances. Rohan’s joyful fulfillment of its martial 
purpose by riding to the defense of Gondor forms a moving spectacle, but one 
that is admired from a carefully situated distance between the Primary and 
Secondary Worlds. 
Although the swords of Rohan represent a fantasy drawn from an 
idealization of the medieval past, the surrounding narrative maintains a critical 
distance that preserves the possibility of further nuance to an ideology centered 
on weapons and their use. Éow yn’s interactions with Faramir hint at such 
criticism as she reconciles herself to a world without a need for battlefield 
heroes, but Tolkien’s primary criticism of swords as objects of power comes 
from the other voices and cultures of Middle-earth. 
 
GONDOR: FRACTURED SWORDS AND SHATTERED SYMBOLS  
While the Rohirrim represent an idealization of sword culture with a 
unified symbolic and martial purpose, Gondorian culture at the beginning of 
The Lord of the Rings has a more fractured sense of the sword’s symbolic potential 
that isolates its utility as a weapon from the social symbolism that would govern 
its use. This separation, in addition to Gondor’s excessive emphasis on the need 
for weapons, problematizes the sword as glorious in -and-of itself. This 
separation of the sword’s symbolism from its util ity does not dismiss the need 
for martial victory, but creates the need for the restoration of rightly -ordered 
values—a restoration that comes with the return of Gondor’s rightful king. As a 
representation of a medieval past, Gondor depicts pointed critici sm of warrior 
culture along with Tolkien’s solution in the form of an idealized king. 
The clearest diagnosis of Gondor’s decline comes from Faramir, whose 
comparative analysis of Gondor and Rohan identifies the Gondor’s treatment of 
martial culture as an end in itself as the root of his people’s dwindling status. In 
his telling of the histo ry of Men, Faramir traces differences in cultural values 
that distinguish “the Middle People” of the Rohirrim from the “High” men of 
Númenor and their remnants in Gondor. Between the cultures, Faramir 
considers excessive emphasis on martial valor to be pro blematic when treated 
as a “good in itself,” becoming a kind of crux that distinguishes “Middle” from 
“High”: 
 
Gavin Davis 
192  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
Yet now, if the Rohirrim are grown in some ways more like to us, 
enhanced in arts and gentleness, we too have become more like to them, 
and can scarce claim any longer the title High. We are become Middle 
Men, of the Twilight, but with memory of other thin gs. For as the 
Rohirrim do, we now love war and valour as things good in themselves, 
both a sport and an end; and though we still hold that a warrior should 
have more skills and knowledge than only the craft of weapons and 
slaying, we esteem a warrior, non etheless, above men of other crafts. 
Such is the need of our days. (IV.5.679) 
 
In this passage, Faramir expresses the diminished relative status of Gondor 
using the language of weapons. Faramir acknowledges the pragmatic necessity 
of “the craft of weapons and slaying” given the circumstances of wartime, but 
implies the need for other  values beyond martial skill-of-weapons to balance a 
society and govern the appropriate use of those weapons. As put by Matthew 
Dickerson, “Faramir sees the love of war, the practice of war as a sport, and the 
esteeming of the warrior not as signs of the c urrent glory of Gondor, but rather 
as signs of its fall” (60). This separation between a weapon’s martial utility and 
greater purpose is most clearly evident in Faramir’s previously quoted 
statement, “I do not love the bright sword for its sharpness, nor the arrow for its 
swiftness, nor the warrior for his glory. I love only that which they defend,” 
which orients the moral justification for the weapon’s use around the object of 
defense (IV.5.672). By calling the sword “bright,” Faramir seems to acknowledge 
the aesthetic beauty in an object so strongly associated with valor. But the value 
of the sword’s sharpness—the quality which turns the sword from a symbol to 
a weapon—is contingent upon the object of the sword’s use. Divorced from a 
clear sense of moral purpose, Faramir has little love for the sword.  
Despite his sophisticated personal ideology, Faramir is an outlier in the 
struggling kingdom of Gondor that is consumed by the pragmatic need for 
weapons in its fight for survival. Boromir, rather, is considered a paragon of 
Gondor’s martial culture, but B oromir is nevertheless enamored by the Ring’s 
martial potential, providing further evidence of the moral decline at the heart of 
Gondor’s martial culture. At the Council of Elrond, Boromir makes his case for 
the need of weapons: “The Men of Gondor are vali ant, and they will never 
submit; but they may be beaten down. Valour needs first strength, and then a 
weapon. Let the Ring be your weapon, if it has such power as you say. Take it 
and go forth to victory!” (II.2.267). When Elrond rejects such an idea based  on 
the Ring’s power to corrupt the heart, Boromir relents with deep reluctance, 
saying “Then in Gondor we must trust to such weapons as we have” (II.2.267 –
68). Boromir’s reasoning builds on the premise that the only thing preventing 
Gondor from victory ag ainst Sauron is the magnitude of Gondor’s power, 
represented here by the potency of their weapons. Boromir fails to see in this 
The Bright Sword and its Sharpness: Swords, Symbolism, and Medievalism 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  193 
instance that might —be it valor or a potent weapon —may make for martial 
victory, but not the moral victory that those gathered at the Council of Elrond 
are seeking. This desire for weapons and power is explicitly connected to 
Boromir’s tragic fall as it ar ises again in the midst of Boromir’s final assault on 
Frodo: “[H]is talk dwelt on walls and weapons, and the mustering of men; and 
he drew plans for great alliances and glorious victories to be; and he cast down 
Mordor, and became himself a mighty king, be nevolent and wise” (II.10.398). 
As described by Dickerson, Boromir’s obsession with military victory and his 
assault on Frodo makes a worldview that glorifies war complicit in the 
subsequent breaking of the fellowship (60 –62). While Rohan’s spectacular 
military victory will later be convenient for the survival of Minas Tirith, 
Boromir’s neglect of the moral frameworks that would constrain the use of 
weapons results in his inability to comprehend the moral danger posed by the 
Ring of Power.  
Gondor’s disconnect between weapons and the morality that would 
govern their use manifests in the fractured symbolism of the sword. While fealty 
and service characterize the symbolic and martial work of Rohan’s swords, a 
comparison of Merry’s and Pippin’s respective declarations of fealty to Théoden 
and Denethor reveals the diminished symbolic weight of Gondor’s swords. 
Merry’s oath of fealty to Théoden is informal, simple, and sincere: Merry asks if 
he may lay his sword as on Théoden’s lap as token of his service, having been 
prompted by a sudden love for the old king. Théoden accepts, bids Merry rise 
an esquire of Rohan, and commands him to take his sword and bear it to good 
fortune (V.2.777). While informal, Merry’s attitude matches the spontaneity of 
the other, earlier symbolic exchanges of weapons between Théoden and his 
retinue, and the simple promise and the exchange of the sword is sufficient 
symbolism to convey the earnestness of the relationship. In contrast, coldness 
marks Pippin’s declaration of f ealty to Denethor, and the sword’s isolated 
martial function diminishes the symbolic weight of the sword during Pippin’s 
ritual of fealty. When he offers Denethor his sword, Denethor says, “Give me 
the weapon!” and proceeds to question Pippin on its provenance. That Denethor 
calls it a weapon rather than a sword distills the object’s value to its use on the 
battlefield, stripping the sword’s procurement of the fealty, loyalty, and service 
that the weapon’s exchange could otherwise suggest (V.5.756). When Pi ppin 
formally swears his service, the sword is symbolically placed on Denethor’s lap 
and Pippen takes the hilt, but must swear a host of additional oaths and 
assurances since, in contrast to Merry’s exchange of oaths, the sword alone is 
not a sufficient sy mbol of their exchange. The sword, distilled to its martial 
utility, has limited social symbolism and must be supplemented by additional 
assurances. 
Gavin Davis 
194  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 Aragorn’s return as the rightful king of Gondor and bearer of the 
Sword Reforged resolves both Gondor’s need for weapons as well as its cultural 
crisis by symbolically restoring martial valor to its appropriate place. Aragorn 
pragmatically rescues Gondor from its immediate crisis by leading the 
Dúnedain of the North, the sons of Elrond, and a coalition of the southern 
fiefdoms to lift the siege of Minas Tirith. While doing so, the narrative displays 
Aragorn’s martial prowess in its fullness as he wields his famous sword with its 
deep provenance: “the Flame of the West, Andúril like a new fire kindled, Narsil 
re-forged as deadly as of old” (V.6.848). But more significant than the practical 
use of Aragorn’s famous weapon is the identity of its wielder that th e sword 
represents. As demonstrated by Michael Brisbois, Aragorn, as bearer of the 
heirloom sword of Elendil that was broken and reforged, becomes with his 
sword a symbol of the millennial renewal brought about by the return of the 
king (Brisbois 98). Bris bois’s analysis provides a more thorough reading of the 
semiotic significance of Andúril than can be supplied here. But while avoiding 
a full repetition of Bribois’s argument, it is worth reiterating both the importance 
of Andúril to Aragorn’s identity as well as the distinct limitations of Andúril’s 
significance. Andúril is a recurrent symbol of Aragorn’s kingly heritage and the 
martial strength he uses to rescue his kingdom from the forces of evil. However, 
Andúril is not the final sign that proves Aragor n’s legitimacy. Rather, it is 
Aragorn’s role as a healer that identifies him as the rightful heir to the throne of 
Gondor (Bribois 100–01). Aragorn’s martial identity as the bearer of Andúril and 
Savior of Gondor is subservient to his primary identities as  “the Elfstone” and 
“the Renewer” (V.8.863). The unnamed Warden of the houses of healing marvels 
at the complexity of this individual and struggles to comprehend how one so 
skilled in swords could also be a healer (VI.5.958), but by demonstrating his 
healing skills as “a lore-master, not merely a captain of war,” Aragorn validates 
his identity as the Renewer, suitable to lead the kingdom to a time of peace 
(V.8.865). Aragorn’s coronation ultimately represents these reordered values 
when his many titles are listed out in increasing significance: “Here is Aragorn 
son of Arathorn, […] bearer of the Star of the North, wielder of the Sword 
Reforged, victorious in battle, whose hands bring healing, the Elfstone, Elessar 
of the line of Valandil, Isildur’s son, Elen dil’s son of Númenor” (VI.5.967). In 
Gondor under the rule of Aragorn, swords retain their practical use, but their 
moral framework is restored by the return of a king whose symbolism submits 
the martial function of swords to the governing values of restor ation and 
renewal.  
Gondor’s medievalism functions differently from Rohan’s because 
Gondor’s redemptive arc represents, in a way, Tolkien’s attempt to “fix” a fault 
he recognizes in medieval literary warrior cultures. Brisbois’s study of 
Aragorn’s relationship with his sword notes Aragorn’s significant departure 
The Bright Sword and its Sharpness: Swords, Symbolism, and Medievalism 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  195 
from earlier medieval models that would have emphasized the military 
authority of a king, a difference which underscores the magnitude of the 
medievalism’s intervention into the historical past (Brisbois 99 –101). Tolkien is 
well aware of the suffering asso ciated with warfare and the self -destructive 
tendencies of chivalric culture —issues he addresses directly with “The 
Homecoming of Beorhtnoth Beorhthelm’s Son,” which have been studied in 
Tolkien scholarship. 10 Many of Tolkien’s criticisms of warfare are associated 
with the increasing separation between commanders and the location of battle, 
and Tolkien consistently saw the increasing mechanization of warfare as 
morally perilous to those who would attempt to use  such depersonalized 
devices as airplanes or atomic bombs. 11 But unlike Rohan’s spectacle in which 
the ideological problems of weapons are momentarily deferred by the narrative 
context, Gondorian sword-culture represents the hollow moral justification for 
weapons that are valued for their power, and the futility of valor pursued for its 
own sake, personified in the tragic figure of Boromir. Tolkien’s medievalism 
reproduces the moral hazards of weapons which are found in medieval world 
and modernity alike, but Tolkien intervenes through the figure of an idealized 
king (and foreshadowed by the ideological musings of Faramir) who 
subordinates the practical necessities of weapons to the superior values that 
would govern their justified use, thereby creating a medievalism that aspires to 
what could have been.  
 
HOBBITS: SWORDS IN A SWORDLESS CULTURE 
 In a manner analogous to our own, largely swordless modern times, 
the swordless -ness of Hobbit culture starkly contrasts the martial cultures of 
Rohan and Gondor. Hobbits, as noted by Tom Shippey, are an intermediary 
between the modern reader and the fict ional medievalism of Middle -earth, 
helping to bridge the distance between the two worlds by representing modern 
interests in the unfamiliar world of medievalist fantasy (6 –7). Whetter and 
McDonald incorporate the symbolism of swords into Shippey’s argument , 
observing that “swords serve as a bridge between the hobbits and the heroic 
world and heroism,” functioning as a semiotic marker for the transfer of the 
hobbits from the familiar world to the world of adventure (Whetter and 
McDonald 24 –25). But this obse rvation, made in passing in Whetter and 
McDonald’s article, is worthy of closer attention due to the essential contribution 
of Hobbit culture’s swordless -ness to the overall function of The Lord of the 
Rings’s medievalism. The swordless Hobbit culture prov ides an in -world 
representation of our familiar, modern world which the hobbits leave and return 
 
10 See Reinhard. 
11 See Croft, War 100–04; Tolkien, Letters 168, #101. 
Gavin Davis 
196  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
to, but just as the hobbits each return changed from their journey, so too has 
their estimation of the sword. These changed perspectives on the sword are not 
uniform across the experience of the hobbits, but all contribute to the hobbits’ 
collective ability to successfully recover the Shire from corruption with minimal 
casualties or moral loss. In this manner, the return of swords to the Shire brought 
by the hobbits becomes an in -world manifestation of medievalism —relics of a 
medieval past reconfigured and deployed to a moment defined by the present. 
In so doing, the hobbits’ experience with swords reflects the reader’s experience 
with the medievalism of the perilous realm, providing a morally resonant 
interface between the primary world and an imagined, medieval experience.  
 Technically, swords were likely present in the Shire by the time of 
Bilbo’s 111th birthday, although the distance between the sword’s martial utility 
and its present existence illustrates the object’s change in meaning from that of 
a weapon to that of an object of memory. In the foreword to the second edition 
(appearing for the first time in 1966), Tolkien writes: 
 
At no time had Hobbits of any kind been warlike, and they had never 
fought among themselves. In olden days they had, of course, been often 
obliged to fight to maintain themselves in a hard world; but in Bilbo's 
time that was very ancient history. […] So, t hough there was still some 
store of weapons in the Shire, these were used mostly as trophies, 
hanging above hearths or on walls, or gathered into the museum at 
Michel Delving. The Mathom -house it was called; for anything that 
Hobbits had no immediate use f or, but were unwilling to throw away, 
they called a mathom. (LotR Prologue.5) 
 
This insight into Hobbit hoarding habits suggests that the nature of the sword -
object had shifted for Hobbits by the time of the War of the Ring. Unlike the 
swords of the Rohirrim which are rich with their symbolism of fealty and 
service, or the weapons ne eded by Gondor in their desperate hour, the swords 
of Hobbit culture have become trophies, objects of memory with latent utility, 
having been transformed in essence and in function to the status of meaningful 
wall décor. This fundamental change from weapon to something else is signaled 
linguistically by the reintroduction of the word, mathom, which is a resurrection 
of the defunct Old English word máðum, which carried the meaning of “a 
precious or valuable thing (often refers to gifts), a treasure, jewel, ornament” 
(Bosworth). This object is still “a useful tool to think with,” but the nature of the 
object is only tangentially related to the similar obje cts born by the warriors of 
Gondor and Rohan. The transformation of the Shire’s swords from weapons to 
mathoms is so complete that they are wholly absent from the inventory of 
weapons gathered by the hobbits in their fight against Sharkey’s ruffians 
(VI.8.1008–09). It could be argued that the list of improvised farm-tool weapons 
The Bright Sword and its Sharpness: Swords, Symbolism, and Medievalism 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  197 
used by the hobbits represents the “some store of weapons” referenced in the 
prologue, but this is unlikely since the tools have too much pragmatic, non -
weapon value to be wall décor or put away in a museum. It is more plausible 
that the swords were hidden in plain sight, or simply didn’t come to mind when 
the need for weapons arose. Such is the extent of the hobbits’ peaceful, pastoral 
existence at the time of the War of the Ring that a dedicated weapon of war 
cannot readily be found amongst them, and the mere appearance of swords in 
the hands of the four hobbits is enough to break the ranks of the ruffians when 
they first (nearly) come to blows (VI.8.1005). 
 The journeys of Frodo, Sam, Merry, and Pippin further illustrate the 
unfamiliarity of Hobbits with swords as they are represented as both 
inexperienced and unglamorous in their handling of swords. The four hobbits 
set out for Bree without weapons, and are  only supplied with the mysterious 
swords of the Barrow-downs by Tom Bombadil following their near catastrophe 
with the Barrow-wights. Even then, the need for these strange objects surprises 
the hobbits: “Their new weapons they hung on leather belts under their jackets, 
feeling them very awkward, and wondering if they would be of any use. 
Fighting had not before occurred to any of them as one of the adventures in 
which their flight would land them” (I.8.146). Bilbo, perhaps the most 
experienced Hobbit with swords, stores his sword in a box which he “fumbles” 
through to find Sting, which he presents to Frodo by rather indelicately stabbing 
it into a nearby beam. 12 Frodo and Sam “fumble for their sword hilts” when 
threatened by Faramir’s host, and although Sam is able to make effective use of 
his sword against Shelob and Gollum, this is due more to the intensity of his 
fury than any latent skill he had with the blade (IV.5.681, IV.9.726–27, IV.10.728–
29).  
The hobbits’ unfamiliarity with swords sharply contrasts the heroic, 
Old English aesthetics of Rohan and the war-weary chivalric culture of Gondor, 
but this unfamiliarity reproduces the reader’s own interface with the world of 
medievalism. Like Hobbit cult ure as described in the prologue, the swords of 
today are largely alien objects—they are invoked in stories and sometimes used 
as decor, and only some sub-cultures use modified swords in sport or recreation. 
For us, as for Hobbits, swords have no immediate  use, and hundreds of years 
separate the present from the last time swords played a significant role on a 
battlefield. The use of swords in war is a distant memory, turning the bladed 
weapons into a relic of the medieval past invoked in medievalism. For th e 
hobbits, as for us, swords have become useful objects to think with, arming the 
 
12 The literary significance of this “sword in the stone” moment is discussed by Flieger. 
Despite the moment’s literary significance in signaling “the emergence of the hero,” it still 
demonstrates the awkwardness of the hobbits’ relationship with swords (Flieger 55, 48). 
Gavin Davis 
198  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
characters of our medieval fantasies and sometimes decorating our walls, even 
if their martial purpose has long since disappeared. 
Representing modern attitudes towards swords through the hobbits 
changes the mechanism of the medievalism by shifting focus onto the audience’s 
own engagement with the Secondary World. Instead of looking directly at an 
imagined Middle Ages where the use of  swords is glamorized or the problems 
of chivalry are addressed, readers look at versions of themselves interacting 
with the medieval world as the hobbits filter through the cultures of Middle -
earth. By reflecting the reader’s own experience of medievalism  through its 
adventurers, The Lord of the Rings  narrows the distance between the imagined 
medieval world and the characters with which readers can identify. 
Importantly, this mediation is not uniform, but reflects differences in each of the 
hobbits’ experiences with the cultures and perils of Middle -earth: Merry and 
Pippin each use their swords to swear their services to two different kings for 
very different reasons, Sam becomes ever more defensive of his friend and 
master, and Frodo turns out nearly a pac ifist. Taken as a whole, the hobbits’ 
mediation provides critical structures for navigating the complexity of Tolkien’s 
contribution to the cultural imagination of the medieval sword since there is no 
obviously “correct” way to engage with a perilously bea utiful weapon, but a 
host of possibilities that the hobbits incorporate together to accomplish their 
ultimate purpose: Merry and Pippin orchestrate the defense of the Shire; Sam 
dutifully defends his loved ones, with force if necessary; and Frodo serves as  a 
moral center that restrains the excessive use of violence. Like the hobbits, we are 
not to arrive at the end of a journey through the perilous realm unchanged or 
having received a single, moral lesson that reduces swords to uncomplicated 
objects of valor or violence. Rather, through the hobbits, readers are invited to 
reckon with new ways of considering swords as useful, beautiful, and 
dangerous. 
 
TOLKIEN’S SWORDS: OBJECTS TO THINK WITH 
 Recent scholarship has developed the sword as an entry -point for 
investigating cultural history, and previous Tolkien scholarship has shown 
swords to be integral to the “symbolic construction of Middle -earth” (Brisbois 
97). More than clever world -building, Tolkien fashions the many swords of the 
Lord of the Rings into multivalent signifiers that present a sophisticated 
relationship with the medieval Secondary World. The image of hobbits 
fumbling for their swords is worlds away from that of warrior-heroes clutching 
desperately for power, or singing, sword -wielding horsemen, crying “Death!” 
as they charge the ranks of Mordor —and it is the inclusion of all of these 
together that produces Tolkien’s mature imagination of the medieval sword. 
The narrative does not overly privilege one or another of these attitudes towards 
The Bright Sword and its Sharpness: Swords, Symbolism, and Medievalism 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  199 
swords but puts them into conversation with each other, holding them in 
tension. This tension is unresolved even at the end of The Lord of the Rings , as 
Frodo’s reluctance to use weapons is balanced by the pragmatic willingness of 
Merry and Pippin in the scouring of the Shire. Because the paradox of a sword’s 
aesthetic beauty and implicit violence is likely never to be fully resolved in the 
real world, the lack of resolution to The Lord of the Rings  facilitates the story’s 
enduring resonance across a wide range of ideologically disparate audiences. In 
the end,  The Lord of the Rings  incorporates the paradox of swords as 
simultaneously lethal and beautiful objects by subsuming that paradox in a 
range of intertwined ideological approaches. Throughout, Tolkien’s treatment 
of swords as complex signifiers creates an ideologically robust medievalism that 
offers profoundly sophisticated structures for appreciat ing the dangerous 
beauty of an enduring symbol of the medieval past.

GAVIN S. DAVIS received his Ph.D. in English from Penn State in 2025 following the 
completion of his dissertation, Reading the Old-Sword: The Narrative Work of Swords in Tenth-

combat mechanics in Dungeons & Dragons, and will soon launch an educational YouTube 
channel that teaches medieval literature and theories of medievalism to audiences online.
