                    LAN GARNER ’S ELIDOR :  
                         THE “A NTI -NARNIAN FANTASY ”?   
 
                                      LAURA N. VAN DYKE 
 
N 2021, BRITISH FANTASY WRITER ALAN GARNER wrote in The Guardian, “I never 
enjoyed CS Lewis’s The Chronicles of Narnia. I read the books with horrid 
fascination. They were, in my opinion, and remain, nasty, manipulative, morbid, 
misanthropic, hectoring, totalitarian and atrociously written” (“The Books of 
My Life”).  Garner’s vehement statement likely raised some eyebrows, 
particularly given how closely his own books have been linked to Lewis’s in the 
minds of many readers for six decades now. His children’s fantasies in particular 
have parallels not only to Lewis’s Na rnia stories, but also to Tolkien’s writing; 
this association is not surprising, given that Garner was a student at Magdalen 
College, Oxford, and while there he had as tutor Colin Hardie, a Dante scholar 
and a member of the Inklings.1 Hardie was a close friend of Lewis, which is likely 
why Garner sent his first published novel, a children’s fantasy novel entitled The 
Wierdstone of Brisingamen, to Lewis to read in 1960. At Oxford Garner met both 
Lewis and Tolkien, which is part of why many readers have assumed Garner 
was continuing their work with his own fantasy novels (Hayes).   
Although Garner had left Oxford by the time he wrote his first novel 
(Lewis, too, had left and taken up a chair at Cambridge), Garner sent Lewis a 
copy for him to read (Howe). In a letter to Roger Lancelyn Green on 15 
September 1960, Lewis wrote, “Have you seen Allan2 Garner’s The Wierdstone of 
Brisingamen? Not bad, tho’ too indebted to Tolkien.3 He seems to be a fairly near 
neighbour of yours—Alderley, Cheshire” (Letters III.1247). Lewis likely wrote to 
 
1 While Colin Hardie (1906–1998) is today not as well known as many other members of 
the Oxford Inklings group, Humphrey Carpenter lists him as one of the nineteen “official” 
members in The Inklings (255–59); a fellow and tutor in Classics at Magdalen College from 
1936 until 1973, Hardie was invited in the 1940’s by C.S. Lewis to join both the Tuesday 
and Thursday gatherings (Bratman 237).  
2 This misspelling of Alan Garner’s name is typical of Lewis, who is never a careful speller 
in his letters or personal diaries.  
3 Lewis is right to point out this connection; while critics like Neil Philip have pointed out 
that the parallels between The Wierdstone of Brisingamen  and The Lord of the Rings  often 
derive from a sharing of sources rather than from a direct influence, it does seem as if 
Garner was adopting the high epic style of writing that Tolkien used, which in his voice 
tends to sound “ponderous or precious” (29). By the time Garner wrote Elidor, his third 
novel, this overly formal, artificial style is gone (29).  
I 
Laura N. Van Dyke 
202  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
Green about Garner’s novel because Green was a prolific writer and researcher 
in the areas of children’s literature, fantasy, and mythology; Green also clearly 
read Garner’s works, since his book Tellers Of Tales : Children’s Books and Their 
Authors from 1880 to 1968 4 includes both Lewis and Garner as noteworthy 
modern-day children’s writers, indicating he saw an affinity between their 
stories.5  
It is evident that there were significant points of connection between 
Lewis and Garner, although Garner has consistently disavowed any notion of 
influence. Nevertheless, he conceded in a 1989 interview that he may have 
“picked things up” without being aware of it; to the question of whether he had 
read “any modern writers,” he states, 
 
Not until I’d written several books myself, and then I read T.H. White. I 
might even have picked things up on the fringe, without knowing. I 
didn’t know Charles Williams, but my tutor at Oxford was one of the 
Inklings. Thus I was on the fringe of all of that, and I’ve no doubt that my 
tutor talked about things that C.S. Lewis had said the night before. (qtd. 
in Thompson) 
 
Similarly, in 1966 Garner wrote to his publisher: “I’ve said that, having finished 
Elidor, I noticed it was THE anti -Narnian fantasy, but this was done 
unconsciously” (qtd. in Howe). Garner’s relationship with the literary legacy of 
the Inklings is clearly a complicated issue for him. What made him, though, label 
his novel “THE anti-Narnia”? This is what this article will explore. I will argue 
that while Garner saw distinct differences between his Elidor and Lewis’s Narnia 
books, alongside these differences  are significance points of confluence, which 
make his novel ultimately complementary to rather than antithetical to Lewis’s 
fantasy books. 
On the surface, Elidor (1965) certainly parallels Lewis’s Narnia stories—
written during the previous decade, between 1950 and 1956—in some noticeable 
ways. As Catherine Butler puts it, the novel certainly “appears at first to be 
retreading Narnian territory” (“Modern Children’s Fantasy ” 227); the most 
obvious similarity is simply that the ensemble cast of characters in Garner’s 
fantasy is also made up of four siblings. Elidor is also a portal fantasy, where the 
four children leave post-war England and enter a secondary world to complete 
 
4 Originally published in 1946, this title refers to a revised and expanded edition that was 
published in 1969.  
5 Nine years after this exchange, Roger Lancelyn Green lent Poulton Hall, his house on the 
Wirral, for use in Peter Plummer’s television adaptation of The Owl Service, filmed in 1969, 
which suggests that by this time he and Garner were on relatively close terms (Ellen 
Garner et al., Filming the Owl Service, 13). 
Alan Garner’s Elidor: The “ Anti-Narnian Fantasy”? 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  203 
a quest and prevent the forces of evil from taking over. While the primary 
narrative frame of Elidor is set in Manchester and northern Cheshire and is more 
urban in setting and tone than most children’s fantasy of the post -war era, 
including Lewis’s, 6 the secondary world of Elidor is a setting inspired by the 
Celtic past, sharing some of the medieval tropes that fantasy stories like the 
Narnia books made so popular. In a Welsh folktale, Elidor was a priest who 
journeyed to a golden castle in a land whe re the sun was dim. 7 In Elidor the 
novel, Garner describes the golden walls of the castle Gorias and the half-lit sky 
of the land of Elidor. A kind of Otherworld, Elidor is represented as having been, 
in its glory, the land of Tír na nÓg, the Irish Land of Youth. Its four castles were 
the four cities of the Tuatha D é Danaan, the first inhabitants of mythic Ireland. 
Garner, though, grounds his fantasy in the primary world: the novel’s 
protagonists do visit Elidor, but only very briefly; most of the novel’s action 
occurs in Cheshire. The house the four children move int o in the beginning of 
the story is based on the house in which Garner himself spent his childhood 
(Garner, “Edge of the Ceiling” 9), and the setting of the novel occurs in and 
around present -day Manchester, lending an atmosphere of realism to this 
fantasy. 
The four children in Garner’s novel are Nicholas (the oldest), Helen, 
David, and Roland (the youngest). We learn in the opening chapter, “Thursday’s 
Child,” that their parents are in a transitional period of moving to a new house, 
and their placial liminal ity is underscored when they stand in the middle of 
Manchester and, out of boredom, decide to explore Thursday Street. Tony 
Watkins points out that, as the chapter heading’s allusion to the children’s 
nursery rhyme reminds us, Roland is “Thursday’s child,” and “Thursday’s child 
has far to go” (58). Roland is the hero of this tale, and much like Lucy Pevensie 
he is more attuned to spiritual reality than his siblings, who find it difficult to 
sustain belief in Elidor once they have returned home. Nicholas dis missively 
comments that Roland “is always imagining things” (Elidor 11), a comment that 
the rest of the family repeats (71). Later in the novel, Nicholas decides that the 
children’s memories of their time in Elidor were simply the result of a “mass 
hallucination,” brought about by shock (89), a dismissal that Roland finds as 
 
6 Garner’s conscious distancing of his work from that written by both Lewis and Tolkien 
likely originates at least in part from class differences coming into play as well: for 
instance, see Butler’s claim in her chapter “Modern Fantasy Literature” that Garn er was 
intentionally moving, in Elidor, away from the “middle -class and London -based” (228) 
protagonists of previous fantasy. 
7 Neil Philip writes that Garner accessed this story via the medieval story of “Elidor and 
the Golden Ball” by Giraldus Cambrensis, in which fairyland seems to be a symbol of lost 
innocence; Garner misunderstood Elidor to be a synonym for fairyland, when in  
Cambrensis’s story is it the personal name of the protagonist (159n2). 
Laura N. Van Dyke 
204  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
distressing as Lucy Pevensie does her siblings’ refusal to believe she has found 
a magical land on the other side of the wardrobe door in The Lion, the Witch and 
the Wardrobe.  
The epigraph for the novel comes from King Lear: “Childe Rowland to 
the Dark Tower came—”. Shakespeare’s reference to the old Scottish ballad was 
picked up by Robert Browning in his poem, titled the same, about the doomed 
quest of the knight Rowland. Interestingly, Walter Hooper gave the title The 
Dark Tower (1977) to one of Lewis’s posthumously published and incomplete 
manuscripts, a reference to the same Browning poem. In the ballad or tale from 
which the line comes, Rowland is one of four siblings, with two brothers and 
one sister, who, while playing next to a church, is kidnapped and taken to the 
“dark tower” by the king of Elfland. In Elidor, Roland, too, is one of four siblings, 
and it is while they are playing together at exploring Thursday Street that they 
encounter a strange church.  
In Garner’s novel, Thursday Street is in a part of Manchester where old 
housing is being demolished in order to make way for new developments. It is 
a wasteland of poverty, absence, and despair; the houses are “empty, and 
broken” (10), with no signs of care: “Grass grew in the cobbles everywhere, and 
in the cracks of the pavement. Doors hung awry. Nearly all the windows were 
boarded up or jagged with glass” (10). Then, though, the children spot a 
building that catches their attention: “Alone and black in th e middle of the 
wasteland stood a church” (12). In this juxtaposition lies symbolic and literal 
power. The children learn later that while it is not easy to cross between their 
world and Elidor, “there are places where they touch,” including this church, 
because it was once “battered by war, and now all the land around quakes with 
destruction. [It has] been shaken loose” (43). In other words, “Wastelands and 
boundaries: place that are neither one thing nor the other, neither here nor 
there—these are the gates of Elidor” (43). Lewis’s four Pevensie children initially 
cross into Narnia via a wardrobe, but in the chronologically -earlier book The 
Magician’s Nephew Digory and Polly enter Narnia from the Wood Between the 
Worlds, a hauntingly evocative place that is also neither here nor there, but what 
Digory calls one of the “in-between places” (III.37) that is only a threshold to the 
“Other World” (36). The first land they visit from this threshold is the ancient 
world of Charn, a ruined wasteland only half-lit by a dull light (IV.41).  
In Garner’s Elidor, the wasteland the four children enter is also a dim 
place, characterized by “gloom and stillness” and lit by a “dull” sky; the light 
has a “brittleness” to it that hurts Roland (24). Malebron, the “maimed king” of 
the wasteland of Elidor, who is a kind o f Fisher King archetype as well as a 
Merlin figure, tells Roland the story of Elidor’s fall:  
 
Alan Garner’s Elidor: The “ Anti-Narnian Fantasy”? 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  205 
The darkness grew  […]. It is always there. We did not watch, and the 
power of the night closed on Elidor. We had so much of ease that we did 
not mark the signs—a crop blighted, a spring failed, a man killed. Then 
it was too late—war, and siege, and betrayal, and the dying of the  light. 
(Elidor 32) 
 
Once, Elidor was a place of golden light, with four castles: “Findias . . . Falias . . . 
Murias . . . Gorias.  […] Men who walked like sunlight. Cloth of gold. Elidor” 
(33). Malebron tasks Roland with saving Elidor: “[You will] save this land! You 
will bring back light to Elidor!” (33). Malebron is a strange and often aloof figure, 
who seems both uninterested in the children and unwilling to offer them 
explanations for the state of the land or clarification of some of his enigma tic 
statements. However, he does tell them that each castle once held a Treasure —
a spear, a sword, a cauldron and a stone —and that the Treasures held the light 
of Elidor; “They are the seeds of flame from which all this land was grown” (34). 
It is these treasures he charges them with recovering.  
Roland’s brothers resist Malebron’s emphatic demands on them, 
reluctant to make personal sacrifices for a world that, to Nicholas, is “all mud 
and dust and rock. It’s dead, finished” (82). But to Roland, Elidor is “the most 
important thing there is” (129), and it is largely through his courage, as well as 
through Helen’s compassion for Findhorn the unicorn, that “life” springs again 
in Elidor and the “morning” comes. In his first novels, Garner often struggled to 
conclude his plots. Here too, Elidor ends ab ruptly: the children have a 
momentary vision of light returning to Elidor, but they remain in their world, 
and the last lines of the novel simply read, 
 
[Roland] cried his pain, and snatching the cup from Helen, he threw it 
and the railing at the windows. Nicholas and David threw their 
Treasures. They struck together, and the windows blazed outwards, and 
for an instant the glories of stone, sword, spear, a nd cauldron hung in 
their true shapes, almost of trick of the splintering glass, the golden light.  
The song faded. 
       The children were alone with the broken windows of a slum. (159–60) 
 
It is difficult to know how to interpret this strange ending to the story. Neil 
Philip calls it “profoundly ambiguous,” and admits he finds it “unsettling” 
despite recognizing that it is the “most powerful thing Garner had written up to 
that point” (15). Tony Watkins, in an early article on the novel, confesses that the 
“ending is enigmatic” and he is “not sure” he has understood it completely:  
 
But in so far as the land of Elidor seems to be symbolic, on one level of 
Roland’s childhood psychic experience and on another to symbolize the 
Laura N. Van Dyke 
206  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
mythic world which modern ways of thinking and living have forgotten, 
the ending points in two directions. First, it suggests that restoring life to 
Roland’s former psychic condition in childhood is not enough —in a 
sense, Roland has grown beyond Elidor wit hout his realizing it. 
Secondly, Garner realizes that there can be no simple return to the ways 
of thinking that the mythic world represents. (63) 
 
Watkins’s astute observation that the novel ultimately treats the 
viability of returning to the “mythic world” is born out by Garner’s own 
writings about myth. In describing myth, Garner writes “I should like to define 
‘myth’ as the dream -thinking of the p eople” (“ Voice in the Shadow” 150). 
However, he immediately follows up this definition by noting the difficulty that 
the modern mind has with reconciling mythic dreaming and the “thinking” we 
associate with the operation of the intellect. In the world today, most of us 
encounter myth in written, not oral, forms. Thus most of our experience of myth 
is “through the written word; that is: after the myth has entered history, linear 
time, been written down in linear words, thought of in lines” (150). For moderns 
trapped in linear time, we struggle to experience the full power of my th: “We 
pay a price for our literacy,” Garner says (151). The myths we have today, that 
have come to us mediated through history and literature, “are the secular 
versions” of myths, versions that are only dim echoes of the full, numinous myth 
(154). Garner does seem to believe that we can sometimes access real myth, or 
real dreaming, through writing —as long as the writer is a visionary or a poet 
who can write, he says, with enough “ambivalence [and] paradox that enables 
us to interpret what is being said, and what we read, just as we would if we were 
dreaming. That is the way, via the poet, to the myth, to the truth” (155).  
The ambiguity of Elidor’s conclusion is arguably Garner’s way of 
opening up his novel—a novel with flaws but also with visionary moments that 
border on the mythic—to this kind of interpretative act. Unlike the high fantasy 
of Tolkien, where the magic associated with the elves is fading as The Lord of the 
Rings ends, leaving the reader with a bittersweet sense of loss and melancholy, 
Cat Ashton argues that the second generation of British fantasy writers, 8 
including Oxford educated writers Susan Cooper and Alan Garner, are more 
likely to consider the nature of magic in a world that was rapidly changing, 
rather than passing away; thus, their stories propose a new relationship with the 
legendary past, “one that draws inspiration from it but prefers that the legends 
 
8 Critics frequently place Garner alongside other post -war British children’s fantasy 
writers such as Susan Cooper, Penelope Lively and Diana Wynne Jones, all of whom 
attended Oxford in the 1950s and were participants in what is sometimes referred to as 
the ‘second Golden Age’ of children’s literature (Butler, Four British Fantasists, 199). 
 
Alan Garner’s Elidor: The “ Anti-Narnian Fantasy”? 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  207 
themselves and the values derived from them be left safely in the past” (78). At 
the end of Elidor, the reader is left unsettled: what is the status of enchantment? 
What Ashton calls “the fantasy element” is, in this book, “intrusive, disruptive, 
and dangerous, […] interfere[ing] with the lives of the children and mak[ing] 
them objects of pursuit and attack” so that, when magic finally loosens its hold 
on the four siblings, the result is relief, not triumph (92). Ashton concludes: “The 
landscape in Garner’s  works seethes with magic, and knowing its history 
enriches the lives of those who inhabit it, but one should not wish for the mythic 
past to encroach on the mundane present” (93).  
 One of the strangest aspects of Elidor’s treatment of myth and magic is 
the portrayal of the unicorn Findhorn. As in Narnia, where the four children are 
the fulfillment of an ancient prophesy,9 in Elidor Malebron tells Nicholas, David, 
Helen and Roland that they are the subject of a prophe cy, or what he calls the 
“fragment of a dream: a dream that no sane man could bear to dream: a waking 
memory of what was to be”:  
 
  And they shall come from the waves. 
  And the Glory of Elidor shall pass with them.  
  And the Darkness shall not fade.  
  Unless there is heard the Song of Findhorn.  
  Who walks in the High Places. (45) 
 
The children discern right away the ambivalence of this cryptic prophesy: even 
if they “win” against the forces of darkness, the glory of Elidor still will pass 
away. As Roland says, “things’ll be worse, not better,” and Malebron agrees, 
though he adds, “W e are not at the end but at the beginning,” and with their 
help in finding Findhorn Elidor can perhaps have, again, at least some “islands 
in the darkness” (46). Findhorn is eventually revealed to be a unicorn, whose 
song does indeed bring light back to Elidor. However, Findhorn is not an Aslan 
figure who, after a sacrificial death, returns to life; Findhorn’s death is violent 
and tragic, and does not appear to readers to be a death he consents to freely. 
Neil Philip points out that, in an early version of Elidor, Garner wrote Findhorn 
as a lion, one who dies willingly (59). The final version of the text, though, 
perhaps to distance Elidor from The Lion, the Witch and the Wardrobe , depicts 
Helen cradling a dying unicorn’s head in her lap. Helen is crying, and the 
unicorn stares up at her, silent and wounded: when Roland looks in his eyes, he 
cannot describe what he sees because “it was almost too strong to bear” (158). 
The song Fin dhorn sings, after the children at last compel him to sing, is 
 
9 When Adam’s flesh and Adam’s bone, 
Sits at Cair Paravel in throne, 
The evil time will be over and done. (The Lion, the Witch and the Wardrobe VIII.76) 
Laura N. Van Dyke 
208  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
described as containing both “beauty and terror,” and bringing a bright light but 
yet being “not enough” to save Elidor; the children need to send their Treasures 
back through the doorway to Elidor as well (159), making it unclear what role 
Findhorn’s death really played.  
 It is also unclear what the final status of Elidor is. Garner himself, in 
interviews following the publication of Elidor, seems to vacillate on its nature: in 
1979 he told Neil Philip that the whole “metaphysics of Elidor is closely 
connected with Platonic philosophy,” and Roland’s narrative arc is a 
problematizing of the allure of this ideal: “when Roland looks into the eyes of 
the dying unicorn he sees the Platonic archetypal reality and realises that in 
order to secure Elidor, which he thought was reali ty but was only a parallel 
strand of Plato’s shadows at the back of the cave10 […] he has sacrificed reality” 
(qtd. in Philip 48–49). Like Philip, I do not find Garner’s statements here to be 
borne out by the novel11; it is not apparent in the narrative that Roland is wrong 
to work for Elidor’s freedom (or that he is “egocentric,” as Garner also claims). 
However, the maimed king of Elidor, Malebron, is a figure who indeed has “a 
sinister undercurrent,” and speaks to t he children in a demanding and overly 
authoritarian tone; Garner gives him sentences full of exclamation marks, 
leading Philip to describe him as “egotistical, unyielding, almost psychotic” (50). 
Nicholas, Roland’s oldest brother and the most skeptical of the children about 
Elidor, says about Malebron that he is “self -centered,” doesn’t care about the 
children’s safety, and has no right to expect them to sacrifice their whole lives to 
guard the Treasures ( Elidor 82). At this point in the novel, many readers likely 
share Nicholas’s evaluation.  
Elidor is not safe; it never becomes home for the children, even for 
Roland who is its fiercest defender. It is here, arguably, that Elidor most departs 
from the spell Lewis weaves in the Narnia books and perhaps leads Garner to 
 
10 It is worth considering this statement alongside Lewis’s use of the cave allegory in 
chapter twelve of The Silver Chair where the Narnian Underland is clearly Plato’s Cave 
and the Overworld functions as what Garner refers to here as “reality.” While Lewis’s debt 
to Plato is clear from his fiction, as James Bryson has pointed out Platonism played a 
significant role in leading Lewis to Christianity, and it remained a perennial influence on 
his thinking, going back at least to his work in the early 1920’s on the Cambridge Platonist 
Henry More, about whom Lewis began working on a doctoral dissertation (1 –2). Like 
Garner, Lewis writes fiction that engages with the “allure” of the Platonic ideal, but unlike 
Garner he does not attempt to “problematize” that allure but to avidly explore its 
philosophical and theological implications for sublunary life.  
11 To some degree Garner invites interpretations of his work that disagree with his own. 
While Garner does on occasion speak as an interpreter of his works, he also insists that he 
writes “imaginative fiction,” which he defines as fiction that is “for ambiva lence, not for 
instruction”: “When language serves dogma, then literature is lost. […] There can be no 
one meaning for a text. Even that of the writer is but an option” (“Aback of Beyond” 27).  
Alan Garner’s Elidor: The “ Anti-Narnian Fantasy”? 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  209 
calls his novel the “anti -Narnia.” While both Garner and Lewis are deeply 
mythic thinkers, Lewis comes to myth via his theological convictions; Garner, 
an avid reader of Carl Jung, 12 accesses myth through the psychological, not the 
religious, dimension. In his essay “The Edge of the Ceiling” Garner recalls a 
childhood illness that caused him to spend long days lying on his bed, in his 
room, staring at the ceiling above him. As he stared, he describes slipping in and 
out of a dream -like state, accessing the “world of the ceiling” which was a 
mythic space for him:  
 
The world of the ceiling was three-dimensional, objects were solid, visual 
perspectives true. I never ate or drank in the ceiling (as I later found was 
the rule for the Other World). There was no wind, no climate, no heat, no 
cold, no time. The light came from no source and was shadowless, as 
neon; but before I knew neon. And everywhere, everybody, everything 
was white. It was the genesis of the dead land of Elidor. (11) 
 
It is significant that Garner draws an explicit connection to this visionary state 
and the land of Elidor. He later writes, in the same essay, that he “felt not that I 
entered a world, but that a world entered me” (14).  
 Narnia is not a dead land, like Charn and like Elidor, but a land 
teeming with life: it is more wish -fulfillment than “Other World,” at least as 
Garner describes it. More precisely, Narnia is an imaginative analogy of what 
Lewis wants to point readers tow ards—a heaven that is a fulfillment of the 
longing Narnia elicits. What Lewis often pursues in his Narnia stories, as in all 
his imaginative writing, is the sensation of sehnsucht that was so integral to his 
personal journey; the search for joy, or that “u nsatisfied desire which is itself 
more desirable than any other satisfaction” ( Surprised 18), is what animated 
much of his fiction and is what continues to draw many readers to Narnia. 
Significantly, Garner has read enough Lewis to recognize this as one of Lewis’s 
central concerns. He refers to “what C.S. Lewis calls ‘joy’” in his essay “Inne r 
Time” (118), as well as in “The Voice that Thunders” when he discusses “what 
C.S. Lewis cunningly calls ‘joy’” (225), and recounts his decision to “pursue the 
Lewisian ‘joy’” (230). Narnia is escapist fiction in the sense Tolkien describes in 
“On Fairy-stories”; it points towards a world to which readers want to escape to, 
not as the flight of a deserter but as the escape of a prisoner. Tolkien and Lewis 
were both in agreement that fiction, especially the faery story or fantasy 
literature, feeds what they saw as a healthy impulse to be dissatisfied and 
maladjusted to an enclosed, human -built prison of a world; thus it is not their 
 
12 Garner read Jung prior to writing Elidor and has said that the Jung’s writing represents 
“the most impressive modern thought” that he has encountered (Alan Garner, “A Bit More 
Practice”; quoted in Butler, Four British Fantasists, 144).  
Laura N. Van Dyke 
210  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
fictional worlds that Tolkien or Lewis want readers to escape to —they do not 
want their reader’s desire to be “stopped” at their fantastical worlds, but to find 
in their sub -creations impetus to go beyond them. Similarly, characters in 
Narnia want to go “f urther up and further in”; like the chivalrous mouse 
Reepicheep, they want to go to Aslan’s Country, a land beyond the Narnia that 
for Lewis is, like our own earth, one of the Shadowlands. As Jewel the Unicorn 
says when he arrives at Aslan’s Country, “I ha ve come home at last! This is my 
real country! I belong here. This is the land I have been looking for all my life, 
though I never knew it till now. The reason why we loved the old Narnia is that 
it sometimes looked a little like this” (Last Battle XV.161). For Lewis’s characters 
to achieve “joy,” they need to leave their world. This is key to Lewis’s entire 
formulation of sehnsucht; it is a desire “which no experience in this world can 
satisfy,” because we were “made for another world” (Mere Christianity 137). This 
longing for our “true country” is what Lewis’s fantasy books are intended to 
elicit; all symbolic expressions of the inexpressible truth that the “object of life” 
is to “press on to that other country and to help others to do the same” give voice 
to it (137).  
 Garner’s posture towards the Other World is quite different. While it 
is the object of desire and longing, at times, it is also often the source of fear and 
anxiety. And most importantly, it is not a transcendent realm but an immanent 
world that is inters ecting and overlapping with our world. Cat Ashton argues 
that at the end of Elidor, when magic loses its hold, “the effect is relief, rather 
than triumph” (92). She continues: “The landscape in Garner’s works seethes 
with magic, and knowing its history enr iches the lives of those who inhabit it, 
but one should not wish for the mythic past to encroach on the mundane 
present” (93). Garner has frequently written and spoken about his deep 
connection to place and his decision to set many of his narratives in and around 
Alderley Edge, Chesire, where he grew up and continues to live. He writes in 
“Achilles in Altjira” about coming “from a line of working-class rural craftsmen 
in Cheshire” (120) and seeing the “physical immobility” of his family as the 
lifeline for his sense of self: “My family is so rooted that it ignores social 
classification by others. On one square mile of Cheshire hillside, Alderley Edge, 
the Garners are. We know our place. And this sense of fusion with a land rescued 
me” (123). Alderley Edge is core to Garner’s identity. In a time when, as Paul 
Kingsnorth puts it, more and more of us are “citizens of nowhere,” products of 
a globalized capitalist machine that so often produces deracinated people 
disconnected from any real roots in a specific, localized place, Garner is radically 
counter-cultural (“Citizens”). Among twentieth -century and contemporary 
British writers, in particular, Garner sits almost alone; few writers are rooted as 
Alan Garner’s Elidor: The “ Anti-Narnian Fantasy”? 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  211 
firmly in a place as he is. 13 In another essay, Kingsnorth attests to the power of 
Garner’s evocation of the local:  
 
A question that has been jabbing at me for some time is: how could a 
novel be written in which a living landscape was not just a backdrop, but 
a character: an actor in the drama, rather than its scenery? Are there 
novels in which non-human places are sensate? In which the mind of the 
world is made manifest in the places its human characters walk through? 
Having just tried to write one myself, I have been looking for precedents. 
So far, I have only discovered two writers who seem to even approach 
the question. […] [T]he writer who has done most to explore the noti on 
of a sensate landscape, though, is Alan Garner. The living, jolting, 
magical power of places is at the heart of almost everything he has 
written for the last five decades. A moor, a hill, a ridge, a wood: in 
Garner’s books these are not landscapes but conduits to an older, wilder 
magic. History tugs at them, and they tug in turn at the feet of the 
innocent people who happen to walk across them. (“We imagine”) 
 
 Thus it is not surprising that for Garner, the allure of finding his “true 
country” is less appealing than for Lewis. Garner’s “true country” is Cheshire—
a place that was once North-West Mercia, a landscape that gave us the language 
of Sir Gawain and the Green Knight, but now there is, Garner laments, no literature 
that “feed[s] from its soil” (“Achilles” 124), apart from his own writings. A 
persistent theme in Garner’s fiction is the presence of the land in the lives of his 
characters. Garner’s characters  live on or around Alderley Edge: readers can 
walk the paths and lanes they walk, and see the same caves, trees, and 
woodlands they saw, making Garner’s stories deeply immersive. This is likely 
part of the reason why readers respond the way they do to his novels; Elidor, 
said Neil Gaiman, “changed the way I saw the world” ( 83). Susan Cooper, a 
British fantasy writer with whom Garner has much in common and to whom he 
has often been compared, acknowledges that while all writers of fantasy today 
cannot help bu t “dip into old Tolkien’s Cauldron of Story,” Garner remains 
distinctive for his “mythic energy” ( 34–35). As Ursula Le Guin, too, has said, 
Garner is “more mythmaker than fantasist,” and, Cooper continues, “the myth 
that he makes is carved out of the land to which he belongs, the land from which 
he and his people came, the land in which he lives. And expressed, of course, in 
its language. He is above al l a writer of place, of a very particular place, and of 
the music that is its speech” (35). 
 This distinctiveness is likely why Garner is consistently averse to 
questions about literary influence posed by interviewers. Garner insists “I’ve no 
 
13 In the context of American literature, Wendell Berry is similarly place-based.  
Laura N. Van Dyke 
212  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
interest […] in being compared with other writers and their work” ( qtd. in 
Almond 9). When he was asked shortly after publishing Elidor about what he 
thought of the growing popularity of the fantasy genre, he stubbornly insisted 
on his claim to literary isolation14: “I’m not aware of it—I don’t read [fiction]. I’m 
sorry, the fantasy market, I know it exists but I don’t know what it is . . .  
[Because] critics start to be rather ignorant people, they start to say Tolkien, 
Tolkien, Tolkien” (Partington 23, qtd. in Taylor 176). In this light, Garner’s 
statements about Lewis’s Narnia books take on more context. It’s likely Garner 
was, understandably, weary of comparison with Tolkien as well as with Lewis, 
and so exaggerated his differences with both writers. Yet, all three clearly share 
a deep interest in myth and in the potential of fantasy literature to evoke 
secondary worlds rich in wonder and enchantment. While Garner saw Elidor, in 
particular, as an “anti -Narnia” text readers today are more likely to see i t as a 
complement to the mythopoeic tales of Lewis and his fellow Inklings.  
 Like Lewis, Garner too is concerned with the forces of modernist -
inflected disenchantment. However, Garner approaches this disenchantment 
from a different angle than does Lewis. Whereas Lewis’s Narnia stories frame 
the problem through a sacramental ontology that takes seriously the way the 
medieval social-cosmic model framed the “Other World,” or the eternal world, 
as Heaven a nd so grounds his secondary world in a temporal setting 
reminiscent of this medieval past, Garner is much more attuned to the way that 
in the modernist world we have lost imaginative contact not so much with time 
as with place. Both Lewis and Garner use th eir fiction to challenge the techno -
industrial reduction of the earth to mere matter, but they do it in different ways. 
It may be that in the twenty -first century, in the context of ever -increasing 
ecological crises that cut us off from the ancient pattern s of nature and remove 
us more and more from our rootedness in what Yi -Fu Tuan has called the 
“topophilic sentiment,” or our affective bond to our material environment (92 –
93), Garner’s fantasy will take on particular resonance with contemporary 
audiences. Elidor is not an anti -Narnia, but it may be a kind of companion text 
to Narnia, one that confronts reductive materialism by imagining this world, 
here and now, as an enchanted Other World. 
 
 
14 According to Garner, he does not feel at home with anything after The Tempest  
(“Achilles” 49), because the English literary style that developed after the early modern 
period and continues to this day “came from the library” and not from inherited culture 
and landscape; “In this way the academic world represented a suppression of individual 
style, which Garner witnessed first-hand when at Oxford. The heart and the head, emotion 
and intellect, ‘just did not meet’ he writes, ‘[and the] English graduates I have met . . .  
admit that they wanted to write, but they admit that because they were aware of the course 
behind them they daren’t: there was nothing else to say” (Taylor 190–1).

INTERVIEW WITH PROFESSOR JOHN MCKINNELL CONCERNING 
TOLKIEN ’S LECTURES ON BEOWULF  
ŁUKASZ NEUBAUER 
 
OHN MCKINNELL (B. 1942) IS PROFESSOR EMERITUS in the Department of English 
Studies and a Member of the Institute of Medieval and Early Modern Studies at the 
University of Durham. He grew up mostly in Scotland, where he received his secondary 
education at George Watsons College, Edinburgh, and went up to Worcester College, Oxford 
in October 1961 to read English. Professor McKinnell became attracted to medieval literature 
(Old Norse in particular), and, having gained his B.A. in 1964, spent eight months studying 
Icelandic manuscripts at the Arnamagnæan Institute in Copenhagen. In April 1965, he was 
appointed to a lectureship in English Language and Medieval Literature at Durham 
University, where he remained throughout his career, retiring as a professor in 2007. His two 
main fields of research interest are Old Norse eddic poetry and medieval drama. He is married, 
with three children and five grandchildren, and lives in Durham. 
 
Łukasz Neubauer (ŁN):  When and how did it happen that you came to be 
taught by Tolkien? In the early 1960s, at the time when you were studying 
as an undergraduate at Worcester College in Oxford, Tolkien had already 
retired (he would deliver his valedictory address on 5 June 1959).1 
John McKinnell (JM):  I went up to Oxford in October 1961, two years after 
Tolkien had retired, and did not specialize in medieval literature until a year 
later, so my memory of him is based only on a few lectures that he very 
generously gave after he had officially retired. He gave a series of eight 
lectures on Beowulf in Michaelmas term of 1962 (14 October –8 December),2 
standing in for C.L. Wrenn during his absence on sabbatical leave (Scull and 
 
1 Tolkien’s retirement speech, ‘Valedictory Address to the University of Oxford, 5 June 
1959,’ delivered in the Merton College Hall, was published posthumously. 
2 Tolkien regularly lectured on Beowulf during his five-year employment at the University 
of Leeds (1920–25) and the twenty-year tenure as a Rowlinson and Bosworth Professor of 
Anglo-Saxon (1925–45). For more information on the specific courses of lectures he gave 
in those years, see Scull and Hammond, Chronology [S&H] (122 ff. [Leeds] and 142 ff. 
[Oxford]). Speaking of his return to lecturing, Tolkien modestly said that it “has proved a 
much greater burden than [he] expected” and that “[i]t has taken much more work that 
[he] guessed to shake off the dust of seven teen years off matter which [he] once thought 
[he] knew” (qtd. in S&H 631).

health.5 I knew him only from these lectures, so not on a personal level. 
Besides, he was based at Merton, while I was at Worcester College. 6 I was 
lucky enough to have a variety of outstanding tutors for my medieval work, 
but they did not include either J.R.R. Tolkien or his son Christopher.

room was so full that some people had to sit in the window seats. Since the 
room was T-shaped, this meant that not everyone had a very good view of 
the lecturer, and some members of the audience could not see each other. 8 
This was, of course, mainly because of the fame of his fiction, but, as for 
myself, I was equally interested in both sides of his work, and perhaps even

more in his academic work, because I wanted to know more about his

ŁN: Professor Przemysław Mroczkowski, a Polish scholar who attended some 
of Tolkien’s classes in the Michaelmas term of 1957, 9 once said that his 
lectures “had a keen following, but they weren’t [...] especially popular” 
(Grotta 78). Of course, in the second half of the 1950s Tolkien’s popularity as 
a writer of fiction was on the upswing, but he was also, at the same time, a 
highly esteemed scholar who had made his name as an authority on Old and 
Middle English literature and language. You only went to a few of his 
classes, but what do you think of them? What was he like as a lecturer? 
JM: His lectures were so absorbing that, after each of them, I found my notes 
quite sparse. I had simply become too fascinated to write more than a few 
lines, which usually amounted to less than a single side. I must say he had 
the kind of enthusiasm for Beowulf that inspired me to want to read it again. 
His lectures gave the impression of being affable performances among 
friends, probably because he assumed that his audience would have the 
same enthusiasm for the poem that he had himself. 
 
ŁN: According to Humphrey Carpenter, Tolkien lectured “fluently, chiefly from 
his notes, but with occasional impromptu additions” (117). Indeed, he is said 
to have regularly made extended digressions, shifting his attention from the 
main topic of his class and, thus focusing on a word or phrase that happened 
to catch his philological eye. Did you have a similar experience when you 
attended Tolkien’s lectures? 
JM: I found his lectures fluent and interesting, but I was very young and 
inexperienced—at that time merely twenty years old —and did not notice 
him making any digressions. However, my overall impression was that he 
lectured primarily on various themes in Beowulf and used individual words 
and phrases mainly as examples or clues to make sure his students would 
acquire a broader understanding of the poem rather than as a succession of 
textual cruces (which would probably have been C.L. Wrenn’s approach), so 
I suppose I did not regard them as digressions.10 
 
9 For more information on Przemysław Mroczkowski and the history of his friendship 
with Tolkien, see my own article on “The ‘Polish Inkling.’”

ŁN: Carpenter also claimed that the quality of Tolkien’s lectures was affected by 
his uncommon quickness of speech and “indistinct articulation” (132), the 
latter of which the writer would ascribe to the tongue injury he once 
sustained during a rugby match (48 ). As a result, his students “had to 
concentrate hard in order to hear” (132) what he was actually saying. 
Mroczkowski even said that Tolkien’s “speech was extremely difficult to 
follow, since it was all but inarticulate” (qtd. in Grotta 78). In the opinion of 
the Polish scholar, “Tolkien didn’t care to articulate; he simply expected and 
assumed that you could follow him with ease” (78). Moreover, in the words 
of Nevill Coghill, who evidently enjoyed Tolkien’s classes, it was sometimes 
hard to keep up w ith “this rapid machine gun fire of his lecturing” (qtd. in 
Grotta 77). Was your experience during the lectures you attended in any way 
comparable to those that are recalled by Tolkien’s biographers? 
JM: I did not find him hard to follow, although his delivery was indeed quite 
quick, but I suppose I was prepared to concentrate hard because I admired 
him, both for his academic work 11 and for his fiction. 12 (Incidentally, I also 
attended Coghill’s 1961 –62 lectures on modern drama, and his style was 
very different—much more leisurely and inclined to personal reminiscences 
of his own discussions with the dramatists he was discussing —equally 
enjoyable, but quite different from Tolkien. 
 
ŁN: According to Carpenter, Tolkien would often commence his classes with a 
sudden cry of Hwæt!, the opening word of many an Old English poem 
(including Beowulf), which some students, mostly, it seems, novice 
undergraduates, would take to be “Quiet!” (133). 13 Does that ring a bell to 
you? 
JM: Oh, yes. I do remember one lecture which he not only began with Hwæt! but 
went on to recite—not at all times from memory, though—the first 150 or so 
lines of Beowulf.14 In Old English, of course, and without any translation. I 
 
comments on the poem’s multiple cruces (?); (VI) kingship and heroic ethos; (VII) peoples 
in Beowulf; (VIII) notes on the poem’s characters.

think the point was to give a sense of what the poem would have been like 
in live performance,15 and he was a very gifted performer of it. His delivery 
was rapid and exciting. Pausing for translation or explanation would have

ŁN: During his lectures, Tolkien is also said to have recited longer passages from 
various texts, from Beowulf to Sir Gawain and the Green Knight  and The 
Canterbury Tales (Bowers 3). As his former student —and, subsequently, 
writer and scholar—J.I.M. Stewart recalls, “he could turn a lecture room into 
a mead hall in which he was the bard and [those who sat in the audience] 
were the feasting, listening guests” (qtd. in Carpenter 133). Do you recollect 
any such ‘magical’ moments? And, if so, would he rather read these passages 
from his notes, the book, or would he rather quote them from memory? 
JM: He normally had the text in front of him —in the case of Beowulf, it was, I 
think, Friedrich J. Klaeber’s edition (1943) rather than that of C.L. Wrenn 
(1953, later revised by W.F. Bolton) —but Tolkien did not seem to refer to it 
much, so he was probably reciting largely from memory, and the 
performative effect was exac tly as Stewart recalls it. He did sometimes 
allude to other literary works—I remember one or two to Sir Gawain and the 
Green Knight —which I think may have been on the spur of the moment. 
Incidentally, student rumor had it that Tolkien disliked Wrenn’s much drier 
style of teaching, and even that the character of Gollum was partly based on 
Wrenn, who was extremely short -sighted and had a habit of referring to 
unsatisfactory tutees (in their presence) as ‘it’ (‘it hasn’t even read . . . ’ etc.), 
but that was, perhaps, only student gossip. 
 
ŁN: You were also lucky to attend some of the lectures given by Tolkien’s 
youngest son (and his future literary executor), Christopher. When was that? 
Which classes were they? 
JM: I went to Christopher’s lectures on the heroic code. I think it was in 1961 –
62, my first year at the university. I found them so useful that I went to them 
again in the following year.

ŁN: It was certainly not easy for Christopher Tolkien to pursue the academic 
career, following in his father’s footsteps. There must have been some people 
within the broadly understood academia who, advertently or not, measured 
his philological expertise against that of the long -time professor of Anglo -
Saxon (Pembroke College, 1925 –45) and English Language and Literature 
(Merton College, 1945 –59). Nevertheless, would you say that there w ere 
some noticeable analogies between J.R.R. Tolkien’s own lectures and the 
ones that were given by his son? Were Christopher’s classes as informative 
and entertaining as those of his father?  
JM: Again, I only experienced Christopher’s lectures, not his tutorials (he taught 
at New College, becoming a fellow of that college in 1963, while I was at 
Worcester; at that time, University lecturers with no college fellowship were 
the most junior members of staff, and could be hired by a college to tutor 
their students with no security of tenure and rather poor rates of pay). 17 He 
was good-looking, charismatic and a bit of a matinée idol, so the front row 
of his lectures was always full of admiring girls. I (and some of his other 
male listeners) used to sit in the back row and pretend not to be too 
impressed, but in fact his lec tures were clear, fluent and interesting. I 
remember particularly how he used the Hrafnkels saga and the eddic poem 
Atlakviða to illustrate the heroic duty of vengeance, and this was part of what 
first attracted me to Old Norse literature. 18 It also made me realize how 
different Germanic traditions influenced each other, and, in turn, led to 
much interesting discussion about the Sîfrit/Siegfried/Sigurðr cycle with my 
friend and fellow Worcester student Chris Wells, later a fellow at St. 
Edmund Hall and Professor of medieval German literature. 
 
ŁN: Do you think that either (or both) of the two scholars had any direct or 
indirect influence on your scholarly interests? Could your decision to pursue 
the academic path have been in any way determined or at least influenced 
by the author of The Lord of the Rings and/or his youngest son? 
JM: I was certainly influenced by both of them —firstly by reading The Lord of 
the Rings when I was still at school. I remember, for instance, reading part of

it on a train journey from Edinburgh to visit my grandmother in Derbyshire

”BETTER TO REIGN IN HELL ”: AMBIVALENCE AND ORCS IN THE LAY 
OF THE CHILDREN OF HÚRIN  
MATTHEW THOMPSON-HANDELL 
 
N AN EARLIER ARTICLE ,1 I EXAMINED THE WAY  in which J.R.R. Tolkien's 
unfinished Lay of the Children of Húrin 2 (written in alliterative verse mainly in 
the first half of the 1920s) demonstrates the aesthetic of the sublime, in the form 
described and formulated by Edmund Burke in his 1757 work A Philosophical 
Enquiry into the Origin of Our Ideas of the Sublime and Beautiful. In that work, Burke 
provided several examples of sources of the sublime by quoting from John 
Milton's Paradise Lost.3 The significance of the sublime to Milton’s work was also 
identified by the 18 th century critic, poet and lexicographer Samuel Johnson, 
who said: “The characteristick quality of his poem is sublimity. He sometimes 
descends to the elegant, but his element is the great. He can occasionally invest 
himself with grace; but his natural port  is gigantick loftiness. He can please 
when pleasure is required; but it is his peculiar power to astonish” (122 –3). In 
terms of connections between Tolkien and Milton, all of this is quite tenuous. 
However, there is a passage in the Lay of the Children of Húrin which can, if one’s 
ear is cocked at the right angle, be heard like an echo of a passage in Book I of 
Paradise Lost. Even if one hears it, that might be of just passing interest, one of 
countless literary resonances which have proved a fruitful hunting ground for 
 
1 “‘Their Limbs Were Loosened in Listening Horror’: The Sublime in the Lay of the Children 
of Húrin”; see bibliography. 
2 Published in The Lays of Beleriand, being the third volume of The History of Middle-earth. 
3 Examples can be found in Burke’s Part II, Sections III, IV and XIV, concerning obscurity 
and light. 
I 
Notes 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  223 
Tolkien scholars during the last seventy years. But the echo in question may 
have a small part to play in the lively debate about the nature of Tolkien’s Orcs. 
The passage from Paradise Lost  describes the moment when the 
unfurling of Satan’s standard prompts the assembly of fallen angels to raise their 
voices and their weapons. The passage from the Lay describes the moment when 
the sight of Thangorodrim prompts the Orc-band to raise their voices and their 
weapons. The passages are reproduced here: 
 
Paradise Lost, Book I, lines 535–49: 
Who forthwith from the glittering Staff unfurl’d 
Th’Imperial Ensign, which full high advanc’t 
Shon like a Meteor streaming to the Wind 
With Gemms and Golden lustre rich imblaz’d, 
Seraphic arms and Trophies: all the while 
Sonórous mettal blowing Martial sounds: 
At which the universal Host upsent 
A shout that tore Hells Concave, and beyond 
Frighted the Reign of Chaos and old Night. 
All in a moment through the gloom were seen 
Ten thousand Banners rise into the Air 
With Orient Colours waving: with them rose 
A Forest huge of Spears: and thronging Helms 
Appear’d, and serried Shields in thick array 
Of depth immeasurable [...]. 
 
Lay of the Children of Húrin, lines 994–1007 
Like a throbbing thunder    in the threatening deeps 
of cavernous clouds    o’ercast with gloom 
now swelled on a sudden    a song most dire, 
and their hellward hymn    their home greeted; 
flung from the foremost    of the fierce spearmen, 
who viewed mid vapours    vast and sable 
the threefold peaks    of Thangorodrim, 
it rolled rearward,    rumbling darkly, 
like drums in distant    dungeons empty. 
Then a werewolf howled;    a word was shouted 
like steel on stone;    and stiffly raised 
their spears and swords    sprang up thickly 
as the wild wheatfield    of the wargod’s realm 
with points that palely    pricked the twilight. 
 
 
Notes 
224  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
There are of course differences in these two descriptions. The passage from 
Paradise Lost  has a grander, almost bombastic tone, in the face of which the 
passage from the Lay appears rather more subdued and gloomy. It is however 
in the imagery of the assembled forces, and in their response to the power that 
rules them, that resemblances begin to appear. 
The first of these can be seen in the martial imagery of the two passages. 
The fallen angels are described as “A Forest huge of Spears” while the Orcs are 
“fierce spearmen” (though also bearing swords). There is also an echo of 
Milton’s “Forest” where Tolkien describes how the weapons of the Orcs “sprang 
up thickly / as the wild wheatfield.” More profoundly, Milton’s fallen angels are 
so moved by the sight of Satan’s standard that they call out in “A shout that tore 
Hell’s Concave.” Though the voices of the Orcs do not carry the same force, the 
same dynamic can be seen when they catch a glimpse of Thangorodrim “mid 
vapours    vast and sable” and are impelled to break into “a song most dire.” 
And just as Satan’s standard represents the sense of comradeship and belonging 
which binds the fallen angels together, so too do Thangorodrim—and its master, 
Morgoth—represent a place which the Orcs (to use Tolkien’s word) call “home.” 
Indeed, that word appears in one of the most remarkable lines in the passage in 
the Lay, a line which not only echoes the infernal setting of Book I of Paradise 
Lost, but also reinforces the sense of belonging —of worship even —that binds 
the Orcs to Morgoth: “and their hellward hymn   their home greeted.” 
All of this might be little more than a literary curiosity. Consider 
though that a major theme in the criticism of Paradise Lost concerns the nature of 
Milton’s depiction of Satan, or more particularly, whether he is (or should be 
seen as) a sympathetic or even heroic figure. This Satan may not be a man of 
wealth and taste, nor is he by any stretch monstrous in the way that Milton’s Sin 
and Death are. His silver-tongued rhetoric in the opening Books of Paradise Lost 
would have him raised up as a paragon of wronged virtue, a champion of the 
unjustly treated fallen angels. Much has been written by critics (including by 
C.S. Lewis in his A Preface to Paradise Lost) that Milton is not presenting Satan as 
the hero of the work, though the fact that this debate has consumed so much 
critical attention is a testament to the ambivalence of Milton’s depiction, an 
ambivalence rather neatly summed up by one critic in his edition of the work: “Is 
[Satan’s] heroism put in its place, or does it run off with the poem?” (Milton, xx). 
Let us return then to Tolkien and the Orcs. Their depiction in the 
passage quoted above has a hint of something high -minded (if not quite noble) 
about it which, in the face of the evil that they undeniably represent, creates an 
ambivalence which is of the same type (though not to the same degree) as that 
which Milton creates. The hint lies in how the Orcs raise their voices in song, 
joining in a hymn —a hymn—to the longed -for home that they glimpse in the 
far distance. I am admittedly playing devil’s advocat e, though the sense of 
Notes 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  225 
ambivalence is amplified by the imagery of the earlier campfire scene soon after 
Beleg joins Túrin and his band of outlaws: “Then a song on a sudden    soaring 
loudly— / and the trees up -looming    towering harkened / […] Their blades 
were bared,    as blood shining / in the flame of the fires    while they flashed and 
touched” (ll.624–38). Here the outlaws raise their voices and their weapons in 
comradeship, just as the Orcs will do. 
The passage considered above is but a fleeting example in an 
unfinished work, but it provides a glimpse of a more nuanced depiction of the 
Orcs, a topic which has been the subject of critical work in recent years. In a 2000 
essay, Tom Shippey argues that Orcs are moral beings, examining their dialogue 
in The Lord of the Rings  in order to highlight how they “recognize the idea of 
goodness, appreciate humor, value loyalty, trust, group cohesion, and the ideal 
of a higher cause than themselves, and condemn fai lings from these ideals in 
others” (186). In a 2010 article, Robert Tally argues that Tolkien “occasionally 
reveals a not entirely unsympathetic view of Orcs, in which what might be 
called their simple humanity becomes visible” (22). He finds this humanity  in 
the dialogues between Shagrat and Gorbag (fearful but at the same time 
skeptical of the “Big Bosses”) and in the personalities and attitudes of the Orcs 
who are taking Merry and Pippin to Isengard.4 In a 2011 article, David Tneh also 
looks closely at the dialogue between Shagrat and Gorbag, drawing out 
elements such as humor and loyalty, as well as a desire for peace and comfort, 
in support of an argument that “the orcs do show some form of human behavior 
that we recognize and relate to” (42). What is particularly interesting is that the 
depiction of the Orcs in the Lay of the Children of Húrin , raising their voices in 
song at the sight of Thangorodrim, provides a further example of what one 
might call “human” behavior, but one whose high -minded quality is rather 
distinct from the earthy and colloquial dialogues in The Lord of the Rings . It 
would have been fascinating to see how Tolkien might have developed this 
thread of Orcish behavior —another reason  to regret the abandonment by 
Tolkien of the Lay.

ST. JACK’S EXPERIMENT HOUSE FOR JUVENILE DELINQUENTS : 
RESPONDING TO ASLAN ’S BREATH  BY MATTHEW DICKERSON  
G. CONNOR SALTER 
 
N ROALD DAHL’S CHILDREN’S BOOK MATILDA,1 Miss Honey asks the protagonist 
what books she likes. Matilda answers, “I liked The Lion, The Witch, and the 
Wardrobe […] I think C.S. Lewis is a very good writer. But he has one failing. 
There are no funny bits in his books” (80). 2 As Robert C. Stroud observes, 
various online writers, most notably on the blogs Like But Better (“Nathan”) and 
Bookworms and Bluestockings (Inkling) have critiqued Matilda’s taste while 
offering compare -and-contrast discussions about Lewis and Dahl. One t opic 
rarely explored in these discussions is how Lewis sometimes offers something 
Matilda finds very funny: revenge. 
 
1 Dahl’s work has been controversial for some decades now, both due to anti -Semitic 
comments which led his estate to apologize “for the lasting and understandable hurt 
caused by some of Roald Dahl’s statements” in 2020 (“Apology for anti -Semitic 
comments”), and to concerns about cruel language or cruel treatment of characters in his 
fiction. I do not condone Dahl’s views but feel his global reputation makes it essential to 
consider the implicit philosophy within his work. I hope this paper helps to consider the 
implications of how Dahl treats characters. 
2 I wish to thank Edwin Woodruff Tait for directing my attention to this passage. 
I 
Notes 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  227 
The topic of Lewis and revenge leads Matthew Dickerson to interesting 
territory in his recent book Aslan’s Breath: Seeing the Holy Spirit in Narnia .3 
Dickerson explores the final Experiment House scene in The Silver Chair, one of 
the “few scenes in Lewis’ writing that bother me in the sense that I think he got 
it wrong (from a moral or spiritual viewpoint)” (85). Throughout this book, 
Dickerson contends that Aslan’s breath or kiss represents the Holy Spirit. Hence, 
when Aslan kisses the foreheads of Jill Pole, Eustace Scrubb, and King Caspian 
before they “enter the school and with the flats of their swords and a switch they 
proceed to overcome and beat the gang of bullies and send them running” (85), 
the kiss must be seen “ as an anointing, and his breath as a sending of the Holy 
Spirit” (86). This is divinely sanctioned violence. 
The implications are troubling. We may think of Rowan Williams’s 
concern that “a lot of the time, [Lewis] inhabits quite uncritically the conventions 
of chivalric adventure, ‘knightly’ slaughter of the wicked” (44). Dickerson 
doesn’t flatly object to Lewis  depicting Holy Spirit -sent warriors but worries 
that the Experiment House cleansing isn’t Christ -like. He notes that the scene 
features Aslan’s followers attacking those who don’t know him; while Christ 
upset members of his own religion (the Jews) by clea nsing the temple, he was 
never violent to the Romans (Dickerson 85–86). Hence, the scene reads more like 
revenge than divine justice; less like comeuppance, more like Lewis “playing 
out his imaginative fantasies from his own awful boarding school experience” (85). 
Dickerson’s point challenges scholars who may want to discuss Christ 
and justice in Lewis’s fiction without discussing pain. For example, Marek 
Oziewicz sees Lewis as primarily exploring compensational justice —which 
Oziewicz defines as emphasizing justice’ s emotional and moral process, 
highlighting an unjust action’s consequences, seeing offenders as responsible 
human beings who must face the consequences of their actions, and finally 
reconciling offenders to others (54).  4 He sees compensational justice in scenes 
like Edmund and Eustace facing their choices’ consequences before repenting 
(55–58). When offenders refuse to repent, compensational justice keeps them 
from continuing criminal activity—such as Governor Gumpus being fired in The 
Voyage of the “Dawn Treader” or Rabadash becoming a donkey who cannot leave 
Tashbaan in The Horse and His Boy  (58–60). Oziewicz also reads cases where 
Lewis’s villains die or gradually suffer as compensational justice since the 
offenders see their fates are consequences of their actions: Jadis realizes in The 
Magician’s Nephew  that she has gained an immortal but miserable life, 
 
3 I must also thank Dickerson and the participants in his Inkling Folk Fellowship 
presentation on the book (“Aslan’s Breath”), especially Roy Wallen, for their wise 
responses to my questions about Dickerson’s argument. 
4 For a summary of Oziewicz’s other work on this subject, see Dilbeck. 
Notes 
228  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
treacherous Miraz gets killed by treacherous Telmarines in Prince Caspian, and 
Tash kills Shift and terrifies Ginger until losing his power of speech in The Last 
Battle (61–62). 
While Oziewicz’s argument is compelling, Dickerson shows that not 
all Narnian justice is compensational. Shift and Ginger may see their 
punishment is clearly consequences for their actions, but neither the Experiment 
House students nor the Head have that o pportunity. If compensational justice 
keeps offenders from repeating mistakes, then the fact that Head continues to 
be a busybody in other school jobs, and a busybody on the national stage when 
her friends “got her into Parliament where she lived happily e ver after” (Silver 
Chair 16.216) is rather unjust. We may also consider smaller Narnian 
comeuppance scenes that aren’t very compensational—like Aslan seeing a man 
beat a boy and turning the man into a tree ( Prince Caspian  14.195). 
Compensational justice explains much of Lewis’ s moral framework in Narnia, 
but there are large, school-bully-sized, exceptions. 
But if Lewis’s vision of justice is multilayered, if he sometimes also 
offers retributive justice, which may verge on vengeance, a more fruitful 
discussion becomes possible. Dickerson is not the first to see the Experiment 
House cleansing as Lewis’s reveng e on real -life tormentors: Terry Lindvdall 
notes in his landmark study Surprised by Laughter 5 that the police assume the 
Head is insane, paralleling how Lewis’s headmaster at Wynard School, 
Reverend Capon, was institutionalized (362). We may easily compare Lewis to 
Dahl here: as the Like But Better article notes, both men had terrible boarding 
school experiences. Helen Lewis observes how many Dahl stories feature 
retributive, perhaps vicarious, justice against bullies and schoolteachers. For 
example, Dahl turned his experience of being caned for allegedly cheating (More 
About Boy  129–38) into fiction in Danny, the Champion in the World  (112–14). 
Danny’s father is enraged at what happened ( Danny 116–18), perhaps offering 
catharsis to Dahl, whose father died when he was three. Lewis’s Christianity 
may have given him moral values that the secular Dahl didn’t follow, but 
Dickerson challenges us to see that Lewis wasn’t always Christlike about school 
trauma. 
Furthermore, Lewis and Dahl enjoy combining retributive justice with 
humor, which means we must extend Dickerson’s point: this is not just revenge, 
but comic revenge. Comic revenge provokes different reactions in readers than 
straight vengeance. It may seem more acceptable. Maria Nikolajeva argues that 
Dahl’s children’s books fulfill a classic function by being “disgusting in 
combination with humour” (Anderson). Dahl’s revenge scenes (especially in 
 
5 I have used the original 1996 edition here because it includes material on Lewis’s story 
“The Shoddy Lands” excised from the revised 2012 edition. 
Notes 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  229 
Matilda) are often comic, as is the Experiment House cleansing. The Experiment 
House cleansing isn’t even Lewis’s darkest comic revenge. In Prince Caspian , 
Aslan and Bacchus pass a school and frighten “a number of boys who looked 
very like pigs,” whom Lewis implies are transformed into pigs (14.196). Bacchus 
is under Aslan’s guidance, so, like in the Experiment House scene, this is 
divinely ordained comeupp ance. Given that Narnians avoid eating talking 
beasts but are not vegetarians (Myers 6), we assume the schoolboys eventually 
became food, a far worse fate than the Experiment House students being 
expelled (Silver Chair 16.216). Yet even the Prince Caspian scene is comic: “[f]ew 
can resist the enjoyment […] of seeing a classroom of smug, stuffy, and conceited 
little brats in starched collars ‘get theirs’” (Lindvall 165). 
These examples challenge us to consider whether Lewis has a more 
provocative sense of humor than often discussed. Many scholars have 
considered Lewis’s approach to humor, sometimes in unexpected works. For 
example, Joe Ricke has recently explored how Lewis  deploys humor in his 
literary criticism.6 Scholars such as Peter J. Schakel have considered Lewis as a 
satirist, generally focusing on one of two books. Many mention The Screwtape 
Letters, as when Charles A. Huttar notes that the book offers “satire” and “moral 
instruction,” two genres with “connections to an epistolary tradition going back 
to ancient times” (91). Others explore That Hideous Strength , as when James 
Prothero compares the novel to Jonathan Swift’s satires (7, 13). Sanford Schwartz 
addresses how important it is to read That Hideous Strength as satire, particularly 
as Lewis wrote that he “wanted farce as well as fantasy” in this novel (185n7; 
c.f. “A Reply to Professor Haldane” 72). 
Some scholars have seen Lewis offering dark comedy. Nancy -Lou 
Patterson sees humor in the N.I.C.E. dinner in That Hideous Strength, comparing 
it to the chaotic feast in Lewis Carroll’s Through the Looking -Glass (“Banquet at 
Belbury” 8). Lindvall sees taboo humor in the scene when Uncle Andrew 
describes his experiments that made guinea pigs explode (274). Chris 
Armstrong’s paper for the 2018 International Congress on Medieval Studies 
considers how Lewis emulates the humor in medieval mystery plays and 
Francis of Assisi’s guidance to view our bodies as “Brother Ass” by treating 
death and desire humorously in works like The Four Loves (“Death, Desire, and 
the Sacramental Function of Humor”). 
By far, Lindvall offers the most detailed summary of Lewis offering 
comic comeuppance scenes. He mentions not just the Experiment House 
 
6 Given Ricke’s point that Lewis deploys satire in under-discussed ways within his literary 
work, more research must be done in this area. Ricke has offered an intriguing suggestion in an 
online conversation: first, scholars need an anthology of Lewis’s humorist writings, from 
contributions to Punch to his essays; second, someone needs to produce a monograph focusing 
on Lewis as a humorist (not just his sense of humor, a topic that Lindvall covers well).

cleansing and the Telmarine schoolboys becoming pigs, but also a teacher and 
her spoiled girl students fleeing Aslan (Lindvall 259; c.f. Prince Caspian 14.193–
194), Rabadash becoming a donkey (Lindvall 210 –211; c.f. Horse and His Boy  
15.208–211), Narnian animals terrorizing animal experimenter Uncle Andrew 
(Lindvall 211–212; c.f. Magician’s Nephew 11.129–133), animals helping to kill the 
N.I.C.E. scientists who experimented on them at the dinner (Lindvall 358; c.f. 
That Hideous Strength 16.347–350), Tash killing Shift (Lindvall 332; c.f. Last Battle 
13.143), and Malacandrians laughing at Weston (209 –210; c.f. Out of the Silent 
Planet 19.128). While Dickerson considers how the shocking events affect the 
bullies, Lindvall prefers to highlight how the bullies’ humiliation shows their 
flaws; for example, he highlights Experiment House’s head teacher being 
shocked when the cleansing occurs  as Lewis satirizing progressive educators 
(361). Rowan Williams concurs and is more critical: for him, “the facile satire 
against progressive education is too obviously Lewis indulging in partisan 
grumbling, and the trouncing of the school bullies is a forced and blustering bit 
of school-story cliché” (44). 
While all of these scholars provide excellent insights, they stop short of 
considering just how dark Lewis can be. To say that Lewis offers darkly comic 
retributive punishments goes beyond satire; Jane Austen satirizes foolish 
minister via Mr. Collins but never has Mr. Collins suffer for his foolishness. To 
say that Lewis offers a comedy beyond satire, mixed with justice too vengeful 
to be Christlike, that mentions the body (riding crops hitting bullies, etc.) but 
not to remind us of “Brother Ass,” means that Lewis is not just a talented 
children’s author. He is a provocative children’s author. Not unlike Dahl. 
We may ask, do Dahl and Lewis parallel each other in other ways? 
They had more in common than the fact they were both world war veterans 7 
who lost a parent young and suffered in boarding school. For example, they 
both declined CBEs (Stroud; c.f. Flood). Their interest in pain may have had 
similar roots: critics have seen Dahl’s emphasis on cruel punishments as sadistic 
(Leith) and Lewis’s Philomastix letters to Albert Greeves discuss having teenage 
sadistic fantasies. We may ponder whether Lewis’s darkest schoolboy revenge 
scenes show traces of his old sadism. 
However, comparing Lewis to Dahl is especially fruitful if we consider 
how darkly comedic punishments combine humor with horror. Many have 
discussed horror’s intersection with humor, from horror authors like M.R. James 
amusing their friends (M.R. James: Ghost Writer, 00:54:41)8 to writers like Richard 
 
7 For a deeper look at Lewis and Dahl as world war veterans who became children’s 
authors, see Reist and Snyder. 
8 It may be worth noting here that Lewis’s personal humor included pranks, as seen when 
he gave Walter Hooper a bath towel when he asked to use the bathroom (Neal). 
Notes 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  231 
Christian Matheson bridging the genres (and analyzing the genres’ intersection 
in his essay “They Laughed When I Howled at the Moon”). Dahl often has an 
in-between place in this conversation. Adrian Schober connects his children’s 
books to Gothic fiction (34–36), while Greg Littman calls Dahl a children’s horror 
author for how he emphasizes threats (later fulfilled in detail) of bodily harm 
(174). In fact, Dahl is “perhaps the greatest writer of children’s horror fiction” 
(173). The stories Littman sees as chi ldren’s horror are the same stories that 
Nikolajeva sees as comic. Lindvall’s words about how many readers will laugh 
at the schoolboys becoming pigs in Prince Caspian  could easily apply to the 
naughty children punished in Charlie and the Chocolate Factory.  One might say 
that Dahl’s children’s stories are horribly humorous. 
The same mix of humor and horror, a mix placing the work between 
conventional categories, appears in Dahl’s adult fiction. His short stories have 
appeared in over 30 horror anthologies, including The Penguin Book of Horror 
Stories. However, Alan Warren sees Dahl as an unusual horror writer, a 
specialist in “what the French term contes cruel, but without the bloodshed. He 
is one horror writer who rarely spills blood” (120). H.P. Lovecraft calls contes 
cruel “a class peculiar to itself […] in which the wrenching of the emotions is 
accomplished through dramatic tantalizations, frustrations, and gruesome 
physical horrors” (50). Dahl’s stories —for example, a woman killing her 
husband with a frozen leg of lamb an d feeding it to the police in “Lamb to the 
Slaughter”9—feature bodily harm, but usually treat the violence as dark 
comedy. Since they are funnier than conventional horror, it’s not surprising that 
Warren writes that Dahl’s contes cruel stories “are not horrific in the usual sense,” 
comparing him to Saki, John Collier, James Thurber, and Charles Addams (121). 
Dahl’s fiction, whatever age group he directed it toward, was convention -
breaking work existing between horror and humor, his uniq ue mix especially 
noticeable when Dahl punishes characters for indiscretions. 
Lewis could equally be considered a horribly humorous author. An 
increasing body of scholarship discusses Lewis as a horror author, from gothic 
horror in That Hideous Strength  (Nicholson) to comparisons to Russell Kirk’s 
ghost stories (Salter, “Russell Kirk and the Inklings”). The Mythopoeic Society’s 
Winter 2022 Online Conference on The Inklings and Horror featured discussions 
ranging from how Lewis communicates horrific momen ts in the Chronicles of 
Narnia (Thompson) to the talking head in That Hideous Strength as body horror 
(Brians). As with Dahl, these horrible scenes are often comic. Schwartz 
 
9 Ian Fleming gave Dahl the concept for this story (Conant 333). Given that Fleming has 
also been analyzed as featuring sadomasochism in his fiction (for a recent example, see 
Sharman), and that Dahl adapted several Fleming stories to film, there may be a l arger 
discussion here about Fleming and Dahl as kindred spirits who enjoyed combining 
humor, horror, and sexual taboos.

highlights the “gruesome, carnivalesque violence with which the traditional 
order reasserts its authority” at the N.I.C.E. dinner in That Hideous Strength (97). 
Lewis even edged toward Dahlesque risqué humor. Lindvall 
highlights the satirical academic double -talk in the short story “Ministering 
Angels” (358). Yet this tale about governments shipping women to the moon as 
astronaut concubines is also grotesque sex f arce, deliberately “full of weird 
stereotypes, unkind moments, and unpleasant philosophies” (Mikolatos 1). 
Lindvall reads “The Shoddy Lands,” in which a man goes into a woman’s mind, 
as a satire of modern values (365); it could equally be read as playing with male 
voyeurism.10 The fact that neither character in “The Shoddy Lands” cares much 
for people (Mikalatos) means it can be read as dissecting misanthropy, a trait 
that Dahl and other contes cruel writers are often accused of. 
Lewis was even capable of Dahlesque twists in stories like “The Man 
Born Blind,” also known as “Light.” Both versions of the story follow a formerly 
blind man wondering where the light is that so many people have told him 
about, ultimately falling off a cl iff as he tries to catch it ( Dark Tower and Other 
Stories 99–103; Starr 6–9). All the right plot twists for a contes cruel story. 
Yet there are crucial differences between how Lewis and Dahl write 
horribly humorous stories. Where Dahl might have used twists to emphasize 
the dark comedy or the bodily suffering, Lewis often leans into a philosophical 
or spiritual theme. “The Man Born B lind” may have different overtones in 
“Light,” the version that Charlie W. Starr published (6–9), but neither version is 
a Saki-style dark comedy about (mis)communication. Lewis has other things he 
wants to say, primarily about language and the act of contemplation. 
While Lewis often has villains suffer in comic ways, he usually doesn’t 
emphasize the bodily harm as strongly as Dahl does —at least not in his 
children’s stories. Villains who die from the consequences of their choices (Shift, 
Miraz, the White Witch) die w ithout much description. When their wounds 
aren’t mortal, their injuries (Gumpus thrown from a chair, Uncle Andrew 
drenched in dirt and water, Rabadash stuck on a building) are minor. Even 
when Jill “plied her crop on the girls and Eustance and Caspian pli ed the flats 
of their swords on the boys,” ( Silver Chair 16.215), Lewis leaves it unclear 
whether the plying breaks flesh. Lewis reserves his darkest villain punishment 
scene for the N.I.C.E. dinner, which covers “much of the last part of [ That 
Hideous Strength]” (Patterson, “Banquet” 7), but occurs in a story addressed to

enters her mind and she looks like a giant, the giant-woman-tiny-man element may make 
Dahl fans recall Oswald Hendryks Cornelius talking about the allure of tall women and 
how many women “go absolutely dotty about tiny men” ( My Uncle Oswald  18). Lewis 
plays with material that could be sexual but prefers to explore other points.
