grownups, punishing villains explicitly described as following demonic entities 
seeking world domination. 
Lewis also offers biblical allusions that Dahl rarely explores, even in 
his schoolboy scenes. Prothero compares the N.I.C.E. dinner in  That Hideous 
Strength to Sodom’s judgment (7), while Lindvall compares it to the Tower of 
Babel (358). As Jem Bloomfield has noted, Aslan via Bacchus turning schoolboys 
into a fleeing pig herd may allude to Jesus sending a pig herd into the sea at the 
Gadarenes (143–47). Particularly since Lewis leaves it unclear how much harm 
Jill’s riding crop causes, the Experiment House cleansing resembles Jesus 
cleansing the temple in Jerusalem; Martyn J. Smith notes how many scholars 
have debated whether Jesus’s whip of cords sc ourged or merely frightened 
people (80–82). Even in his least Christlike passages, Lewis can be rather biblical, 
which may explain Dickerson’s point that Lewis’s less -than-Christlike 
punishments are unsettling: they carry a “close, but not quite” quality. 
If Lewis’s horribly humorous punishments seem sadistic, it’s worth 
noting that he generally reserves the worst physical experiences for his heroes. 
Since Patterson highlights how the N.I.C.E. dinner takes up “twenty-five pages” 
in her 1945 Bodley Head edit ion (“Banquet” 7), I checked my 1965 Collier box 
set of the Ransom trilogy. In that edition, the dinner covers 19 pages ( That 
Hideous Strength 16.343–16.352). However, Ransom’s fight with the Un -Man in 
Perelandra takes 30 pages (12.152–14.182). Since Oziewicz emphasizes Edmund’s 
compensational pest-to-hero journey, I explored what happens when the White 
Witch takes Edmund captive. Edmund suffers immensely —being drenched 
with melted snow, struck on the face, tied up, whipped, and forced to walk past 
exhaustion and hunger —across 15 pages ( The Lion, the Witch and the Wardrobe  
11.107–18, 13.130–34). 
If we measure suffering by gruesome detail instead of page count, 
Lewis’s heroes still get the worst. Few wounds are more awkward than foot 
injuries, and the Un -Man gnaws at Ransom’s foot with “the nasty, blunt teeth 
[…] which crush and grind more than the y cut” ( Perelandra 15.187). When 
Puggleglum stamps out the Green Lady’s fire with his bare foot, Lewis tells us 
about the pain (“he knew it would hurt him badly enough; and so it did” [Silver 
Chair 12.158]), and underlines the ordeal cooked Puddleglum’s flesh (“the sweet 
heavy smell grew very much less […] what remained smelled very largely of 
burnt Marsh-wiggle” [ibid]). Lewis’s most famous visceral scene is Aslan tearing 
off Eustace’s skin for his undragoning ( “Dawn Treader” 12.88–91). Lewis and 
Dahl both like having villains experience pain; but Lewis reserves the longest or 
most gruesome gauntlets to characters resisting evil or growing into goodness. 
Notes 
234  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
The pain that heroes suffer also means something more in Lewis’s 
stories. Dahl’s heroes suffer, but their pain rarely gets referenced after a scene.11 
Mr. Fox bemoans losing his tail (Fantastic Mr. Fox  15), but it doesn’t stop him 
from agilely performing his greatest heist. Lewis lets his heroes have wounds 
affecting them long -term (Ransom’s foot never healing, Puddleglum still 
limping at the book’s end); the pain informs their ongoing journeys. As 
discussed elsewhere, Lewis uses biblical allusions to make his heroes’ pain into 
sanctifying experiences (Salter, “Tellers of Dark Fairy Tales” 159 –60). Ransom 
and Puddleglum become Christ figures through their foot wounds, echoing 
Genesis 3:15 when the (literal or symbolic) 12 serpent strikes the Messiah’s heel. 
Lewis may like punishing his villains, but he cares more about having his heroes 
struggle to resist evil or grow into their heroic roles, struggles which undercut 
any sense that their prowess saves the day.13 
In contrast, Dahl offers no consistent moral scheme to their heroes’ 
pain. His short stories often feature people suffering for sins (the husband who 
deserts his wife in “Lamb to the Slaughter”) or foolishness (the man with a back 
tattoo who trusts a generous stranger in “Skin”). But many stories offer no clear 
moral: “Galloping Foxley” describes a man recalling school bullying when he 
sees someone resembling his old prefect but the plot never resolves. Pain and its 
remembrance prove valueless. Dahl’s child ren’s books are similarly 
inconsistent. Marc Napolitano sees the naughty children suffering in Charlie and 
the Chocolate Factory as fitting Charles Foucault’s theory in Discipline and Punish 
about enforcing conformity: the bland conformist Charlie experiences no pain 
but every other child gets punished according to their abnormal quirk (81 –92). 
In Matilda, the reverse is true: her quirky magic powers save her. Pain rarely 
sanctifies in Dahl’s stories either: Bruce Bogtrotter being forced to eat a giant 
cake in Matilda or James’s aunts abusing him in James and the Giant Peach  don’t 
 
11 I have noted elsewhere how Dahl’s attitude to pain influenced J.K. Rowling, who also 
offers an abused hero who doesn’t experience plausible, long -term trauma (Salter, “The 
Wart, The Wizard, and the Cathedral” 74–75, 82, 89). 
12 The Green Lady turns into a serpent and the Un -Man is a tempting serpent figure. For 
more on the serpent imagery, see Patterson, “Halfe Like a Serpent,” and Muth. 
13 While not key to this discussion, the same could be said about Peter’s terror when he 
kills a wolf in The Lion, the Witch and the Wardrobe . By emphasizing the hero’s fear or 
treating the villains’ pain comedically, Lewis undercuts celebrating his heroes’ valor. Even 
his heroes who kill repulsive villains don’t glory in it: Williams notes how when Jewel and 
Tirian kill the Telmarine slavers in The Last Battle, they “feel deeply ashamed. They have 
killed unarmed men, and for all that there is provocation, there is no glory about it” (44). 
Christian men’s conferences may enjoy quoting the movie Braveheart alongside Mere 
Christianity, but Lewis never gives readers anything like William Wallace screaming 
“Freedom!” before entering battle. The difference may explain why Lewis often has heroes 
suffer, but rarely faces Mel Gibson’s charge of being a sadomasochistic storyteller. 
Notes 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  235 
make the heroes grow. For Dahl, pain is a lesson in endurance and biding time 
to strike back, not a sanctification opportunity.14 
Dickerson’s point about The Silver Chair is valid. Lewis is not a perfect 
Christologist, and his fiction featuring school tormentors particularly show a 
tension between justice and vengeance in his theology. Depicting revenge with 
humor means that Lewis has rarely been labeled a children’s horro r author as 
Dahl has. Yet as librarian Kirsten Kowaleski observes, the difference between a 
scary book for children and a children’s horror book is ambiguous (“Where Are 
the Scary Books?”). Lewis is often more provocative, more horribly humorous, 
than readers may realize. 
Given Lewis’s skill at depicting pain and how he didn’t mind edging 
into risqué territory in stories like “Ministering Angels,” he could have taken 
Dahl’s route. Yet by preference or choice, Lewis stops short of the full focus on 
visceral pain or bleak comic twists that define contes cruel writers like Dahl, Saki, 
and Addams. In particular, his (frequent if not consistent) emphasis on 
compensational justice, and his preference for heroic pain that changes his 
characters place him just outside Dahl’s circle. He offers “funny bits,” but not 
quite as gruesome ones as Matilda’s creator offers. 
In fact, the Experiment House cleansing may show how Lewis 
manages to simultaneously be as transgressive as Dahl, yet also more 
redemptive. The school’s bullies suffer, but get off lightly compared to the 
schoolboys in Prince Caspian. Eustace’s central place in The Silver Chair, a much 
kinder boy than he was in Voyage of t he “Dawn Treader ,” shows that even 
Experiment House students can grow. We may read the pain that Eustace, 
Edmund, and the unnamed bullies experience in the first four published Narnia 
books as Lewis’s own experiment: how best to discipline and reform 
schoolboys, becoming kinder with each attempt.

WEAVING WIKIPEDIA ’S MIDDLE -EARTH WEB 
IAN F. ALEXANDER 
 
BEGINNINGS 
HE ONLINE ENCYCLOPEDIA WIKIPEDIA WAS FOUNDED IN 2001, just when Peter 
Jackson’s films were bringing Middle -earth to a mass audience. It grew 
rapidly to become the world’s largest and most-read work of reference. Articles 
on Tolkien and The Lord of the Rings, and on some Middle-earth characters such 
as Frodo Baggins, were started in 2001. The articles were initially short, uncited, 
and devoid of analysis. Gradually, the community created rules demanding that 
each claim be verified by a reliable source, and th at each article’s notability be 
demonstrated by multiple sources.  
Most of the hundreds of Middle-earth articles in existence by 2019 were 
of in-universe topics like characters and places. They were written from a fan’s 
perspective: accurate, but the few existing citations were to Tolkien’s writings, 
with no independent sources to prove notability. A campaign of article deletion 
began: minor characters and places swiftly vanished.  
COMPETITORS 
Wikipedia’s Middle -earth project exists alongside free sites like 
Tolkien Gateway. That site demands accuracy and even citations, but limits 
those to the Tolkien canon. All Middle -earth characters, locations, and objects 
described by Tolkien are taken to b e worth covering, with no requirement for 
T 
Notes 
240  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
independent sources; this results in large number of articles on minor subjects 
like the Dwarves of Belegost and Hildórien, for a total of over 12,800 articles. 
These are supplemented by almost 15,000 images, many of them made by fans 
(“Community Portal”).  I find the Tolkien Gateway useful for details of 
characters and so on, where it is solidly reliable, and for tracking down primary 
sources. It does describe some real -world objects such as films, games, actors, 
and books (even scholarly ones); it links to external reviews but doesn’t provide 
its own analysis or explore scholarly criticism in any way. In the special case of 
Peter Jackson’s film adaptations, it describes differences from Tolkien’s 
narrative without comment or citations (e.g. “The Lord of the Rings: The Fellowship 
of the Ring”). 
 
A WEB OF WORDS 
Tolkien scholars do sometimes write about surface topics that would 
instantly be recognised by fans, such as characters like Frodo, but they’re much 
more likely to be writing about underlying topics like themes. Their topics are 
what fans would consider speculation, on themes such as the nature of the hero, 
Northern courage, or the relevance of Arthurian chivalry; influences from 
Classical, Celtic, and Shakespeare all the way to modern sources; elements like 
artwork, maps, and poetry; and literary technique s like frame stories, the 
impression of depth, and interlacing. In 2019, there were hardly any Wikipedia 
articles on topics of that sort: one on magic, one on themes, one on influences, 
and one on fandom. 
To cover the themes, influences, elements, and literary techniques in 
the scholarly literature, I created over 100 new Middle -earth articles between 
2020 and 2024, on scholarly topics like “Heroism in The Lord of the Rings.” I cited 
each one to multiple reliable sources (“Chiswick Chap,” “Articles#Tolkien”). In 
the same period, I brought over 200 articles to “Good Article” status (“Tolkien 
Articles”). I revised around half of these from existing fan -style articles like 
“Aragorn,” probably saving many of the m from deletion, by adding scholarly 
discussion and sources.  
The “Heroism” article consists mainly of analysis of the literary effect. 
I supported it with references to 7 Tolkien sources and 11 scholarly books and 
journal articles.  
The “Aragorn” article, in contrast, provides a substantial in -universe 
account of the character’s life and actions. I supported that with 28 references to 
chapters in The Lord of the Rings. The rest of the article consists of analysis: of the 
character’s creation and names in English and Sindarin, and of the character’s 
significance, including its possible historical basis, Aragorn’s role as a Christ-as-
King figure, and his status as a Roman tic hero with physical stamina and 
lifespan beyond those of ordinar y men. I made that verifiable with fourteen 
Notes 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  241 
secondary sources in the shape of scholarly books and journal papers, and a 
dozen primary sources for the quoted examples. I ended the article with an 
account of the character’s appearances in film and radio adaptations, supported 
by 18 secondary sources, mostly news websites. 
The resulting Wikipedia articles are neither like items on a fan website, 
nor quite like scholarly papers. Wikipedia’s rule against “Original Research” 
forbids editors from adding anything in the way of opinions or conclusions 
inferred by reasoning from th e cited materials ( Wikipedia, “No original 
research”). On the other hand, writing an article is certainly a creative process. I 
selected sources, arranged materials, explained concepts, chose examples, and 
prepared illustrations, shaping something that did  not exist before. I went 
further with extensive cross -linking, weaving all the articles into a newly 
coherent web. In a conventional encyclopedia, cross -references are made 
typographically, with phrases like “ see Hobbit.” In a Wiki, the reader clicks on 
a “Hobbit” hyperlink, and is at once taken to that article. The result, I believe, is 
a body of information accessible to a new and very large audience. 
 
DIAGRAMS AND TABLES  
In addition to cited text, I created at least 75 Middle -earth diagrams 
(“Chiswick Chap,” “Diagrams”), and many tables embedded in articles, 
summarising scholarly analyses. The rules on illustrations are slightly more 
relaxed than those on claims in the tex t, but the same considerations apply: the 
material must be relevant, and any claims made must be supported by citations 
in the image caption. For example, Figure 1 shows a simple diagram that I made 
presenting Tom Shippey’s textual explanation of the origins of the names 
“Huggins” and “Baggins”. The diagram explains their surface similarity but 
their quite different etymologies (Shippey 8). 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 1: A diagram presenting a textual description by Tom Shippey. “Chiswick 
Chap,” on Wikimedia Commons. 
 
Notes 
242  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
An alternative approach used in many Middle -earth articles is to 
summarize and display the outlines of a scholar’s analysis using a table. For 
instance, Table 1 shows my table of Ben Reinhard’s analysis of types of hero in 
the article “Heroism in The Lord of the Rings. ” My goal was to present the key 
elements of a scholarly analysis concisely for the general reader.  
 
Type of 
hero 
Example Characteristics 
Chivalric 
knight 
Lancelot Noble, French-style; powerful, armoured, on 
horseback; courtly love; success assured 
Hobbit Frodo 
Baggins

success far from guaranteed

Table 1: A table from the article “Heroism in The Lord of the Rings,” based on analysis by Ben 
Reinhard in “Tolkien’s Lost Knights.” 
 
READERSHIP 
Wikipedia’s Middle-earth articles were together viewed over 7 million 
times in August 2024. The top 20 articles by number of views included those on 
Tolkien, The Lord of the Rings , and The Hobbit , but also some actors in Peter 
Jackson’s film series, namely Ian Holm (who played Bilbo), Christopher Lee 
(Saruman), and Elijah Wood (Frodo), and several film and television articles. 
The top Middle -earth character was Sauron, followed by Morgoth, Galadri el, 
and Isildur, all of whom feature in the Amazon series The Lord of the Rings: The 
Rings of Power, which has been showing since 2022; its series article was the top 
article in the month, with over 21,000 views a day (Wikipedia, “WikiProject 
Middle-earth/Popular pages”). There is considerable month-to-month turnover 
among the most viewed articles.  
The top Middle -earth article on a scholarly theme is “Cosmology of 
Tolkien’s legendarium,” with over 1,400 views per day. It is followed by 
“Tolkien and race” at over 300 views per day, still equating to well over 100,000 
views per year; “Christianity in M iddle-earth” at over 240 views per day; and 
some way further down the list, “Sexuality in The Lord of the Rings” at over 100 
views per day. The popularity of these themes shows that Wikipedia views 
broadly follow the trend identified by Karen Viars and Cait Coker, namely that 
Tolkien fans have “unsurprisingly focused on many of those topics Tolkien 
scholars are fond of today —homosexual overtones [...], racism [...], Tolkien's 
literary sources and forebears, and the roles of women in his world” (37).

WHAT WILL WE DO WITH THE CHRISTMAS PUDDING ?:  
JOY DAVIDMAN , WILLIAM LINDSAY GRESHAM , AND “CHRISTMAS IN 
THE WORKHOUSE ” 
G. CONNOR SALTER AND CLARK SHELDON 
 
OST INKLINGS SCHOLARS FAMILIAR WITH JOY DAVIDMAN (1915–1960) and 
her first husband William Lindsay Gresham (1909 –1962) know they 
were both involved in American leftwing literary circles. They were active 
members of the Communist Party of the United States of America (CPUSA) 
from the 1930s until the early to mid -1940s, becoming far less active after they 
M 
Notes 
244  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
became parents. Abigail Santamaria notes they likely met through a key 
leftwing writers’ circle, the League of American Writers, in the late 1930s and 
that the first record of their appearing at the same event was in 1941 at the 
Fourth Annual Congress of the League of American Writers (118, 123). 
Their politics informed their writing in various ways. Davidman was a 
poetry editor and contributor to the New Masses and edited two leftwing poetry 
collections: They Look Like Men  and War Poems of the United Nations . Gresham 
volunteered to serve in the Spanish Civil War and served as a medic in the 
Abraham Lincoln Brigade, contributing one poem about his experience, “Last 
Kilometer,” to War Poems of the United Nations  (319). The poem has proved 
popular enough to be reprinted several times (Nelson 157, Bios ca 177 –80, 
Herrmann 13). While more detailed work has been published on Davidman’s 
two novels Anya and Weeping Bay (Hammond 235 –45, King 114 –35, 143 –52), 
including notes on how leftwing ideas inform those books, there is also room to 
discuss these political concerns in Gresham’s fiction. Alan M. Wald has 
suggested that Gresham’s two novels Nightmare Alley  and Limbo Tower  are 
informed by at least collectivist if not pro -communist concerns (Salter, “Joy 
Davidman, William Lindsay Gresham, and the American Literary Left” 1). 
The leftwing politics becomes especially important when discussing 
some of Gresham’s archival works. As G. Connor Salter and Sørina Higgins 
have reported (“Friar Jack the Sci-Fi Apologist”), the folder “Poems, 1941–1944” 
in the William Lindsay Gresham Pape rs in the Marion E. Wade Center at 
Wheaton College1 shows that Gresham was a more prolific poet than previously 
assumed. The folder contains several never -published poems written by 
Gresham, two drafts of “Last Kilometer” (Gresham’s only published poem 
during his lifetime), and several transcripts of famou s leftwing poems by other 
authors. Since 2023, two of Gresham’s original poems from this folder, 
“Rahnhild” and “The Friar of Oxford,” have been published (see Salter, 
“William Lindsay Gresham and Norse Poetry,” and Higgins and Salter, “Friar 
Jack”). 
One of the more interesting items in the folder is the following poem: 
 
Christmas in the Workhouse 
It was Christmas in the workhouse, 
Happiest day of all the year, 
And the paupers was contented 
‘Cause their guts was full of beer. 
 
 
1 Joe R. Christopher was the first writer to mention one of the folder’s contents in print: he 
notes the existence of “The Friar of Oxford” in his review of the Don W. King -edited 
collection of Davidman’s poetry, A Naked Tree (186). 
Notes 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  245 
The Master of the workhouse 
Strode through the dismal halls 
And cried, “A Merry Christmas!” 
But the paupers answered, “Balls!” 
 
Then angry was the Master 
And he swore by all the gods 
That they’d have no Christmas pudding, 
The lousy bunch of twatts. 
 
Up spake a war-scarred veteran 
Who had stormed the Khyber Pass, 
“You can tyke yer Christmas puddin’, sir, 
“An’ stick it up yer ass!!!” 
 
Following the poem’s ending line is an indented two -sentence note in 
parentheses. It describes the poem as, in the writer’s opinion, “one of the better 
contributions of Great Britain to world culture in general and poetry in particular” 
and argues that “on ly a member of the British lumpen proletaria t [sic] can get the 
full effect of scorn, hate, disgust and respect for authority out of the last two lines.” 
To say the least, this poem is intriguing. Stylistically, it is very different 
from other contents in the folder. The other poems are primarily about Spain 
and in the same style as “Last Kilometer”: harrowing first -person accounts of 
battlegrounds. “Rahnhild” and “The Friar of Oxford” are exceptions to this rule, 
but also very different from “Christmas in the Workhouse”: one is a faux Norse 
myth piece, the other an homage to Winnifred M. Letts’s World War I poem 
“The Spires of Oxford” intermingled with science fiction imagery. 
In fact, “Christmas in the Workhouse” has a long history that 
predates Gresham.  
 
LOCATING “CHRISTMAS IN THE WORKHOUSE” 
As Victorian history or literature specialists may know, “Christmas in 
the Workhouse” was originally a ballad by George Robert Sims titled “In the 
Workhouse. Christmas Day” (Sims 7). Sims first published his ballad in 1877 in 
The Referee (Jakobs 152). Many variations have appeared since then. Since the 
original ballad was published well before 1923 (the cutoff year in American 
copyright law) and the variations are mostly transmitted orally with no known 
lyricist, the poem and its many off shoots are necessar ily outside copyright. 
Sims’ original version, printed below, has a much more formal style than 
Gresham’s transcript. 
 
 
 
Notes 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  246 
IN THE WORKHOUSE. CHRISTMAS DAY. 
 
It is Christmas Day in the Workhouse, 
And the cold bare walls are bright 
With garlands of green and holly, 
And the place is a pleasant sight: 
For with clean-washed hands and faces, 
In a long and hungry line 
The paupers sit at the tables 
For this is the hour they dine. 
 
And the guardians and their ladies, 
Although the wind is east, 
Have come in their furs and wrappers, 
To watch their charges feast; 
To smile and be condescending, 
Put pudding on pauper plates, 
To be hosts at the workhouse banquet 
They’ve paid for—with the rates. 
 
Oh, the paupers are meek and lowly 
With their “Thank’ee kindly, mum’s” 
So long as they fill their stomachs, 
What matter it whence it comes? 
But one of the old men mutters, 
And pushes his plate aside: 
“Great God!” he cries; “but it chokes me! 
For this is the day she died.” 
 
The guardians gazed in horror, 
The master’s face went white; 
“Did a pauper refuse their pudding?” 
“Could their ears believe aright?” 
Then the ladies clutched their 
husbands, 
Thinking the man would die, 
Struck by a bolt, or something, 
By the outraged One on high. 
 
But the pauper sat for a moment, 
Then rose ‘mid a silence grim, 
For the others had ceased to chatter 
And trembled in every limb. 
He looked at the guardians’ ladies, 
Then, eyeing their lords, he said, 
“I eat not the food of villains 
Whose hands are foul and red: 
 
“Whose victims cry for vengeance 
From their dank, unhallowed graves.” 
“He’s drunk!” said the workhouse 
master, 
“Or else he’s mad, and raves.” 
“Not drunk or mad,” cried the pauper, 
“But only a hunted beast, 
Who, torn by the hounds and mangled, 
Declines the vulture’s feast. 
 
“I care not a curse for the guardians, 
And I won’t be dragged away. 
Just let me have the fit out, 
It’s only on Christmas Day 
That the black past comes to goad me, 
And prey on my burning brain; 
I’ll tell you the rest in a whisper,— 
I swear I won’t shout again. 
 
“Keep your hands off me, curse you! 
Hear me right out to the end. 
You came here to see how paupers 
The season of Christmas spend. 
You came here to watch us feeding, 
As they watch the captured beast. 
Hear why a penniless pauper 
Spits on your paltry feast. 
 
“Do you think I will take your bounty, 
And let you smile and think 
You’re doing a noble action 
With the parish’s meat and drink? 
Where’s my wife, you traitors— 
The poor old wife you slew? 
Yes, by the God above us, 
My Nance was killed by you! 
 
“Last winter my wife lay dying, 
Starved in a filthy den; 
I had never been to the parish,— 
Notes 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  247 
I came to the parish then. 
I swallowed my pride in coming, 
For, ere the ruin came, 
I held up my head as a trader, 
And I bore a spotless name. 
 
“I came to the parish, craving 
Break for a starving wife, 
Bread for the woman who'd loved me 
Through fifty years of life; 
And what do you think they told me, 
Mocking my awful grief? 
That ‘the House’ was open to us, 
But they wouldn’t give ‘out relief.’ 
 
“I slunk to the filthy alley— 
‘Twas a cold, raw Christmas eve— 
And the bakers’ shops were open, 
Tempting a man to thieve; 
But I clenched my fists together, 
Holding my head awry, 
So I come to her empty-handed 
And mournfully told her why. 
 
“When I told her ‘the House’ was open; 
She had heard of the ways of that, 
For her bloodless cheeks went crimson, 
And up in her rags she sat, 
Crying, ‘Bide the Christmas here, John, 
We’ve never had one apart; 
I think I can bear the hunger,— 
The other would break my heart.’ 
“All through that eve I watched her, 
Holding her hand in mine, 
Praying the Lord, and weeping, 
Till my lips were salt as brine. 
I asked her once if she hungered, 
And as she answered ‘No,’ 
The moon shone in at the window 
Set in a wreath of snow. 
 
“Then the room was bathed in glory, 
And I saw in my darling’s eyes 
The far-away look of wonder 
That comes when the spirit flies; 
And her lips were parched and parted, 
And her reason came and went, 
For she raved of our home in Devon, 
Where our happiest years were spent. 
 
“And the accents, long forgotten, 
Came back to the tongue once more, 
For she talked like the country lassie 
I woo’d by the Devon shore. 
Then she rose to her feet and trembled, 
And fell on the rags and moaned, 
And, ‘Give me a crust—I’m famished— 
For the love of God!’ she groaned. 
 
 “I rushed from the room like a 
madman, 
And flew to the workhouse gate, 
Crying, ‘Food for a dying woman!’ 
And the answer came, ‘Too late.’ 
They drove me away with curses; 
Then I fought with a dog in the street, 
And tore from the mongrel’s clutches 
A crust he was trying to eat. 
 
“Back, through the filthy by-lanes! 
Back, through the trampled slush! 
Up to the crazy garret, 
Wrapped in an awful hush. 
My heart sank down at the threshold, 
And I paused with a sudden thrill, 
For there in the silv’ry moonlight 
My Nance lay, cold and still. 
 
“Up to the blackened ceiling 
The sunken eyes were cast— 
I knew on those lips all bloodless 
My name had been the last; 
She’d called for her absent husband— 
O God! had I but known!— 
Had called in vain, and in anguish 
Had died in that den—alone. 
 
 
Notes 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  248 
“Yes, there, in a land of plenty, 
Lay a loving woman dead, 
Cruelly starved and murdered 
For a loaf of the parish bread. 
At yonder gate, last Christmas, 
I craved for a human life. 
You, who would feast us paupers, 
What of my murdered wife! 
                        * * * 
“There, get ye gone to your dinners; 
Don't mind me in the least; 
Think of the happy paupers 
Eating your Christmas feast; 
And when you recount their 
blessings 
In your smug parochial way, 
Say what you did for me, too, 
Only last Christmas Day. 
 
 
SIMILAR VARIATIONS ON “CHRISTMAS IN THE WORKHOUSE” 
The version of “Christmas in the Workhouse” in Gresham’s folder does 
not appear to have been printed anywhere until now. A similar version (rude 
final line and the Khyber Pass reference included, but some words changed) is 
quoted in George Henry Wilkin’s memoir Certified (179). A character played by 
Ed Asner recites another variation in the 1977 Christmas movie The Gathering 
(57:00–58:35), but this variation also changes some wording (for example, “And 
the paupers was contented” is rendered “And the paupers all was happy”). The 
fact that The Gathering  features Asner’s character attributing the poem to 
Rudyard Kipling (57:00–58:35) gives an idea how this Sims poem had survived 
and evolved in public memory until the general public forgot its origin. 
But how did this poem arrive in Gresham’s papers? Aside from a 
handwritten letter written to Gresham containing a poem, there is no clear 
evidence that “Poems, 1941 –1944” features any documents that Gresham or 
Davidman received from others. Therefore, one of them presumably transcribed 
“Christmas in the Workhouse.” Given that the poem (both Sims’ original and 
this more profane variation) are leftwing protest anthems a gainst managers 
exploiting workers, it is precisely the kind of poem that both Gresham and 
Davidman would have heard in 1930s –40s CPUSA circles. However, since the 
poem has many variations, it is hard to say exactly when they would have heard 
it. The folder is tentatively dated 1941–1944 because most of its poems appear to 
have been written afte r Gresham returned to America in 1938 from Spain. 1 As 
Higgins and Salter discuss, “The Friar of Oxford,” was likely written several 
years later in 1946–1950 (Higgins and Salter 250–251). 
 The note attached to the poem may offer further clues. 
 
 
 
 
1 Five of these poems were recently published in The Volunteer, the magazine devoted to 
scholarship on The Abraham Lincoln Brigade (Salter, “Beyond the Last Kilometer.” 
Notes 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  249 
CONSIDERING THE “LUMPENPROLETARIAT” NOTE 
The note and the poem are both typed, which makes it impossible to 
use handwriting analysis to determine whether Davidman or Gresham wrote it 
or date its composition period based on how their handwriting styles evolved. 
Instead, one must consider whether the note’s language or style offers any hints. 
The word “lumpenproletariat” is German for “rabble proletariat,” and 
a Marxist term for the very lowest social class (“Lumpenproletariat”). Therefore, 
this note could have been written by either Davidman or Gresham: any writer 
in 1930s–40s leftwing artist circles would have been familiar with the phrase. 
Since the poem was British, and Davidman was a self -described 
Anglophile, she may seem the better candidate for writing the note. Don W. 
King was consulted and said he could not recall seeing the poem anywhere in 
Davidman’s papers and felt it was unlikely she transcribed it or wrote the note 
(“Weird Davidman Research Question”). He pointed out that “Christmas in the 
Workhouse” could easily be something Davidman received while editing the 
New Masses and rejected since it has a didactic tone; she regularly re jected such 
submissions and criticized the submitters for sending in propaganda instead of 
well-crafted work (ibid). 
However, “Christmas in the Workhouse” seems unlikely to be a New 
Masses submission since the manuscript does not feature any address or other 
contact information on its header. Print magazines (both in the 1930s and today) 
nearly universally expect submitters to include such information. 2 It is also the 
common protocol for writers to state their opinions about the work being 
submitted (for example, why “Christmas in the Workhouse” is such a great 
example of Great Britain’s contributions to world culture) on an attached cover 
letter, not paste their comments on the poem itself. Therefore, this would be an 
exceptionally amateur submission to the New Masses . Even then, Davidman 
would follow the usual protocol: mail the poem back with a rejection notice, 
using the return address listed on the submission’s postal envelope. As many of 
her letters show, Davidman never suffered fools lightly. If a writer was fool ish 
enough to submit something this badly formatted, it is hard to imagine her 
passing up the chance to cow that writer with an incendiary rejection notice. 
One of this article’s authors, Clark Sheldon, has done extensive work 
on Gresham and has his own theory about how “Christmas in the Workhouse” 
entered Gresham’s files. 
 
 
 
2 For example, the only poem in “Poems, 1941 –1944” that Gresham published, “Last 
Kilometer,” has his name and address and date added to the second draft, the version he 
presumably sent to Dial Press. 
Notes 
250  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
“CHRISTMAS IN THE WORKHOUSE” AND JOY DAVIDMAN 
When initially asked about the poem (“Funny poem from Gresham 
archives”), Sheldon noted it is hard to say from the note’s style whether 
Gresham wrote it. Gresham’s normal writing style was chatty but it changed 
whenever Gresham was researching something. Given those factors, it seems 
more likely that the “lumpenproletariat” note is something Davidman wrote or 
that Gresham wrote with her in mind. 
The second option raises a new query: what could Davidman have 
been working on which Gresham would think a profane leftwing protest poem 
was suitable for? 
Of Davidman’s projects, the most likely answer is her second poetry 
collection. War Poems contains 150 leftwing war poems from around the world, 
and “Christmas in the Workhouse” has a strong war theme, especially 
compared to Sims’ original poem. The old man in the original becomes a “war -
scarred veteran” and the line about how his “face was hard as brass” becomes a 
reference to how the veteran “Stormed the Khyber Pass.” 
“Christmas in the Workhouse” does not appear in War Poems of the 
United Nations , although two poems with similar titles are included: 
“Christmas” by Frantisek Halas (66) and “Christmas Song” by Rudolf Leonhard, 
the latter translated from German into English by Jean Star Untermeyer (122 –
23). Therefore, it is quite possible that Gresham knew Davidman was seeking 
war poems and transcribed the poem (with the perhaps tongue -in-cheek note) 
for her to use, only for Dial Press to refuse its inclusion because of its profanity. 
The fact that “Christmas in the Workhouse” is a British war poem 
makes it even more likely to be material for War Poems of the United Nations . 
According to Lyle W. Dorsett’s book And God Came In, Davidman struggled to 
find British leftwing war poems for the collection, so she wrote some poems 
herself under two British pseudonyms, Megan Coombes -Dawson and Hayden 
Weir (43; c.f. Glyer 10–11).3 Weir even got a tragic backstory: alas, he “was killed 
in action in 1942” ( War Poems 50). “Christmas in the Workhouse” not only fits 
the militaristic theme Davidman was seeking for her collection; its British origin 
filled a blatant gap in her collection. 
If “Christmas in the Workhouse” is something Gresham collected for 
Davidman to use, it presents a new direction for Davidman -Gresham 
discussions. Much has been said about Davidman influencing Gresham’s work. 
Nightmare Alley is dedicated to her. Sheldon has discussed elsewhere how much 
editorial feedback she gave on the book, how Davidman helped to create 
structure and other traits conspicuously absent from Limbo Tower  (Salter, 
 
3 The poem attributed to Coombes-Dawson is “For My Son” (War Poems 25). The two poems 
attributed to Weir are “Four Years After Munich” and “Peccavimus” (War Poems 49–51). 
Notes 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  251 
“Exploring William Lindsay Gresham”). However, outside of King mentioning 
that Gresham influenced a carnival scene and an alcoholic priest character in 
Weeping Bay (Yet One More Spring  149; c.f. Out of My Bone  123), little has been 
said about Gresham influencing Davidman. “Christmas in the Workhouse” may 
be positive proof of one occasion when the influence took the other direction. 
 
FURTHER NEW DIRECTIONS FOR DAVIDMAN-GRESHAM RESEARCH 
Time will tell if new research shows how “Christmas in the 
Workhouse” made its way into Gresham’s files. As of this writing, King has 
published some of Davidman’s uncollected letters ( King, “Joy Davidman’s 
Unpublished Letters”; cf. Salter, “The Women that C.S. Lewis Loved”), which 
may open possibilities for researching War Poems  in more detail. Various 
archival projects on Gresham remain under -explored. For example, no one has 
published a collection of his letters. As noted elsewhere (Salter, “What Are 
Magic and Myth Good For?” 218), there is currently no complete bibliography 
of his published work. If either project comes to fruition, they may reveal new 
information about “Christmas in the Workhouse,” and almost certainly new 
insights into the leftwing community that fostered Davidman and Gresham as 
young writers. 
Whatever happens next with Davidman -Gresham scholarship, 
“Christmas in the Workhouse” is an entertaining read giving hints of their 
political connections and influences during an under-studied time in their lives. 
It is also well worth stating for the academic record that while Sims did 
not write this version of “Christmas in the Workhouse,” Rudyard Kipling did 
not write it either.

“PERILOUS AND FAIR” IN “A BLEAK , BARREN LAND ”: 
A FEMINIST RESPONDS TO DYLAN LEE HENDERSON ’S ESSAY  
ROBIN ANNE REID 
 
“I don’t know about perilous,” said Sam. “It strikes me that folk takes their 
peril with them into Lórien, and finds it there because they’ve brought it. 
But perhaps you could call her perilous, because she’s so strong in herself. 
You, you could dash yourself to pieces on her, like a ship on a rock; or 
drownd yourself, like a hobbit in a river. But neither rock nor river would 
be to blame.” (LotR IV.5.680, bolded emphasis added) 
 
ANET BRENNAN CROFT AND LESLIE A. DONOVAN open their joint introduction to 
the first (and so far only ) anthology on “ Women in the Works and Life of J.R.R. 
Tolkien,” with two quotes from The Lord of the Rings . The first quote is three 
J 
Notes 
254  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
paragraphs from Faramir’s and Sam’s conversation about Elves (LotR IV.5.679–
80). The second is the paragraph about Éowyn’s challenge to the Witch -king 
before she kills the Fell Beast (V.6.841). I choose to quote Sam’s insight about 
Galadriel, although I was tempted to start with Éowyn’s defiant laugh and her 
claim that “no living man am I! You look upon a woman. Éowyn I am, Éomund’s 
daughter. You stand between me and my lord and kin. Begone, if you be not 
deathless! For living or dark undead, I will smite you, if you touch him” 
(V.6.841).1 Galadriel and Éowyn are both, if differently, perilous and fair, and 
both, judging by the varied critical interpretations made about them over the 
decades, are ambiguously gendered figures.2  
 Sam’s analysis of the ambiguous nature of Galadriel’s perilousness is a 
response to Faramir’s lengthy comment about the history of declining 
interactions between Men [sic] and Elves in Middle -earth which has led him to 
conclude that: “I deem it perilous now for mortal men [sic] willfully to seek out 
the Elder People” (emphasis added). Yet Faramir’s own ambiguous feelings in 
regard to Galadriel show in his next sentence: “Yet I envy you that have spoken 
with the White Lady” ( LotR IV.5.680.679). In Faramir’s  sentence, perilous 
modifies “it” which is a dummy subject standing in for the phrase “mortal men 
[who are] willfully” searching for the Elves (“Dummy Subjects”). Faramir 
acknowledges his own longing to meet and speak with Galadriel despite the 
peril that he believes exists. Sam disclaims any authoritative knowledge about 
the perilousness of Galadriel, the individual, as opposed to that of the “Elder 
People.” In this instance, the word carries the meaning of first definition in the 
Oxford English Dictionary  (OED): “Dangerous, hazardous. . .causing or 
resulting in danger […] also: spiritually dangerous.” 3 I suspect that Galadriel’s

presumed spiritual dangerousness is primarily what Faramir and other male

 Sam then shifts to using the noun peril plus adding a simile to identify 
two possible types of peril he speculates may apply to Galadriel. The first is 
internal to the men seeking her, meaning their assumptions about the nature of 
Galadriel which they project onto her. The second is external to the men who 
enter the Golden Wood: it signifies Galadriel’s o wn strength, her peril, which is 
inherent, and, additionally, in Sam’s simile, functions the same way that rocks 
in oceans and rivers do at times. Those p arts of the natural world can kill 
mortals, but neither rocks nor rivers intend to kill anyone. Sam’s choice of words 
and use of figurative language assign some level of agency to the hypothetical 
mortal males who chooses to seek out Galadriel, to sail on the ocean, or to take 
a boat out on a river in spite of, or because of, their assumptions.  
 All the named characters in the scene where the Fellowship meet 
Galadriel are male except for Galadriel herself. Aragorn is the only one who has 
visited Lothlórien previously, and many, if not all of the group, have heard the 
stories about the Golden Wood  and about the peril of Galadriel told in their 
home cultures; apparently no tales are told about Celeborn who, as an Elf Lord, 
is presumably also perilous, but, as a male, is presumably not spiritually perilous! 
All the male characters have different pers pectives and assumptions about 
Galadriel, and the ones who are later willing to describe their responses to her 
greeting and testing illustrate how different individuals respond to the same 
experience.  
 While Sam is describing an encounter between (mostly) strangers 
meeting for the first time in Tolkien’s sub-created world, I think that Sam’s first 
definition of the perceived peril of Galadriel to “mortal men” (and, presumably, 
to mortal, and male, hobbi ts and dwarves) is analogous to how some male 
readers in the Primary World project stereotypes upon women’s and/or 
feminists’ interpretations of Tolkien’s work. Unfortunately, this outdated

stereotype of women and/or feminist critics as shrewish man -haters appears 
early (in the second paragraph) of Dylan Lee Henderson’s 2023 essay, “A Bleak, 
Barren Land’: Women and Fertility in The Lord of the Rings ,” and shapes his 
overall argument. He opens his two -paragraph long “Background” section, 
which serves as a basic review of literature, by saying that “many feminist 
scholars [since 1971] have condemned Tolkien’s depiction of women as inadequate 
and denounced Tolkien himself as a misogynist” (88, emphasis added). This sentence 
ends with a superscript number referencing a footnote citing my essay, “The 
History of Scholarship on Female Characters in J.R.R. Tolkien’s Legendarium: A 
Feminist Bibliographic Essay” (Croft and Donovan, Perilous and Fair 
[subsequently P&F]). The footnote reads, in its entirety: “For a more detailed 
history of this debate, see Reid” (88n2). Henderson choosing to cite my essay at 
the end of a sentence that is his own argument implies that my essay makes the 
same, or similar argument, only with more “details” about the many feminists 
who have “condemned […] and denounced Tolkien himself as a misogynist” (88).  
 While my essay does provide more details about more of the relevant 
scholarship than Henderson cites in his essay, my analysis of the scholarship 
differs a great deal from his. My goal in the essay, as with any bibliographic 
essay, is not to make an argument about Tolkien’s work (let alone the author 
himself) but to analyze the types of arguments made in the scholarship about 
his work. In order to achieve that goal, I analyze changes in the “development 
of scholarship on [Tolkien’s] female characters over time, including not only the 
increasing attention paid to these characters, but also a growing application of 
newer critical theories and methods” (13). My analysis includes, but is not 
limited to, all the essays in P&F, seven of which were reprints of earlier essays. 
But I also cover all the published essays on the topic that I could find in order to 
identify areas of agreement and of disagreement in the scholarship as well as 
gaps that needed filling. Some scholars do characterize elements of Tolkien’s 
fiction as sexist, even misogynistic, but many do not, presenting a range of 
different arguments and drawing on different critical or feminist theories. At no 
point in the essay do I use loaded language like condemn or denounce. 
 My conclusion includes a suggestion for the future of Tolkien 
scholarship on women and female characters which is to: “move beyond the 
perceived need to defend Tolkien (whether the author, the body of work, or the 
human being) from the outdated stereotype  of ‘feminist critics’ who exist only 
to rend and destroy” (39). It is frustrating to see this stereotype asserted nearly 
ten years after the publication of P&F, and even more frustrating because 
Henderson cites, in addition to my essay, seven of the fifte en chapters in the 
collection (this count includes the “Introduction”) which present a more 
complex picture of the scholarship than he conveys. 
Notes 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  257 
 Croft and Donovan’s argument in “Perilous and Fair, Ancient and 
Modern, Luminous and Powerful” is similar to the one I make in my essay. They 
challenge the extent to which the stereotype of feminists attacking Tolkien is a 
weakness in the critical history;  they acknowledge that some critics dismiss 
Tolkien’s fictional women as stereotypes, claiming that his work “[seems] either 
to ignore women or to place them on unattainable pedestals […] preserving 
cultural stereotypes of female roles, and reflecting ant ifeminist tendencies” (1). 
However, they point to other scholars who challenge those interpretations with 
“detailed, innovative scholarship that recognizes myriad positive contexts for 
Tolkien’s female characters and argues more nuanced understandings of 
Middle-earth’s women” (2). Acknowledging that the overall critical consensus 
tends to be “that the historical context of Tolkien’s life prevents him from being 
considered a feminist of any sort […] published discussion frequently interprets 
female characters in The Lord of the Rings  and The Silmarillion as imbued with 
unconventional power” (2). They note that the earliest critical engagement with 
the topic of “female characters” originated in the early 1970s (citing Myers’s 1971 
essay, as does Henderson, and as I did) and reflects “philosophies central to the 
United States civil rights movements of the 1960s and 1970s that inspired interest 
in marginalized fiction […] as well as new investigations of those works from 
feminist perspectives” (1). Later work, pu blished in “the twenty-first century,” 
in contrast shows that “scholars note increasingly that, where other works of 
modern fantasy or mythopoeic literature still offer fairly traditional 
presentations of female characters, Tolkien’s women highlight less common and 
more affirming positions of power” (2).4  
 Croft and Donovan also note that some recent scholarship tends to 
“echo [the stereotypical claims] from the earlier period of Tolkien scholarship,” 
rather than incorporating the different arguments from the later work. I agree 
with them that this reliance  is “troubling after so much research effort and 
printer’s ink has gone to correct similarly ill -informed positions” (2). 5 
 
4 I quote from the “Introduction” at some length to show the difference between Croft and 
Donovan’s analysis of the conflicting critical positions compared to Henderson’s claim of 
the four-decades-long feminist denunciations and attacks on Tolkien and his work that he 
follows with a brief acknowledgement in the next paragraph in which he notes the 
possibility that “Tolkien’s female characters could be both empowering and insipid  at the 
same time” (89, emphasis added). 
5 The first feminist reading of Tolkien I am aware of is Edith Crowe’s 1996 essay which 
was reprinted in P&F, “Power in Arda,” which opens with the observation that “fantasy 
readers of my generation would have had precious little to read in the beginning if we had 
limited ourselves to works fully in tune with our feminist principles” (272). Having met 
and talked with Edith, I know that we are both more or less in the same generational 
cohort, are both feminists, both read a number of the same fantasy novels (mostly by men),

258  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
Henderson, like the example cited by Croft and Donovan, ignores the later work 
or, if citing it, mis -represents the overall arguments and selects quotes that fit 
the stereotype. In fact, he not only ignores Croft and Donovan’s own analysis of 
the scholarship on women and Tolkien: he ignores almost half of the essays in 
P&F as well as mis-representing most of the ones he cites.6 I document examples 
of the mis-representations in a post in Writing from Ithilien, my Substack, because 
the list of examples I collected was too long to include in the published version 
of this article (“The Online Supplement”). 
 While my bibliographic essay was out of date by the time the anthology 
was published, given the schedule of academic publishing, I think it provides a 
foundation for anyone who is interested in the question of women (or female 
characters) and Tolkien and who is willing to engage in the work necessary to 
see how earlier views have been expanded by later scholarship often drawing 
on different critical theories. Reading a bibliographic essay is not equivalent to 
reading and engaging fully with the original so urces, as my comparison of 
excerpts from original sources compared to Henderson’s quoting patterns in the 
“Online Supplement” shows, but bibliographic essays are a good start for a 
research process because they provide an overview, a bibliography, and analysis 
of the discourse patterns in the published scholarship at the time of writing. The 
question then for scholars becomes the extent to which scholarship published 
after the bibliographic essay continues to reflect those patterns, and whether or 
not gaps or areas of conflict identified in the bibliographic essay have been 
addressed, and what new arguments are being made (or could still be made).  
Engaging with the scholarship in this manner does not require agreement with 
the arguments, but, as I used to t ell my students, the ethics of scholarship 
requires that they accurately summarize even those argument that they disagree 
with.7 The time I spent researching, writing, and revising that essay is why I was 
 
and both read Tolkien for the first time in the mid -1960s. She spends the next four 
paragraphs of her essay covering different definitions of “feminism,” something that 
Henderson (and many others) still fail to do. Their failure to realize that “feminism” 
includes a variety theories and praxes and consists of a centuries -long intellectual and 
political body of works that crosses national boundaries and languages and is best 
described by the plural, “feminisms,” is a significant problem.   
6 Although Henderson claims that the history of feminist denunciations goes back to 1971, 
he does not distinguish between the earliest scholarship and the later scholarship, 
completely ignoring the fact that Christopher Tolkien’s extensive posthumous 
publications of his father’s work as well as the development and circulation of later critical 
theories and methods exist. As a result, Henderson’s claim about the “history” of feminist 
attacks is ahistorical, homogeneous, and static, assuming that the only text that is relevant 
to his argument is The Lord of the Rings.

I maintain a bibliography on feminist, gender, and queer scholarship on “Tolkien” (which

able to immediately question Henderson’s use of selected short quotes (as 
opposed to accurate summaries of overall arguments) in support of his claim of 
a long history of feminist scholars attacking Tolkien’s work and him.  
 In the second and final paragraph of the “Background” section, 
Henderson acknowledges that some “critics have insisted that, though only a 
few female characters appear in The Lord of the Rings, they play crucial roles or, 
in some cases, act as role models” (89). This claim moderates his earliest claim 
of a history of condemnation to some extent but falls short of conveying the 
range of arguments that have been made especially at the conclusion of the 
paragraph where Henderson evaluates the two conflicting claims (“feminist 
denunciation” vs. “critics” [who may or may not be feminists?] who praise some 
of the “few female characters”) which he sees in the scholarship. Neither of the 
“antithetical” positions is “necessarily wrong,” he admits, because 
“theoretically, Tolkien’s female characters could be both empowering and insipid 
at the same time” (89, emphasis added).  
This conclusion is an insulting dismissal of work done by scholars who 
see more than Henderson does in Tolkien’s work (and who include more than 
The Lord of the Rings in their scholarship). Reducing a body of complex and often 
conflicting interpretations about female characters in the legendarium, some of 
which result from the differences in critical approaches and disciplinary 
methods chosen by the scholars, to a sexist stereotype of man-hating feminists can 
be achieved only by ignoring or dismissing the s ignificant amount of evidence 
(in hard-copy and online) from women, non -binary, agender, and even some 
men fans and scholars.  
 Henderson’s “Background” section, which are the second and third 
paragraphs of his nineteen -page essay, serves as the scholarly context which 
should provide the scholarly background that shows the originality of his 
overall argument in the essay. Henderson’s argument appears at the end of the 
first paragraph of the essay where, after emphasizing the scarcity of female 
characters and their insignificance to the plot as mere appendages to the male 
characters, he makes this claim: 
 
And yet, I would argue that the conspicuous absence of women in 
Tolkien’s epic, while potentially unsatisfying, nevertheless shapes the 
reading experience by emphasizing the threat that Sauron’s forces, with

their limitless growth, pose to the Free Peoples of Middle -earth, whose

The gap that Henderson perceives in the twelve publications he selected for his 
review of the scholarly literature (by thirteen writers, ten of them women) 9 is 
that they “overlook the effect that women, and their absence have on the reader 
and his [sic] conception of Middle -earth, a world that, without women and children, 
appears disturbingly sterile and depressingly barren” (89, emphasis added).  
 Neither I, nor any of the scholars whose work I am familiar with, would 
disagree with the claim that the female characters are relatively few and are 
given less narrative time (and dialogue!) than the major male characters; that 
claim has been made for decades and is hardly original. Where we would differ, 
I think, is the claim about how Tolkien’s narrative choices “shapes [our] reading 
experience[s]” and our perceptions about Middle -earth (specifically that it is 
“disturbingly sterile and depressingly bar ren”), and the lack of evidence for 
anyone beyond Henderson and his hypothetical (ungenerous and male) readers 
sharing that perception. In addition, the use of the word “barren” as a metaphor 
for sterility not only has extremely negative denotations and co nnotations but 
was also historically more likely to be applied to the woman in a marriage who 
was assumed to be at fault; this assumption is yet another sexist stereotype. 10 
The problem of what specialists now call the “infertility stigma” that women

Given how recent findings on the “’urgent, global’ crisis of [dropping] sperm 
counts” (Hunt) have drawn attention to the diminishment of male fertility, it is 
worth asking why Henderson’s judgement is so gendered, especially in how 
Henderson describes Goldberry (and by implication other female characters)  11 
as being solely responsible for the world’s sterility: “And yet, while Goldberry 
differs from Arwen and Galadriel, she, incredibly, has no children. The 
importance of this can hardly be overstated: Tolkien’s living symbol of feminine 
domesticity is, unless recently married, presumably sterile” (97). 
 There is, of course, no way to diagnose a fictional character’s 
reproductive capabilities, or lack of them, because they are only words on a page 
to be interpreted by readers (who will bring very different experiences to the 
reading) but if the lack of na med characters who are identified as the children 
of Goldberry and Tom is to be declared a problem, I would like to argue that it 
is equally possible to assume that Tom, who is self -described as “old. Eldest 
that’s what I am,” who came into the world “befo re the seas were bent. […] 
before the Dark Lord came from Outside” (LotR I.7.131), has a low sperm count 
due to his immense age (Harris et al., Hunt). There is only one male character 
associated with “sterility”: in Henderson’s last paragraph, he describes Frodo at 
the end of the novel as “[remaining] apart, a relic of sterility in a now-bountiful 
world” (103).12   
 Additionally, Henderson’s claim that none of the existing scholarship 
has noticed what he has, that the scarcity and barrenness of female characters 
(and the resulting lack of their children) creates a “conception of Middle -earth 
[as] disturbingly sterile and depressingly barren” (89), is inaccurate. I know of 
one essay by a woman scholar who analyzes a very different effect that the few 
female characters in the novel have upon at least one other reader’s perceptions 
of the land in Middle-earth in which they live: Maria Błaszkiewicz’s 2013 essay,

are be ing developed elsewhere with some preliminary work done in my personal

12 While Henderson describes the world of Men, Dwarves, Ents, and Elves as sterile (102),

make it clear that the Hobbits were an extremely fecund group before the discovery and 
destruction of the One Ring as well as after. I will also note that essays using queer 
approaches foreground how Bilbo and Frodo, as never -married and childne ss, are 
considered “queer” by the majority of the Hobbits around them. For an excellent 
discussion of just how often the word “queer” is applied to Bilbo and Frodo, see Kisor.

“Tolkien’s Queen-Women in The Lord of the Rings ,” which I summarize in my 
bibliographic essay in P&F. Her argument differs from Henderson’s (and a 
number of other scholarly publications) because she criticizes the scholarly 
tendency to read:  
 
the scarcity of female characters in Tolkien’s work as a sign of sexism or 
lack of authorial skill; instead, she argues that Tolkien’s choice is “ a 
conscious design which aims not to diminish the importance of the female 
element but, on the contrary to glorify it”  […]. Błaszkiewicz argues that 
Tolkien’s careful construction of female characters associated solely with 
one specific type of sanctuary, places safe from evil (as opposed to resting 
places where characters may still be at risk), is an important th ematic 
element. These “omphalic spaces” associated with a female character 
range from Farmer Maggot’s house to Lórien and are contrasted with the 
dying and wasted lands in areas where no female characters are present. 
She concludes that “Tolkien’s design in The Lord of the Rings  has been to 
highlight the exceptional and royal function of a woman, a source of life in all its 
senses” (Reid, “History of Scholarship, Tolkien” 36–37, emphasis added).13 
  
 Clearly, Błaszkiewicz, who has a positive interpretation of the impact 
of Tolkien’s female characters, is neither Henderson’s default male reader, nor 
is she at all similar to his hypothetical “ungenerous reader [who] might say, not 
entirely without justification, that most of Tolkien’s women belong to one of two 
types: the shrew and the goddess” (88, emphasis added). 14 Henderson does not

and differences between the two essays, their overall arguments, and the range of evidence 
chosen, that comparison is outside the scope of this response.

Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  263 
cite Błaszkiewicz’s reading of female characters and their relationship with the 
land in his essay which allows him to avoid having to refute her argument which 
does not fit into the patterns he describes in his “Background” paragraphs. 
However, his failure to acknowledge her work exists is not unique. Scholarship 
analyzing academic citation practices shows a systemic pattern of excluding of 
women’s work (Healy, and Savonick and Davidson). This sort of exclusion, the 
refusal to see or to acknowledge the many readers who love to read and engage 
with Tolkien’s work in ways that challenge such stereotyping, affects the quality 
and development of scholarship and makes it difficult to create spaces for 
exploring complicated questions about how to define the different categories, or 
types, of sexisms or misogynies, let alone the different types of feminisms which 
exist; how it may be possible for a work of literature to contain both feminist 
and misogynistic elements; and how systems of oppression intersect! 15 
 However, there are signs that the scholarship o n Tolkien’s work is 
beginning to acknowledge the extent to which contradictory readings of 
Tolkien’s work co -exist (in academia, popular culture, and fandom). Those 
contradictions are increasingly supported by the evidence from the posthumous 
publications that Christopher Tolkien began making available in 1977 (the 
publication date of The Silmarillion). As Verlyn Flieger points out in “The Arch 
and the Keystone”: 
 
We have pasted labels on [Tolkien], called him a medievalist, a modernist, 
a post -modernist, a royalist, a fascist, a misogynist, a feminist, a racist, an 
egalitarian, a realist, a romantic, an optimist, a pessimist. He’s been variously 
characterized as homophobic and homo-social in both work and life. His fiction 
has been interpreted as Boethian, Manichean, Augustinian and Aquinian.  
He’s been typed as a radical and a conservative, a Christian apologist and a 
pagan, a Catholic who believed in Fairyland, a m onarchist who exalted little 
people, a Tory whose political views leaned toward anarchy  […]. The fact that 
all these labels can find a fit only adds to the confusion (6, emphasis 
added). 
 
 
impression of barrenness that hangs over the novel like her shocking preference for war 
and death.” (98–99) 
“Sam, of course, finds Rosie Cotton, whose very name evokes images of blood -stained 
underwear and bedsheets and, by extension, menstruation and the tearing of the 
hymen.” (102) 
15 As an intersectional feminist, I know there are major unasked questions about the 
heteronormative Whiteness of the “perilous and fair” female characters in Tolkien’s 
legendarium that are worth exploring; see Redmond for a start. However, that topic 
deserves much more space and thought than can fit into this response.

264  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
And it is these same forces creating this same friction that invite the 
disagreeing and debating Tolkien scholars and critics to find in Tolkien’s 
work what they are looking for.  I am not saying they’re wrong . I’m saying 
they’re right. What they see is there, even when they’re seeing contradictory 
things (16, emphasis added).  
 
Flieger’s argument is supported by extensive textual analysis from 
across Tolkien’s legendarium showing the extent of his contradictory statements 
in his fiction and nonfiction.  Even more evidence of the impact of Tolkien’s 
contradictory statements on the state of Tolkien scholarship is provided in 
Flieger’s earlier essay about how scholars have ignored contradictions in what 
Tolkien says about religion (“But What Did  He Really Mean?”), contradictions 
which have resulted in Christian and Pagan scholars cherry-picking some of the 
same quotes to support their argument about Tolkien’s religious ideas and to, 
in effect, claim “Tolkien” for their side.  
The existence of Tolkien’s contradictory statements, leading to 
conflicting interpretations, is something I think Tolkien scholars should, ideally, 
keep in mind when presenting their own interpretative claims in the context of 
the relevant published scholarship. To do so requires critical thought about what 
specific evidence is given by scholars to support their claims as well as following 
Flieger’s advice to keep in mind that analysis of evidence from the edited, 
selected, published letters must take into consideration elements such as the 
date of composition and the specific correspondent Tolkien addresses.  
While Flieger notes that one of the many contradictory interpretations 
about Tolkien’s work, and about him, is that of “misogynist vs. feminist,” she 
does not explore that conflict in either of her two essays, being more interested 
in the “Christian vs. pagan” conflict in “But What Did He Really Mean.” In “The 
Arch and the Keystone,” a published version of her Guest of Honor presentation 
at Mythcon, written to be delivered to an in-person audience, she cannot stop to 
provide detailed examples of all the con flicting interpretations she identifies. 
Because the purpose of her argument in the talk is not to “prove” one side or the 
other correct but to argue that there is evidence in Tolkien’s work for both (and 
more varied) interpretations, I do not think her es say is weaker for that focus; 
instead, she sets up a number of intriguing questions for us to explore.  
I am one of a number of scholars in Tolkien studies (many of whom I 
know personally) who define ourselves as feminists. Building on the fifty-three 
years of work exploring the questions around Tolkien’s female characters, we 
are currently in the process of considering not only Tolkien’s own publications, 
but the growing number of adaptations of his work and how actual readers, not 
the hypothetical/default readers imagined by many scholars, respond to his 
work. For instance, Dawn Walls -Thumma has been runnin g an online 
Notes 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  265 
longitudinal study of Tolkien fans for almost twenty years. Her work is available 
in Cultus Dispatches at the Silmarillion Writers’ Guild. Walls-Thumma is the one 
of the few Tolkien scholars I know of who has done quantitative scholarship on 
Tolkien fandom, and the only one who has administered surveys over time. In 
her column on the demographic data she collected, she notes a change between 
the 2015 and the 2020 surveys: “The two surveys had 1,052 [2015] and 746 [2020] 
valid responses. The 2015 survey collected some demographic data; the 2020 
survey collected more.”  In regard to gender, in the 2015 survey , 89% of the 
respondents were women, 6% were non -binary; 4% were men. In 2020, while 
73% of the respondents were women, the significant demographic change was 
not a major increase in the number of men writing, but in the number of 
nonbinary respondents: 15% were non-binary, and 7% were men.16 
When I first read The Lord of the Rings  in 1965, I was told by my peers 
and the adults around me that “girls don’t read science fiction or fantasy” 
apparently because we are girls. 17 I did not consider myself a feminist until my 
late twenties, but even at age of ten I rejected any attempt to tell me what I could 
or could not read. I was in love with Éowyn and with Middle -earth. I still am. 
As a childfree woman who decided not to marry or have children when I was 
ten, I never noticed or particularly cared about whether the female characters 
had babies. Decades later, I incorporated my queer reader response to her in my 
essay on “Female Bodies and Femininities”:  
 
The author Tolkien constructed Éowyn to serve his purpose in his 
narrative, but her development through multiple revisions is complex. 
His very obvious dislike of the concept of a woman picking up arms to 
fight as men do is clear; yet he is the sub-creator who showed her voicing 
that impassioned statement to Aragorn criticizing his, and the Rohirrim, 
attitudes about women who are denied the chance for battle but can still 
be killed after the battle is lost. She fears a cage—bars that keep her from 
doing great deeds […]. She expresses herself in the language of the heroic 
epic because that is the language she has; that is the language of her 
people and culture. Tolkien’s own work revises the epic heroic model by 
embedding later, Christian, elements, but it is  equally possible for a 
reader of Tolkien’s work to read in sympathy with Éowyn and against

nature of sexism in small-town Idaho in 1965. The only result of this directive was to add 
some additional evidence of my not being “normal.” Luckily for me, my father was an SFF 
fan and encouraged my reading (including providing multiple trips to the public library). 
I have never denied that some women and some feminists do not like Tolkien/see his work 
as sexist; however, some feminists are #notallfeminists.

the grain of her sub -creator who once planned for her death because of 
her masculine qualities. Such a reader can imagine much more in the 
character than the author could show directly. There are gaps in every 
text, and the gaps in this text are large enou gh to allow for a queer 
reading. (Reid, “Light” 109–110). 
 
Now, in 2025, sixty years after first falling in love with The Lord of the Rings, I am 
beyond tired of reading or hearing variations on the over-generalized claim that 
feminists/women/girls don’t like/don’t read Tolkien’s work, or don’t like/hate 
Tolkien, because sexism, a claim which is often presented as self -evident to the 
(often but not always) male authority.  
Years ago, when I first read Edith Crowe’s feminist essay, “Power in 
Arda: Sources, Uses and Misuses,” I was so pleasantly surprised by the scope 
and feminism of her analysis that I wrote her a fan email! I consider her essay to 
be the first academic feminist essay on Tolkien (“feminist” meaning, written by 
someone who is willing to state she is a feminist and who uses feminist theories). 
Her conclusion is one that opens up the possibility of dialogue and engagement, 
rather than closing it off. I consider C rowe’s essay to be required reading for 
anybody who wants to make any argument about “feminist readers” of Tolkien: 
 
This, then, is why I conclude that a person of feminist persuasion, while 
not necessarily agreeing with Tolkien’s attitudes in toto, can find much to 
appreciate in his work. Arda is a world in which females share power in 
spiritual and temporal realms, although not always to the degree one 
might wish. More importantly, it is a world in which attitudes and 
values associated in the Primary World with the feminine are highly 
valued. Indeed, these “feminine” values triumph at the end of the Third 
Age, though no t always incarnate in female bodies.  Though Tolkien’s 
road was his interpretation of Christianity, and Eisler’s (and mine) our 
interpretation of feminism, the destination seems to have a great deal 
in common. Our mutual task in the Fourth Age is to resist the temptation 
to divide and dominate, whether we characterize this misuse of power as 
that of the Blade or the Ring. May Varda look with favour upon our 
efforts (from 1996 Mythlore, 277, bold emphasis added).  
 
Henderson paraphrases one small aspect of Crowe’s overall argument in the 
second paragraph of his “Background” section: “Edith L. Crowe notes that, 
while Tolkien divides the masculine and the feminine into different spheres, he 
does not denigrate the femin ine or prize the former over the latter, ‘ their 
relationship [being] one of linking, not ranking’  (89).” The partial quote Henderson 
includes in his paragraphs occurs three pages before the concluding paragraph 
I excerpt above. His single sentence does not mis-represent one of her arguments 
Notes 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  267 
(as I argue is the case for some of his other choices and document in “The Online 
Supplement18), but a comparison of her original with his paraphrase shows that 
he fails to accurately convey her overall argument about feminist readers and 
the common ground we may find with Christian readers.  
 My exhaustion with the problem that Henderson is the latest example 
of is one of the reasons I began writing feminist and queer scholarship about 
Tolkien’s legendarium (and about the works of other fantasy writers, especially 
a number of the women who ack nowledge Tolkien’s influence on their fantasy 
and whose work is, in my reading of it, not only transformative of Tolkien but 
critically engaged with different feminist theories). I also began writing feminist 
bibliographies because of the significant incre ase in published Tolkien 
scholarship during the 21st century, a growth many of us credit to the release of 
Peter Jackson’s films (Fimi 200). I think it impossible to read, or create, a 
comprehensive bibliography or complete analysis of the past seventy yea rs of 
publications which means that there will always be choices made to exclude 
some publications. Since my experience leads me to believe that scholarship by 
women (or about feminist topics) which interest me greatly is more likely to be 
excluded, I thought it a useful project (Reid “History of Scholarship, Tolkien”; 
Reid “History of Scholarship, Bujold”). I am grateful to Janet and Leslie who 
allowed and encouraged me in my first attempt to develop this approach. 
 My bibliographies are feminist because they are written by “a feminist 
academic trained in cultural studies,” not because “the scholarship is defined as 
feminist, or written by self -identified feminists (although some is), or is even 
limited to authors wh o identify as women” (“History of Scholarship, Tolkien” 
13). At no time do I make any argument regarding Tolkien’s personal feelings 
about women; in fact, I am not interested in the question of Tolkien’s own 
feelings or attitudes about women, or about “feminism” (especially if there is no 
definition of the word provided). I have no interest in trying to write 
biographical scholarship, or to do some version of analyzing his 
feelings/ideas/intent (as opposed to some limited analysis of his writing which 
can i nclude quotes from his letters). Instead, I would rather write about his 
work, about how his work has been received and understood over the decades 
I have lived through, and about the fans and scholars now living who are 
creating new interpretations of his work, especially those of us who apparently 
are not supposed to exist, or at least, should not expect to be seen while existing.
