                         EVIEWS  
 
 
  
 
 
J.R.R.  TOLKIEN : A VERY SHORT INTRODUCTION . Matthew Townend. 
Oxford UP, 2024. 160 p. 9780192882042. $12.99 
 
OMING IN AT JUST OVER ONE HUNDRED PAGES , Matthew Townend’s J.R.R. 
Tolkien: A Very Short Introduction may be concise, but it is a well-researched, 
clearly written and engaging analysis of one of the twentieth century’s most 
creative and original writers. While Townend’s focus is (not surprisingly) on The 
Hobbit and The Lord of the Rings —what he calls two of the most frequently re -
read books today —analyses of Tolkien’s other lesser -known works, including 
academic writings and work that was posthumously published, are effectively 
integrated alongside the discussion. This recently included publication in the 
Very Short Introduction series is overdue (there was a C.S. Lewis VSI in 2019) but 
is a welcome addition to Tolkien Studies. I can see this VSI being particularly 
valuable for new readers of Tolkien who want to learn more about him and his 
writing, and for students who want a compact guide through Tolkien’s major 
concerns. This would thus make an excellent resource for teachers and 
professors seeking to introduce Tolkien to new readers.  
Townend takes a thematic approach rather than a chronological review 
of Tolkien’s major writings, and so the book is structured into eight chapters that 
are arranged to explore recurring ideas in his body of work: 1) Reading Tolkien 
2) Life and work 3) Sto ries 4) Faërie 5) Language 6) Sources 7) Middle -earth 8) 
Elegy. The first chapter is essentially a brief introduction or preface that sets out 
the wide -ranging effect of Tolkien’s work. Townend notes that for many 
readers, The Lord of the Rings  offers an e xperience of re -enchantment, opening 
the door to a world of myth, folklore, and fairy tale; for other readers, 
encountering Tolkien’s world has led to an environmental awakening, or a 
newfound interest in language, or a renewed appreciation for art and cra ft, or 
has heightened their sense of justice for the small and often overlooked. 
Comparing Tolkien to other cultural phenomena like The Beatles or Star Wars, 
Townend argues, as many other scholars have, that Tolkien has “permanently 
altered the forms and f lavours of popular culture” across several media —"not 
just fiction, with the explosion of the fantasy genre that followed in his wake,” 
but in music, gaming, and film and television (2).  
C 
Reviews 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  271 
The second chapter, “Life and work,” begins with a brief biography of 
Tolkien that effectively summarizes the key events in his life. Townend then 
offers a response to the question “What sort of a person was Tolkien”? by noting 
some character traits that e merge from comments both from people who knew 
him personally and from those who have studied him closely: he was “quite 
diffident” though “he could grow animated in conversation”; he was a 
“dedicated and affectionate family man” and someone who had a great love for 
the natural world; in terms of his politics he is best described as conservative but 
“with a fascinating admixture of the liberal and even radical”; he was both a 
procrastinator and a perfectionist to the point where he was a “chronic non -
finisher of tasks;” he was sociable and yet independent, and often dealt with a 
lack of confidence in his work (10). Like most people, Townend concludes, “he 
was complex and contradictory” (10). An interesting observation Townend 
makes here is that Tolkien’s deep Catholic faith gave him a consciousness of his 
own weakness that was reinforced by his experience in the First World War, 
from which he emerged with a sharp sense of his own moral limitations, in terms 
of both courage and endurance (10). This is a useful biographical insight that 
helps in framing what Tolkien is doing with his hobbit characters, in whom both 
courage and endurance are so memorably manifested.  
Townend also emphasizes that although to many readers today 
Tolkien might look like a conventional and stereotypical Oxford don, it is 
important to remember that while he did spend thirty -five years in the 
professoriate, Tolkien always retained a sense of himself as an outsider and 
possessed “a warm sympathy for other outsiders”: he had been an orphan, he 
had known poverty, he was born outside of England, his wife Edith was 
illegitimate, and he experienced prejudice as a result of his Catholicism (11). 
Tolkien was also not a professional writer —he wrote as a hobby, in his spare 
time, and was only tenuously connected to the literary world of publishers, 
prizes, and periodicals. He didn’t write book reviews, and The Lord of the Rings 
itself became an unplanned, unexpected success through word -of-mouth, from 
the ground up because of actual readers, not as the result of a media campaign. 
Thus, Townend concludes that while Tolkien’s works have been “canonized,” 
at this point, “this has be en a movement from below, by millions of readers” 
(16). 
Chapter Three, entitled “Stories,” offers a valuable look at the tension 
in Tolkien between storytelling and world -building. Townend states that 
Tolkien was “above all a storyteller,” whose first instinct was towards narrative, 
rather than towards analysis (21). Townend rightly points out that these are two 
distinct modes of creativity, and that in Tolkien, the dynamic between story -
making and system-making was a wellspring of imaginative energy and helped 
generate some of his most beloved and mysterious ch aracters, like Gollum and 
Reviews 
272  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
Beorn, who both initially arose out of Tolkien’s story -making impulse in The 
Hobbit but then, later, in The Lord of the Rings are given further back stories and 
explication. He argues that as Tolkien aged, he was increasingly inclined 
towards system-making, and that this is one of the major reasons he was unable 
to complete the Silmarillion or bring together the body of legends he had been 
working on for nearly sixty years into an integrated whole. Tolkien was a 
literary pioneer, who brought together genres that had not before been 
combined in the way that he brought them together. So, for i nstance, aspects 
from fairy tale and aspects from realist fiction cohere in The Lord of the Rings and 
in The Hobbit  (where Bilbo is a modern interloper into a legendary world, 
wearing a waistcoat and possessing “trousers with pockets”) and this 
innovation was one of his greatest sources of difficulty (32). In The Hobbit, says 
Townend, no reader weeps when the fairy -tale-like goblins have their heads 
chopped off; but in the more realist The Lord of the Rings, Tolkien could not help 
taking seriously questions about the origins of orcs, or whether they had free 
will, or whether Morgoth was really able to create them. In effect, questions like 
this over which Tolkien hesitated led to an attempt to rationalize and explain 
the mythic content of the legendarium, a process that sometimes threatened to 
disenchant his tales of the very quality his younger self had so valued —the 
quality of Faërie (36). In a time when what audiences have come to call “world-
building” is ever more foregrounded in contemporary fantasy, Townend’s 
insights here are timely.  
Chapter Four, “Faërie,” treats the specific nature of the enchantment 
that Tolkien most sought to convey in his writing. Here, Townend appropriately 
begins with an overview of “On Fairy -stories” and proceeds to outline how 
Tolkien’s theories are born out i n the praxis of his primary texts, particularly 
those that lean towards fairy -story. Tolkien claims in his essay that one of the 
most profound longings of the human heart that fairy tales satisfy is the “desire 
to converse with other living things,” and Townend shows how talking animals 
are found frequently in Tolkien’s early works, from Tevildo the Prince of Cats, 
to Roverdandom, to Roäc the raven in The Hobbit. Tolkien argues in “On Fairy-
stories” that stories primarily concerned with fairies, or with elves, are as a rule 
not very interesting, but that “most good ‘fairy stories’ are about the aventures 
of men in the Perilous Realm or upon its shadowy marches”; this mortal desire 
to encounter fairies is a constant motif in Tolkien’s works, Townend emphasizes, 
from the first writings in The Book of Lost Tales  that feature Eriol, later Ælfwine 
or “elf -friend” travelling to Tol Eresesëa, to the later tales of mortal men like 
Beren, Túrin, and Tuor (39). 
“Language” is the title and subject of the fifth chapter, and here 
Townend’s expertise in Old Norse and his interests in medievalism and 
philology allow him to offer a concise but very useful overview of Tolkien’s 
Reviews 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  273 
linguistic aesthetics and the multilinguistic character of the legendarium. While 
there exist many treatments of Tolkien’s use of Quenya and Noldorin/Sindarin, 
Townend points out that in The Lord of the Rings we encounter other languages 
as well, including Dwarvish or Khuzdul, Entish, and the “Black Speech” of 
Mordor, as well as background hints as to the language of Númenor (Adûnaic) 
brought to Middle -earth from Númenórean exiles, that eventually became 
“Westron” or “Common Speech.” Middle-earth is “thus a multilingual world,” 
and Tolkien’s stories are “filled with moments of bilingualism, translation, 
language contact, and diglossia (that is, language -switching according to 
context)—in addition to discourses on linguistic history” (57 –58). This leads 
Townend to a discussion of the issue of translation, and the way Tolkien draws 
on different framing devices for his stories, beginning with the wanderer Eriol 
in The Book of Lost Tales, that remained a core aspect of Tolkien’s mythology for 
many decades and led him to present his fictional texts as ancient stories he had 
translated rather than invented. While this posed increasingly complicated 
problems for Tolkien, it was also in many ways “a brilliant conceit,” according 
to Townend, and superbly carried out by  an author who was uniquely poised 
to do so.  
The sixth chapter, “Sources,” begins with a treatment of modern 
medievalism, and the way Tolkien’s use of source material was revolutionary: 
unlike so many other writers who are unconsciously influenced by their 
reading, Tolkien’s creative process involved  a very deliberate act of adoption 
and adaptation. He was making “a new kind of literature out of medieval 
ingredients,” not simply retelling medieval stories (65). However, Townend 
emphasizes that Tolkien wasn’t exclusively a medievalist; his knowledge of the 
Latin and Greek classics was profound, and he was well read in post -medieval 
literature from Shakespeare to contemporary fiction. It is Old English, or Anglo-
Saxon, though that Townend says especially saturated Tolkien’s imagination, 
and he gives a detailed close reading of specific passages in The Hobbit and The 
Lord of the Rings  that echo passages in Beowulf. He also shows how Tolkien’s 
source material manifests not only in word-forms but also in word-meanings, as 
with the word reek, from the Old Eng lish rēc, or what Townend calls a 
“Tolkienian favorite”: rēc means “smoke,” rather than the modern meaning of 
“smell,” for which Tolkien prefers “stench,” from the Old English stenc (71). So, 
for instance, Frodo sees that “Mount Doom was burning, and a great reek rising” 
(71). This subtle influence from medieval sources also explains some of Tolkien’s 
distinctive diction. Townend argues that Tolkien had a preference for syntactic 
inversion or sentence -fronting, gained from his reading of William Morris 
especially, which is one of the most prominent features of his prose for many 
readers today: as examples, he cites the single paragraph when the Fellowship 
passes by the Argonath and the narrator declares that “Giants they seemed to 
Reviews 
274  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
[Frodo]” and “Great power and majesty they still wore” versus the more 
standard “They seemed giants to Frodo” and “They still wore great power and 
majesty” (74 –75). Because Tolkien’s prose is so lyrical, often even poetic, this 
kind of formal analysis of his diction is helpful, and not engaged in often 
enough.  
Townend titled chapter seven “Middle-earth,” and in it he analyzes the 
ways in which Tolkien succeeds in drawing connections between our earth and 
Arda. While, as previously mentioned, Townend praises Tolkien’s skills as a 
world-builder, he acknowledges he re that Tolkien’s world -building activities 
are not evenly distributed, but (naturally) reflect his own interests; so Middle -
earth is very detailed in terms of its socio -linguistics and history, but less well 
developed in regards to its agriculture or econ omics (as G.R.R. Martin has 
famously complained). W.H. Auden was “bothered by the lack of pets in The 
Lord of the Rings,” and there are indeed inevitable gaps in depicting a full world 
(85). However, Townend declares that where Tolkien most shines, apart from 
linguistics, is in botany. The world of Middle -earth is a world of biodiversity, 
and this is painstakingly rendered so as to feel as “real” as a fictional world can. 
Also realistic is Tolkien’s portrayal of different cultures and their 
interrelationships with each other; the legendarium is full of stories of migration 
and movement, so that “[f]ew of the peoples of Middle-earth are associated with 
an inalienable homeland,” meaning that Tolkien’s stories probe at questions of 
xenophobia and prejudice (89–90).  
The last chapter, “Elegy,” looks at the genre in which Tolkien classified 
Beowulf—not an epic but a “heroic-elegiac” work—and applies this same generic 
category to The Lord of the Rings . As all readers of Tolkien know, this is a story 
of poignantly sorrowful loss and change, and our relationship to the text mirrors 
the experience of the members of the Fellowship as they travel through a 
Middle-earth covered with the physical remnants of the past, from old standing 
stones and burial chambers to ruined watch towers to broken roads hidden 
under weeds; these scenes give us “half -hidden glimpses of a ‘vanished past,’” 
and contribute to the “ache of mortality” running all throughout Tolkien’s 
mythology (99). That Tolkien understood the grief of this ache so thorou ghly 
makes it all the more remarkable that his stories are also stories that champion 
the virtue of hope, or what Townend glosses as an implicitly Christianized hope 
(estel) that involves trusting in “the ultimate efficacy of divine providence” (102). 
Remarking on this sense of eucatastrophe is an appropriate way to conclude his 
introduction to Tolkien, as is his short discussion of Leaf by Niggle  and the 
sudden, eucatastrophic turn at the very end of this stunningly profound 
“Christian fairy tale”: “He gazed at the Tree, and slowly he lifted his arms and 
opened them wide. ‘It’s a gift!’ he said” (qtd. 106 –7). The Tree is not only a gift 
to Niggle, but to other travellers who come to value it as well, so that it turns 
Reviews 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  275 
out to be “very useful indeed […] as a holiday, and a refreshment” (qtd. 107). 
We can take Tolkien’s whole body of writings as the Tree, and agree with 
Townend that journeying to the worlds he created is indeed both a holiday and 
a refreshment.  
 
LAURA N. VAN DYKE  teaches in the Department of English and Creative Writing at 
Trinity Western University, in Langley, British Columbia. She is a co-editor of The Inklings 
and Culture (2020) and helps to direct the Inklings Institute of Canada. 
 
 
 
TOLKIEN ’S COSMOLOGY : DIVINE BEINGS AND MIDDLE -EARTH . Sam 
McBride. Kent, OH: Kent State UP. 2020. 272 p. 978-1606353967. $55.00.  
 
AM MCBRIDE WRITES THE FOLLOWING  in his Introduction to Tolkien’s 
Cosmology: Divine Beings and Middle-earth: “Exploring Tolkien’s legendarium 
will show the cosmological structure of Middle -earth, but also reveal moments 
in The Hobbit  and The Lord of the Rings  when the metaphysical impacts the 
physical” (x). The book delivers on its promise to show the reader places within 
the stories of The Hobbit  and The Lord of the Rings  likely to be influenced by 
spiritual beings who are described in The Silmarillion and the other volumes of 
the Legendarium. McBride’s book gi ves the reader a convincing description of 
the divine interventions of both visible and invisible powers from the earlier 
Ages of Middle-earth as they appear during the Third Age.  
The voice of the book is so approachable and genuine that it was easy 
to forget that I was, in fact, reading a book, and not having a conversation with 
the author. I never felt that I was being lectured at by a literature professor. At 
the same time, McBride’s argument is founded upon a thorough knowledge of 
J.R.R. Tolkien’s works and of Tolkien literary criticism up to the writing of the 
book published in 2020. The conversational feel and erudition of the work make 
this book a serviceable tool to both novice and seasoned readers of the works of 
J.R.R. Tolkien. One of the many things I like about the author is that he is aware 
that he might be guilty of confirmation bias in some or many of his examples. 
His humility is laudable as he both makes his argument and seeks to be certain 
he carries it only as far as it is justified (x). 
The author’s focus is to show the ways that The Silmarillion  is a 
foundation to what appears in the stories of The Hobbit and The Lord of the Rings. 
He emphasizes The Silmarillion  because it provides the first description of 
Tolkien’s Legendarium after his death (xiv –xvii). McBride secondarily 
S 
Reviews 
276  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
references J.R.R. Tolkien’s other works to secure all of the points of his 
argument. I wrote above that Tolkien’s Cosmology can be a serviceable tool for 
different levels of readers. At one point the author observes of The Silmarillion, 
“Its mythological style makes it the most widely purchased unread book 
published under Tolkien’s name” (xv). Personally, I was in my thirties before I 
was able to finish reading and then became entranced by The Silmarillion. I share 
the experience the author describes of man y Tolkien fans who have reread The 
Hobbit and The Lord of the Rings multiple times but cannot get through Tolkien’s 
daunting work that looks like The Old Testament and doesn’t feel like his other 
books the reader has come to love. Like other fans, I tried many times to read 
The Silmarillion and eventually made the conscious decision to never try again. 
By chance, I listened to audiobooks, ones I knew I was never going to read in 
print, while I created stained glass. A few hours into listening to The Silmarillion 
read by Martin Shaw and I stopped working on stained glass and simply 
listened to Tolkien’s beautiful language and sequence of deep tales. At the end 
of the recording, I bought and read a print copy. That experience led to many 
re-readings of The Silmarillion and ultimately to my reading all of the volumes 
of The History of Middle-earth, some volumes as many as three times.  
Sam McBride’s Tolkien’s Cosmology  could give today’s readers an 
experience similar to my own with the Martin Shaw recording to become a 
gateway to The Silmarillion and possibly the rest of the Legendarium. I suggest 
that Tolkien’s Cosmology  could be such a gateway to The Silmarillion because 
McBride’s book is so approachable and pleasant to read that a novice can be 
introduced to Eru Ilúvatar, the Valar, the rebellion of Morgoth, the creation of 
Arda, and the downstream effects of Morgoth’s rebellion to the  history of all of 
the Ages of Middle-earth, and ultimately the stories in The Hobbit and The Lord 
of the Rings . His introduction to the spiritual beings and creation story is 
followed by chapters providing convincing instances of divine intervention that 
McBride sees in The Hobbit  and The Lord of the Rings . The book ends, 
appropriately, with a chapter on Eucatastrophe. 
For more seasoned readers Tolkien’s Cosmology does a fine job of 
summarizing much of the Legendarium, which can be useful in reminding us of 
the roles of the cast of characters within Tolkien’s works. There are times when 
McBride articulates and develops observations that a seasoned reader might 
have observed but not formally expressed during their own reading. It is likely 
that McBride will provide new observations for most experienced readers, 
which makes the book worthwhile.  
 Tolkien’s Cosmology: Divine Beings and Middle -earth is more a piece of 
literary criticism than a reference work such as The Lord of the Rings: A Reader’s 
Companion by Wayne G. Hammond and Christina Scull. McBride’s book should 
not be expected to be as exhaustive or to be used in the same way as Hammond 
Reviews 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  277 
and Scull’s great reference work is used. Still, Tolkien’s Cosmology can be used as 
an approachable reference work on Tolkien’s Legendarium and on the 
connections between The Silmarillion and The Hobbit and The Lord of the Rings . I 
believe its strength is in the author’s ability to point to the many appearances of 
divine intervention from The Silmarillion into The Hobbit and The Lord of the Rings. 
I agree with the author that his overall interpretation of The Hobbit and The Lord of 
the Rings does add depth to a reader’s experience of the three books discussed here.  
Another useful point of this text is its connection with world religions. 
While it will be natural for religious people to be interested in the book, being 
so isn’t necessary. Tolkien has many unbelieving or skeptical fans, who I think, 
will be just as int erested, as religious people are, in McBride’s description of 
Tolkien’s divine beings from The Silmarillion influencing The Hobbit and The Lord 
of the Rings.  
I especially recommend this book to readers who are attempting to get 
a toehold into an appreciation of The Silmarillion. I will end by repeating that I 
find Sam McBride’s Tolkien’s Cosmology: Divine Beings and Middle-earth to be such 
a pleasant experience that I recommend it for pleasure reading to all Tolkien 
fans. I know I will be rereading it in the future. 
 
PHILLIP FITZSIMMONS is the Steward for Society Archives for the Mythopoeic Society. His 
professional title is the University Archivist and Special Collections Librarian at

the SWOSU Digital Commons. He manages Mythsoc's physical and digital collections 
through the SWOSU archive facilities. The Mythsoc digital repository is found at 
https://dc.swosu.edu/mythsoc/.  His research interests include the works of J.R.R. Tolkien, 
Owen Barfield, the Inklings, and Joseph Campbell , and digital services for academic

administration, and library reference services. 
 
 
 
DIARY OF AN OLD SOUL . George MacDonald. Annotated edition with 
introd. and notes by Timothy Larsen. Downers Grove: InterVarsity Press 
Academic, 2024. 296 p. 9781514007686. $30.00. 
 
IRST PUBLISHED IN 1880, A Book of Strife in the Form of the Diary of an Old Soul by 
George MacDonald is a collection of 366 poems, one for each day of the year 
including one for the twenty-ninth day of February that occurs in leap years. In 
this republished edition with the shorter title  Diary of an Old Soul , Timothy 
F 
Reviews 
278  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
Larsen provides an introduction with occasional annotations in the poems. 
Larsen writes about poems that are “designed to slow us down,” a characteristic 
shared with devotional literature (3), which he explores briefly in the context of 
the Victorians and MacDonald. Larsen also evaluates the meaning of 
MacDonald’s book title and how “strife” and “old soul” relate to the poems’ 
content (10–12), along with a key conclusion being that Diary of an Old Soul is “a 
collection of prayers” that “draw close to the he art of the deepest forms of 
spiritual devotion” (13). The poems express MacDonald’s faith in God, 
sometimes with allusions to passages from the Bible and often rich with imagery 
from nature, and how his faith intertwines with and illuminates his life 
experiences.  
There have been other republications of Diary of an Old Soul  since the 
1880s, and this republished edition claims to follow the original text closely. As 
an annotated edition, the actual annotations seem sparse, though this is fine to 
avoid having a plethora of footnotes and reference numbers that can be 
distracting, even when they are minimal, on the same page as the poems. The 
notations vary; there may be one to three footnotes for a poem, or no footnotes 
for several pages. The content is of scholarly or general interest and helpful as 
definitions of words less well known for the general reader or providing 
biographical information behind the poem or the time it was written.  
While this is an academic edition, the introduction’s  scholarly 
information is intermingled with personal commentary, and Larsen refers 
readers to “scholarly treatments” of MacDonald’s book in a footnote (2n1). It 
could have been interesting to share more background on MacDonald during 
the time of writing and publishing his poems, so to better complete the picture, 
I looked through MacDonald’s published letters. For instance, in a letter to his 
sister Georgina (Mrs. William Cowper -Temple), dated January 26,  1880, 
MacDonald writes her, “I send you at last a copy for yourself of my little book,” 
his privately published Diary of an Old Soul. He writes further: “Where shall we 
send the twenty copies you were so kind to order? Looking over it with more 
interest that I have yet felt for a book of my own, I think it is not a failure, but a 
great deal will depend, not only on the degree and kind of spiritual training but 
on the mood of the reader. That you will be with me in it I have every hope” 
(301). Notably here, MacDonald implies that the book’s success will also depend 
on how readers approach the poems and possibly to what depth of spiritual 
insight and understanding they have in appreciating what the poems 
communicate. This corresponds with what Larsen writes i n his introduction 
about the devotional nature of the poems.  
Following Larsen’s introduction, MacDonald’s poems are divided like 
chapters into the twelve months of the year from January to December, with 
each poem numbered for its respective day in the month. The book’s format 
Reviews 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  279 
easily serves as a regular companion throughout the year as a daily reading with 
a blank page next to each page of poems —like MacDonald’s first edition and 
intention—where readers can write or reflect on paper extending the 
“devotional conversation” (20). There is often a continuity between the ideas 
and themes (such as life, death, and love) running through the days that add to 
the interest in following how they may unfold or weave other thoughts and 
prayers together. For example, November’s poems begin th e first day with the 
concept that “Thou [Christ] know’st it all” and introduces the word “dark” 
briefly. In the poem for the second day of November, MacDonald expands on 
what Christ knows, including human experiences and the difficulty of being, 
and again mentions the dark. This motif becomes more prominent through the 
next three poems and connects with the night, culminating on the fifth day of 
November’s poem with the poet looking for Christ in the dark (243–44).  
MacDonald’s spiritual impetus might not resonate with all readers, but 
the depth and insights he writes about can speak to those willing to read and 
reflect on one or many of the poems. The beauty of this book is MacDonald’s 
poetic words and imagery portraying his deep belief and trust in God no matter 
how difficult or painful life can be, and readers may also find encouragement 
and hope in the pages. This particular published version is red cloth with gold 
embossing, under the dust jacket, and can be an at tractive gift or addition to a 
personal library. Its appearance both inside and out would probably please 
MacDonald himself who “loved books as physical objects” (19), and more 
importantly, anyone who values poems will likely find enjoyment or something 
meaningful to contemplate within them.  
 
BIBLIOGRAPHY 
MacDonald, George. An Expression of Character: The Letters of George MacDonald. Ed. Glenn 
Edward Sadler. Eerdmans, 1994.  
 
TIFFANY BROOKE MARTIN has a PhD in English and the Teaching of English from 
Idaho State University. Her dissertation was on Owen Barfield’s writings with a 
focus on fantasy and fiction, and she has been an adviser and editor for the Owen 
Barfield Literary Estate. Tiffany currently works as global editorial content manager 
for Roquette, an international company that produces ingredients for food, health, 
and nutrition markets. 
 
 
 

Reviews 
280  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
MIMETIC THEORY & MIDDLE -EARTH : UNTANGLING DESIRE IN 
TOLKIEN ’S LEGENDARIUM . Matthew J. Distefano. Chico, CA: Quoir, 2024. 
202 p. ISBN 9781957007922. $19.99. 
 
ATTHEW DISTEFANO’S FRIENDLY VOLUME  extends considerably the 
conversation about resonances between two twentieth -century literary 
giants begun by Hayden Head’s 2007 Mythlore article, “Imitative Desire in 
Tolkien’s Mythology: A Girardian Perspective.” René Girard (1923 –2015) rose 
to prominence as a literary scholar with the publication of his first book, 
Mensonge romantique et verité romanesque,  in 1961 (translated in 1965 as Deceit, 
Desire and the Novel ), around the time Tolkien’s The Lord of the Rings came into 
vogue, but there is no eviden ce that they knew each other’s work. Yet, as 
Distefano ably shows, Tolkien’s work manifests the insights Girard found in the 
novels of Cervantes, Stendhal, Flaubert, Dostoevsky, and Proust, which led him 
to form the theory of desire, violence, and cultural origins he went on to pursue 
across other classic literature, from Greek tragedy to Shakespeare, and into fields 
such as anthropology and biblical studies. Most important for Distefano, 
however, is that both authors, each of whom he has written about sepa rately, 
distill valuable wisdom for living. Bringing them together not only clarifies 
essential aspects of Tolkien’s work by reading it through the lens of Girard’s 
mimetic theory but also highlights, extends, and one might even say enacts 
aspects of Girard’s theory that are easily overlooked. 
Distefano’s short, digestible chapters are organized into five sections 
that move from an overview of mimetic theory, with Tolkienian examples, to a 
more extended focus on elements that most reward mimetic analysis: the One 
Ring and, especially, Hobbits.  
The aptness of illustrations from Tolkien’s works make this an excellent 
introduction to Girard’s theory. Part of mimetic theory’s beauty is the simplicity 
and power of its two central ideas. First, human desire is fundamentally 
imitative and unconsciously  so—hence the term “mimetic.” Beyond our 
responses to what meets immediate physiological needs and appetites, we copy 
the desires of others, which leads us into rivalry for the objects of these shared 
desires. Girard primarily studied the violent consequen ces of mimetic desire, 
and sometimes even used the term to refer just to rivalrous desire, but, as 
Distefano rightly emphasizes, he also made clear that mimetic desire can have 
either good or bad results and is simply a fact of our nature. Indeed, Girard saw 
it as essentially good because it makes us free to choose our models rather than 
be driven just by appetite and instinct. The steady drumbeat of Distefano’s 
explication is how hobbits can serve as models of positive mimesis, desire that 
leads to friendship rather than strife. 
M 
Reviews 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  281 
Yet Distefano also pays strong attention to Girard’s second thesis: that 
human groups have had a persistent pattern, since prehistory, of temporarily 
resolving the escalating violence of mimetic rivalry that always threatens to 
engulf them by directing their violence at a single, essentially arbitrary victim—
what we have come to call in English, using a word from Tyndale’s translation 
of Leviticus, a scapegoat. Spontaneous scapegoating gives rise to the universal 
components of primitive religion: repetition of the scapegoat mechanism in the 
form of sacrificial ritual, prohibitions of the most virulent causes of rivalry, and 
myths that misremember the original victims of scapegoating as gods and 
sacralize violence. From this, according to Girard, develop the major institutions 
of human culture. Here Distefano reaches deepest into Tolkien’s legendarium 
for illustrations: Melkor’s mimetic envy as the source of proliferating violence, 
the ban of the Valar against sailing to the undying lands, the sacrifices initi ated 
by Sauron in Númenor. Readers of the Silmarillion will be able to add more. 
Again, however, where Girard’s analysis of culture focuses on the 
workings of sacred violence, Distefano emphasizes Tolkien’s alternatives. The 
culture of the Shire, for instance, balances a minimum of prohibitions with the 
freedom of imitating non -conflictual models of desire and celebrating non -
sacrificial rituals of friendship. While myth in Girard’s negative sense is present 
as the discord introduced by Melkor into the music of the Ainur (and variously 
darkening the minds of the peoples of Middle-earth), the stories told by Tolkien 
are what Distefano calls “subversive mythology.” Thus Beren is an “attempted 
scapegoat” of Thingol who becomes instead a model of heroic self-sacrifice, and 
Thingol a model of conversion from resentment to pity. The whole stor y of 
Smeagol/Gollum, Frodo, and Sam is a similarly subversive myth that Distefano 
deals with a much greater length. Gandalf’s story of how Gollum’s original 
community scapegoated him allows him to be seen with pity. Frodo’s self -
sacrifice becomes a reversal of scapegoating when he chooses his final exile from 
the Shire. 
Distefano points out one instance of Tolkien mythologizing in a 
negative sense: the depiction of Goblins in The Hobbit as the embodiment of evil, 
a myth that justifies their destruction at the Battle of the Five Armies, where they 
function as a scapegoat that makes temporary peace between Dwarves, Elves, 
and Men. Though Distefano does not consider the more complex depictio n of 
Orcs in The Lord of the Rings, he briefly raises the issue through a quotation from 
George R. R. Martin complaining that Tolkien fai ls to answer questions about 
Aragorn’s policy toward them as king. Distefano suggests that Tolkien gives us 
enough to imagine that Aragorn would show the kind of pity that Frodo shows 
Gollum. I would add that Tolkien strongly implies as much, and answers 
Martin’s concern, when he writes, in the chapter “The Steward and the King,” 
that “the slaves of Mordor he released and gave to them all the lands about Lake 
Reviews 
282  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
Núrnen to be their own.” While calling the Goblins “Manichean ‘bad guys’” (97, 
136) may fit the polarization of good and evil in The Hobbit, the depiction of Orcs, 
along with the other antagonists in the larger legendarium, ultimately conforms 
to Tolkien’s (and Girard’s) Augustinian vision of evil as a privation, and 
perversion, of good. 
The two chapters devoted to the One Ring well demonstrate how it 
rewards a mimetic analysis, with emphasis again on the culture and models of 
positive mimesis that help Hobbits resist the contagion of desire for power that 
it represents. Readers will be able to extend this analysis to the Silmarils and the 
Arkenstone as both objects and symbols of rivalrous desire (the latter discussed 
by Joshua Hren; see below) as well as Tolkien’s depiction of dragon-sickness in 
The Hobbit and his poem “The Hoard.” 
In the final chapters, Distefano’s interest in Tolkien’s models of positive 
mimesis takes center stage, primarily with reference to the Shire but also with 
mention of non -Hobbit members of the fellowship of the Ring —which could, 
again, be extended to Éomer , Éowyn, Faramir, and others. Brief discussion of 
“Boromir’s folly” could likewise point to the fuller representation of mimetic 
rivalry in his father, Denethor. But the mutual illumination of Girard and 
Tolkien on the positive potential of mimesis is the strength of this book, which 
comes through also in the “lessons from Middle -earth” at the end of each 
chapter, Distefano’s reflections on his own efforts to live these out, his friendly 
tone and informal style, and his hope that conscious desire really can save us all. 
To explain why Tolkien and Girard would be so deeply compatible, 
Distefano adverts to their shared Catholic faith and to the importance Girard 
places on Judeo-Christian revelation for showing how to interpret myth. Indeed, 
Girard finds the Bible supreme fo r its insight into mimetic desire and 
scapegoating, and thus for conceiving peaceful modes of communion. The 
greatness of many works of literature, for Girard, comes from how they 
anticipate or reflect these insights. The fact that Girard found his way to these 
insights by way of his reading of classic literature invites the question of whether 
something similar might be true for Tolkien, whose creative works were 
nurtured by his responses to the literature he studied and loved. Distefano’s case 
for adding Tolkien to the Girardian canon invites consideration of the two side-
by-side as interpreters of Western literary tradition. 
One key here, with further support for Distefano’s mimetic reading, is 
Tolkien’s major work of literary theory, “On Fairy -stories.” The functions of 
recovery, escape, and consolation that Tolkien identifies in fairy -stories, 
achieved above all by “eucatast rophe,” map well onto what Distefano calls 
“subversive mythology.” Likewise, the distinction Tolkien makes in this essay 
between magic motivated by possessive desire to dominate and enchantment 
that seeks shared enjoyment (evoked by Galadriel’s comments to Frodo and Sam 
Reviews 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  283 
before they look into her mirror) corresponds to, and is illuminated by, the 
distinction between negative and positive mimesis. Tolkien calls such 
enchantment an “elvish craft,” which implies that Elves might be seen alongside 
Hobbits as equally, though differently, positive models of desire. 
Distefano has made good music from the resonances between Tolkien 
and Girard. There are more songs to be sung, and I’m sure Distefano would 
welcome the singers around his fire with a cup of their favorite equivalent of 
Old Winyards. 
 
BIBLIOGRAPHY

Mythlore, vol. 26, no. 1/2, #99/100, 2007, pp. 137–48. 
Hren, Joshua. “Acquisitive Imitation and the Gift-Economy: Escaping Reciprocity in J.R.R. 
Tolkien’s The Hobbit.” Contagion: Journal of Violence, Mimesis, and Culture , vol. 24, 
2017, pp. 217–32. 
 
CURTIS GRUENLER is a professor of English at Hope College and editor of the Bulletin 
of the Colloquium on Violence & Religion, the international academic group for those 
interested in mimetic theory. He is the author of Piers Plowman and the Poetics of 
Enigma: Riddles, Rhetoric, and Theology (University of Notre Dame Press, 2017) and, 
most recently, “‘On Fairy-stories as a Theory of Literature: Tolkien Meets Ricœur and 
Girard on the Field of Myth” (Religion & Literature 55.2). 
 
 
 
POTTERVERSITY : ESSAYS EXPLORING THE WORLD OF HARRY 
POTTER . Edited by  Kathryn N. McDaniel and Emily Strand . Jefferson, NC: 
McFarland, 2024. 276 p. ISBN 978-1-4766-9053-7. $29.95.   
 
THE IVORY TOWER , HARRY POTTER , AND BEYOND : MORE ESSAYS ON 
THE WORKS OF J.K.  ROWLING . Edited by  Lana Whited. Columbia, MO: 
University of Missouri Press, 2023. 424 p. ISBN 9780826223005. $66.00.  
 
O MY MIND , THE MOST COMPELLING SCHOLARSHIP  makes precise, well -
researched arguments and raises serious questions about both the specific 
object of inquiry and the larger assumptions and goals of the field. With these 
criteria in mind, this review considers two excellent recent anthologies: Lana 
Whited’s The Ivory Tower, Harry Potter, and Beyond: More Essays on the Works of 
J.K. Rowling and Potterversity: Essays Exploring the World of Harry Potter, edited 
T 
Reviews 
284  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
by Kathryn McDaniel and Emily Strand. The various chapters of these books 
run the gamut of theoretical and applied approaches to J.K. Rowling’s texts, and 
are incisive, inclusive, intellectually stimulating, and innovative. Given their 
shared focus, I will be reviewing both together here.  
 That said, what precisely is their focus? Before even opening the texts, 
their titles raise an interesting question as to how this field self -identifies. 
Potterversity is much more focused on happenings internal to the world of Harry, 
Hermione, Ron, et al, whereas The Ivory Tower emphasizes the Beyond of its title 
as it explicitly considers Rowling’s other writings. To be clear, Rowling’s output 
extends far beyond her Harry Potter fame, now including “three companion 
books; a novel of social realism for ad ults, The Casual Vacancy; seven Cormoran 
Strike detective novels; the story basis for a London and Broadway play, The 
Cursed Child; a book-length fairy tale, The Ickabog; a holiday fable, The Christmas 
Pig, and three screenplays in the Fantastic Beasts film series,” as well as 
numerous other essays (Whited 3). With such a broad range of texts and styles 
by the same author, the overlap–and distinctions–between Potterversity and The 
Ivory Tower serve as a sort of microcosm of a very present question in this 
domain of scholarship: how precisely to divide up the field. As these works of 
Potter studies, Rowling studies, or something else? 
We often speak of Tolkien studies or mythopoeic studies, young adult 
literature or the fantasy genre; even as much as these differentiations are 
artificial, they still serve a purpose to scholars and the wider community. Titles, 
and the associations they i mplicitly raise, serve to divide the huge complexity 
and breadth of knowledge into digestible portions that can rapidly convey 
information. Harry Potter, though a massive cultural phenomenon, is a relatively 
recent entrant onto the scene of culturally sign ificant texts, and the field is in 
some ways still working to define itself, in terms of its aims and scope and also 
in relation to the larger literary canon (Ide 127). The titles of these two texts offer 
two different views on how to partition this realm of scholarship. McDaniel and 
Strand write in their introduction that their work is in the realm of Potter studies; 
Whited, while nodding towards the prominence of the core Harry Potter novels, 
emphasizes that there is much to be learned from Rowling’s othe r works 
(McDaniel and Strand 1, Whited 13).  
 The Ivory Tower, Harry Potter, and Beyond functions as a follow -up to 
Whited’s previous volume, The Ivory Tower and Harry Potter, published in 2002. 
Twenty-odd years later, much has changed for the scholars and fans as 
Rowling’s oeuvre has grown; these and the author’s other projects are now 
requisitely in dialogue with many of the socio-intellectual movements that have 
pushed the bo undaries of the humanities more broadly —intersectionality, 
critical race theory, post -colonial theory, etc. Potterversity, born out of the 
popular Potterversity podcast, similarly looks forward: born out of a medium 
Reviews 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  285 
that has seen a massive amount of growth in recent years, Potterversity brings 
scholarship to interested fans who may not be academics themselves (Rime et al 
1261; McDaniel and Strand, “Potterversity” ). As McDaniel and Strand write, 
“[We are] bringing to the conversation a wide range of voices and making sure 
that our ideas are accessible—to magical folk and Muggles alike” (McDaniel and 
Strand 1). The podcast features discussions by researchers, and this dialogic 
approach is continued in the anthology: each  thematic section includes 
transcribed discussions between the essay contributors and the editors that 
provide levity, accessibility, and further perspective to the complex issues faced 
in the text. And there certainly are complex issues at hand: Rowling’s  stated 
political positions, breaking into the mainstream of her fandom in 2020, have 
caused seismic ruptures in how scholars, fans, and some laypeople view and 
interact with the texts. Both anthologies do admirable jobs of including 
discussion of these notably complex and fraught issues. As McDaniel and Strand 
write: 
 
A recent challenge in the world of Potter Studies is how (and whether) to 
respond to Rowling’s controversial statements about trans people, which 
began in June 2020 and have continued. Like the Potter fandom in general, 
the scholars participating in our podcast and this book express a range of 
responses to the controversy . […] You will, however, find in the essays 
below that some scholars have adjusted their analytical perspectives to 
consider Rowling’s world in the light of her statements. (McDaniel and 
Strand 2) 
 
This intentionality is both brave and needed; in few other major realms of 
literary inquiry is the author of the text actively commenting on their creation, 
its meaning, or its relationship to society and their fan base. Tolkien is no longer 
alive to explore his legendarium, but Rowling has become an active presence in 
the discourses she has generated. 
 In The Gay Science, Nietzsche’s Madman famously declares that “God 
is dead. God remains dead. And we have killed him.” But this is immediately 
followed with a question of meaning: “How shall we comfort ourselves, the 
murderers of all murderers?” (181). Reading over these an thologies, I kept 
returning to this the Madman and his questions, because they cut to the core of 
one of the key debates in literary studies —that of the importance of the author 
in interpretation. All of the various essays included in th e two texts are forced 
to navigate a question that has always been central to literary studies, even as it 
has been answered many different ways over time: how do the author, the text, 
the reader, and larger society interact? In recent Potter studies, this  issue has 
become far more acute than abstract academic debates as certain portions of the 
fandom have rebelled against J.K. Rowling and her political positions, 
Reviews 
286  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
particularly on trans rights. In the introduction to The Ivory Tower, Harry Potter, 
and Beyond , Whited recognizes that this has become a “death of the author” 
moment in Potter/Rowling studies, noting that Potter fans “have had to 
reconsider or end their own relationships with the texts” (Barthes 142; Whited 
10). Some authors within the anthologies still talk of Rowling’s intentions within 
their exegeses; however, others like Brent Slatterly, in his chapter of Potterversity, 
declare Rowling “anathema,” recasting her as “She-Who-Must-Not-Be-Named” 
(McDaniel and Strand 239–240). Rowling is dead, and we have killed her—or, at 
least, some of us. This tension —between engagement and erasure of the 
author—is not clearly resolved within the chapters of these books.  
I suspect that this juxtaposition speaks to a likely trend in upcoming 
scholarship: a distinction between Potter studies narrowly and Rowling studies 
more broadly. In this way, Potter fans who refuse to support Rowling’s further 
business ventures (for her works, certainly, have material value) may continue 
to engage with the world of Harry Potter that they have come to love, and those 
who are disinclined to dispose of her other projects can retain their ability to 
engage with them (Whited 372). That said, The Ivory Tower and Potterversity 
misses its opportunity to explicitly suggest this kind of reordering of the field. 
Both editorial teams refer to themselves as operating within Potter studies, and 
little work is done to establish Rowling studies as a related but distinct field. 
Perhaps this will change over time if Rowling’s other texts become more popular 
and scholars focus more on them, but for now each reader and critic must find 
their own way through this political and intellectual morass.  
 Implicit in this critique is an assumption that anthologies like these 
function to both indicate the state of the field and to delineate the expectations 
for other scholars working in their domain; they are both descriptive and 
normative. There seems to b e a tacit understanding across the editors and 
various authors of these manuscripts that Rowling’s political statements are 
problematic, and that arguments considering marginalization, tokenism, race 
relations and identity more broadly are academically and  socially acceptable 
and necessary. Both books deftly note that the political leanings of many 
academics and Harry Potter fans have come into conflict with Rowling and other 
more conservative perspectives, though this phenomenon is replicated in the 
broader children’s literature space and across many academic divisions 
(Duggan 148). Politics may be omnipresent in humanistic inquiry, but it can also 
be corrosive if ideological adherence becomes a requirement for academic 
acceptance. Political scientists often speak of big-tent parties; is the Harry Potter 
studies world a big-tent interpretive community? Does it want to be?  
I suspect that the editors and contributors of these volumes would 
respond to this question emphatically in the affirmative —that “the characters, 
stories, and situations of the magical world promote thinking that helps us 
Reviews 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  287 
navigate our more mundane but no less dangerous world” (McDaniel and 
Strand 1). For example, Alyssa Lowery’s chapter in The Ivory Tower  explicitly 
argues against overly racialized readings of Hermione’s S.P.E.W. and the house-
elves, and Emily Strand’s piece in Potterversity explicitly utilizes Christian 
theology in her exploration of Christmas scenes in Harry Potter . The books 
suggest that regardless of one’s political affiliation, one can find an interpretive 
community within the acafandom.  
Stepping away from this heavy discussion of politics, I wish to call out 
one particularly exciting inclusion in Potterversity: its use of “Conversation” 
chapters—transcribed dialogues between the editors and chapter authors of 
each thematic section. Not only did this give these various stakeholders 
additional opportunities to expound upon their views, they were able to engage 
in a level of critical discourse that is uncommon in these kinds of texts. For 
example, the “Playing Potter” conversation between Lau rie Beckoff, Tyson 
Pugh, and Kathryn McDaniel and Emily Strand ties together the games of 
medieval fiction, pedagogical approaches to teaching literature, and the games 
internal to the Harry Potter world in an exchange that is interdisciplinary and 
evocative (McDaniel and Strand 210 –212). Plus, the conversations are more 
informal, allowing for the text’s orientation as a work of acafandom to stand out 
as a piece of scholarship that is centered at the junction between the work and 
its cultural situation (McDaniel and Strand 1).  
And this injection of levity and joy is important in such a fraught 
cultural discourse. To many, especially the more casual readers, Rowling’s texts 
are simply an escape from the mundanity of life; they are a way to become more 
than a Muggle. A final quest ion I was left with after reading these anthologies 
was whether all of this interpretive activity detracts from the magic of Harry 
Potter. To paraphrase Tolkien, fantasy works because of its access to the 
numinous and the magical world beyond our own munda ne reality (Tolkien 
150). To my mind, these anthologies succeed precisely because they allow for 
interpretation while retaining the magic and joy of the texts. Each chapter and 
conversation is a celebration of the excitement and amazement of the secondary 
world hidden just beyond Platform 9 ¾ (in the case of Harry Potter). I had the 
opportunity to meet the editors—and many of the authors—of these volumes at 
the 2024 Harry Potter Academic Conference, and was delighted to find that all 
of these accomplished scholars kept the spirit of the magic of the stories central 
to their experience of scholarship and acafandom. I feel that these volumes 
succeed precisely because they allow for this synergy between creativity and 
analysis.  
 While there are many open questions of interpretation in the world of 
Potter/Rowling studies, these volumes go a long way toward answering a core 
question that vexes literature studies more broadly: what is the point of 
Reviews 
288  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
analyzing these texts? The answer, according to The Ivory Tower and 
Potterversity, is to find new ways to feel the magic of the wizarding world each 
time one reads the books, watches the movies, or thinks about the stories. We 
can debate the politics of Harry Potter or J.K. Rowling, but we cannot and must 
not forget the joy of going to a world of wonder and of looking inside of 
ourselves to discover our own magic. If I abide by the criteria I set out at the 
beginning of this review —precision in scholarship and attention to larger 
assumptions and goals —then these two books certainly succeed, especially 
when taken together.

LIAM BUTCHART, MD, MA, is currently a resident physician in psychiatry at the Icahn 
School of Medicine at Mount Sinai –Morningside/West program (New York, NY). He 
graduated from the Stony Brook University Renaissance School of Medicine and received 
his MA in Medical Human ities, Compassionate Care, and Bioethics from Stony Brook 
University, where he currently teaches on narrative medicine and virtue ethics. His 
research interests include psychoanalysis and literature, medical humanities, bioethics, 
and the intersection of the mental health illness experience with the arts.
