HARRY POTTER AND RESISTANCE . Beth Sutton -Ramspeck. Routledge, 
2023. 224 pp. $40.00 pbk. 978-1032319872;  also available in hbk. and eBook. 
 
ARRY POTTER AND RESISTANCE IS A CHALLENGING BOOK TO READ, or at least this 
reader found it to be so. This is a partly a product of the extremely small font 
size that the book is printed in, but it is also a reflection of how densely 
populated with citations to other works the book is. Sutton -Ramspeck’s 
scholarly approach to analyzing a book series that often is dismissed as “just 
children’s books” is mostly welcome, though I sometimes found myself thinking 
“tell me what you think, not what everyone else thinks!” Still, all in all, Sutton -
Ramspeck offers a compelling analysis of the Harry Potter series, emphasizing 
the centrality of rule-breaking and resistance in the narrative.  
Sutton-Ramspeck argues that Harry’s defiance transcends mere 
adolescent rebellion, serving as a fundamental element in combating systemic 
evils within the wizarding world. She explores how Harry and his allies 
challenge the Ministry of Magic’s corruption a nd the entrenched racist 
ideologies that gave rise to Voldemort. The book draws parallels between the 
actions of Dumbledore’s Army and the Order of the Phoenix with historical 
resistance movements, such as those during World War II and the U.S. Civil 
Rights era. And while the book was published in 2023, some might view the 
consideration of resistance to the second return to power of Voldemort to be 
particularly timely in 2025. 
Sutton-Ramspeck’s interdisciplinary approach mostly enriches the 
analysis, as she incorporates insights from political science, psychology, 
philosophy, history, race studies, and women’s studies. This allows for a 
nuanced examination of themes like systemi c oppression, the role of creativity 
in resistance, and the ethical complexities of rule-breaking. The book delves into 
the portrayal of house -elves, particularly Dobby, through a feminist lens, 
challenging interpretations that view the series as condoning  slavery. 
Additionally, it investigates the language associated with dirt and purity, 
uncovering deep-seated prejudices in both magical and Muggle societies.  
While the extensive use of critical theory may be dense for casual 
readers, it provides a thorough exploration for those interested in a deeper 
understanding of the series’ social and political dimensions. By embracing the 
unresolved issues within the narr ative, she underscores the series’ capacity to 
inspire readers to contemplate and challenge societal injustices.  
However, any current discussion of the Harry Potter franchise must 
grapple with the controversy surrounding J.K. Rowling’s public comments on 
transgender rights. Sutton -Ramspeck does not shy away from this issue, 
providing a fairly thoughtful consideration  of how Rowling’s transphobic 
remarks have impacted the series’ legacy. She acknowledges the harm caused 
H 
Reviews 
290  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
by Rowling’s rhetoric, particularly its contradiction with the series’ themes of 
inclusivity, empathy, and resistance to bigotry. Sutton -Ramspeck seeks to 
detach these tensions from the broader context of her analysis, emphasizing that 
the text and its creator should be critically separated. Nonetheless, she notes that 
while the series contains messages of acceptance and social justice, readers must 
also reckon with the ways in which Rowling’s views complicate its reception 
and the cultural space it occupies. 
Sutton-Ramspeck encourages readers to approach the series critically, 
embracing its potential to inspire resistance while remaining mindful of the real-
world implications of Rowling’s statements. In doing so, Harry Potter and Resistance 
contributes to an ongoing conversation about how fans and scholars can engage 
with works of art whose creators have been accused of perpetuating harm.1 
The book opens by framing rule -breaking not as mere adolescent 
rebellion but as a deliberate act of resistance. Sutton-Ramspeck explores Harry, 
Hermione, and Ron’s defiance of both school rules and societal norms, arguing 
that these acts undermine corrupt systems, such as the Ministry of Magic’s 
authoritarian policies. The chapter draws parallels to historical resistance 
movements, emphasizing how even small acts of disobedience can catalyze 
significant change. 
The second chapter examines Dumbledore’s Army (DA) as a case 
study in grassroots activism. Sutton-Ramspeck details how the DA mirrors real-
world resistance groups, such as World War II resistance fighters and civil rights 
organizations. She discusses the i mportance of leadership, solidarity, and 
shared purpose in mobilizing against oppressive regimes, highlighting the DA’s 
defiance of Dolores Umbridge and the Ministry’s overreach. 
Next, Sutton -Ramspeck delves into the wizarding world’s deeply 
entrenched notions of blood purity and its parallels to real -world racism. She 
analyzes characters like Hermione, Hagrid, and Lupin, who resist systemic 
prejudices through their actions and adv ocacy. This chapter critiques the 
limitations of the series in fully addressing these issues, encouraging readers to 
engage critically with the text. 
The fourth chapter focuses on female resistance, from Hermione’s 
intellectual defiance to Ginny’s active participation in battles against 
Voldemort’s forces. Sutton-Ramspeck examines the gender dynamics within the 
series, addressing both empowering moments and the occasional reinforcement 
of traditional gender roles. The chapter also critiques Rowling’s inconsistencies, 
particularly regarding intersectionality in her portrayal of resistance. 
 
1 This conversation is all the more timely given the recent revelations of allegations of truly 
despicable conduct by otherwise beloved fantasy author Neil Gaiman. 
Reviews 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  291 
Sutton-Ramspeck then highlights how creativity becomes a tool for 
defiance, analyzing Fred and George Weasley’s pranks, Luna Lovegood’s 
eccentricity, and the satirical resistance of The Quibbler. These moments of levity 
and subversion are framed as vital counterpoints to the oppressive atmosphere 
of Voldemort’s reign, showing how humor and individuality can undermine 
fear and conformity. 
Chapter 6 is the final chapter directly addressing the text of the books. 
This chapter explores the ethical complexities of resistance. Sutton -Ramspeck 
addresses moments when Harry and his allies cross moral lines, such as using 
Unforgivable Curses or enda ngering others. She argues that these ambiguities 
mirror real -world ethical dilemmas faced by resistance movements, adding 
depth to the series’ portrayal of rebellion. 
In the seventh chapter, Sutton -Ramspeck again addresses Rowling’s 
transphobic remarks and their impact on the Harry Potter fandom, which she 
had already touched upon in the preface to the book. As noted above, she 
critiques how these statements conflict with the series’ themes of inclusion and 
justice, but advocates for separating the text from its author, proposing that fans 
and scholars can reinterpret the series in ways that resist the harm caused by 
Rowling’s views. 
The final chapter reflects on the Harry Potter fandom’s activism, from 
charitable initiatives inspired by the series to grassroots movements challenging 
Rowling’s statements. Sutton -Ramspeck celebrates the ways in which readers 
have turned the series’ mess ages of resistance into real -world action, 
demonstrating how literature can inspire meaningful change. 
While Sutton -Ramspeck critiques Rowling’s views and some of the 
series’ limitations, the book often leans heavily into celebrating the text as a 
beacon of resistance. This optimism risks overshadowing the deeper systemic 
issues within the narrative, such a s its lack of diverse representation and the 
implicit reinforcement of certain hierarchies (e.g., the marginalization of house-
elves and other magical creatures). Although the book addresses themes of race, 
gender, and class, the analysis occasionally feels compartmentalized rather than 
fully intersectional. For instance, the exploration of gender resistance could have 
been deepened by examining how these dynamics intersect with race and class 
in the wizarding world, particularly given the series’ Eurocentr ic framing. 
Although the final chapter discusses the Harry Potter fandom’s activism, it 
could have delved further into the complex dynamics within the fan 
community, particularly with regard to the schism between those who continue 
to support Rowling and those who reject her entirely. 
While Sutton-Ramspeck engages somewhat with some critiques of the 
series, she fails to sufficiently engage with other criticisms of the Potter books, 
such as claims that that the series upholds traditional power structures (e.g., the 
Reviews 
292  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
privileged position of pureblood families like the Malfoys and the focus on the 
elite world of Hogwarts). She touches on these assessments but does not fully 
explore how these structural elements might undermine the series’ broader 
themes of resistance. In addition, while the chapter-by-chapter structure is clear 
and well -organized, certain themes —such as rule -breaking and grassroots 
movements—are revisited multiple times, leading to some redundancy. A more 
concise integration of overlapping ideas could have streamlined the analysis. 
All in all, despite its challenges, Sutton -Ramspeck’s Harry Potter and 
Resistance is a valuable addition to the body of scholarship on the Harry Potter 
series. Its strengths lie in its interdisciplinary lens, candid engagement with 
Rowling’s controversies, and thoughtful exploration of resistance as a 
multifaceted theme. However, the b ook’s optimism sometimes overshadows 
deeper critiques of the series’ structural issues. Nonetheless, for fans and 
scholars alike, this book is a thought-provoking resource that invites readers to 
reflect on the complexities of resistance in both fiction and reality. It is most 
impactful when viewed as a starting point for deeper conversations about 
activism, morality, and the role of literature in shaping societal values. 
 
DOUGLAS C. KANE is an attorney specializing in employment discrimination and 
harassment cases and other civil rights matters.  He is also a Middle-earth enthusiast who 
has loved the works of J.R.R. Tolkien for more than forty years.  He co-founded and runs 
the Tolkien Int ernet discussion site thehalloffire.net.  His first book, Arda Reconstructed: 
The Creation of the Published Silmarillion, was published by Lehigh University Press in 2009, 
with a paperback edition released in 2011. He has also had numerous papers and reviews 
published in Tolkien Studies, Mythlore, and The Journal of Tolkien Research .  Doug lives in 
Santa Cruz, California with his partner, Beth Dyer, and their cat, Legolas Lateef. 
 
 
 
MAPPING A SENSE OF HUMOR : NARRATIVE AND SPACE IN TERRY 
PRATCHETT ’S DISCWORLD NOVELS . Daniel Lüthi. Altadena, CA: 
Mythopoeic Press. 2023. 291 p. ISBN 9781887726238. $19.95.  
 
HEN TERRY PRATCHETT WROTE HIS FIRST DISCWORLD NOVELS in the early 
1980s, they were primarily parodies of fantasy novels in general and of 
the Lord of the Rings  trilogy specifically. The further development of the 
Discworld series beyond this into its own secondary world over a span of 32 
years is explored by Daniel L üthi. This work begins with an exploration of 
fantasy works and their place in literature, starting with an attempt to define 
what the term “fantasy” means. He then delves into the similarities fantasy has 
W 
Reviews 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  293 
with postmodern fiction and how both these genres reflect the real world. While 
investigating this topic, Lüthi compares Pratchett’s works with other well -
known fantasy novels, not just Tolkien’s. In comparison with The Hobbit and the 
Lord of the Rings  trilogy, the first Discworld novels are quest fantasies but the 
quest is for our nominal hero to show a tourist the sights of Discworld. The 
“hero” in the first two novels is a failed wizard and primarily a coward. As 
Pratchett continued to write about this world, he used those characters that are 
normally in the background of fantasy novels and made them the focus of the 
novels. So our heroes in other Discworld novels are a witch, a city guard, or a 
con man, all of whom fight against what role the typical fantasy narrative would 
have them do but also fight against becoming the focus of the story. “Pratchett 
imbues his protagonists with an awareness of the power of stories that challenge 
the manner in which their counterparts in other fantasy books accept their roles” 
(81–82). His characters fight the narrative imperative that would have them 
behave in expected ways for others in their roles in other stories.  
All of this leads to a discussion of the mapping of Discworld in the 
seventh chapter. Pratchett did not map out Discworld for his first two novels 
and, according to Lüthi, this was a deliberate decision. He did, eventually, work 
with Stephen Briggs to create a map of Discworld. In some ways, this is 
understandable. Once a map is constructed, the boundaries of the map constrain 
the stories that can be told. However, par ticularly for the novels set in the city -
state of Ankh -Morpork, having a consistent struct ure to refer to became 
increasingly important to prevent confusion to the author and the reader. Still, 
Lüthi explains that readers asked Pratchett if the mapping of Ankh -Morpork 
would stifle the stories. This, needless to say, did not happen. 
The last section of the book covers the further development of the 
Discworld and Ankh-Morpork. Lüthi points out that Pratchett’s works are not 
just parodies of fantasy novels but are satires of our real world as well. In the 
later novels, Ankh -Morpork begins to industrialize which, of course, brings 
changes to the entirety of Discworld. While magic s till has a central role in the 
novels, the Discworld equivalents of steam engines, movies, and 
telecommunications enter into the world. Since most fantasy novels are content 
to stay in a quasi-medieval state, this is a marked difference between Pratchett’s 
works and the genre he began parodying. This section also discusses the 
multimedia resources developed from the novels. There are video games, 
movies, and the possibility exists of further movies and television series. 
This book is a revision of the author’s dissertation, which at times 
comes through, especially with the copious footnotes. It is, however, an 
informative treatise on the works of Terry Pratchett. This reviewer would have 
liked more about the mapping of Dis cworld and found the title of the book 
Reviews 
294  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
somewhat misleading. Still for fans of the Discworld novels, this will be an 
interesting read. 
 
SUSAN M. MOORE is a catalog librarian and collection strategist at the University of 
Northern Iowa. Prior to working at the University of Northern Iowa, she was a catalog 
librarian at the University of Arizona. She received her MLS from the University of Iowa 
in 1984 an d holds a M.A. in Geography from the University of Arizona and a B.A. in 
Political Science from the University of Northern Iowa.  
 
 
 
ART AND ENCHANTMENT : HOW WONDER WORKS . Patrick Curry. 
London and New York: Routledge, 2023. 244 p. ISBN 978-1-032-40467-7. $48.99.  
 
OLKIEN WROTE AN ESSAY TO ACCOMPANY  Smith of Wootton Major and at the 
very end he stated that “‘Faery’ is as necessary for the health and complete 
functioning of the Human as is sunlight for physical life […]” ( Smith 144–5). 
Faerie was to Tolkien both an embodiment and a source of enchantment ( OFS 
32) and from this remarkable statement it is clear that he regarded enchantment 
as essential for human flourishing. We have Tolkien’s thoughts on enchantment 
in “On Fairy-stories,” in the essay that accompanies Smith of Wootton Major, and 
in a few of his letters (#131 in particular), but there is much more that could be 
said about both enchantment and disenchantment, which is its lack or opposite.  
Patrick Curry has written extensively on Tolkien, art and enchantment, 
and Art and Enchantment is his most academic in nature on these subjects, and 
the most fully developed and detailed. He lays out an ambitious plan for the 
book at the very beginning: 
 
This book celebrates and defends the experience of wonder in art, and of 
art as enchanting. It explores the characteristics, conditions, and 
dynamics of artistic creativity and appreciation. But it also concerns the 
absence of enchantment, and the reasons for that. I don’t altogether rule 
out the possibility of any art enchanting, but I do suggest that some kinds 
are more like to than others, and we shall see why. (2) 
 
The book itself begins with an Introduction, and a lengthy chapter called “What 
is Enchantment?” Chapters follow on specific kinds of art with two chapters 
each on Painting, Music and Fiction, a short chapter on Poetry, a Conclusion, 
and an ample Index. The book is heavily notated and referenced, and if nothing 
else the reader will find many suggestions for further reading on enchantment, 
T 
Reviews 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  295 
art and the intersection of the two. In addition, a worthy chapter on jazz that 
was cut for space has been posted on the author’s website at patrickcurry.co.uk.  
Enchantment is difficult to define simply, as it is a rich, complex 
phenomenon. It includes wonder, but it is a particular kind of encounter with 
wonder.  With enchantment Curry is speaking of a specific phenomenon, or 
class of phenomena: 
 
The heart of enchantment is an experience of wonder. It varies in intensity 
from charm, through delight, to full -blown joy. The last distinguishes 
radical or deep enchantment, rare but life changing. (8) 
 
We most often encounter enchantment in the context of falling in love or an 
encounter with nature, but there are other ways it can be found, such as religion, 
food and drink, learning, humor, art, even sports (29). 
Curry emphasizes that enchantment is not something that can be 
planned on, or willed or forced to occur. It cannot be bidden, created, 
commanded or managed. It cannot be found where either domination and 
control over others are an objective. Curry explains that:  
 
Enchantment is fundamentally relational. It is wonder at, or enchantment 
by, another. It’s not power over them, nor being the object of power. […] 
It also follows that because no one is in charge, enchantment is wild, 
unbiddable. That inconvenient truth means that although it can be 
invited, it cannot be created, commanded, or managed. (8) 
 
Enchantment cannot even be counted upon to reoccur under identical 
conditions, as it is deeply connected to a unique time, place and situation. Curry 
presents an example: 
 
It was that movement of the fourth Brandenburg Concerto, just as the 
English Consort, as it then was, played it, in that evening’s slanting light, 
there in Holland Park, interspersed with that blackbird’s song, which 
transported us. (19) 
 
Curry distinguishes enchantment from a mystical or dionysiac ecstasy 
or union, as enchantment is an encounter over a “gap of difference” (9). When 
enchanted we experience the “other” in a heightened way. A gap or boundary 
will still be present, but ceases to matter temporarily. We are still ourselves, but 
the “other” becomes a source of pure joy, sometimes overwhelming, for a 
usually too brief time. The “other” here can be almost anything: an animal, plant 
or place, or even an experience of the senses. Most  of us have some experience 
Reviews 
296  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
of enchantment, perhaps an artwork that moved us, the first time we tasted a 
favorite food or the first time we met a loved one.  
Curry develops his understanding of enchantment from at least two 
starting points. One of them is Weber’s well-known argument that the world is 
becoming increasingly disenchanted as it (supposedly) moves towards a view 
that “we can in principle control everything by means of calculation ” (Weber 12 –
13). Religion, myth, magic are no longer necessary as “we need no longer have 
recourse to magic in order to control the spirits or pray to them. Instead, 
technology and calculation achieve our ends” (13). Curry ap pears to accept 
Weber’s understanding of some of the causes of disenchantment: 
“rationalisation and intellectuali sation” (Curry 198) and the drive towards 
“rational cognition and mastery of nature” (15). These are hostile to 
enchantment, as they are focused on control and possession, whereas 
enchantment is almost always a surprise, and requires an openness, what the 
travel writer Freya Stark calls “fearless receptivity” ( qtd. in Curry 30). Curry 
points out that possessiveness is antithetical to enchantment,  saying that “false 
enchantment” is “a desire either to possess the other or be possessed by them” 
(9, quoting W.H. Auden). Enchantment presumes a desire for the other to 
flourish (9). As Tolkien says about Faerie (a source of enchantment), it 
“represents love: that is, a love and respect for all things, ‘inanimate’ and 
‘animate’, an unpossessive love of them as ‘other’“ (Smith 144). 
Whereas Weber is focused on identifying disenchantment, the loss of 
enchantment, Tolkien is positively interested in identifying and re -awakening 
it. In Tolkien’s usage of the term enchantment is an “elvish craft” or “living, 
realised sub -creative art” tha t “does not seek delusion, nor bewitchment and 
domination; it seeks shared enrichment, partners in making and delight, not 
slaves” (OFS 64). Enchantment is concerned with evoking wonder and creating 
beauty. In a letter to Milton Waldman, Tolkien describes the elvish craft of 
enchantment as a particular kind of ‘magic’:  
 
Their [elves] ‘magic’ is Art, delivered from many of its human limitations: 
more effortless, more quick, more complete (product, and vision in 
unflawed correspondence). And its object is Art not Power, sub-creation 
not domination and tyrannous re-forming of Creation. (Letters 205, #131)   
 
In Tolkien’s understanding (which is largely Curry’s as well) enchantment seeks 
partners, not possessors or possessions. It seeks to create and delight, rather than 
dominate. 
While enchantment cannot be made or forced to occur, conditions can 
be created which make it more likely to occur, and much of the focus of Art and 
Enchantment is on art, artists and artistic movements and Curry’s thoughts on 
Reviews 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  297 
whether they might be more, or less, conducive to enchantment. Curry’s point-
of-view is definitely an opinion. Readers will not find much in the way of 
scientific studies or data in the book. But it is a point -of-view that has been 
carefully and extensively developed, and at the end it may be seen to have a 
great internal consistency, and perhaps usefulness for understanding an 
important phenomenon. This consistency and analysis begins from a particular 
understanding of enchantment and prevents the book fro m merely being an 
enumeration of what Curry does and doesn’t like in art.  
Curry also claims the book is not meant to be a discussion of what is 
and isn’t art (2). Not all artists are trying to enchant and Curry gives examples 
of works such as Guernica by Pablo Picasso and The Handmaid’s Tale by Margaret 
Atwood that are moving and powerful, but few would call enchanting (2). Curry 
is also willing to acknowledge that there are subjects like opera and hip-hop that 
are a “closed book” (3) to him, where he feels unqualified to speak for various 
reasons.  
A few examples of art that Curry believes is likely to elicit enchantment 
are in order. As an example of a painter Curry proposes Claude Monet (49). This 
is not a particularly controversial position as Monet (and many of the other 
Impressionists) have been  and continue to be well -regarded for the beauty of 
their works. Curry suggests that popularity may actually be a hindrance to 
enchantment because of over -familiarity, but encourages getting to know 
Monet’s works better anyways (49–50). In particular he recommends the lesser-
known Nymphéas (Water Lilies) cycle of paintings from later in Monet’s life, 
which he compares to Beethoven’s final string quartets as high points in Western 
art (50). 
In contrast, Damien Hirst (perhaps better known in the UK than North 
America) is an example of an artist that Curry thinks is disenchanting, part of 
an art world that is actively hostile to enchantment (103–104).  Hirst is infamous 
for artworks consisting of animal carcasses in formaldehyde. Another work 
features 9000 dead butterflies. It is easy to see how far this is from enchanting; 
its purpose instead is to shock or horrify. Curry’s scorn for Hirst is obv ious, 
describing him as “a foul -mouthed brutalist  whose grotesque self -pity has 
survived extravagant wealth (c. $384 million) —the reward of an art world run 
by business” (104). 
Curry sees Damien Hirst as an example of the modernism that is a 
particular enemy to enchantment in his view (104). Curry draws a distinction 
between the modern sensibility that we are all affected by, and the modernism 
that developed later as an ideology (31). He notes that in the 19th century some 
people in the West and Japan began to see themselves as modern, which led to, 
among other things, a relaxed attitude towards tradition. This sensibility led 
them to ignore tradition at times and experiment and i nnovate. However, 
Reviews 
298  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
towards the end of the 19th century modernism as an ideology got its start. It 
was a sometimes virulent reaction to tradition, which was associated by 
modernists with superstition, oppression and ignorance, and which therefore 
needed to be destroyed (31). Modernism was intended to be progressive, but it 
“needs icons to smash and enemies, both past and present, to smite” (31) and 
has seemed to delight in only systematic destruction and deconstruction. With 
the overturn of tradition, scientific naturalism and  materialism (31) take its 
place, and Weber has pointed out that these are about control and possession, 
and therefore hostile to enchantment. Curry argues that the movement is still 
ongoing, and continues to be programmatically opposed to enchantment: 
 
Now there’s no doubt that modernism was originally experienced by 
some as tremendously liberating and exciting, nor that it has resulted in 
some good and occasionally great art, along with a lot of rubbish. But 
wonder, delight, and joy in art are things that modernists largely reject. (32)  
 
Concerning music, it has been said (perhaps by Debussy) that “space 
is the music between the notes,” and Curry notes that enchantment takes place 
across gaps and spaces (9). He writes that “Enchantment lives even in the cracks, 
in the sense of imperfections, within the notes—one reason why I think acoustic 
instruments, improvised music, and live performances have the greatest 
potential for enchantment” (123). Among composers he recommends Debussy 
and Ravel as particularly conducive to enchantment, as well as some lesser -
known 19th and 20th century composers such as Satie, Fauré, Messiaen, and 
Takemitsu. 
Curry is not afraid to include “children’s literature” in his discussion 
of enchantment in literature. He mentions The Wind in the Willows  (Kenneth 
Grahame), which many would agree with. The chapter “Piper at the Gates of 
Dawn” contains some of the most enchanting moments in literature. It may 
surprise everyone except those who have read them, but he commends the 
“Mary Poppins” novels by P.L. Travers for their portrayal of the titular character 
as “at once a force of nature and a spiritual being of indeterm inate sort […] she 
is completely unbiddable, a barely-self-controlled cyclone of wild enchantment” 
(183). Curry is less fond of His Dark Materials for its didacticism and Winnie-the-
Pooh for its preciousness, though he recognizes that some will see wonder in 
these (180, 179). 
Readers of this journal who are interested in enchantment or Tolkien’s 
understanding of enchantment may find much to think about in Art and 
Enchantment. Curry’s knowledge of art is vast and the discussion is fast and 
wide-ranging. A familiarity, or willingness to get familiar, with the artists and 
movements he discusses will be helpful towards gaining from the book. Curry 
Reviews 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  299 
keeps primarily to the 19th through 21st centuries, but there is still a lot to take 
in.  
With all the discussion on art and artists in the book it is easy to lose 
sight of perhaps the most important argument that Curry makes, that 
enchantment is worth pursuing. He says that “it is not a luxury but something 
which can make life worth living, an d even be life -changing” (31). Tolkien 
would likely agree, saying that enchantment is “essential to […] full and proper 
human development ” ( Smith 131). Glamour, what Curry calls “false 
enchantment, the desire for possession” (30), and disenchantment are prevalent. 
It takes an effort to maintain an openness towards enchantment, but Curry and 
Tolkien would likely say the results are worth it.

N. TREVOR BRIERLY has a background in the humanities with an MLIS from the 
University of Texas at Austin, a MA from Signum University and a BA in English 
from George Mason University. His research interests include worldbuilding in 
speculative fiction, Tolkien, Frank Herbert, Le Guin, Shakespeare, and the 
Renaissance. He has published an essay “Worldbuilding Design Patterns in the 
Works of J.R.R. Tolkien” in “Sub-creating Arda” (Walking Tree Publishers, 2019) and 
is co -editor of “Discovering Dune” from McFarland Books (2022). He lives in 
Northern Virginia and is sometimes enchanted by jazz, tea and cats.

BRIEFLY NOTED  
 
HARRY POTTER AND THE MYTH OF MILLENNIALS: IDENTITY, RECEPTION, 
AND POLITICS. Priscilla Hobbs. Lanham, MD: Lexington Books, 2022. 204 pp. 
Hardcover: 978-1-7936-2027-9. $95.00; ebook: 978-1-7936-2028-6, $45.00. 
 
RISCILLA HOBBS’S HARRY POTTER AND THE MYTH OF MILLENNIALS adds to an 
important and growing literature about the intersection between popular 
culture and politics. As she notes, the Harry Potter series was formative for a 
generation of young readers and almost certainly influenced their views of 
social justice, government power, and war. Hobbs combines an academic 
analysis of the series’ political themes with her personal reflections about the 
franchise and American political trends during the early 2000s. The book is 
probably best suited to readers seeking a short overview of why J.K. Rowling’s 
stories resonated with the millennial generation, and what lessons they might 
have drawn from it.  
The first half of the book examines how the Harry Potter series became 
a cultural phenomenon. Hobbs initially frames Harry Potter’s success as another 
example of the power of the hero’s journey archetype. Rowling’s story, however, 
departs from the hero’s journey by emphasizing how Harry, Ron, Hermione, 
and the other protagonists created a “found family” that often challenged the 
social norms of their community. Hobbs argues that the series in effect gave 
young readers permission “to embrace their unique sel ves […] even if it 
deviated from illusory social norms” (64), helping to explain millennials’ 
passion for expressing and defending one’s identity.  
The second half explores lessons that fans have drawn from the Harry 
Potter series on a variety of issues, including worker’s rights, media, and 
education, and how the fandom mobilized to support activism. Drawing heavily 
on Gierzynski and Eddy’s survey of Harry Potter readers, Hobbs argues that 
millennials who read Harry Potter were more likely to accept religious and 
gender diversity, oppose authoritarian leaders, and condemn torture. The last 
chapter touches on fan criticism of Rowling’s comments about t rans rights. 
However, Hobbs’s limited coverage of the backlash, which started years before 
the book’s publication, prematurely dates  Harry Potter and the Myth of 
Millennials, and indeed raises underexplored questions of how much Harry 
Potter will shape millennials’ political discourse in the future.

Gierzynski, Anthony and Kathryn Eddy. Harry Potter and the Millennials. Baltimore, MD: 
Johns Hopkins University Press, 2013.

DOMINIC J. NARDI received his Ph.D. in Political Science from the University of 
Michigan and currently teaches at George Washington University. He has edited 
books about Star Wars TV shows (2020) and Dune (2022), and is working on a book 
about Studio Ghibli films as adaptations (2025). He has also written about politics 
and identity in Blade Runner, The Lord of the Rings, and Star Trek. 
 
 
 
MAGIC , MAGICIANS AND DETECTIVE FICTION : ESSAYS ON 
INTERSECTING MODES OF MYSTERY . Edited by Rebecca Josephy. 
Jefferson; McFarland & Co., Inc, 2025. 240 pp. ISBN: 978 -1-4766-8820-6. $65.00 
(paperback). 
 
HIS ESSAY COLLECTION OFFERS NINE CHAPTERS  exploring how magic 
(primarily the performance variety) intersects with detective fiction. Three 
chapters apply the typology of magic to detective fiction, three consider 
overlapping spaces between detective fiction and magic (magicians who solve 
crimes, etc.), and three showcase international (Japanese, Russian, Mexican) 
authors using magic in detective stories. 
The second section offers the most useful material for scholars 
interested in Inklings -adjacent Dorothy L. Sayers and other Golden Age 
detective writers. Beatrice Ashton -Lelliott discusses Victorian -era magicians 
who were seen simultaneously as rogues and detectives (93–111), Christopher 
Pittard explores the magician antagonist Colonel Clay in Grant Allen’s novel The 
African Millionaire  (112–130), and Rebecca Josephy considers how Maurice 
Leblanc applies magical descriptors to Arsène Lupin (131–154). Josephy’s essay 
offers an interesting counterpoint to claims that the detective story is inherently 
religious when she argues that magic shows and detective stories follow similar 
structures. “The genre, like the magic show, becomes a pseudo -religious 
narrative iterative experience: a new, modern, and scientifically advanced opiate 
for the post -enlightened masses” (149). Whether religious authors l ike Sayers, 
Ronald Knox, and G.K. Chesterton turned the would-be opiate into a channel to 
re-enchant the masses is of course up for discussion. 
Other fruitful insights for Golden Age fans appear in Zi -Ling Yan’s 
chapter in the typology section (13 –39). Yan applies Robert Neale’s five -tier 
typology of magic to detective fiction: the tiers range “from primary magic (the 
self-unaware predisposition to magical thinking) to reflexive magic (a fully self-
aware experience or meta-magic)” (21). If applied t o fiction, Neale’s tiers show 
how detective stories offer a spectrum of magical content (proven or disproven 
T 
Reviews 
302  Mythlore 146, Spring/Summer 2025 
 
supernatural causes?) and a spectrum of attitudes toward magic (is the final plot 
reveal not unlike a magic show?). For example, Yan places Sayers’s Lord Peter 
Wimsey despairing of justice prevailing in Murder Must Advertise and Unnatural 
Death in the final tier, “works that interrogate the magical effect in a serious 
mode,” questioning whether the detective can perform the desired magic trick 
to conquer evil (31). 
While Yan does not mention Charles Williams when discussing the 
second tier, which features ritual magic (22–25), his discussion of characters like 
Sax Rohmer’s Moris Klaw shows why Williams is under -discussed in crime 
fiction studies. War in Heaven may be a detective story (see Higgins, “A Christian 
Detective Story?”), and Williams admitted that Rohmer inspired him to write 
his first novel (Lobdell 104), but his approach fits outside Rohmer’s occult 
detective formula. The occult detective tale explores “cause-and-effect relations” 
involving paranormal forces that are “strictly retributive” if crossed. 
Neutralizing these forces requires “identifying sources of contamination and 
conducting purification rites”; since the contamination is usually something 
exotically foreign “best purged […] from the national body,” occult detective 
stories ultimately if “surreptitiously defend political, racial, class and gendered 
interests” (24). Williams rarely connects paranormal activity to “bad karma” and 
would rather comp licate than affirm national visions of foreignness (see the 
ambiguous Nigel Considine in Shadows of Ecstasy ) or gender (see Nyman on 
Williams’s complex female figures). Williams writes detective stories about 
magic but evades easy cliches, which also means he evades easily labeled genre 
categories. 
These essays offer useful ideas for scholars thinking about Sayers, 
Williams, and their associates as detective writers interested in magic, but little 
direct discussion. The focus is very much on performance magic over ritual 
magic (no references to Willi ams, almost no references to Aleister Crowley’s 
crime stories). The focus is also very much on American authors over British 
ones (Chesterton only gets mentioned in one of Yan’s footnotes [37], and in 
passing when Tobin discusses Chesterton scholar Martin Gardner [47]). 
That said, the book offers fresh content on two under-discussed figures 
adjacent to Sayers and the Inklings. Neil Tobin contributes two essays, one on 
detective fiction authors who explored performance magic (40–63) and the other 
on performance magicians who solve crimes in detective stories (64 –90). Both 
essays extensively discuss John Dickson Carr, much influenced by Chesterton 
and much supported by Sayers. Carr was also friends with William Lindsay 
Gresham, Joy Davidman’s ex-husband and author of the crime novel Nightmare 
Alley. While often mentioned in Davidman scholarship or discussions of 
Nightmare Alley adaptations, few crime fiction studies analyze Gresham. Tobin’s 
two pages discussing Gresham’s crime fiction and friendships with Carr, 
Reviews 
Mythlore 43.2, Spring/Summer 2025  303 
Clayton Rawson, and Bruce Elliot (49 –50) might be the first time an academic 
book has discussed Gresham as a crime writer since Charles Shapiro’s 1968 
essay. Carr is better -known but limited academic work has been published on 
him beyond Douglas G. Greene’s 1995 biography. 
Readers interested in the Detection Club will be disappointed by Magic, 
Magicians and Detective Fiction , but it does fill in some important gaps in the 
American detective fiction conversation. Read alongside existing scholarship, it 
may help scholars wanting to bridge Carr and Chesterton, Williams and Sayers, 
and other topics that intersect detective fiction with magic or myth.

Patrick Wynne 
 
A note on the cover design:  
The Mythopoeic Society has long used an inward-oriented triskelion design as its logo. In Mythlore 
#3 (July 1969), editor Glen GoodKnight reproduced a sketch by BG Callahan which labels the three 
arms to represent J.R.R. Tolkien, C.S. Lewis, and Charles Williams, and identifies them with the 
authors’ key concepts of, respectively, Eucatastrophe, Sehnsucht, and Romantic Theology, all 
leading to Joy at the center. Starting with issue #142, Mythlore’s cover features a plurality of 
outward-reaching triskelions, which  represent an open and inclusive approach to mythopoeic 
fantasy, still centered on the Joy it brings us as readers and scholars, and still anchored by, but 
quite rightly reaching well beyond, our original core three authors. The open source triskelion 
design used on the cover and interior of the journal is https://openclipart.org/ 
detail/278014/triskel by roshellin. 
 
A note on the type: 
The main body of the text of Mythlore is set in Palatino Linotype, a balanced and graceful lyrical 
modernist face created in 1948 by Hermann Zapf and based on humanist Renaissance designs. It 
was initially cut by hand and cast as a foundry type, even though phototype machines and early 
computers were in use at the time. This combination of modernism and deliberate anachronism, as 
well as the date of the type’s design, makes Palatino especially suitable for studies of the works of 
Tolkien, Lewis, Williams, a nd other modern fantasists. Mythlore first started using Palatino in 
1988, when it switched to computer typesetting. Starting in 1989 a face called Tolkien, adapted 
from Kelt, was used for titling for a number of years but later phased out. We have returned to 
Tolkien for titles and other accents in 2007. 
 
  

 
 
MYTHLORE  Submission Guidelines  
 
Mythlore is a refereed scholarly journal , available in print, electronic, 
and online open access formats.  Mythlore welcomes submissions 
focusing on the works and scholarship of J.R.R. Tolkien, C.S. Lewis, and 
Charles Williams, as well as articles on other authors and creators of 
mythic and mythopoeic literature  and media. We invite articles from 
all critical perspectives. All published articles have been recommended 
by at least one referee; in the case of authors connected with the journal 
or on the Society’s board, a minimum of two recommendations is 
required. Notes and reviews are not refereed. A more detailed  
statement of editorial purpose is available at 
http://dc.swosu.edu/mythlore/aimsandscope.html 
 
All submissions for publication should conform to The MLA Handbook 
9th ed. (New York: Modern Language Association, 2021), include a list 
of works cited, and normally should be 5,000-10,000 words. A detailed 
style guide  can be found at  https://dc.swosu.edu/mythlore/ 
style_sheet.pdf. Reviews of books should be 1,000-5,000 words. Please 
inquire with the editor before submitting book reviews or shorter 
Notes. Letters are also welcome. Submissions should be formatted as 
Microsoft Word or plain ASCII text files and submitted by setting up 
an author account at  http://dc.swosu.edu/mythlore/. Authors may 
expect a decision regarding publication within six to nine months. 
 
Janet Brennan Croft 
Editor, Mythlore
